# Preface and Introduction.

---

It might seem a daring undertaking to present these visions, mystical outpourings, or "revelations" of a medieval nun in their full extent and original form to the public, had not the learned Dr. Carl Greith, currently Bishop of St. Gallen, paved the way in his excellent book *German Mysticism in the Order of Preachers* <note>original: "die deutsche Mystik im Predigerorden"; the Order of Preachers is the formal name for the Dominican Order</note> ¹). He did this not only by drawing attention to the work and its author, but also by sharing a significant number of poetic fragments of these visions in modernized language. Regarding the manuscript—which, to my knowledge, is the only one containing the visions of Sister Mechthild—as well as the author herself, the most important points have already been stated in the aforementioned book; these details must be partially repeated and certainly expanded upon here.

The manuscript, No. 277, belonging to the Abbey Library of Einsiedeln, is a well-preserved volume, with its original white

---

¹) Freiburg im Breisgau (Herder) 1861. Octavo. <note>The "8." in the original text refers to "Octavo," a standard book size of the period.</note>

<summary>This preface introduces the publication of Sister Mechthild's medieval mystical visions, acknowledging the foundational scholarly work of Bishop Carl Greith and describing the primary manuscript held at Einsiedeln Abbey.</summary>

# Preface and Introduction.

A codex provided with a solid leather binding in octavo form <note>a book size resulting from folding a sheet of paper three times to form eight leaves</note>, containing 221 leaves, and written very clearly and boldly in the familiar Gothic minuscule of the 13th to 14th centuries. It is written in one column up to leaf 15, but from there onwards in two columns. The first part, *Mechthild’s Visions*, ends with leaf 166 a., which is followed by a few more pious thoughts on the seven canonical hours <note>original: "Tagzeiten"; the set times of prayer in the Christian liturgy</note> by the same hand, and a fragment from the unknown writing of a "**Friend of God**" <note>original: "Gottesfreund"; a reference to the 'Friends of God', a 14th-century mystical movement in the Rhineland and Switzerland</note>, a subject to which we shall return later.

The second part of the manuscript, from leaf 169 onwards, is by a different hand—just as beautiful and clear, but written smaller—and, like the first part, is quite richly provided with easily decipherable abbreviations. It contains essays and sermons by German mystics, primarily Master *Eckhart* <note>original: "Ekhart"</note>. This second part has been almost entirely published by Dr. *Franz Pfeiffer* in the second volume of his "**German Mystics**" ¹).

The language of the manuscript is Upper German <note>original: "oberdeutsche"</note>, as it was spoken and written around the Upper Rhine at the end of the 13th and the beginning of the 14th centuries. This language has largely been preserved among us Swiss in our dialects and is, in any case, easily understood, although many familiar words have now acquired a different meaning. ²) The style is powerful and quite concise, though the orthography <note>spelling</note> is not consistently uniform. It could not be carried out consistently in this printed edition either, and I therefore held myself,

---

¹) *The German Mystics of the 14th Century*. Leipzig 1845. (2 vols.)
²) For example, in this book: *Mûtwillen* <note>willfulness/mischief</note> instead of courage, *bekañtniss* <note>confession</note> instead of knowledge, *erlich* <note>honest</note> instead of magnificent, *leichtfertig* <note>frivolous/reckless</note> instead of easy, *wunderlich* <note>strange</note> instead of wonderful, *unmenschlich* <note>inhumane</note> instead of superhuman, *aber* <note>but</note> instead of again, *in* <note>in</note> instead of them, *vernehmen* <note>to hear/interrogate</note> instead of to recognize, *wan* <note>when</note> instead of then, *allein* <note>alone</note> instead of although, *durch* <note>through</note> instead of for, *verklagen* <note>to sue/accuse</note> instead of to lament, *diemütig* <note>humble</note> instead of lowly <note>original: "niederträchtig"; in modern German this means base or malicious, but historically it meant lowly/humble</note>, the sweet God, etc.

<summary>This page provides a codicological description of the manuscript, noting its transition from single to double columns and its contents, including works by Mechthild of Magdeburg and Meister Eckhart. The editor also notes the linguistic challenges of the Upper Rhine dialect and the shifts in word meanings since the 14th century.</summary>

# Preface and Introduction.

Where obvious errors do not occur, I have adhered strictly to the spelling of the original manuscript. I believed I could allow myself this liberty all the more easily because we are dealing here with the only—and indeed a high-quality—manuscript of a medieval work. In this regard, I appeal to <term>Franz Pfeiffer</term> <note>A prominent 19th-century German philologist known for editing medieval texts.</note>, who remarked on the publication of Herrmann's *Lives of the Saints*:

> For a work that exists in only one manuscript, a strictly critical treatment of the text—as is usually applied to works of the 13th century—was not quite feasible; indeed, I would have been fully justified in providing a literal reproduction... however, I did not wish to evade the requirements rightly placed upon editors of Old German writings, even at the risk of having misunderstood something here and there.

My minor changes in the present book mostly concern the <term>punctuation</term> <note>original: "Interpunktion"</note>, which had to be corrected as necessary for the understanding of the text. I also took the liberty, though only in rare cases, of restoring the frequently recurring rhymes, assonances, and alliterations, which clearly harmonized better in the original.

A peculiar difficulty arose from determining which parts of the text should be set in verse, as everything in the manuscript runs continuously as prose, even though many sections decidedly point toward verse, while on the other hand, only faint echoes of such are noticeable. The deciding factor for me here, alongside the <term>rhyme</term>, was the higher flight of speech or feeling, which in most cases also makes the language more poetic. This is often the case in this writing where persons are introduced speaking, or where the contemplation or the <term>vision</term> <note>A common literary device in medieval mystical texts where the author describes a divine revelation.</note> hurries toward its conclusion.

The history of the manuscript is not without interest and

<summary>The editor explains his conservative approach to the manuscript's spelling and his methods for distinguishing between prose and verse in the original continuous text. He justifies his editorial choices by citing the scholar Franz Pfeiffer and emphasizes the importance of preserving the poetic "flight" of the work.</summary>
<keywords>editorial methodology, Franz Pfeiffer, medieval manuscripts, verse vs prose, punctuation, philology, poetic structure, visions</keywords>

# Preface and Introduction.

...is also linked to the history of religious life in the High Valley of **Einsiedeln** <note>A village in Switzerland famous for its Benedictine abbey and the "Black Madonna"; it was a major center of medieval pilgrimage.</note> during the Middle Ages. Specifically, from a very early time—the exact date cannot be determined—pious **Forest Sisters** <note>original: "Waldschwestern"; these were semi-monastic women or anchoresses who lived religious lives without being fully enclosed nuns.</note> lived there, first individually and then distributed among four houses, which were later united into a single convent. They were called the Forest Sisters. In one of these four houses, called the "Front Au" <note>original: "vorder Au"; "Au" refers to a meadow or floodplain.</note>, **Heinrich von Rumerschein** of Basel (at St. Peter’s) wrote the following note in the 14th century, which was later attached to the manuscript:

> To the sisters in the Front Au: You should know that the book which was given to you by the woman at the Golden Ring, which is called *The Light of the Godhead* <note>original: "das liecht der Gotheit"; this is a reference to the famous mystical work by Mechthild of Magdeburg.</note>, you must take good care of it, so that it shall serve all the houses of the forest and shall never leave the forest. It should remain in each house for one month, circulating from one to the other as needed, and you must be its special guardians, for she held you in special trust. Pray also for me, who was her confessor, though unworthily, alas. From me, Lord Heinrich von Rumerschein of Basel, at St. Peter’s. — *on the reverse:* to the Front Au. <note>original Middle High German: "Den swesteren in der vorderen öwe: Ir sônt wissen, dz das bûch, dz v́ch wart von der zem Guldin Ringe, dz do heist, das liecht der Gotheit..."</note>

A similar note from Rumerschein is attached to **Codex 268**, which, also containing mystical content, was given by the same benefactress to the sisters in Alleg (Albegg, another of the four sister houses in Einsiedeln). To these sisters, Rumerschein writes:

> You should know that the book given to you by the young lady Grete at the Golden Ring, which Kunigund... <note>original: "Ir sônt wissen, dz das bûch, dz v́ch wart von jungfrow Greten zem gůldin Ring, dz kůngunt ..."</note>

One line has been erased here; the remaining content matches the letter to the sisters in the Front Au mentioned above, except for this addition:

> You should know that you must not lend <note>original: "leng"</note> it outside of the forest.

Now, remarkably, this "Grete at the Golden Ring" in Basel is most likely none other than **Margaretha**, the daughter of the well-known religious enthusiast **Nikolaus of Basel** <note>A controversial 14th-century layman and leader of the "Friends of God" movement, later executed for heresy.</note>...

<summary>This page describes the historical ownership of the manuscript, tracing it to the "Forest Sisters" of Einsiedeln and a gift from Margaretha of Basel, daughter of the mystic Nikolaus of Basel. It includes translated letters from their confessor, Heinrich von Rumerschein, regarding the circulation of the book.</summary>

VII

# Preface and Introduction.

Basel, whose mystical writings Dr. Karl Schmidt ¹) has edited and accompanied with a very solid biographical sketch of the author, part of which had already been shared in the book *Basel in the 14th Century* and in other writings. "Margaretha," as K. Schmidt relates (p. 71), "became a <term>Beguine</term> <note>A member of a lay religious community of women who lived a life of poverty and devotion without taking permanent vows.</note>, a <term>conversa</term> <note>original: "Conversa"; a lay sister who often entered a religious community later in life.</note>, acquired a piece of wood from Christ’s Cross that had been stolen from the Cathedral, and returned it; in 1376 she bequeathed her entire fortune to the <term>Dominicans</term> <note>An order of friars known for preaching and scholarship, closely associated with the medieval mystical movements.</note> and chose her grave among them. She is without doubt the Margaretha of the Golden Ring, who is remembered in the letters of Heinrich von Nördlingen as a particular <term>Friend of God</term> <note>original: "Freundin Gottes"; a member of the "Friends of God" movement, a 14th-century network of laypeople and clergy seeking an intense, internal spiritual life.</note>. The Beguine House of the Black Bear was adjacent to the House of the Golden Ring; in the latter <unclear>itself</unclear> a collection <note>Likely referring to a religious "Sammung," a small community of laypeople living together.</note> seems to have existed for a time. From all this, it can be concluded that in the family of the Golden Ring, the inclination toward a spiritual, mystical life was well-established." So far Dr. Schmidt.

It is now evident from the aforementioned letter who the confessor of the charitable benefactress of the Golden Ring was, and it is clear that connections existed between the <term>Friends of God</term> in Basel and their patrons and patronesses and the <term>Beguines</term> in central Switzerland. This is further supported by other facts, such as the relationship of the <term>Friends of God</term> to Engelberg, their settlement in the Herrgottswald at the foot of Mount Pilatus, etc., which C. Schmidt (in the work cited above) recounts.

In Einsiedeln itself, no further trace of this connection has been found other than this manuscript along with the second one mentioned earlier, which, however, contains only writings of actual German mystics...

¹) Vienna, 1866. 8vo.
²) Basel 1856, p. 283: *Nicholas of Basel and the Friends of God* and especially p. 92 in the *Topography of Old Basel* by Dr. Fechter.

VIII

# Preface and Introduction.

<note>The sentence continues from the previous page: "It is now evident from the aforementioned manuscript which..."</note> contains and from which much was shared by Franz Pfeiffer in *Haupt’s Journal for German Antiquity* (Vol. 8, p. 209), and in Volume 2 of the *German Mystics*. At what time these two excellently written and preserved manuscripts came into the Abbey Library of Einsiedeln is uncertain. A hand from the 15th century notes: "This book belongs in the four houses in the forest," <note>original: "Dis bůch håret in die vier hůser in dem walde"; this refers to four small religious hermitages in the forest near Einsiedeln.</note> while a later hand from the 16th century notes: "Belonging to the House of God of St. Peter on the Brook in Schwyz." <note>original: "Dem Gotshuss S. peter vff dem Bach in Schwitz gehörig"; referring to a specific convent in the region.</note>

Regarding the content and author of the present writing, the text itself provides information right at the beginning, first in English <note>original: "lateinischer" (Latin)</note> and then in English <note>original: "deutscher" (German)</note>. It was revealed in the German language in the year 1250 <note>There is a minor hole in the manuscript near this date.</note> and during the fifteen years thereafter to a pious <note>The word "pious" (frommen) is obscured by an ink stain in the original manuscript.</note> sister. This sister lived a godly life for over forty years, according to the rule of the <term>Order of Preachers</term> <note>Also known as the Dominicans, an order focused on preaching and intellectual study.</note>. Her "Revelations," however, were collected and written down by a brother of the same order. The name of this brother could not be determined; however, the graced sister is named several times, both in the text and in the chapter headings. For example, on page 168: "How Sister Mechthild gives thanks, etc." and on page 215: "This writing that stands in this book has flowed out from the living Godhead into Sister Mechthild’s heart and is thus faithfully set down here, just as it was given out from her heart by God and written by her hands." <note>original: "Dise schrift die in disem bůche stat, ist gevlossen vs von der lebenden gotheit in Swester Mehtilden herze..."</note>

The apparent contradiction of the passage just cited with the previous remark—that the book was collected and written by a Dominican brother—finds its natural resolution in the word *collected*. Thus, one may assume, along with Greith (p. 207), that this brother collected and transcribed the individual sheets written by Mechthild herself. That she wrote these "Revelations" herself is also proven by what she says on page 140: "Master Henry, me..."

It explains that while she wrote the original pages, a Dominican brother was responsible for collecting and organizing them into the current book format.</summary>

IX

# Preface and Introduction.

"...[It] grieves [me] . . . that I, a sinful woman, must write, for I can describe the true knowledge and the holy, glorious contemplation to no one, but only these words; they seem all too small to me compared to the eternal truth." <note>original Middle High German: "iamert . . . das ich sündig wip schriben můs, das ich die ware bekantnisse und die heligen herlichen anschŏwunge nieman mag geschriben, sunder dise wort alleine, si dunken mich gegen die ewigen warheit allzekleine."</note> Master Heinrich was her biological brother and also a member of the <term>Order of Preachers</term> <note>Another name for the Dominican Order, a Catholic religious order founded by St. Dominic.</note>.

That Sister Mechthild belonged to this order is likely beyond doubt, for on every occasion reference is made to this—at that time so freshly blooming and fruitful—order and its founder, whom Mechthild explicitly calls her father. Furthermore, the testimony at the beginning of the book, "Following perfectly the footsteps of the brothers of the Order of Preachers," <note>original Latin: "Sequens perfecte vestigia fratrum ordinis praedicatorum"</note> is clear enough. While she is also called a <term>Beguine</term> <note>The Beguines were a lay religious group of women who lived in community but did not take formal vows like nuns.</note> and calls herself such a few times, this is corrected by the passage just cited; moreover, at that time the term "Beguine" still carried the more general meaning of a soul seeking God in a special way, similar to how one might say "Sister."

Thus, Dominic is named and praised above all other founders of orders, and his image is depicted most lovably. A highly important task is envisioned for his order, both for the present and for the end of times. Specifically mentioned are Brother Heinrich—perhaps the childhood friend of the famous Brother Jordan—as well as a Brother Baldwin and a Brother Albert, likely <term>Albert the Great</term> <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), one of the most famous medieval philosophers and theologians.</note>. We are not dealing here with Mechthild of Sponheim, the contemporary of <term>Saint Bernard</term> <note>Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a mystic and leader of the Cistercian order.</note> and Saint Hildegard of Bingen, nor with Mechthild of Diessen and Edelstetten, who died as early as 1160, nor with Mechthild of Helfta, who belongs to the Benedictine Order, but rather with a completely different personality.

"Regarding the homeland and the Dominican convent¹), where our—"

¹) Greith, in the place cited, p. 207. Below, p. 243.

X

# Preface and Introduction.

Since Sister Mechthild lived [there] for over forty years, some passages seem to point toward Thuringia or Saxony. We read: "Of the distress of a war <note>Middle High German: *vrluges*.</note>. I was commanded with a holy earnestness that I should pray for the distress that is now in the lands of Saxony and Thuringia," in which war, according to Mechthild’s description, terrible atrocities were committed against houses of God and through highway robbery. In another meditation ¹) she speaks of the messengers whom God sent for the rescue of fallen Christendom and names among these Saint Elisabeth and the saints Dominic, Francis, and Peter Martyr—the first martyr of the <term>Order of Preachers</term> <note>The Dominican Order.</note>—and finally Sister Jutte of Sangerhausen, about whom it was revealed to her: I have <unclear>sent</unclear> <note>The original German word "gesant" is obscured by an ink smudge in the manuscript.</note> her to the heathens as a messenger with her holy prayer and her good example. <note>original Middle High German: "die han ich den heidene gesant ze botten mit irme heligen gebete und mit irme gûten bilde."</note> "This likely occurred around the year 1260, when the <term>Teutonic Order</term> <note>A German medieval military-religious order of knights.</note> under the <term>Grand Master</term> Anno of Sangerhausen undertook a new crusade against the Prussians."

This Jutte of Sangerhausen and the mention of the secularized <term>cathedral canons</term> <note>Administrative clergy attached to a cathedral; Mechthild often criticized them for living too luxuriously.</note> of Magdeburg, against whom Mechthild speaks with sharp rebuke, led Mone ²) to place this sister in the Monastery of St. Agnes near Magdeburg. He writes in a note: "The Monastery of St. Agnes lies on the east side of the new city of Magdeburg and originally followed the rule of the <term>Cistercian Order</term>. H. Beyer published a history of the nunnery of St. Agnes in the 17th volume (pp. 59, 156, 260, and 330) of the *General Archive for the History of the Prussian State* by L. v. Ledebur, in which precisely the most important point is missing, namely, that

---

¹) p. 166.
²) *Collection of Sources for Baden History*. Vol. 4. p. 31. <note>original Latin/German: "Quellensammlung z. Bad. Geschichte."</note>

It also introduces the scholarly theory by Mone that Mechthild resided at the St. Agnes monastery in Magdeburg.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly investigation into the identity of the mystic Mechthild, specifically debating her affiliation with the Monastery of St. Agnes in Magdeburg and the shifting religious orders of that house.</meta>

# Preface and Introduction.

XI

the poetess Mechthild, whose works Greith published, was Abbess there in 1273. The superior Jutte of Sangershausen named in Mechthild’s poems is the Abbess Jutte of St. Agnes from 1270." Mone then asserts that Mechthild was not a Dominican; however, as previously stated, she likely was one, as the entire work demonstrates. A great many monasteries of the Cistercian Order adopted the <term>Rule of Dominic</term> <note>The set of regulations followed by the Dominican Order, emphasizing preaching, study, and apostolic poverty.</note>. — However, a great difficulty arises again from the documentary evidence showing that in the period from 1250 and the following decades, St. Agnes was actually inhabited by Cistercian nuns. Through the kind mediation of my highly esteemed friend, Count R. von Stillfried, Count of Alcantara, I gathered inquiries on this matter from the most proven researchers and experts in the history of Prussia. What the gentlemen Riedel, Ledebur, and Mülverstädt (Archivist in Magdeburg) communicated along with Count Stillfried himself, indicates that the nuns of St. Agnes in the New Town near Magdeburg were still called "Cistercian nuns" in the year 1260, according to a charter of this house. Later, it seems the monastery adopted the Benedictine Order; as early as 1270 it is described as following the rule of St. Benedict <note>original Latin: *de regula S. Benedicti*</note> and in 1311 even more specifically, of the Order of St. Benedict <note>original Latin: *ordinis S. Benedicti*</note>. — (Beyer, in the work cited above, p. 276). "The Mechthild in question," Mr. Ledebur remarks, "is indeed listed at the conclusion of the treatise on page 370 in the line of abbesses, specifically with the dates 1271 and 1281; the year 1271 is documented there by records. However, Beyer had no knowledge of the interesting circumstance that Mechthild composed German songs." Mr. von Mülverstädt simply refers to the "Magdeburg History Sheets" (published by the local historical society), Volume II (1867), page 339 ff., and to the aforementioned "General Archive" by Ledebur.

# Preface and Introduction.

XII

Anyone who reads the following pages attentively will find it hard to imagine how our sister Mechthild later became the Abbess of a <term>Bernardine convent</term> <note>A monastery of the Cistercian Order, named after St. Bernard of Clairvaux.</note>. That there was, at that time and specifically in Magdeburg, a spiritual connection between both orders <note>The Cistercians and the Dominicans.</note> and the respective convents, is also evident from a miracle recounted by Mone (in the work cited, p. 30): namely, how in Germany <note>original Latin: *Theutonia*</note> (Magdeburg) a Cistercian Abbess and her sisters offered many prayers for a deceased <term>Friar Preacher</term> <note>A member of the Dominican Order (Ordo Praedicatorum).</note> named Albert, after which this brother appeared to them. While Mone is likely correct in thinking here of the Abbess Jutte and that Albert who was the brother of our Mechthild, the mystery is not yet solved by this. The work now presented in print will provide more capable experts and critics with starting points for new research. In the meantime, to distinguish her from the aforementioned and others of the same name, the author may well continue to be called Mechthild of Magdeburg.

However, let us not forget the main point—the content of this remarkable and rare book—amidst our focus on the poetess and the external appearance of the manuscript. Regarding this, I refer above all to what Greith says in his frequently cited book. It would also lead too far afield if I were to lose myself here in the wonderful and often charming labyrinth of medieval German mysticism.

One thing above all I ask you to note: the type of mysticism in this book is significantly different from the speculative writings of the slightly later Masters, especially a Master Eckhart and the so-called <term>Friends of God</term> <note>A 14th-century mystical movement that emphasized a personal, inner relationship with God.</note>. To be sure, this term is mentioned a few times by Mechthild, but there it is to be understood in a more general sense, for she stands throughout much more firmly on the ground of the monastic rule, and her

# Preface and Introduction.

XIII

Visions almost exclusively bear the hallmarks of emotional and imaginative outpourings rather than rational speculation. The difference in perception and language is already strikingly evident in a small fragment of a writing by a <term>Friend of God</term> <note>The "Gottesfreunde" were a 14th-century mystical group, primarily in the Rhineland, who sought a deep, internal relationship with God.</note> (see below, p. 283) attached to the manuscript, which in any case does not originate from Mechthild.

To say something of the content, however, I will let Greith speak here:

> “Mechthild drew the material for her songs, meditations, and moral teachings from the Christian faith and her own personal experiences of her mystical states. In them, she celebrates the intimate relationship between God and the soul, mediated by <term>Minne</term> <note>A Middle High German word for love; in this context, it refers to the mystical, often intensely personal and poetic love between the human soul and God.</note>, and further down, the reciprocal relationship between soul and body (sensuality, the ‘corpse’), which has developed into mutual hostility through the desires of sin. Her didactic aphorisms dwell on the virtues and vices, the perfections and flaws of the soul on its pilgrimage upward; at times, she shows a particular preference for the form of the dialogue, which she knows how to conduct skillfully between God and the soul, Love and the soul, Love and Knowledge, and between Knowledge and Conscience... However, the ‘Revelations’ she received in her hours of contemplation also extend across the otherworldly regions of Hell, Purgatory, and Heaven with a distinctive style of depiction. Repeatedly, and not without a certain sharpness in the manner of the <term>Blessed Hildegard</term> <note>original: "Hildegardis." This refers to St. Hildegard of Bingen, the influential 12th-century German mystic and polymath.</note>, she laments the fallen state of Christendom in both Church and Empire, among the clergy and the laity. This, combined with some daring doctrines, may have brought upon her the misunderstanding of her fellow sisters, about which she at the...

# Preface and Introduction.

->XIV<-

often complains. The enlightenment that has been granted to her, she wishes to owe to no school of human wisdom, "with which one, as she so beautifully says somewhere, can gain much and also lose much"; on the contrary, she testifies to having received it from above.

The views and expressions in this book are indeed often daring, and anyone wishing to apply a strictly dogmatic standard could easily find heretical ideas. Thus, Mechthild says of Mary: "Her son is God and she a goddess" <note>original: "Ir sun ist got und si göttine"</note> and in another place the soul is called the "goddess of all creatures." <note>original: "aller creaturen göttine"</note> In Book 2, Chapter 19, it says: "Thus she (the soul) truly sees and acknowledges how God is all things in all things." <note>original: "got ist allú ding in allen dingen"</note> In addition to the explanations that Greith (cited above) provides regarding the meaning of such statements, I mention a passage by a mystic from the same manuscript which contains Mechthild’s visions. It says on folio 169: "The saints say: all things are God, insofar as they have eternally been in God. Not that we were in God in the coarseness as we are now; we were in God eternally as the art is in the master. <note>This is a common medieval philosophical analogy: just as a building exists as a perfect concept in the mind of the architect before it is built, all of creation exists eternally as a 'thought' within God.</note> God looked upon himself and saw all things."

Striking from a dogmatic perspective is the vision in which the ecstatic sister saw how Saint John the Baptist "read Mass for the poor girl," <note>The phrase "poor girl" (armen Dirne) is Mechthild’s humble way of referring to herself.</note> although he was a layman. ¹) It gives the impression that this was intended to hint at the doctrine of a <term>universal priesthood</term> <note>The belief that all believers have a direct relationship with God and share in the priestly office, a concept that could be seen as challenging the exclusive authority of the ordained Catholic clergy.</note>. However, she says later in her justification ²): "That John the Baptist sang Mass for the poor girl was not fleshly, it was thus spiritual, so that the soul alone beheld and enjoyed it; but the body had nothing of it, except what it [received] from the soul's nobility in its human [nature]..."

# Preface and Introduction. XV

"...[no sense] could grasp, therefore the words must sound human." <note>This fragment completes a sentence from the previous page, explaining that because divine experiences transcend human senses, they must be described using earthly, human language.</note>

These correct and beautiful words may also provide a pointer toward the right understanding of many other daring and unusual images and words. I am thinking particularly of the descriptions of spiritual <term>Minne</term> <note>A Middle High German word for love, used here to describe the intense, often ecstatic relationship between the soul and God.</note> which, according to modern views, are often all too free. In this, one is involuntarily reminded of the unselfconscious naivety of devout medieval artists—of both Germanic and Romance heritage—in their depiction of the sensual and natural, specifically the sexual. Holy Scripture, especially the Song of Solomon <note>Also known as the Song of Songs, a book of the Bible historically interpreted by mystics as an allegory for the passionate love between Christ and the soul.</note> in its symbolic application to spiritual love, gave such depictions a higher consecration. Right at the beginning of Mechthild’s visions, sixteen types of love are briefly described; the most striking of these is likely:

->The German love from God’s teaching,<-
->It bows itself most gladly toward a child.<-

What is this "German love"? Is its holy simplicity perhaps indicated by the second verse? Greith (p. 212) says: 

> "The science and particularly the poetry of Christian mysticism has at all times found in the Song of Solomon an analogous ideal for what it attempted to express regarding the supersensory communion that prevails between God and the soul in love... Just as those who are pure—in their contemplation of the eternal mystery which found its purely human expression in that 'Song of Songs'—take no offense at its lush imagery, because, as the Apostle teaches, 'to the pure, all things are pure, but to the defiled, nothing is pure,' <note>A reference to the New Testament, Titus 1:15: "Omnia munda mundis."</note> so too in the profound era of the Middle Ages did it rarely cause serious concern when the mystics exercised a freedom in their depictions that would necessarily cause grave offense in our own time."

<summary>This page explores the eroticized language of medieval mysticism, justifying Mechthild of Magdeburg's "daring" imagery by linking it to the biblical Song of Solomon and the artistic naivety of the Middle Ages.</summary>
<keywords>Minne, Mechthild of Magdeburg, Song of Solomon, Christian mysticism, Greith, spiritual love, medieval art</keywords>

### Preface and Introduction. XVI

All of this justifies the descriptions of our poetess all the more, because they belong more to the realm of poetry than to science <note>In this context, "science" (*Wissenschaft*) refers to formal theological scholarship or academic study.</note>. These outpourings of a rapturous soul are poetry and therefore lack all those forms of scholarship which so often stray too far from beauty. Consequently, there are no citations to be found—not even from Holy Scripture—for everything here is purely an immediate depiction of inner states of the soul. As these states change, so too do the <term>style</term> <note>original: "Styl"</note> and the language, which not infrequently rises with striking power and beauty. Mechthild rightly speaks at times of a <term>courtly language</term> <note>original: "Hofsprache." This refers to the refined, elevated language used in royal courts, as opposed to common speech.</note>: she greets her God "in the courtly language, which is not heard in this kitchen." <note>original: "in der hovesprache, die man in diser kuchin nit vernimet." The "kitchen" is a metaphor for the mundane, physical world or the body.</note> This courtly language is none other than the poetic, and it possesses both its external and internal beauties.

There is generally more harmony to be found in this old High or Upper German dialect, and in Mechthild’s hands, it often becomes true music. The abundance of rhymes, assonances, and alliterations—precisely because they appear entirely unforced, natural, and artless—produces all the more impact. "Your wonder has wounded me," <note>original: "Din wunder hat mich verwundet." This is a classic example of alliteration and wordplay common in mystical literature.</note> says Mechthild, for example; and this is no mere pun, for it came entirely unbidden in a state of inspiration, of enthusiasm, of ecstasy.

Mechthild herself describes, right in the second chapter of the work, how the soul leaves the body and comes to God, her Host, her Bridegroom, and what she sees and hears there. When she then returns to the body, the body asks: "Where have you been? You return so lovely, beautiful and powerful, free and wise? .. Then she says: Be silent, murderer, let your complaining be." <note>The soul calls the body a "murderer" because the physical senses and needs were often viewed by mystics as things that "kill" or stifle the spiritual life.</note> And when she adds: "That is a greeting that has many veins, which presses out from the flowing God into the poor..."

### Preface and Introduction. XVII

"...dry souls at all times with new knowledge and in new contemplation and in special <term>enjoyment</term> <note>original: "gebruchunge" (modern German: *Genuss*). In mystical theology, this refers to "fruition," the blissful, experiential union with God.</note> and new presence," then with this, four of the most important characteristics of all genuine <term>inspiration</term> <note>original: "Begeisterung," here meaning a divinely inspired state or spiritual fervor rather than simple excitement.</note> are described: the recognition of reason, the vision of imagination, the enjoyment of feeling, and the immediacy of a higher state—the concentration of past and future into the present, of heaven and earth, hell, purgatory, and paradise within the eye of the soul. These four qualities are found in Mechthild’s poetry. The thinker will find in this writing, which might appear to the sober mind as mere <term>fantasizing</term> <note>original: "Phantasterei."</note>, grains of gold of deep speculation. This spiritual vision explains the truth and vibrancy of the depictions, as well as the individual images and the truly epic content of many visions. This is especially true of those that look upon the secret of the <term>Incarnation</term> <note>original: "Menschwerdung," the theological concept of God becoming human in the form of Jesus Christ.</note> of the Son of God (Book 5, Chapter 23), in which Lucifer and his servant Satan are so excellently portrayed as the hostile principle. Furthermore, the detailed description of the typical images of the <term>Lenten veil</term> <note>original: "Hungertuch" (literally "hunger-cloth"). A large decorated textile used in the Middle Ages to veil the altar during Lent; it often depicted scenes of the Passion to instruct the faithful.</note> even recalls the shield of Achilles or Aeneas in the works of <term>Homer and Virgil</term> <note>The author compares Mechthild's descriptive power to the "ekphrasis" (graphic verbal description) found in the greatest epics of Greek and Roman antiquity.</note>.

Such descriptions often transition into ingenious allegories, several of which were treated in similar ways many times during the Middle Ages. For example, the Passion of Christ applied to the spiritual life, suffering, and death of the soul (Book 7, Chapter 53), or "The Spiritual Cloister," a title under which four old German manuscripts can be found in Munich alone. ¹)

### Preface and Introduction. XVIII

A similar manuscript, which attributes this allegory to Saint *Bernard* <note>original: "Bernhard"; referring to Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), the influential Cistercian mystic whose writings deeply affected medieval spirituality.</note>, is also held at Einsiedeln <note>A Benedictine monastery in Switzerland where the most important manuscript of Mechthild’s work was rediscovered.</note>.

Described in a detailed and excellent manner are the golden *Penny of the Mass*, the *Crown of the Communion of Saints*—analogous to the *Golden Smithy* by *Konrad von Würzburg* <note>A major German poet of the late 13th century known for his complex allegories.</note>—the *Dwelling of the Soul*, *Hell*, *Purgatory*, and especially lovely, *Paradise*, where *Enoch* and *Elijah* still dwell today. Furthermore, there is the *Spiritual Court Life*, the *Knight's Struggle*, the *Resting Place of Grace*, the *Church*, the *Eagle of Contemplation*, and so forth.

In these descriptions, the poet often finds herself at a loss for words. Of heaven, she knows no more to say than:

->As a bee [carries] honey<-
->From a full hive on its foot.<-
<note>original Middle High German: "Als ein bini honiges / Vs einem vollen stok an sinem fůss mag getragen." This metaphor suggests that the soul can only bring back a tiny, sticky residue of the vast sweetness it experiences in the presence of God.</note>

On the other hand, words flow only too abundantly when she describes ghastly and terrifying things, such as the pains of hell or the struggle of demons for a departing soul; it is as if such images were executed with a certain preference. This too is analogous to and in the spirit of medieval art, especially the visual arts, which led even an *Orcagna* <note>Andrea Orcagna (c. 1310–1368), a Florentine painter known for his monumental "Triumph of Death."</note> and *Giovanni da Fiesole* <note>Better known as Fra Angelico (c. 1395–1455); the editor refers here to his vivid depictions of the Last Judgment.</note> to such monstrosities. (Cf. e.g., p. 83.) The sculptural representation of the different sections of the otherworldly realms, above all of Paradise (Book 7, Chapter 57) and Hell (Book 3, Chapter 21), and the various punishments according to the variety of the sin, are reminiscent of *Dante*, of whom, of course, *Mechthild* could have had no knowledge.

Such appalling descriptions are also found in the vision of the final times, the time of the Antichrist, in which a great and difficult task is intended for the <term>Order of Preachers</term> <note>original: "Predigerorden"; another name for the Dominican Order, who were Mechthild's contemporaries and spiritual directors.</note>.

<summary>The editor discusses Mechthild’s use of vivid allegories and contrasts her difficulty in describing heaven with her graphic, Dante-esque descriptions of hell and the end times. These descriptions are compared to the works of medieval poets like Konrad von Würzburg and painters like Fra Angelico.</summary>
<keywords>Mechthild von Magdeburg, allegory, Paradise, Hell, medieval art, Dante, Order of Preachers, Antichrist, Konrad von Würzburg</keywords>

# Preface and Introduction. XIX

Alongside these descriptions bordering on the crude, one finds again the most delicate and charming images, such as in the description of the tomb of the holy Apostle John (Book 4, Chapter 23), regarding which the angels sing every seven hours:
> "Between his body and the creation of the kingdom of heaven there is nothing more than a thin wall like the skin of an egg, and yet it is as eternally firm that no body may pass through it until the Last Judgment."
<note>original Middle High German: "Zwischent sinem lichamen und der schöpfnisse des himelriches ist nit me deñe ein dūne want als eines eies hût, und ist doch als ewig veste, das dar kein lichame me dur mag untz an den jungesten tag."</note>

In rich abundance, the spring of the smallest poetic figures bubbles through the entire work, especially in the first book. Many of these images also appear in Holy Scripture or among older poets. For instance, Otfrid <note>Otfrid of Weissenburg (c. 800–870), a monk and poet who wrote a famous Gospel harmony in Old High German rhyme.</note> already calls the Holy Virgin a "dove without gall"; most, however, are new and all the more vivid.

Poetry also speaks through individual short sayings, such as: "Whoever dies of <term>Love</term> <note>original: "miñe" (Minne). In this context, it refers to the mystical, selfless love for God that defines Mechthild's spirituality.</note>, him shall one bury in God." Or where life in God is discussed: "the fish cannot drown in water, etc." (p. 21). Grace comes from above: "That the eagle flies so high, he does not have to thank his own will." <note>original: "der ŵelen danken." Here, "will" or "choice" implies that the eagle's height is a gift of nature/grace rather than personal effort.</note> The soul should guard itself against sin: "just like a mouse that sits in the trap and waits for its death." On one occasion, the poetess is enraptured in God,
> "so that she lifted herself up without any effort of her own and wrapped herself rightly into the Holy Trinity <note>original: "helige drivaltekeit"</note>, as a child wraps itself in the cloak of its mother and lays itself right against her breast."

Amidst such poetry, the frequent fragmentation of thoughts into numbered lists sounds somewhat prosaic, as was the fashion in medieval prose, and as we find almost throughout the work of mystics and preachers, such as Brother Berthold <note>Berthold of Regensburg (c. 1210–1272), the most celebrated German preacher of the 13th century, famous for his structured, popular oratory.</note>. Examples of this in the present writing are found in abundance even in the chapter headings.

# Preface and Introduction. XX

The echoes of the cultural conditions and customs of the Middle Ages are pleasant to encounter. The "Empire" <term>Kaiserreich</term> <note>The Holy Roman Empire served as a primary model for how medieval people imagined the hierarchy of Heaven.</note> is held in high regard: "it shall also be depicted, adorned, and flowered upon the crown (the glory of God) until the lowliest of peasants <note>original: "jungesten geburen"</note> forevermore, according to how worthily they have served God." The court, courtly life, and courtly manners are often used as imagery, as is already evident from the aforementioned "courtly language" <note>original: "Hofsprache"</note> "which one does not hear in the kitchen. A game begins of which the body knows nothing, nor the peasants <note>original: "dörper" (laborers/villagers)</note> at the plow, nor the knights in the tournament." The soul is dressed for her "journey to court" <note>original: "Hofreise"</note> "with the clothes one should wear at the palace." Chapter 4 of the first book speaks of this journey to court, as does Book 4, Chapter 17: *Of a woman who loved being at court.* Remarkable in this regard is also the chapter (Book 3, 18) "of the knight’s struggle with full weapons against desire." If, for example, it says there, a man untrained in battle wanted to

> enter a princely tournament,
> his life would soon be taken from him.
> Therefore I (says God) must spare the people
> who so easily come to a fall:
> I let them fight with the children,
> so that they may win a garland of flowers as their reward.
<note>original Middle High German: "in fürsten turneien komen, / dem were schiere sin lip benomen. / Darumbe mûs ich (sagt Gott) der lûte schonen, / die so lihte ze valle komen: / Die lan ich striten mit den kinden, / vf de si ein blůmenschappel ze lone gewiñen."</note>

The Crusades are also mentioned in several places. The vice of highway robbery is evident from the previously cited description of the war in Saxony and Thuringia, where it says: "Those who rob on the road on foot—if there were no war, they would be thieves and dishonest people." <note>original: "Die die strasse röbent ze fůsse, were kein urlůg, so weren sie diebe und valsche lûte."</note>

But enough, and perhaps already too much of this. I now submit this writing to the judgment of the public, and hope to have provided with it a fine contribution to the knowledge of older German literature. While Mone <note>Franz Joseph Mone (1796–1871), a German historian and archivist.</note> (in the place cited) notes that Mr. Greith <note>Carl Johann Greith (1807–1882), a Bishop and scholar who discovered the manuscripts of Mechthild's "The Flowing Light of the Godhead."</note> had edited the works of Mechthild...

He concludes by presenting the edition as a contribution to German literary history, mentioning fellow scholars Mone and Greith.</summary>

# Preface and Introduction. XXI

If one examines the text, a glance at Greith’s—otherwise highly valuable—book on *German Mysticism* will show that only a small portion of the present work is shared there. This portion, moreover, was translated into modern language as the book’s purpose and readership required. Finally, regarding the poetry, mostly only the lyrical love songs <term>Minnelied</term> <note>These were poems of courtly love, which mystics like Mechthild adapted to describe the soul's relationship with God.</note> and some didactic and allegorical passages were included, while the poetically most beautiful and sublime parts—such as the aforementioned chapter on the *Incarnation of Christ*—were omitted. Generally, I would consider the epic <note>Here, "epic" refers to the narrative or descriptive storytelling elements of her visions.</note> content of the work, viewed as poetry, to be of higher value and more significant for literary history than the lyrical *Minnesang*. I hope, therefore, that our literary historians will not completely overlook this book, however little taste many of them may find in it in other respects.

Among Prussian scholars, who have done so much for the German language and its history, this oversight is even less to be feared, as the poetess is their fellow countrywoman. According to a communication from Mr. von Ledebur <note>Leopold von Ledebur (1799–1877) was a prominent historian and researcher of German nobility.</note>, another Mathilde—namely, a Countess Mathilde von Sayn—earned merit regarding the German language around the same time through her legal charters <term>Urkunden</term> composed in the German tongue. ¹) Thus, quite early in the North, in two of the most varied intellectual fields, two equally different women joined hands in the cultivation of our German language; both likely without even suspecting such a service. A portion of that merit also falls to the Preaching Brother <term>Predigerbruder</term> <note>A Dominican friar. The Dominicans were known as the "Order of Preachers." This brother assisted Mechthild by recording her visions in writing.</note> who wrote down these visions, and I wish it might—

---

¹) Höfer, *Selection of the Oldest Documents in the German Language*. Preface, VII.

<summary>The editor argues that Mechthild’s narrative poetry is more significant than her lyrical songs and highlights her role, alongside Countess Mathilde von Sayn, as a pioneering female voice in the development of the German language.</summary>
<keywords>Greith, German mysticism, Minnesang, Incarnation of Christ, literary history, Mathilde von Sayn, Northern Germany, Dominican Order, historical linguistics</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the editor's introductory remarks. It follows a discussion of individuals who contributed to the preservation and study of the German language, ending the previous page with a mention of Countess Mathilde von Sayn and her contributions to German language and literature.</meta>

# Preface and Introduction. XXII

[...language,] nor should the Benedictine brother <note>The editor, Father Gall Morel (1803–1872) of the Benedictine abbey in Einsiedeln, refers to himself here in the third person.</note>, who shares this writing with the reading
world so late, go away completely empty-handed.

For the time being, this edition in the original language <term>Ursprache</term> <note>The specific medieval dialect—a blend of Low and High German—in which Mechthild originally recorded her visions.</note> will find a 
smaller circle of readers; however, care has already been taken to ensure
that the book will also be made accessible to a larger circle of readers 
in translation.

<meta>Digitized by Google watermark in bottom right corner</meta>

<summary>The editor, Gall Morel, expresses hope for his own recognition in preserving the text and announces that while this edition is in the original medieval German, a modern translation is already in progress.</summary>
<keywords>Gall Morel, Benedictine, original language, translation, readership, preface</keywords>

<meta>This page serves as the formal introduction to the collected revelations of Mechthild of Magdeburg. <page-num>1</page-num>
<sig>Saint Mechthild.</sig>

# **Of the Revelations of a Loving Soul.**

Around the year of our Lord 1250, and for nearly fifteen years, this book was provided in German <note>original: *teutonice*</note> to a certain Beguine <term>Beguine: a member of a lay religious community of women in the 13th century who lived lives of poverty and prayer without taking permanent vows</term>. She was a holy virgin in body and spirit, inspired by the grace of the Lord <note>original: *inspirata*; the manuscript erroneously uses the masculine form *inspiratus*</note>. As is evident in this writing, she served the Lord most devoutly for more than forty years in humble simplicity, in wandering poverty, in lowly status, and in heavenly contemplation. She followed perfectly in the footsteps of the brothers of the Order of Preachers <note>the Dominicans</note>, progressing from day to day and always improving herself. The book was recorded by a certain brother of the aforementioned order and contains many good things, as indicated in the titles listed below.

Of the Trinity: Book II, Chapter 3; Book III, Chapter 9; Book IV, Chapters 12 and 14; Book V, Chapter 26.
Of Christ: Book II, Chapter 3; Book IV, Chapter 24; Book V, Chapter 23.
Of our Lady <note>The Virgin Mary</note>: Book I, Chapter 4; Book II, Chapter 3; Book V, Chapter 23.
Of the nine orders of angels: Book I, Chapter 6; Book III, Chapter 1; Book V, Chapter 1.
Of the special status <note>original: *prerogativa*</note> of certain Saints: Book IV, Chapters 20, 21, and 23.
Of the malice of demons: Book IV, Chapter 17; Book II, Chapter 24; Book V, Chapter 29; Book V, Chapter 9.
Of the dignity of man: Book I, Chapter 44; Book IV, Chapter 14.
Of the ecstasy and separation of the soul from the flesh: Book I, Chapter 2.
Of the description of heaven: Book III, Chapter 1.
Of the description of hell: Book III, Chapter 21.

<summary>This introductory page provides a biographical sketch of Mechthild of Magdeburg, identifying her as a Beguine who lived a life of extreme devotion and contemplation, and provides a topical index for her revelations.</summary>
<keywords>Mechthild of Magdeburg, Beguine, Order of Preachers, Dominicans, Trinity, Christ, Virgin Mary, angels, demons, heaven, hell, spiritual ecstasy</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the topical index from page 1, transitioning from a list of theological themes (purgatory, virtues, and the Antichrist) into a historical preface that identifies the author as a "sister" (Mechthild of Magdeburg) and the scribe as a Dominican brother.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>

Concerning the many forms of purgatory: Book II, Chapter 7; Book III, Chapters 15 and 16; Book IV <note>original: XIV; likely a scribal error for IV</note>, Chapter 15.

Concerning many virtues and vices: Book I, Chapters 22 and 25; Book III, Chapters 7 and 14; Book IV, Chapter 4; and especially concerning charity <note>original: *caritate*; divine love</note>: Book III, Chapter 13.

Concerning the preachers at the end of the world in the time of the Antichrist: Book IV, Chapter 27; and concerning many unheard-of things which you will understand if you read through this book nine times with faith, humility, and devotion. This is a prophecy of the past, present, and future. Here also is the distinction of the three persons <note>referring to the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit of the Trinity</note>: Book V, Chapter 26.

In the year of our Lord’s birth twelve hundred and fifty <note>original: *drizehendhalphundert*; literally "thirteen-half-hundred," a medieval way of saying 1250</note>, and for fifteen years thereafter, this book was revealed in German <note>original: *tůsche*</note> by God to a sister. She was a holy virgin in both body and spirit. She served God devoutly in humble simplicity, in wandering poverty, in heavenly contemplation, and in oppressed contempt for more than forty years. She followed firmly and perfectly the light and teaching of the Order of Preachers <note>the Dominicans</note>, and she grew from day to day and improved herself daily. But a brother of the same order gathered and wrote the book, and much good is contained in this book regarding many matters, as is indicated in the tables of contents. You should read this over nine times in a praiseworthy, humble, and devout manner.

# This is the first part of this book.

---

->This book should be received gladly, for God himself speaks the words.<-

I now send this book as a messenger to all spiritual people, both the bad and the good, for when the pillars fall, the work cannot stand. It signifies me alone and gloriously makes known my secrets. All those who wish to understand this book should read it nine times.

->This book is called *A Flowing Light of the Godhead* <note>original: *ein vliessendes lieht der gotheit*</note>.<-

->O Lord God, who has made this book? I have made it in my weakness, for I cannot withhold myself from my gift. O Lord, what shall this book be called, solely to your honor? It shall be called: *A Flowing Light of my Godhead*, into all the hearts that live there without falsehood.<-

### I. *How Love and the Queen spoke together.* <note>1) Greith, *German Mysticism*, p. 222.</note>

The soul came to Love <note>original: *miñe*; the personified divine love often depicted as a noble lady</note>
And greeted her with deep feeling
And said: "God greet you, Lady Love."
"God reward you, dear Lady Queen." <note>The soul is addressed as a queen, reflecting its high status in the eyes of God</note>
"Lady Love, you are very perfect."
"Lady Queen, because of that I am above all things."
"Lady Love, you have struggled for many years,
Before you compelled the high Trinity <note>original: *drivaltekeit*</note> to this,
That it has poured itself entirely
Into Mary's humble virginity."

# First Part.

Lady Queen, this is your honor and benefit.
Lady Love <note>original: *miñe*; personified Divine Love</note>, you have taken from me
Everything I ever gained on the earth <note>original: *ertrich*</note>.
Lady Queen, you have made a blessed exchange.
Lady Love, you have taken from me my childhood.
Lady Queen, in return I have given you heavenly freedom.
Lady Love, you have taken from me all my youth.
Lady Queen, in return I have given you many holy virtues.
Lady Love, you have taken from me good friends and kin.
Ah, Lady Queen, that is a wretched complaint.
Lady Love, you have taken from me the world, worldly honor, and all worldly
->wealth.<-
Lady Love: Queen, I will repay you for that on earth in a single hour with the Holy Spirit
->according to all your will.<-
Lady Love, you have so greatly overpowered me that my body has fallen
->into a singular sickness. ¹)<-
Lady Queen, in return I have given you much high knowledge.
Lady Love, you have consumed my flesh and my blood.
Lady Queen, through that you are purified and drawn into God.
Lady Love, you are a female robber, yet you must still repay me.
Lady Queen, then take my very self.
Lady Love, now you have repaid me a hundredfold on earth.
Lady Queen, you still have God and all His kingdom to claim.

### II. Of three persons and of three gifts.

The true greeting of God, which comes from the heavenly flood out of the fountain of the flowing Trinity <note>original: *drivaltekeit*</note>, has such great power that it takes away all the body's strength and makes the soul manifest to herself, so that she sees herself as like the saints and then receives into herself a divine radiance. Then the soul separates from the body with all her power, wisdom, love, and longing; only the smallest part of her life remains with the body, as if in a sweet sleep. Then she sees a whole God in three persons and recognizes the three persons in one God, undivided. Then He greets her with the courtly speech <note>original: *hove sprache*; the refined language of the nobility, here used for divine communication</note> that one does not hear in this kitchen <note>a metaphor for the physical world or the lower functions of the body</note>, and clothes her in the garments that one should wear in the palace, and gives Himself into her power. Then she may ask and inquire whatever she wishes, and she will be informed. The reason why she is not informed of some things is the first of three causes. Then He draws her further to a secret place. There she must

# Chapter II.

<note>The author is likely Mechthild of Magdeburg (c. 1207–1282/1294), a Beguine mystic. Her work, "The Flowing Light of the Godhead," is famous for its "Brautmystik" or bridal mysticism, using the language of courtly love to describe the relationship between the Soul and God.</note>

She asks for no one and prays for no one, for He wishes to play a game with her alone—a game of which the body knows nothing, nor the peasants at the plow, nor the knights in the tournament, nor even His lovely mother Mary; she cannot attend to her there. So they soar further to a place rich in delights, of which I cannot and will not say much. It is too dangerous; I dare not, for I am a very sinful person. But when the infinite God brings the bottomless soul into the heights, she loses the earth out of wonder, and finds no trace that she ever came to earth. When the game is at its very best, one must leave it. Then the blossoming God says: "Maiden, you must bow," and she is startled: "Lord, now you have delayed me here so long that I can no longer praise you in my body with any <term>religious order</term><note>original: "orden"; refers to the structured rules of monastic life or the physical order of the senses</note>, but instead I suffer misery and struggle against the body." Then He says: "Ah, you dear dove, your voice is a stringed instrument to my ears; your words are spices to my mouth; your longings are the generosity of my gifts." Then she says: "Dear Lord, it must be as the host commands." Then she sighs with all her might, so that the body is moved. Then the body says: "Ah, Lady, where have you been? You return so lovely, beautiful and powerful, free and wise. Your journeying has taken away my taste, rest, color, and all my strength." Then she says: "Be silent, murderer, let your complaining be; I will forever guard myself against you, so that my enemy <note>The "enemy" here refers to the physical body or the lower self that hinders spiritual union.</note> is wounded; that does not trouble us, I rejoice in it."

This is a greeting that has many veins; it flows out of the flowing God into the poor, parched souls at all times with new knowledge, and in new contemplation, and in the special enjoyment of the new presence. Ah, sweet God, fiery within, blossoming without; now that you have given this to the least, if I could yet experience the life that you have given to your greatest, I would be willing to suffer all the longer for it. No one can or should receive this greeting unless they have been overcome and turned to nothing.

->In this greeting I wish to die while living;<-
->The blind saints can never ruin this for me.<-
->Those are the ones who love and do not know.<- <note>A reference to those who have emotional piety but lack the mystical "knowledge" or direct experience of God's essence.</note>

<summary>This page describes a mystical "game" or union between God and the Soul, emphasizing the soul's detachment from the physical body and the "blind" piety of those who lack direct divine experience.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the soul's mystical journey. In the previous page, the soul had returned from a divine encounter, feeling weary and alienated from its own body. This section transitions into a dialogue between the Soul and Lady Love (Minne), personified as a powerful and demanding force.</meta>

Part One.

### *III. Of the maidens of the soul and of the blows of Love.*¹)

All holy Christian virtues are the soul's maidens.
The soul's sweet vexation laments its distress to Love:

> **The Soul:** Ah, most beloved maiden,
> You have long been my chamberlain;
> Now tell me, how shall I fare in this?
> You have hunted me, captured me, bound me,
> And wounded me so deeply
> That I shall never be healed.
> You have given me many a <term>chilling blow</term><note>original: "kulenschlag"; literally a "club-blow" or a "cooling blow." In mystical literature, this refers to a divine touch that simultaneously strikes the soul with power and "cools" the heat of worldly desires.</note>;
> Tell me, shall I at last recover from you?
> Shall I not be killed by your hand?
> If so, it would be better for me if I had never known you.
> **Love:** That I hunted you delighted me;
> That I captured you was my desire;
> That I bound you made me rejoice.
> When I wounded you, you were united with me;
> When I give you chilling blows, I gain power over you.
> I drove almighty God from the kingdom of heaven
> And took from Him His human life
> And returned Him with honor to His Father.
> How could you, wretched worm, recover from me?
> **The Soul:** Speak, my empress, I fear a small, secret medicine
> Which God has often given me,
> That I might recover through it.
> **Love:** When one does not wish for captives to die,
> One gives them bread and water.
> The medicine that God has often given you
> Is nothing other than a reprieve in this human life.
> But when your <term>Easter Day</term><note>Here, the soul’s "Easter" refers to the moment of death, viewed as a resurrection and final union with God.</note> comes,
> And your body receives the death-blow,
> Then I will embrace you all around
> And I will go through you entirely;
> I will steal you from your body
> And I will give you to your beloved.
> **The Soul:** O Love, I have written this letter from your own mouth; now, Lady, give me your seal.
> **Love:** Whoever has ever loved God more than themselves knows well where to find that seal; it lies between the two of us.
> **The Soul speaks:** Be silent, beloved, say no more.
> May all creatures and I bow to you,
> Most beloved of all maidens.

---

¹) <note>Greith, p. 225. Refers to the 1861 edition by Karl Greith.</note>

Cap. III — V.

->Tell my beloved that his bed is prepared<-
->And that I am <term>lovesick</term><note>original: "miñesiech"; a central term in courtly and mystical literature, describing a physical and spiritual longing for the beloved that transcends ordinary health.</note> for him.<-
->If this letter is too long, the fault is this: I was in the<-
->meadow, where I gathered many kinds of flowers.<-
->This is a sweet lament of sorrow: Whoever dies of love, him should<-
->one bury in God.<-

### IV. *Of the courtly journey of the soul to which God shows Himself.* ¹)

When the poor soul comes to court, she is wise and well-bred; then she gazes upon her God with joy. Oh, how lovely she is received there! Then she falls silent and desires His praise beyond all measure. Then He shows her His divine heart with great longing. It is like red gold burning in a great coal-fire. Then He places her into His glowing heart, just as the high prince and the little maiden <note>original: "die kleine dirne"; in Middle High German, "dirne" meant a young girl or servant-maid, without modern negative connotations.</note> embrace one another and are united as water and wine. Then she becomes as nothing and passes out of herself, as if she could do no more, while He is lovesick for her, as He ever was, for to Him (neither) more nor less can be added. Then she says: "Lord, You are my comfort, my desire, my flowing fountain, my sun, and I am Your mirror." — This is a courtly journey of the loving soul, which cannot exist without God.

### V. *Of the torment and of the reward of hell.*

My body is in long torment, my soul is in high joy, for she has beheld and embraced her Beloved entirely with her arms. From Him, she has this torment, the poor thing. As He draws her, so she flows. She cannot contain herself until He brings her into Himself. Then she would gladly speak, but she cannot. Then she is completely wound into the wondrous <term>Trinity</term><note>original: "drivaltekeit"</note> with high union. Then He leaves her for a little while, so that she may recover. Then she longs for His praise, but she cannot find it according to her will. Indeed, she would even wish that He would send her to hell, so that He might be praised beyond measure by all creatures. Then she gazes at Him and speaks to Him: "Lord, give me Your blessing." Then He gazes at her and draws her back again, and gives her a greeting.

Where the body can no longer speak.
Then the body says to the soul:

### VI. *Of the nine choirs, how they sing.*

Now listen, dear one, listen with spiritual ears; thus sing the **nine choirs**:

> We praise You, Lord, that You have visited us with Your humility.
> We praise You, Lord, that You have preserved us with Your mercy. ¹)
> We praise You, Lord, that You have honored us with Your ignominy <note>original: "smahheit"; referring to the "shameful" suffering of Christ on the cross which brings honor to humanity.</note>.
> We praise You, Lord, that You have led us with Your generosity.
> We praise You, Lord, that You have ordered us with Your wisdom.
> We praise You, Lord, that You have protected us with Your power.
> We praise You, Lord, that You have sanctified us with Your nobility.
> We praise You, Lord, that You have guided us with Your intimacy <note>original: "heimlichkeit"; a key mystical term referring to the private, secret knowledge or presence of God.</note>.
> We praise You, Lord, that You have exalted us with Your Love <note>original: "miñe"; the personified divine Love that governs the universe.</note>.

### VII. Of God’s curse in eight things.

> I curse you: your body must die,
> Your word must perish,
> Your eyes must close,
> Your heart must flow away,
> Your soul must ascend,
> Your body must remain.
> Your human senses must pass away,
> Your spirit must stand before the Holy Trinity.

### VIII. *The least one praises God in ten things.*

> O You burning mountain, O You chosen sun!
> O You full moon, O You bottomless well!
> O You unreachable height, O You clarity without measure!
> O wisdom without ground!
> O mercy without hindrance!
> O strength without resistance!
> O Crown of all honors!
> The least one You ever created praises You!

### Chapters VI—XIV.

### *IX. With three things you dwell in the heights.*

Those who burn in true Love <term>Minne: the Middle High German term for divine, courtly, and mystical love</term> and build upon a steady foundation of truth, and bring forth fruit in great abundance at their blessed end, they dwell in the heights. <gloss>Gloss: that is, above the seraphim.</gloss> <note>The seraphim are the highest rank of angels, characterized by their "burning" love; the author suggests that the human soul can surpass even them through suffering and devotion.</note>

### *X. He who loves God conquers three things.*

Whichever person overcomes the world,
And takes away from his body all useless will,
And overcomes the devil:
That is the soul who loves God.
If the world gives her a blow,
She suffers little distress from it.
If the flesh causes her to waver,
The spirit does not become sick from it.
If the devil casts a glance at her,
The soul pays it no heed;
She loves and she loves,
And she can begin nothing else.

### *XI. There are four in the battle of God.*

O dove without gall! <note>original: "tube ane gallen"; in medieval animal lore, doves were believed to lack gall bladders, symbolizing a nature free of bitterness or malice.</note> O maiden without sorrow!
O knight without wounds! O undaunted servant!
These are the four who please God well in His battle.

### *XII. The soul praises God in five things. <gloss>¹)</gloss>*

O Emperor of all honors! O Crown of all princes!
O Wisdom of all masters! O Giver of all gifts!
O Freer of all imprisonments.

### *XIII. How God comes into the soul.*

->I come to my beloved<-
->Like a dew upon the flowers.<-

### *XIV. How the soul receives and praises God.*

Oh, joyful contemplation! Oh, lovely greeting! Oh, loving embrace! Lord, Your wonder has wounded me; Your grace has overwhelmed me <note>original: "verdruket"; literally "pressed" or "squeezed," suggesting a physical sensation of the weight of divine presence.</note>. O You high rock, You are so well hollowed out; in You none may nest but doves and nightingales. <note>The "hollowed rock" is a common mystical image for the wounds of Christ, which serve as a refuge for the faithful.</note>

<header>First Part.</header>

### XV. *How God receives the soul.*

Welcome, dear dove! You have flown so much in the earthly realm that your feathers have grown in the kingdom of heaven. <note>The "dove" is a traditional symbol for the soul, and its "growing feathers" represents the spiritual progress made through earthly trials.</note>

### XVI. *God compares the soul to four things.*

You taste like a winter grape, you smell like balsam, you shine like the sun, you are an increase of my highest love <term>Minne: the Middle High German term for divine, courtly, and mystical love</term>.

### XVII. *The soul praises God in five things.*

> O you pouring God in your gift!
> O you flowing God in your love!
> O you burning God in your longing!
> O you melting God in the union with your beloved!
> O you resting God upon my breasts, without whom I cannot exist!

### XVIII. *God compares the soul to five things.*

> O you beautiful rose among the thorns!
> O you flying bee in the honey!
> O you pure dove in your being!
> O you beautiful sun in your radiance!
> O you full moon in your standing!
> I cannot turn myself away from you.

### XIX. *God caresses the soul in six things.*

You are my bolster <term>legerkůssin: a pillow or cushion for resting</term>, my lovely bed, my most secret rest, my deepest longing, my highest honor. You are a delight of my divinity <term>gotheit: God’s divine nature</term>, a comfort for my humanity <term>mỏnscheit: Christ’s human nature</term>, a stream of my heat.

### XX. *The soul praises God in return in six things.*

You are my mountain of mirrors, a feast for the eyes <term>ỏgenweide: literally "eye-pasture," something delightful to behold</term>, a loss of myself, a storm of my heart, a fall and a renunciation of my power, my highest security.

### XXI. *Of knowledge and of enjoyment.*

> Love without knowledge <term>bekantnisse: spiritual recognition or cognitive understanding of God</term>
> Seems to the wise soul a darkness.
> Knowledge without enjoyment <term>gebrůchunge: the mystical "fruition" or experiential delight in God’s presence</term>
> Seems to her a hellish pain.
> Enjoyment without death <note>original: "mort"; in this context, it refers to the "mystical death" of the self or the physical death that prevents final, total union</note> she cannot lament enough.

<header>Cap. XV — XXII.</header>

### XXII. *Of Saint Mary’s annunciation and how one virtue follows another; and how the soul was made into a song of joy <term>jubilus: a wordless, ecstatic song of mystical praise</term> for the Trinity, and how Saint Mary suckled all the saints and continues to suckle them.* ¹)

The sweet dew of the beginningless Trinity has sprinkled itself from the wellspring of the eternal Godhead into the flower of the chosen virgin, and the fruit of that flower is an immortal God and a mortal man, a living comfort of eternal love, and our Redeemer has become a bridegroom. The bride has become intoxicated by the sight of his noble countenance. In the greatest strength she departs from herself, and in the greatest blindness she sees most clearly. In the greatest clarity she is both dead and living. The longer she is dead, the more joyfully she lives. The more joyfully she lives, the more she experiences. The less she becomes, the more flows into her. The more she fears herself ²) .... The richer she becomes, the poorer she is. The deeper she dwells, the broader she is. The more she commands, the deeper her wounds become. The more she storms, the more lovingly <term>miñenklicher: from "Minne," referring to divine or courtly love</term> God is toward her. The higher she soars, the more beautifully she shines from the reflection of the Godhead, and the closer she comes to Him. The more she labors, the more softly she rests. The more she grasps, the more quietly she keeps silent. The more loudly she calls, the greater wonders she works through His power according to her might. The more His desire grows, the greater her wedding becomes, and the narrower the bed of love becomes. The closer the embrace, the sweeter the kiss. The more lovingly they look at each other, the more painfully they part. The more He gives her, the more she consumes, and the more she has. The more humbly she takes her leave, the sooner she returns. The hotter she remains, the sooner she is ignited. The more she burns, the more beautifully she shines. The more God’s praise is spread, the greater her longing remains.

Ah, where has our redeeming Bridegroom gone in the joy <term>jubilus</term> of the Holy Trinity! When God could no longer contain Himself within Himself, He created the soul and gave Himself to her as her own out of great love. From what are you made, soul, that you climb so high above all creatures, and mingle yourself into the Holy Trinity and yet remain

...entirely in yourself? <sup>1)</sup> You have spoken of my beginning; now I tell you truly: I was created in that same place by Love <term>Minne: a Middle High German term for divine, ecstatic, or courtly love</term>, therefore no creature can comfort or satisfy me according to my noble nature except Love alone.

Lady Saint Mary, you are a mother of this wonder. When did this happen to you?

When our Father's song of joy <term>jubilus: a wordless, ecstatic song of mystical praise</term> was troubled by Adam’s fall, such that he had to be angry. Then the eternal wisdom of the almighty Godhead received the anger along with me. Then the Father chose me as a bride, so that He would have something to love <note>original: "ze miñende"</note>, because His beloved bride, the noble soul, was dead. And then the Son chose me as a mother, and there the Holy Spirit received me as a beloved. Then I alone was the bride of the Holy Trinity and the mother of the orphans <note>The word "weisen" can mean "wise ones" or "orphans"; in this context of maternal care, "orphans" refers to humanity lost after the Fall</note>, and I carried them before God’s eyes, so that they did not sink altogether as some indeed did.

Since I was thus the mother of many a noble child, my breasts became so full of the pure, unstained milk of true, gentle mercy that I suckled the prophets and the seers before God was born. Afterward, in my childhood, I suckled Jesus; further on, in my youth, I suckled God’s bride—Holy Christendom—by the cross, such that I became so withered and wretched that the sword of Jesus’ fleshly pain cut spiritually into my soul. Then both stood open: His wounds and her breasts. The wounds poured, the breasts flowed, so that the soul became living and completely healthy. When He poured the bright red wine into her red mouth, when she was thus born from the open wounds and became living, she was childlike and very young. If she were to fully recover after her death and her birth, then God’s mother had to be her mother and her nurse.

By God, it was and is quite fitting. God is her true father and she is His true bride, and she is like Him in all her suffering. Lady, in your old age you suckled the holy apostles with your motherly teaching and with your powerful prayer, so that God did His honor and His will through them. Lady, thus you suckled then and suckle still the martyrs in their hearts with strong faith,
