...the confessors with holy protection for their ears <note>A reference to the spiritual strength needed by priests to hear confessions without being corrupted by the sins they hear</note>, the virgins with your purity, the widows with steadfastness, the devout with generosity, and the sinners with your intercession.

Lady, you must still suckle <note>The original German uses "sagen," but the context of breasts and milk clearly implies "säugen" (to suckle)</note> us, for your breasts are still so full that you cannot hold it back. If you would not suckle us further, the milk would cause you great pain. For truly, I have seen your breasts so full that seven streams <note>Likely representing the seven gifts of the Holy Spirit or the seven sacraments</note> poured out all at once from one breast over my body and over my soul. In that hour, you take from me a labor that no friend of God <term>Gotzfrúnt: a term for a mystic or a person in a close, personal relationship with God</term> can bear without heartache. Thus you shall continue to suckle us until the Last Day, until you see that God’s children and your children are weaned and fully grown in the eternal body. Ah, thereafter we shall acknowledge and see in unspeakable delight the milk and also that same breast that Jesus has so often kissed.

### XXIII. *You should pray that God loves you very much, often, and for a long time; then you will become pure, beautiful, and enduring.* <note>1) Greith p. 33.</note>

Ah Lord, love me greatly and love me often and long; for the more often You love me, the purer I become; the more intensely You love me, the more beautiful I become; the longer You love me, the holier I become here on earth <note>original: "ertrich"</note>.

->#### *XXIV. How God answers the soul.*<-

That I love you often, I have by nature, for I myself am Love <margin>God is love <note>original Latin: "Deus caritas est"</note></margin>. That I love you greatly comes from my desire <note>2) The word "gerunge" implies a longing or yearning</note>, for I desire that one loves me greatly. <note>3) There seems to be a gap in the text here.</note> That I love you long comes from my eternity, for I am without end.

->#### *XXV. Of the way to suffer pain gladly for God’s sake.*<-

God leads His children, whom He has chosen, along wondrous ways. This is a wondrous way and a noble way and a holy way, which God Himself walked: that a human being suffers pain <note>original: "pine"</note> without sin.

<page-num>14</page-num>
<header>Part One.</header>

...and without guilt. In this way, the soul that yearns for God rejoices, for she rejoices by nature in her Lord, who through His benevolence suffered many torments. And His dear Lord, the Heavenly Father, gave His most beloved Son to be tortured by the heathens and martyred by the Jews without any guilt of His own. And the time has come when certain people who appear spiritual <note>Likely a critique of hypocrites or those who focus only on outward religious observance</note> torture God's children in the body and martyr them in the spirit, for He wishes to make them like His dear Son, who was tortured in body and in soul.

->*XXVI. In this way, the soul withdraws her senses and is free from heartache.*<-

It is a rare and a high way in which the faithful soul wanders and leads her senses after her, just as one who sees leads the blind. In this way the soul is free and lives without heartache, for she desires nothing other than what her Lord wills, who does all things for the best.

->*XXVII. How you may be worthy of this way, and keep it, and be perfect.*<-

Three things make one worthy of the way, so that he may recognize it and enter into it. The first is that a person compels himself in God without any mastery <note>Original: "meisterschaft." In this mystical context, it suggests acting without pride or trying to be one's own master, instead submitting entirely to God's guidance</note> and holds God's grace secretly and carries it willingly, in the renunciation of all things according to human will. The second keeps the person in the way: that all things are received with gratitude, except for sin alone. The third makes the person perfect in the way: that one does all things equally to the honor of God; for I wish to account my lowliest necessity <note>Referring to mundane physical needs like eating or sleeping</note> to God as highly as if I were in the highest contemplation <term>Contemplacie: a state of deep spiritual meditation or mystical union with the divine</term> that a person can enter; for why? If I do it in love to the honor of God, then it is all one. But whenever I sin, I am not on this way.

<summary>This page describes the mystical path of the soul, emphasizing that spiritual perfection is found in aligning one's will with God's and treating even mundane tasks as acts of high devotion. It frames the suffering of the faithful as a way to become more like Christ, who suffered innocently.</summary>

<header>Chapters 26—29.</header>

### *XXVIII. Love should be deadly, without measure, and without ceasing; this is the folly of fools.* <note>original: "Die miñe sol sin mortlich âne masse âne vnderlass." The term "mortlich" here suggests a love so intense it leads to the "death" of the self; "Minne" refers to the Middle High German tradition of mystical, courtly love for God.</note>

I rejoice that I must love Him who loves me, and I desire this: that I love Him unto death <note>original: "mortlich"</note> without measure and without ceasing. Rejoice, my soul, for your Life has died out of love for you; love Him so fervently that you may die for Him. Then you shall burn forever unquenched, like a living spark in the great fire of the Living Majesty.

->So you will be full of the fire of love,<-
->Which makes it so well for you here.<-
->You need not teach me anymore,<-
->I cannot turn myself away from love;<-
->I must remain its prisoner,<-
->I cannot live otherwise. —<-
->Where love dwells, there I may remain<-
->Both in death and in the body.<-
->That is the "folly of fools,"<-
->Who live without heartache.<-
<note>The "folly of fools" is a recurring mystical paradox; what the world sees as madness—giving oneself entirely to an unseen God—is the highest wisdom to the mystic.</note>

### *XXIX. Of the beauty of the bridegroom and how the bride should follow Him.*

<term>See, my bride</term> <note>original Latin: "Vide mea sponsa"</note>: See how beautiful my eyes are, how right my mouth is, how fiery my heart is, how light my hands are, how swift my feet are, and follow me. You shall be martyred with me, betrayed in envy, sought in danger, captured in hatred, bound in hearsay; your eyes shall be blindfolded so that men will not acknowledge the truth to you; you shall be struck on the neck by the grimness of the world, dragged before judgment in confession, struck with penance, sent to Herod with mockery, stripped by misery, scourged by poverty, crowned with temptation, and spat upon with disgrace. You shall carry your cross in the hatred of sins, be crucified in the renunciation of all things according to your own will, nailed to the cross with the holy virtues, wounded with love, and die on the cross in holy perseverance. You shall be pierced in your heart with constant union, and released from the cross in the true victory of all your... <note>The text here mirrors the "Imitatio Christi" or the "Imitation of Christ," where the spiritual trials of the believer are mapped directly onto the physical sufferings of Jesus during the Passion.</note>

<header>Part One.</header>

...enemies, buried in insignificance <note>original: "unahtbarkeit," referring to a state of being overlooked or despised by the world</note>, to rise from death in a holy end, and to travel to heaven in a breath of God’s spirit.

### XXX. *Concerning the Seven Hours of Prayer.* <note>These represent the "Canonical Hours" of the Divine Office, the daily cycle of prayer used by clergy and monastics.</note>

Matins: full of love in sweet well-being.
Prime: a desire for love, a sweet burden.
Terce: love’s delight, a sweet thirst.
Sext: a feeling of love, a sweet cooling.
None: love’s death, a sweet distress.
Vespers: love’s flowing, a sweet pouring out.
Compline: love’s rest, a sweet joy.

### XXXI. *You should not regard mockery.*

I was greatly despised; then our Lord spoke: "Do not wonder at this too much; since the Lord was so severely rejected and spat upon in this vessel <note>referring to Christ’s physical body</note>, what should then happen to the vinegar vessel <note>a metaphor for the sinful human soul</note>, in which there is nothing good of its own accord?"

### XXXII. *You should not care for honors, pain, wealth, or sorrow for sins.*

When people offer you honor, you should be ashamed; when they torture you, you should rejoice; when they do good to you, you should be afraid; when you act against me, you should be sorrowful from your heart. If you cannot feel sorrow, then look at how greatly and how long I was sorrowful for you.

### XXXIII. *Concerning the provision of comfort and love.*

My soul spoke thus to her beloved: "Lord, your generosity is the wondrous sustenance <note>original: "pfrůnde," which usually refers to an ecclesiastical endowment or food provision</note> of my body; your mercy is the special comfort of my soul. Love <term>Minne</term> <note>Middle High German "Minne" describes a specific form of intense, reciprocal, and often mystical love between the soul and God.</note> is the rest of my life eternally."

### XXXIV. *In your suffering, you should be a lamb, a turtledove, a bride.*

->You are my lamb in your suffering.<-
->You are my turtledove in your sighing <note>original: "sůfzunge," referring to the mournful cooing of the dove, a common symbol for the soul’s longing for God</note>.<-
->You are my bride in your waiting <note>original: "beitunge"</note>.<-

The text concludes with God addressing the soul as a lamb, dove, and bride, emphasizing the virtues of patience and longing.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the instructional and conversational sequence between God and the soul. While the previous page focused on the "Seven Hours of Prayer" and the necessity of suffering, this page outlines the discipline of spiritual "wilderness" and celebrates the soul’s victory over worldly vices through the metaphors of predatory animals.</meta>

<page-num>17</page-num>
<header>Chapters XXX—XXXVIII.</header>

### XXXV. *The wilderness has twelve things.* <note>original: "wůstin." In medieval mysticism, the "wilderness" represents a state of total detachment where the soul is alone with God.</note>

You shall love the "nothing,"
You shall flee the "something," <note>This refers to the "Nothing" of God—which is beyond being—and the "Something" of the material world.</note>
You shall stand alone
And shall go to no one.
You shall be very industrious
And be free from all things.
You shall unbind the prisoners
And constrain the free. <note>likely referring to subduing one's own free will to God's will</note>
You shall refresh the sick
And yet possess nothing yourself.
You shall drink the water of suffering
And kindle the fire of love <term>love: "miñe," a Middle High German term for mystical, courtly, and divine love</term> with the wood of virtues;
Then you dwell in the true wilderness.

### XXXVI. *Concerning malice, goodness, and wonders.*

With the malice of your enemies you shall be adorned.
With the virtues of your heart you shall be honored.
With your good works you shall be crowned.
With our mutual <note>original: "zweiger" (of us two)</note> love you shall be exalted.
With my delightful wonders you shall be sanctified.

### XXXVII. *The soul answers God, so that she may be worthy of grace.*

O most beloved! I long for innocent disgrace,
I desire heartfelt virtues,
Good works I have, alas, none,
Our mutual love I corrupt,
Of your beautiful wonders I am entirely unworthy.

### XXXVIII. *God boasts that the soul has overcome four sins.*

Our Lord boasts in the kingdom of heaven
Of His loving soul, whom He has on the earth,
And says: See how she comes ascending,
She who has wounded me! <note>A reference to the Song of Solomon, where the lover is "wounded" by love.</note>
She has cast from her the ape of the world, <note>The "ape" traditionally symbolized vanity or empty imitation.</note>
She has overcome the bear of unchastity,
She has trodden the lion of pride under her feet,
She has torn open the belly of the wolf of greed
And comes running like a hunted deer
Toward the spring that I am.
She comes soaring like an eagle
Out of the depths into the heights.

### XXXIX. *God asks the soul what she brings.* <note>1)</note>

->You hunt fiercely in love.<-

### XL. *She answers him that it is better than four things.*

Lord, I bring you my treasures: <note>original: "kleinöter," referring to precious jewels or ornaments.</note>
It is larger than the mountains, it is broader than the world, deeper than the sea, higher than the clouds, more beautiful than the sun, more manifold than the stars; it outweighs all the earth.

### XLI. *God asks with praise what the treasure is called.*

Your <note>2)</note> image of my divinity, honored by my humanity, adorned by my holy spirit—what are your treasures called?

### XLII. *The treasure is called the heart's desire.*

Lord, it is called my heart's desire; I have withdrawn it from the world, preserved it for myself, and denied it to all creatures; now I can carry it no further. Lord, where shall I lay it?

### XLIII. *Lay your desire in the Trinity.*

You shall lay your heart's desire nowhere else than in my divine heart and upon my human breast. There alone will you be comforted and kissed by my spirit.

### XLIIII. *Concerning the way of love in seven things, the three garments of the bride, and the dance.*

**God speaks:** O, loving soul, do you wish to know what your way is like?

**The soul:** Yes, dear Holy Spirit, teach it to me. Just as you come past the distress of remorse <term>remorse: "rúwen," the deep spiritual sorrow for sin that precedes confession</term> and the pain of confession,

> Oh Lord, that is exceedingly great,
> That you are the companion of your love,
> Which has no love in itself,
> Unless it is first moved by you.

Then the soul speaks to the senses, who are her chamberlains: "Now I am tired of dancing for a while. Leave me, I must go where I may cool myself." Then the senses say to the soul: "Lady, if you wish to cool yourself in the tears of love of Saint Mary Magdalene, that may well satisfy you." The soul:

> Be silent, you masters; you do not all know what I mean.
> Leave me unhindered;
> I want to drink a little of the unmixed wine <note>The "unmixed wine" is a mystical metaphor for the pure, unmediated essence of God.</note>.

*Senses:* Lady, in the chastity of virgins
Is the great love prepared.
*Soul:* That may well be, but that is not the highest thing in me.
*Senses:* In the blood of the martyrs you may cool yourself greatly.
*Soul:* I have been martyred for so many days,
That I cannot go there now.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from Chapter 44, where the Soul, exhausted from the "dance of love" described on the previous page, seeks a place to "cool" herself. ### Chapter XLIIII.

*The Senses:* Pure people gladly dwell in the counsel of the confessors <note>original: "bihteren," referring to those who hear confession or live lives of penance</note>.
*The Soul:* I will always stand by good counsel, both in what I do and what I leave undone; yet I cannot go there now.
*The Senses:* In the wisdom of the apostles you will find great security.
*The Soul:* I have wisdom here with me; with it I will always choose the best.
*The Senses:* Lady, the angels are bright and beautiful, colored with love <note>original: "miñevar"</note>; if you wish to cool yourself, then raise yourself to them.
*The Soul:* The bliss of the angels causes me love-pain <note>original: "miñen we"</note> whenever I do not see their Lord and my Bridegroom.
*The Senses:* Then cool yourself in the holy, harsh life that God gave to John the Baptist.
*The Soul:* I am ready for the pain, yet the power of love <term>love: "miñe," the Middle High German term for mystical, courtly love between the soul and God</term> surpasses all labor.
*The Senses:* Lady, if you wish to cool yourself in a lovely way, then bow yourself into the Virgin's lap to the little child, and see and taste how the joy of angels sucked the supernatural milk from the Eternal Virgin.
*The Soul:* That is a childish love, to suckle and rock a child; I am a full-grown bride, I want to go to my Beloved <note>original: "trût," an archaic term for a dear one or spouse</note>.
*The Senses:* O Lady, if you go there, we must go completely blind, for the Godhead is so fiery hot—as you yourself well know—that all the fire and all the glow that makes heaven and all the saints shine and burn has all flowed out from His divine breath and from His human mouth by the counsel of the Holy Spirit; how can you remain there even for an hour?
*The Soul:* The fish cannot drown in the water; the bird cannot sink in the air. Gold cannot perish in the fire, for there it receives its clarity and its shining color. God has given this to all creatures: that they may follow their own nature.

<meta>Digitized by Google watermark at bottom right</meta>

<summary>In this allegorical dialogue, the Soul rejects the comforts of the saints and even the Christ child, insisting that her "nature" is to be united with the fiery heat of the Godhead. She argues that just as water is for fish, the divine fire is her natural and life-sustaining element.</summary>
<keywords>Soul and Senses, Bride of Christ, Minne (mystical love), Godhead as fire, spiritual maturation, nature of creatures</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from Chapter 44. Having rejected the comforts of the saints and angels, the Soul now explains her irresistible drive toward union with God and enters the "hidden chamber" of the Godhead for a mystical encounter.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>
### Part One.

How then could I resist my own nature?
I must go out of all things and into God,
Who is my father by nature,
My brother through his humanity <note>original: "mônscheit"</note>,
My bridegroom through love <term>love: "miñe," the Middle High German term for mystical, courtly love between the soul and God</term>,
And I am his without beginning.
Do you think I do not feel his glow?
He can do both: burn with power and cool with comfort.
Now, do not be too troubled.
You <note>the Senses</note> shall yet teach me.
When I return,
Then I will truly need your teaching,
For the earth is full of many snares.

Then the most beloved goes to the most beautiful in the hidden chambers of the innocent Godhead; there she finds the bed of love and the vessel of love, prepared by both God and humanity. Then our Lord speaks: "Stand, Lady Soul." — "What do you command, Lord?" — "You shall be out of yourself." — "Lord, what shall then happen to me?" — "Lady Soul, you are so deeply of my nature <note>original: "genaturt," meaning the soul shares the same essence as God</note>, that nothing can be between you and me. No angel was ever so high that this was granted to them even for an hour, which to you is given eternally. Therefore, you must lay aside both fear and shame and all outward virtue. Rather, only that which you carry within you by nature shall you desire to feel eternally. That is your noble longing and your bottomless desire <note>original: "grundelose girheit"</note>, which I will eternally fill with my endless generosity <note>original: "miltekeit"</note>."

> Lord, now I am a naked soul <note>In mystical tradition, the "naked soul" is one stripped of all earthly attachments and even its own ego</note>,
> And you in yourself are a well-adorned God.
> Our two-fold fellowship
> Is eternal love without death.
> Then a blessed silence occurs
> According to the will of them both.
> He gives himself to her and she gives herself to him.
> What happens to her now, she knows—
> And in this I take comfort.
> Now, this cannot last long.
> Where two lovers come together in secret,
> They must often part from one another unseparated.

Dear Friend of God <note>original: "Gottesfrúnd," a term used for a circle of 14th-century mystics</note>, I have written this way of love for you;
May God grant it to your heart. Amen.

<summary>The Soul describes her essential nature as being one with God and enters a state of "naked" mystical union and "blessed silence" before returning to the world. The passage concludes with a personal dedication to a fellow "Friend of God."</summary>

<meta>This page continues from the Soul’s mystical entry into the "hidden chamber" of the Godhead. Having established the intimate, essential union between God and the Soul, the text now transitions into a liturgical and allegorical framework, describing the spiritual "week" of the soul and the royal procession of the Soul as a Bride prepared for her Groom.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>
<header>Chapters XLV, XLVI.</header>

### *XLV. Of eight days in which the yearning of the prophets is fulfilled.*

This is a day of yearning <note>original: "gerunge," a central term in German mysticism referring to the intense, active striving of the soul toward God</note> and of blessed joys in the Annunciation of Christ.
This is a day of rest and of bodily tenderness in the birth of Christ.
This is a day of faithfulness and of blessed union, Maundy Thursday <note>original: "hohe Donrstag," literally "High Thursday"</note>.
This is a day of generosity and heartfelt love, Good Friday <note>original: "stille vrītag," literally "Silent Friday"</note>.
This is a day of power and joyful delight, the Resurrection.
This is a day of faith and of wretched sorrow, the day of Ascension <note>Ascension is associated with "sorrow" here because it marks the physical departure of Christ from his followers on earth</note>.
This is a day of truth and of burning comfort, Pentecost.
This is a day of justice and of the true hour, the Last Judgment.
This is a week, of which we shall celebrate seven days of Constancy <note>original: "Stetegunge"</note>.
One day our Lord will celebrate on the last day with us all.

### *XLVI. Of the manifold adornment of the bride, and how she comes to the bridegroom and what her retinue is like, which is ninefold.*

The bride is clothed with the sun and has trodden the moon under her feet, and she is crowned with union <note>This imagery draws on the "Woman Clothed with the Sun" from Revelation 12:1, frequently interpreted in mysticism as the Soul or the Church</note>. She has a chaplain, which is Fear; he has a golden rod in his hand, which is Wisdom. The chaplain is clothed with the blood of the Lamb, and is crowned with honor. And Wisdom is clothed with bliss and is crowned with eternity. The bride has four maidens. Love <term>Love: "miñe," the Middle High German term for the intense, reciprocal love between the Soul and God</term> leads the bride. Love is clothed with chastity and is crowned with dignity. The second is Humility; she holds the bride. She is clothed with lowliness <note>original: "vnahtbarkeit," meaning being considered of no account or insignificant</note> and is crowned with height. The third maiden is Repentance <note>original: "růwe"</note>; she is clothed with winter grapes <note>original: "wintrůbelin"; likely a metaphor for the bitter or sour taste of penance that precedes the sweetness of joy</note> and is crowned with joy. The fourth maiden is Mercy; she is clothed with ointment and is crowned with delight <term>delight: "wuñe," meaning ecstatic joy or bliss</term>. These two <note>Repentance and Mercy</note> lift up the bride’s cloak, which is holy reputation. She has a bishop, which is Faith; he brings the bride before the bridegroom. The bishop is clothed with precious stones and is crowned with the Holy Spirit. The bishop has two knights; one is Fortitude, who is clothed with strife and is crowned with victory. The other is Boldness; he is clothed with grace and crowned with all blessedness. She has a chamberlain, which is Guardedness <note>original: "hůte," referring to the monastic practice of guarding the senses and the heart from distractions</note>,

He is clothed with steadfastness and is crowned with perseverance; he carries the light before the bride and carries the tapestry behind her. She has a minstrel, which is Lovability. The bride has five kingdoms. The first is the eyes, which are built with tears and adorned with constraint <note>original: "getwange," referring to the ascetic discipline of the senses</note>. The second is the thoughts, which are built with struggle and adorned with counsel. The third is speaking, which is built with necessity and is adorned with faithfulness. The fourth is hearing, which is built with the Word of God and is adorned with comfort. The fifth is touch, which is built with power and is adorned with pure habit.

These five kingdoms have a warden, which is Guilt; she is clothed with confession and crowned with penance. Then he has a judge, who is clothed with discipline and crowned with patience. The bride has a pack-horse, which is the Body; it is bridled with unworthiness, and humiliation is its fodder, and its stable is confession. The pack-chest that it carries is Innocence. The bride has a processional attendant <note>original: "pellelbovivir," a rare term likely referring to a servant who carries a ceremonial cloth or canopy</note>, which is Hope; she is clothed in truth and crowned with song. She has a palm in her hand, which is the victory over sin, and a jar in the other hand, which is full of yearning and love, which she wishes to bring to her beloved. She has a peacock hat, which is a good reputation on earth and high honor in the kingdom of heaven. Then she walks a path, which is Gentleness; it is clothed with flowing honey and crowned with certainty. Then she sings:
> Chosen beloved, I yearn for you. You take and give to me so many...

<meta>This page transitions from the first part of the book, which concluded with the soul’s coronation, into the "second part." It shifts from a narrative of the mystical marriage to a more didactic reflection on the nature of divine love (Minne) and the dangers of false holiness.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>

# This is the second part of this book.

---

I. *Love creates height in the soul, not through human senses, which come from one's own will.*

The height of the soul occurs in Love <note>original: "miñe," referring to *Minne*, the Middle High German term for a specific kind of ecstatic, divine, and often courtly love between the soul and God</note>, and the adornment of the body occurs in holy Christian baptism; for above Love there is no height, and outside of Christendom there is no adornment. Therefore, they fool themselves greatly, those who with gruesome, inhuman labors <note>this refers to extreme physical penances or asceticism performed without the right spiritual intention</note> think to climb the heights, and yet carry a bitter <note>original: "griñes," suggesting a scowling or resentful spirit</note> heart, because they do not have the holy, humble virtue that can lead the soul into God; and there false holiness likes to set its throne, where one's own will carries the mastery in the heart.

II. *Of two songs of Love, regarding what occurred in Love.*

->I would gladly die of Love, if it could happen to me,<-
->For him whom I love, him have I seen,<-
->Standing with my bright eyes <note>referring to the "eyes of the soul" or spiritual perception</note> within my soul.<-
->Whatever bride has harbored her beloved,<-
->She does not need to go far.<-
->You, Love, cannot easily pass away.<-
->Wherever the maidens go often after the youth <note>an allegorical reference to the Soul or the virtues following Christ</note>.<-
->His noble nature is so ready,<-
->That he gladly receives them again, and leads<-
->Them after him from the heart. That may easily escape the foolish,<-
->Who do not gladly stand after Love.<-

<term>miñe</term> — Divine Love; <term>sele</term> — Soul; <term>cristan tőffe</term> — Christian baptism; <term>dieműtige tugent</term> — Humble virtue; <term>juncfrőwen</term> — Maidens or virgins; <term>jungelinge</term> — Youth or young man.

<summary>This page begins the second part of the manuscript, defining the soul's elevation through divine Love rather than through human will or extreme physical penance. It includes poetic verses describing the soul's intimate vision of the beloved within herself.</summary>
<keywords>Minne, divine love, baptism, humility, false holiness, asceticism, spiritual vision, bride of Christ</keywords>

<header>Second Part. Chapters I—III.</header>

### III. Of the tongue of the Godhead, of the light of truth, of the four rays of God into the nine choirs <note>The nine choirs of angels in medieval theology: Angels, Archangels, Principalities, Powers, Virtues, Dominions, Thrones, Cherubim, and Seraphim.</note> and the Trinity, and of St. Mary. <note>Greith p. 237.</note>

> O noble eagle! O sweet lamb!
> O fire's glow, kindle me!
> How long shall I be thus dry?
> One hour is far too heavy for me.
> One day is to me a thousand years.
> If You would be a stranger to me,
> Should it last but eight days,
> I would rather go to hell,
> Where I already am,
> For God is a stranger to the loving soul <note>original: "miñenden sele." The soul feels she is in hell whenever she feels alienated or "estranged" from God's presence.</note>.
> That is a pain beyond human death
> And beyond all pain,
> Believe me this. The nightingale
> She must always sing,
> For her whole nature plays entirely of love <note>original: "miñen," referring to *Minne*, the ecstatic divine love.</note>.
> If one took that from her, she would be dead.
> Ah, great Lord, consider my distress.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Then spoke the Holy Spirit to the soul:
> Ah, noble maiden, prepare yourself,
> Your Beloved wishes to come.
> Then she was startled and became inwardly joyful
> And spoke: Ah, dear messenger, if only it would ever happen so!
> I am so wicked and so completely unfaithful,
> That without my Beloved I can nowhere find rest.
> When I find this,
> That I cool even a little from His love,
> Then I am in pain in every limb
> And it is my desire,
> That I must go after Him in mourning.
> Then spoke the messenger: You should wish
> And water and make the bed and scatter flowers.
> Then spoke the wretched <note>original: "ellende," which in Middle High German implies being in exile or a stranger in the world.</note> soul:
> When I wish, then I must be ashamed,
> When I water, then I must weep,
> When I make the bed, then I must hope,
> When I pluck flowers, then I must love.
> When my Lord comes, then I come out of myself,
> For He brings me so many sweet sounds of strings <note>original: "seitenklang," a musical metaphor for divine harmony or communication.</note>,
> That it takes away all the wavering of my flesh,
> And His string-play is so full of all sweetness,
> That with it He takes away all my heartache.

# Second Part.

The great tongue of the Godhead
Has spoken many powerful words to me,
Which I have received with the small ears of my unworthiness. <note>original: "snôdikeit." This refers to the mystic's sense of being "lowly" or "wretched" in comparison to the divine.</note>
And the greatest of all lights has opened
Before the eyes of my soul.
There I have seen the unspeakable order
And recognized the countless honors,
The incomprehensible wonder
And the special intimacy with distinction,
The highest state of sufficiency
And the great discipline in knowledge,
The enjoyment paired with renunciation <note>original: "abebrechunge." A mystical concept where the soul simultaneously enjoys God and practices self-denial or detachment.</note>
According to the power of the senses,
The unmixed joy in the union
Of the company, and the living love of eternity,
As it is now and shall ever be.
There I saw four rays,
Which shoot all at once
Out of the most noble crossbow of the Holy Trinity, <note>Mechthild frequently uses bold imagery; here, the Trinity is compared to a crossbow, an instrument of precision and power, firing grace into the universe.</note>
From the divine throne through the nine choirs. <note>The nine choirs of angels.</note>
There no one remains, whether so poor or so rich,
Whom He does not strike lovingly.
The ray of the Godhead shoots them
With an incomprehensible light.
The loving humanity <note>original: "mônscheit." This refers specifically to the human nature of Jesus Christ.</note> greets them
In brotherly fellowship,
The Holy Spirit touches them
With the flowing-through
Of the wonderful creation
Of the eternal dwelling.
The undivided God feeds them
With the gaze of His noble countenance
And fills them with the irresistible breath
Of His flowing mouth.
And how they move without effort like the birds
In the air, when they move no feathers,
And how they travel wherever they wish
With body and with soul,
And yet remain unmixed in their appointed place,
And how the Godhead rings,
The humanity sings,
The Holy Spirit strikes the lyre <note>original: "liren." In medieval mysticism, the Holy Spirit is often depicted as a musician or the music itself that harmonizes the soul.</note> of the heavenly kingdom,
So that all the strings must ring,
Which are stretched there in love. <note>original: "miñe." This is the Middle High German term *Minne*, representing the ecstatic, reciprocal love between the soul and God.</note> ¹)

¹) <margin>Mary still lacks the final adornment.</margin> <note>This marginal note likely refers to the theological idea that Mary's glory will only be fully "complete" at the end of time or the general resurrection.</note>

<summary>Mechthild describes a majestic vision of the Holy Trinity as a "noble crossbow" firing rays of light and love through the celestial ranks. She portrays the effortless movement of souls in heaven and the divine harmony created by the Godhead, Christ’s humanity, and the Holy Spirit.</summary>

# Chapter III.

> There was seen that same precious vessel, <note>original: "trisem vas." This likely refers to the human body of Christ as a "vessel" or tabernacle for the Godhead.</note>
> In which Christ now dwells
> With soul and with body,
> As he shall ever remain;
> Not alone in the great beauty
> Which the heavenly Father on the Last Day
> Shall give to all blessed bodies.
> Of this, Our Lady <note>The Virgin Mary.</note> must still do without,
> As long as this earth floats upon the sea.

Then it was seen how beautifully Our Lady stood at the throne at the left hand of the heavenly Father, revealed in all her maidenly form; and how the human body is tempered and shaped into the noble radiance of Our Lady’s soul; and how her lovely breasts are revealed, full of the sweet milk from which the drops flow to the honor of the heavenly Father and for the love of humanity, so that humans may be welcome above all creatures. For the high princes—the archangels—marvel so greatly at this, that other "princes of men" <note>Referring to the redeemed souls of humans who are elevated above the angels in the celestial hierarchy.</note> have come to be placed above them, so it is right that our full witness should be there.

At the right hand <note>original: "vordern hant," literally "fore-hand."</note> of our Lord stands Jesus, our redeemer, with open wounds,

> Bloody, unbound,
> To overcome the Father’s justice,
> Which lies very heavily upon many a sinner, <note>¹) Manuscript: *lit*.</note>
> For as long as sin lasts upon the earth,
> So shall Christ’s wounds stay open,
> Bloody, without pain.
> But after the Judgment,
> Then Christ shall put on such a garment
> As was never seen,
> Unless God showed it before it came to pass.
> Then the sweet wounds shall heal,
> As if a rose petal
> Were laid upon the place of the wound.
> Then one shall see the joyful hue of love, <note>original: "miñevar." This refers to a mystical, radiant color associated with divine love.</note>
> Which shall never pass away.
> Then the uncreated God will
> Make all His creation new,
> And so new, that it may never grow old.

<header>Part Two.</header>

Now I lack German <note>original: "tůsches." Mechthild often laments that the vernacular (German) is insufficient to describe her divine visions.</note> and I do not know Latin; so if there is anything of worth found here, it is not by my merit, for no dog was ever so bad that if his master coaxed him with a white wheat roll, <note>original: "wissen simelen." A fine bread used as a treat.</note> he would not come most gladly.

## IV. *Concerning the poor maiden, the Mass of John the Baptist, the transformation of the hosts into the lamb, the beauty of angels, four kinds of hallowed people, and golden pennies.*

Oh, dear Lord, how profitable it is for a person to be of good will, even if they lack the strength for works; our dear Lord showed this to a poor maiden <note>original: "armen dirne." In this period, "dirne" meant a young girl or servant, without its later negative connotations.</note> when she could no longer be alone, yet sadly was not fit for his service. Then she spoke thus to God:

->Oh my dear Lord,<-
->Must I be without Mass today?<-

In this longing, God took away all her earthly senses and brought her miraculously into a beautiful church. She found no one inside; then she thought: "Oh woe, you poor thing, slow-poke! Now you have come too late because you did not get up early enough. This will benefit you little here." Then she saw a youth coming who brought a bundle of white flowers; these he strewed at the bottom of the tower and went away. Then came another and brought a bundle of violets; these he strewed in the middle of the church. Then yet another came and brought a bundle of roses; these he strewed beautifully before the altar of Our Lady. Then came a fourth and brought a bundle of white lilies and strewed them in the choir. When they had done this, they bowed beautifully and went away. These youths were so noble and beautiful to look upon, that no human suffering could ever be so great in the body that, if he could look upon them rightly, all his pain would not have to vanish.

Then came two scholars <note>original: "schůler," referring to acolytes or young clerics in minor orders.</note> in white garments; they brought two lights, which they placed upon the altar. Then they walked very beautifully and remained in the choir. Then came an unusually tall man, who was very thin and yet not old. His clothes were so poor that his arms and his legs were bare. He carried a

...a white lamb before his breast, and he carried two cruets <note>original: "ampellen." Small vessels used to hold the wine and water for the Eucharist.</note> on his fingers. Then he went to the altar and placed the lamb upon it and bowed lovingly toward it. That was John the Baptist, who was to sing the Mass. Then came a youth who was truly delicate in his bearing; he carried an eagle before his breast. That was John the Evangelist. Then came a simple man, Saint Peter. Then came a tall youth who brought a bundle of vestments <note>original: "gegerwedes." From "gerwen," meaning to prepare or dress; likely referring to the liturgical garments or equipment needed for the service.</note> with which those three lords prepared themselves. Then came a great host—that was the powerful household of the kingdom of heaven—and they filled the church so full that the poor maiden could find no place where she could remain. 

So she went to stand down in the tower. There she found a certain kind of people in white clothing who had no hair, but they had simple crowns upon their heads. These were those who had not lived according to the Law <note>original: "ê." This refers to God’s law, religious rules, or the state of marriage. Here it implies they did not follow a formal religious path during their lives.</note>. They did not have the adornment of hair—that is, of good works. By what means, then, had they come to the kingdom of heaven? Through repentance and through good will at their end. Further on she found even more beautiful people dressed in many-colored clothes; they were adorned with the beautiful hair of virtues and crowned with God's honor. Still further she found even more beautiful people, who were dressed in rose-colored clothes; they had a beautiful sign of widowhood and a crown of accepted chastity.

The poor maiden was poorly dressed and was sick in body, and she could not remain anywhere among those three groups. Then she went to stand before the choir and looked inside, where our dear Lady <note>The Virgin Mary.</note> stood in the highest place, along with Saint Catherine, Saint Cecilia, bishops, martyrs, angels, and very many virgins. When this poor human saw this great lordship, she also looked at herself to see if she dared to remain there in her unworthiness. She was wearing a reddish-brown cloak, which was made of love <term>minne: The medieval German concept of mystical or divine love, often characterized by intense longing.</term> and according to the burning <note>original: "brunekeit." Literally "brownness," but in mystical texts it often refers to the "glow" or "burning" of spiritual intensity.</note> of the senses toward God and toward all good things. The cloak was adorned with gold and also with a song; it sang thus: 

> I would gladly die of love.

She also saw herself looking like a noble maiden and wore on her head a magnificent chaplet <note>original: "schapel." A decorative wreath or circlet worn on the head.</note> of gold, upon which another song was set, which sang thus:

### *V. A song of the Soul to God regarding five things, and how God is a garment for the Soul and the Soul for God. <note>original: Greith p. 240. This refers to a 19th-century edition of the text by Carl Greith.</note>*

->You shine into my soul<-
->Like the sun against gold.<-
->Whenever I must rest in You, Lord,<-
->Then my joy is manifold.<-
->You clothe Yourself with my soul<-
->And You are also its closest garment;<-
->That a parting must occur there—<-
->Truly I never found greater heartbreak.<-
->If You would love me more intensely,<-
->Then I would surely leave this place <note>That is, the Soul would leave the physical body/earth to be fully united with God.</note>,<-
->Where I might love You without ceasing<-
->According to my heart's desire.<-
->Now I have sung to You,<-
->Yet I have not yet succeeded;<-
->If You would sing to me,<-
->Then I must succeed.<-

### *VI. A responsive song of God in the Soul regarding five things.*

When I shine, you must be radiant;
When I flow, you must be stirred <note>original: "wủthen." In this mystical context, it suggests an intense, boiling motion of the spirit moved by grace, rather than modern "rage."</note>;
When you sigh, you draw My <note>The manuscript originally read "your," but the editor suggests "My" fits the context of God speaking.</note> divine heart into yourself.
When you weep for Me, I take you into My arms;
But when you love <term>Minne: the high medieval concept of sacred, courtly, and reciprocal love between the soul and God</term>, then we two become one,
And when we two are thus one, no parting can ever occur there;
Rather, a constant awaiting dwells between us both.
Lord, then I wait with hunger and with thirst,
With hunting and with desire,
Until the playful hour
When from Your divine mouth
Flow the chosen words,
Which have been heard by no one
But by the Soul alone,
Who has stripped herself of the earth
And laid her ear before Your mouth; —
Yes, she grasps the treasure of love.

### *VII. In the praise of suffering He appears to you. Of the two golden cups of suffering and of consolation.*

I, sinful and sluggish, was supposed to pray at a certain hour, but God acted as if He would give me no grace at all. Then I wished...

# Chapters V — VIII.

I troubled myself miserably regarding my bodily sickness, which I thought was a hindrance to spiritual enjoyment. "Ah, no," said my soul, "think instead of all His faithfulness and praise your Lord thus: <note>original: "Gloria in excelsis deo"</note> Glory to God in the highest."

In that praise, a great light appeared to my soul, and with that light, God revealed Himself in great honor and incalculable clarity. Then our Lord lifted two golden cups in His hands, both of which were full of living wine. In the left hand was the red wine of suffering <note>The "red wine" symbolizes the blood of Christ and the participation in His passion through physical pain.</note>, and in the right hand was the supreme consolation. Then our Lord spoke: "Such are those who drink this wine; for although I pour both from divine love, the white is nobler in itself, and noblest of all are those who drink both the white and the red."

### VIII. *Concerning the whole of purgatory <term>vegefủr: the place of cleansing or 'purging' before entering heaven</term>; how a person released a thousand souls from it with the tears of love.*

A person should pray with great desire
Very simply for the poor souls to God of the heavenly kingdom.
Then God showed them the terrible purgatory in its entirety,
And then so many kinds of torment
According to how the sins were upon them.
Then that person's spirit became so fiercely moved <note>original: "kreftgrimig"</note>,
That they gathered the whole of purgatory into their arms.
There they endured it with difficulty
And desired with love <note>original: "miñeklich"</note>.
Then God of the heavenly kingdom spoke:
"Do not let yourself be hurt now,
It is too heavy for you."
Then the spirit spoke piteously:
"Ah, most Beloved, now release at least some of them."
Then our Lord spoke: "How many do you want?"

The spirit spoke: "Lord, as many as I may repay through Your goodness." Then our Lord spoke: "Now take a thousand and bring them where you will." Then they rose up out of the pain—black, fiery, foul, burning, bloody, and stinking. Then the person's spirit spoke again: "Ah, my dear Lord, what shall happen to these poor ones now? For in such a loathsome state they shall never enter Your kingdom." Then God inclined His nobility down exceedingly low and spoke a word that stands as a great comfort to us sinners: "You shall bathe them in the tears of love <term>miñetrehenen: tears shed out of mystical love or 'Minne' for God</term>."

<summary>The author reflects on the spiritual value of suffering through the imagery of red and white wine, followed by a vision where their intercession and 'tears of love' release a thousand souls from the torments of purgatory.</summary>
<keywords>purgatory, suffering, mystical marriage, intercession, tears of love, visions of Christ, healing, redemption</keywords>

# Second Part.

...tears, which now flow from the eyes of your body <term>lichamen: the physical body or fleshly form</term>." Then a great circular pool was seen. There the souls lifted themselves with a single swing all at once into it, and bathed in love <term>miñe: a Middle High German term for divine, mystical love</term> as clear as the sun. Then the person's spirit received incalculable joy and spoke: "Praised be you, most Beloved, by all creatures eternally! Now they belong to you well in your kingdom." Then our Lord leaned down to them from the heights and set upon them a crown of love, which they had released from there <note>referring to the souls released from purgatory</note> and spoke: "This crown you shall wear eternally so that all in my kingdom may know that they were released through your tears of love nine years before their proper time."

### *IX. God praises His bride in five things.*

->You are a light of the world,<-
->You are a crown of the virgins,<-
->You are a balm for the wounded,<-
->You are a faithfulness for the false,<-
->You are a bride of the Holy Trinity <term>driualtekeit: the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit</term>.<-

### *X. The bride praises God in return in five things.*

->You are a light in all lights,<-
->You are a flower above all crowns,<-
->You are a balm above all pains,<-
->You are an unchanging faithfulness without falsehood,<-
->You are a host in all lodgings.<-

### *XI. Concerning sevenfold <term>sibenhande: of seven kinds or sevenfold</term> love of God.*

Right divine love has seven beginnings:
Joyful love steps onto the path,
Fearing love receives the labor, strong love can do much,
Loving love receives no worldly fame,
Wise love has discernment,
Free love lives without heartache,
Powerful love is forevermore joyous.

### *XII. Concerning sevenfold perfections <term>vollekomenheiten: the state of being complete or spiritually perfect</term>.*

->Gladly unhonored, gladly unfeared, gladly alone,<-
->Gladly quiet, gladly low, gladly high, gladly in community.<-

### *XIII. Between God and the Soul, love shall be.*

Between you and God, love shall evermore be.
Between earthly things and you, there shall be anxiety and fear.
Between sins and you, there shall be hatred and conflict.
Between the kingdom of heaven <term>himelriche: the realm of God</term> and you, there shall be constant hope.

<summary>This page concludes a vision of intercession where tears of love release souls from purgatory, followed by a poetic exchange of praises and a list of sevenfold spiritual perfections.</summary>

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of spiritual teachings and rhythmic prose following the praises of God and the soul in Chapters IX through XI. It shifts from the visionary release of souls into a more philosophical and allegorical reflection on the nature of the soul and its union with God.</meta>

### ***XIV. Where purity, weakness, sickness, guidance, intensity, distress, exile, and rare comfort come from.***

Bitterness of heart comes from humanity,
Weakness of the body <term>lichamen: the physical or fleshly form</term> comes from the flesh alone,
A swift spirit comes from the nobility of the soul <term>sele: the eternal soul</term>,
Anguish before pain comes from guilt,
Sickness of the body comes from nature,
Miserable distress comes from self-will,
Rare comfort comes from restlessness.

### ***XV. How the one wounded by love is made whole.***

Whichever person at any hour
By true love <term>miñe: divine, mystical love</term> is rightly wounded,
That person shall nevermore be healthy,
Unless they kiss that same mouth
By which their soul was wounded.

### ***XVI. Concerning seven gifts of a brother.***

<note>original: "eis brûders" – This likely refers to a spiritual brother or a soul in a state of fraternal grace.</note>
The soul is bottomless in its longing <term>gerunge: intense spiritual desire</term>, burning in its love, loving in its presence, a mirror of the world, small in its greatness, faithful in its help, gathered together in God.

### ***XVII. How God frees the soul and makes it wise in His love.***

In this way God frees the simple soul and makes it wise in His love. "O dear dove, your feet are red, your feathers are smooth, your mouth is right, your eyes are beautiful, your head is sleek, your conduct is pleasant, your flight is swift, and yet you are all too swift to return to the earth."

### ***XVIII. How the soul touches God’s freedom in eight things.***

"Lord, my feet are colored with the blood of your true redemption <term>lůsunge: ransom or spiritual release</term>, my feathers are smoothed with your noble election, my mouth is directed by your Holy Spirit, my eyes are cleared in your fiery light, my head is smoothed by your faithful protection, my conduct is pleasant because of your generous gift, my flight is quickened by your restless desire, my earthly sinking comes from your union <term>einunge: the mystical union of God and the soul</term> with my body. The greater the release you give me, the longer I must soar within you."

<summary>This page outlines the spiritual origins of human suffering and the nature of the soul, culminating in an allegorical dialogue where God compares the soul to a dove and the soul responds by attributing its virtues to divine grace.</summary>
<keywords>soul, divine love, redemption, mystical union, allegory of the dove, spiritual longing</keywords>

It follows a series of short reflections on the soul's relationship with God (Chapters XIV-XVIII) and introduces a personified dialogue between Knowledge and the Soul, a common trope in medieval mystical literature where different faculties of the human spirit converse.</meta>

# Part Two

### XIX. *How Knowledge and the soul speak together, and she [the soul] says that she is threefold from three heavens. Knowledge speaks first.* <note>Reference to Greith, page 267.</note>

->O loving soul, I looked upon you,<-
->You are very lovely and wondrously formed.<-
->A light was lent for this purpose<-
->That I might behold you,<-
->Otherwise it never would have happened to me.<-
->You are threefold in yourself,<-
->You may well be God’s image.<-
->You are a manly man in your struggle, <note>In medieval mysticism, the soul (typically feminine in grammar) is often described as "manly" when exhibiting the virtue of Fortitude or spiritual strength in battle against temptation.</note><-
->You are a well-adorned maiden<-
->In the palace before your Lord.<-
->You are a delightful bride in your love-bed.<-
->God’s loving soul, in the struggle<-
->You are armed with immeasurable strength,<-
->And with such great gathering of your mind,<-
->That all the multitude of the world,<-
->Nor all the help of your flesh,<-
->Nor all the hosts of devils,<-
->Nor the power of hell<-
->May not fell you from God.<-
->You defend yourself as if with flowers;<-
->Your sword is the noble rose, Jesus Christ, <note>original: "die edel rose Jesus Christus" — The manuscript uses the feminine article 'die' for the rose; the scribe corrected a 'der' to 'die' here.</note><-
->With which you defend yourself.<-
->Your shield is the white lily, Mary.<-
->It helps your enemies nothing that they oppose you,<-
->Rather, it only serves to adorn you and increase in you<-
->The immeasurable glory of God.<-
->All who stand purely in this struggle<-
->Shall receive a rich reward from the Emperor.* <note>The "Emperor" is a common medieval metaphor for God as the supreme sovereign.</note><-

Ah, noble soul, in your palace of the Holy Trinity, where you stand so lovingly adorned before your Lord, what is your glory?

Lady <term>bekantnisse: Knowledge or Spiritual Awareness</term>, you are wiser than I; why do you ask me?

Lady <term>sele: Soul</term>, God has chosen you above all things; you are my lady and my queen.

<summary>This page presents an allegorical dialogue where personified Knowledge praises the Soul for its threefold nature as a warrior, a maiden, and a bride. It emphasizes the Soul's spiritual strength, protected by the "rose" of Christ and the "lily" of Mary.</summary>

# Chapter XIX. 39

Lady Knowledge, I am noble and free-born; I must not be without honor in that which I alone love. Thus I must win that which loves me, cherishes me, and honors me.

->You holy Trinity<-
->And all that heaven and earth hold<-
->Must be eternally subject to me.<-
->If I now let Love <note>original: "miñe"</note> have power over me,<-
->So that I give her the place,<-
->That she must bind me in holy patience,<-
->So that I do not increase my guilt,<-
->Then she leads me into noble gentleness,<-
->So that I must be ready for all good things,<-
->And harnesses me into strong obedience,<-
->That I must be lovingly subject<-
->To God and all creatures.<-

*Knowledge.* Ah, Lady Bride, will you tell me one more sign
->Of the unspeakable intimacy,<-
->That lies between God and you?<-

Lady Knowledge, I will not do that;
Brides must not all say what happens to them.
Holy contemplation and the most precious enjoyment
You shall have from me.
The chosen <term>experience</term> <note>original: "bevindunge" — a direct, felt perception of God's presence, as opposed to intellectual knowledge</note> of God
Shall be hidden from you and all creatures forevermore,
Except to me alone.

Lady Soul, your wondrous visions and your lofty words
That you have seen and heard in God,
When you force me to this,
That I bring forth even a little of it,
Then I place the Emperor’s light
In a dark, decaying stable. <note>This metaphor suggests that human language and intellectual knowledge are too lowly and "dark" to properly contain or describe the brilliance of a divine encounter.</note>

The cattle eat their straw well enough;
For some, who appear to be God’s children,
Yet butt their heads like unrestrained cattle
In the dark stable
And say what use such deception <note>original: "getůsche"</note> is to them,
That it is thought up out of whim,
And brought forth in false holiness.

Lady Knowledge, it is found written
That Saint Paul was led into the third heaven. <note>Refers to 2 Corinthians 12:2. Paul's ascent to the "third heaven" was a central proof-text for medieval mystics regarding the possibility of direct divine revelation.</note>
It would never have happened to him, had he remained Saul. <note>Saul was Paul's name before his conversion on the road to Damascus; the text implies that spiritual transformation is necessary for higher revelation.</note>
Had he found the truth
In the first and the second heaven,
He would never have ascended to the third.

<summary>The Soul explains to Knowledge that while contemplation can be shared, the intimate experience of God remains private, warning that words can diminish divine glory. It concludes with a reflection on St. Paul's spiritual transformation as a prerequisite for reaching the highest heaven.</summary>

<keywords>Lady Knowledge, Lady Soul, Trinity, Divine Love, Minne, contemplation, experience, Saint Paul, third heaven, spiritual transformation</keywords>

## Second Part

<margin>The Soul:</margin>
> There is a heaven that the devil has made,
> With his beautiful, false cunning.
> Within it, thoughts wander with sorrowful senses <note>original: "siñen" — the faculties of the mind and perception</note>,
> And the soul lies quite still,
> For she does not find the love <note>original: "miňe" — Minne, the specific medieval concept of ecstatic, reciprocal love between the soul and God</note> of her true nature.
> There the soul remains uncomforted
> And deceives the simple senses.
> In this heaven the devil shows himself
> Like a shining angel,
> Yes, even like God in His five wounds.
> Simple soul, beware!
> The second heaven is made
> Of the holy longing of the senses,
> And of the first part of love.
> In this heaven there is no light,
> The soul does not see God within it.

> She tastes an incomprehensible sweetness,
> Which goes through all her limbs.
> She also hears a voice regarding certain things,
> Which she still desires,
> For it is still mixed with earthly senses.
> If the depth of all humility <note>original: "diemútikeit"</note> is not there,
> Then the devil offers his light;
> That which then happens there,
> Is not from God.

> But if full humility is there,
> Then the soul must travel further
> Into the third heaven;
> There the true light will be given to her. —
> Then the senses say:
> Our lady, the soul, has slept since childhood,
> Now she is awake in the light of open love.
> In this light she looks all around her
> [To see] who he is, who shows himself to her,
> And what that is which is spoken to her.
> Then she truly sees and recognizes
> How God is all things in all things. —

> Now I lay all sorrow down
> And travel with Saint Paul into the third heaven.
> For God lovingly strikes my sinful body down there.
> This third heaven is vaulted and ordered,
> And shines beautifully with the <term>three Persons</term> <note>The Holy Trinity: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit</note>,
> Who begin thus:
> The true greeting of God,
> Which comes from the heavenly blood. <note>original: "blůt" — This could also be interpreted as "blossom" or "bloom" in this dialect, suggesting the "flowering" of the Godhead.</note>

<summary>The Soul describes three spiritual "heavens," warning that the first two can be mimicked by the devil or clouded by earthly senses, and only the third heaven—reached through deep humility—offers the true vision of God in all things.</summary>
<keywords>three heavens, spiritual discernment, Minne (Love), humility, Saint Paul, Holy Trinity, demonic deception</keywords>

Cap. XX.

## XX. *How Sister Hiltegunt is adorned in the kingdom of heaven with mantles, with seven crowns, and how the nine choirs praise her.*

On the feast day of a blessed virgin, Saint Barbara, Sister Hiltegunt received her honor. God showed this to a lame dog, who still licks its wounds with sorrow <note>The author frequently uses the self-deprecating image of a "lame dog" to refer to herself as a humble, suffering sinner.</note>. It happened thus in my prayer that I do not know whether the kingdom of heaven was inclined toward me, or if I was drawn into the wondrous house of God. There stood Hiltegunt before the throne of the heavenly Father, adorned like a new bride whom the King has brought home. She had three mantles about her and wore seven crowns on her head, and the nine choirs <note>The nine traditional orders of angels (Seraphim, Cherubim, etc.)</note> praise her especially. When I saw her, I recognized her in all the gifts she had received from God. Yet I desired to speak with her and questioned her while in this state of fruition <note>original: "gebruchunge" — the mystical experience of "enjoying" or possessing God’s presence</note>, so that I might be with her all the longer.

"Oh, from what do you have this rose-colored mantle?" Then Hiltegunt spoke: "I was a martyr in fiery love <term>Minne: the Middle High German term for the ecstatic, reciprocal love between the soul and God</term>, so that my heart's blood often poured over my head." Then I questioned her further: "From what do you have this golden mantle, which shines so beautifully?" She spoke: "From the image of good works." Then I spoke: "From what do you have this flowered white mantle?" She answered: "From the yearning love <note>original: "notlichen miñe" — a love born of spiritual necessity, distress, or intense longing</note> which I carried secretly in my soul and in my senses."

These were the seven crowns: the Crown of steadfastness, the Crown of holy faith, the Crown of loyalty, the Crown of gentle mercy, the Crown of holy discernment, the Crown of love [Minne], and the Crown of virginity. Then I asked further: "Dear one, where is the crown of humility <note>original: "diemůtikeit"</note>, which so well becomes spiritual people?" She answered: "I do not have one of my own, nor did I ever win one, except so much as God took away my pride." These seven crowns are all adorned especially with the garland of the nobility of pure, sublime <note>original: "heren" — the translator notes the manuscript writes "herren" (lords), but the context implies "high" or "exalted" virtue</note> chastity.

In this way the choirs praise her for nine virtues: "We praise you for your rest, for your good will, for your truth, for your wisdom, for your sweet sorrow, for your willing poverty..."

<summary>The author experiences a vision of the deceased Sister Hiltegunt standing as a bride of Christ in heaven, adorned with symbolic mantles and crowns representing her earthly virtues and spiritual suffering. The vision emphasizes that her heavenly glory is a direct reflection of her internal "Minne" and secret devotion.</summary>

<keywords>Sister Hiltegunt, Vision, Heaven, Seven Crowns, Three Mantles, Minne (Divine Love), Humility, Nine Choirs of Angels, Saint Barbara</keywords>

...in your strength, in your justice. The Thrones <note>original: "throni" — the third-highest order of angels, associated with God's justice and steadiness.</note> praise her thus: "We praise the Bridegroom in the beauty of the bride."

> Of which I will now be silent,
> Except only that the kingdom of heaven is love-colored <note>original: "miñevar" — colored by divine Love (Minne), often envisioned as rosy or fiery.</note>,

### XXI. *If you wish to look upon the mountain, you must have seven things.*

> A mountain have I seen,
> That happened very quickly (*sic*),
> For no body could endure
> That the soul should be there for even an hour.
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The mountain was white below, cloud-colored,
> And above at its height, clear as the fiery sun.
> Its beginning and its end I could nowhere find,
> And within it played in itself,
> Flowing gold-colored in uncounted love <term>Minne: the ecstatic, reciprocal love between the soul and God</term>.
> Then I said: "Lord, blessed are the eyes
> That shall eternally behold the soaring of love <note>original: "miñesweben" — the fluid, hovering movement of divine love.</note>
> And recognize this wonder;
> I can never name it."
> Then the mountain spoke: "Your eyes,
> Which are thus to see me,
> They must be adorned with seven things;
> It can never happen otherwise."
> They speak thus: "Gladly paying necessary debts,
> And holding nothing for oneself,
> Faithful against hatred
> And loving against ferocity,
> Pure of guilt and ready
> Toward the reception <note>Likely referring to the reception of the Eucharist or divine grace.</note>."

### XXII. *How Contemplation asks the loving souls about the Seraphim and the lowest human.*

Lady Soul, would you rather be an angel of the Seraphim or a human with body and soul in the lowest choir of the angels?

<header>Chapter XXIII.</header>

*The Soul to Contemplation:* Lady Contemplation, you have clearly seen that the angels of the Seraphim <note>The highest order of angels, whose name means "the burning ones."</note> are high princes, and that they are one love, one fire, one breath, and one light with God.

*Contemplation:* Lady Soul, you have clearly seen that the angels are simple beings <note>original: "einvaltige personen" — in a theological sense, this means they are uncompounded and pure in nature.</note> and that they do not praise, nor love, nor know God more than what is innate to them; and the lowliest human may likewise recover himself through Christian faith, through repentance, through longing, and through good will, yet his soul cannot burn so fiercely within the Godhead.

*The Soul:* Lady Contemplation, you have clearly seen that the angels of the Seraphim are God’s children and yet His servants. The lowliest soul is a daughter of the Father and sister of the Son and a friend of the Holy Spirit and truly a bride of the Holy Trinity.

> When the game is played out,
> Then one sees who weighs the most:
> The most worthy angel, Jesus Christ, <note>Christ is here called an "angel" in the sense of a messenger (Greek: *angelos*), a common medieval trope despite His divine nature.</note>
> Who hovers above the Seraphim,
> Who must be one undivided God with His Father.
> Him I, the lowliest soul, take into my arms,
> And eat Him and drink Him <note>A reference to the physical and spiritual union found in the Eucharist.</note>
> And do with Him what I will.
> That can never happen to the angels,
> However high they dwell above me;
> And His Godhead shall never be so distant ¹) to me,
> That I should not, without ceasing,
> Fully experience it in all my limbs,
> So that I may never cool down again.
> What does it trouble me then, what the angels experience?

### XXIII. How Love questions and teaches the dull soul and would gladly bring her to her love, and speaks first, and the dull soul answers.

*Love:* Ah, foolish soul, where are you,
Or what is your dwelling and by what do you live?
Where can you find rest, now that you do not love
Your delightful God above your own will
And above all your power?

¹) Manuscript: *tûre*. <note>Meaning "dear," "precious," or in this context, "prohibitively distant."</note>

<header>Part Two.</header>

*Soul:* Leave me unawakened;
I do not know what you are saying to me.
*Love:* One must certainly wake the Queen
When her King wishes to come.
*Soul:* I am in a holy order,
I fast, I keep vigil, I am without mortal sin <note>original: "hŏptsůnde" — the "head" or cardinal sins that sever one's relationship with God.</note>,
I am bound enough by my rules.
*Love:* What use is it to bind an empty vessel tightly
If the wine still leaks out?
Then one must fill it with the stones of external work <note>original: "vswendigen arbeit" — outward religious activities performed without inner devotion.</note>
And with the ashes of transience.
*Soul:* I dwell in the delights of my kinsfolk
And my dear spiritual friends;
And how could I joyfully love Him
Whom I do not know?
*Love:* Alas, can you not recognize the Lord
Whom one names to you so often?
You are more concerned with your hound-like body <note>original: "huntlichen lichamen" — a vivid medieval metaphor for the flesh's persistent and base appetites.</note>,
Than with Jesus, your sweet Lord.
Because of this, you shall never win honor in His eyes.
*Soul:* I live according to my own will,
For I desire to fulfill it.
*Love:* If you wish to show God true loyalty,
Then you should follow His spirit in His love.
*Soul:* I rest in the world of my body.
*Love:* You should be ashamed before God this day
That you nevertheless bear a spiritual name <note>Referring to her formal status as a religious sister or nun.</note>,
And yet go about entirely according to your body.
*Soul:* How should I sustain myself
If I were to burden myself with you?
*Love:* Oh, faithless one! He who made the soul so noble
That she can eat nothing but God,
Will not allow her body to perish.
*Soul:* You scold me greatly;
If I knew where He was,
I might yet be converted.
*Love:* If you wish to dwell with Him in noble freedom <note>original: "vrîheit" — a central concept in the mysticism of Mechthild von Magdeburg, referring to the soul's detachment from all things but God.</note>,
You must first leave this dwelling of evil habits.
*Soul:* Alas! Many a man who is wise in learning
And in natural intellect does not do this:
He does not dare to place himself
Into the power of naked love <note>original: "nakkenden miñe" — love stripped of all self-interest, comforts, and external religious trappings.</note>.
*Love:* But the simple and pure ones <note>original: "einvaltigen" — those who are "one-fold" or single-minded in their devotion.</note>,
Who purely intend God in all their doing—
Toward them God must naturally incline.

<summary>This page features a dialogue where Lady Love (Minne) rebukes the Soul for being satisfied with external religious observance while remaining attached to worldly comforts and her own will. Love calls the Soul to "noble freedom" and "naked love," warning that intellectual learning is no substitute for the simple, pure intention of the heart.</summary>

### Chapter XXIII.

*Soul:* I thought, when I gave myself up for God's sake, that I had then climbed very high.
*Love:* What use is it to dress a sleeping man in fine clothes and set noble food before him while he sleeps, if he still cannot eat? Ah, dear soul of mine, let yourself be awakened.
*Soul:* Ah, now tell me, where is His dwelling?
*Love:* There is no other lord who dwells simultaneously in all his houses, except for Him alone. He dwells in the peace of holy loving-kindness <note>original: "miñesamkeit" — a state of being filled with divine love; intimacy.</note> and whispers with His love in the narrow solitude <note>original: "enôte" — a wilderness or desolate place; here referring to the interior "desert" of the soul where God speaks.</note> of the soul. He also embraces her in the noble delight of His love. He greets her with His lovely eyes when the beloved truly gaze upon one another. He kisses her through <note>original: "durkůsset" — a powerful mystical term suggesting a kiss that permeates the entire being.</note> with His divine mouth. Ah, well for you! More so than that supreme hour! He cherishes her with full power in the bed of love. Thus she enters the highest bliss and the most loving pain, if she becomes truly aware of Him.
*Soul:* Ah, dear one, now let yourself be loved, and do not defend yourself with bitterness. Who are those who defend themselves with bitterness?
*Love:* Those are the ones who burden other people and themselves with their malice. Now I tell you who He is: He is the highest of all heights, and that same highest Height has bowed down into the lowest valley <note>A reference to the Incarnation, where God takes on lowly human form.</note>, and this lowest valley has placed itself within the highest of all heights. Dull soul, look all around you and open your blind eyes.
*Soul:* If He has stepped down from the highest height for the sake of my love, and has given Himself entirely to me along with all creatures—indeed, if He would not take His goodness away from me—then I should forever be ashamed before His eyes that I would ever want to trade my worthless copper <note>The soul’s own meager efforts or worldly attachments.</note> for His precious gold. Alas, where have I been, I miserable blind one, that I have lived so long without powerful love, with which I truly overcome all my distress despite all my enemies? Now, I alone in my poverty have missed out on <note>original: "versumet"</note> so much good, <insert>I have.</insert> <note>The manuscript uses "habe" here instead of "hân".</note>

<summary>In this dialogue, Love describes the intimate union between God and the Soul, using the metaphor of the "highest height" descending into the "lowest valley." The Soul experiences a breakthrough, realizing her own spiritual poverty and the "worthless copper" she previously valued compared to divine love.</summary>

### *XXIV. How the loving soul joins herself to God and His chosen loved ones, and shall be like all the saints. How the devil and the soul speak together. ¹)*

<margin>We are to blame for our own pains.</margin>
Ah, Lord Jesus Christ, your innocent suffering comforts me, ²) for I am to blame for all my own pains; and your holy death keeps my memory <note>original: "hůgenisse" — mind, memory, or consciousness of God.</note> alive in you, and your unstained blood has flowed through my soul.

Mary, beloved mother, I stand with you by the cross with all my Christian faith, and the sword of holy sorrow cuts through my soul, because there are so many who appear spiritual but are worldly <note>original: "wandelber" — changeable, fickle, or focused on transitory things.</note>.

John the Baptist, I am imprisoned with you, for the unfaithfulness of the handmaid of falsehood has killed God’s word in my mouth.

John the Evangelist, I have fallen asleep with you in heartfelt love upon the breast of Jesus Christ, and from there I have seen and heard such glorious wonders that my body has often gone out of itself <note>original: "von im selber komen" — an ecstatic state where the soul leaves the physical body.</note>.

Peter, I am betrothed to God with you, for I am never at ease in a human way, and I often feel a spiritual longing <note>original: "we" — a painful yearning or "woe."</note> for the praise of Jesus Christ.

Paul, I have been wondrously caught up <note>original: "vfgezuket" — snatched up, referring to Paul’s rapture into the third heaven.</note> with you and have seen such a house that nothing has ever made me wonder so much as how I could remain a living human being since that time. When I think that the Heavenly Father is there as the cup-bearer for the blessed, and Jesus is the cup <note>original: "kopf" — a drinking vessel; the scribe adds "(Becher)" to clarify this archaic term.</note>, the Holy Spirit is the pure wine, and how the entire Trinity is the full cup, and Love is the powerful cellar-master — God knows, I would gladly accept that the...

<header>Chapter XXIV.</header>

...love <note>original: "miñe"</note> prays there at home. Now I will gladly drink gall here. Oh, dear Jesus, now reward all those kindly who serve me bitterness here, for they make me rich in grace. A cup of gall came to me that was so potent it went through my body and soul. Then I prayed especially to God for my cup-bearer, that He would serve him the heavenly wine. Truly He did this and said: "You maiden, be of good cheer. The greatness of my wonder shall pass over you; the lions shall fear you, the bears shall feel safe with you, and the wolves shall flee from you. That shall be your companion." I am certain of this; and just as it has happened to me until now, I must still drink many a cup of gall, for unfortunately the devil still has many cup-bearers among religious people who are so full of poison that they cannot drink it alone; they must serve it bitterly to God's children.

Stephen, I kneel with you before the Jewish hearts beneath the sharp stones, for they fall upon me, both great and small. Those who appear to be good people stone me behind my back and flee, wishing me not to know that it was done by them. Yet God has seen it.

Lawrence, I was bound in you for more than twenty years upon a gruesome gridiron <note>St. Lawrence was martyred by being roasted on a grill; the author uses this to describe a long period of intense spiritual or physical trial</note>; yet God kept me unburned and has now quenched me for more than seven years.

Martin, I dwell with you in insignificance <note>original: "vnahteberkeit" — being disregarded or held in low esteem</note>, and true love for God has martyred me above all other labor.

Dominic, my dear father, I have a small part with you, for I have desired it for many days:

> That my sinful heart’s blood must still flow
> Under the feet of unbelieving heretics.

Catherine, I go to battle with you, for the masters of hell would gladly strike me down. One came to me, beautiful as the shining of the sun, so that I should believe he was an angel, and he brought a glowing book and spoke: "Take the peace <note>original: "peize" (pax) — referring to the 'pax-board' or 'osculatorium,' a liturgical object kissed by the faithful during Mass as a symbol of peace.</note> since you cannot come to the Mass." Then the soul spoke with disciplined wisdom: "He who has no peace himself cannot give me peace." Then he vanished and transformed—
