...himself and came back like a very poor sick man whose intestines were falling out, and said: "Alas, you are so holy, make me healthy." Then the soul spoke again: "He who is sick himself cannot heal anyone. It is written: 'He who is more capable should help the other.' It is also written that one should help no one against God; yet what one does well is not against God. Where there is no good within, no one can bestow any good. You have an eternal sickness; if you wish to recover, then go and show yourself to a priest, or a bishop, or an archbishop, or the pope. I have no power except only that I am able to sin." <note>This reflects a common medieval mystical sentiment: that a human's only "natural" possession is their capacity for sin; all healing and virtue come from God alone.</note> Then he spoke with wrath: "That I will never do." Then he became like black smoke and moved away rudely and departed. Yet I was not afraid of him.

Mary Magdalene, I dwell with you in the wilderness, for all things are alien <note>original: "ellende" — meaning to be in exile or a foreign land; the soul feels like a stranger to the physical world</note> to me, except God alone. Lord, heavenly Father, between You and me there goes without ceasing an incomprehensible breath, in which I recognize and see many wonders and unspeakable things, and unfortunately receive little benefit, for I am so wretched a vessel that I cannot endure Your smallest spark. Unbound love <term>miñe: the Middle High German term for divine, mystical love</term> dwells in the senses, for it is still mixed with earthly things, so that a person may cry out: "In grace, love is externalized in the senses and has unfortunately not yet ascended into the soul." Many people have fallen because their soul remained unwounded. Solomon and David received the Holy Spirit in their human senses; but when their senses changed, they fell into false love. God knows, their soul was not sunk into the lowest depths beneath all creatures, nor wounded with the powerful part of love,

->Of he who never tasted the best wine,<-
->He boasts the most loudly and the most often<-

Bound love dwells in the soul <term>sele: the spiritual essence of the person</term> and rises above human senses and allows the body <term>licham: the physical corpse or body</term> none of its own will. It is disciplined and very still. It lowers its wings and listens for the unspeakable voice, and looks into the incomprehensible light and strives with great desire for her Lord's will. If then the body may flap its wings, the soul cannot [reach] the highest...

<summary>The soul dismisses a demonic deception by pointing toward the Church's authority and then describes the difference between superficial love in the senses and true "bound" love in the soul.</summary>
<keywords>demonic deception, Mary Magdalene, Solomon, David, Minne, mystical love, the senses, the soul, humility</keywords>

<header>Chapter XXV.</header>
<sig>4</sig>

...what can happen to a person can never be fully experienced. In this "bound" love <term>miñe: the Middle High German term for mystical, divine love</term> the wounded soul becomes rich, and her outward senses become very poor; for the more riches God finds in her, the more she humbles herself more deeply out of the true nobility of love. Whichever person is thus bound with the deep-touching <note>original: "gruntrůrunge" — a stirring in the very depths or "ground" of the soul, a key concept in Rhineland mysticism</note> of powerful love, I can find in them no fall toward the mortal sins, because the soul is bound; she must always love. May God bind us all in this way!

### XXV. Of the lament of the loving soul, how God spares her and withdraws His gifts; of wisdom, how the soul asks God who He is and how He is. Of the orchard, of the flowers, and of the song of the maidens. ¹)

O You countless treasure in Your richness! O You incomprehensible wonder in Your multiplicity! O You endless honor in Your lordship of Your nobility! How painful it is for me to be without You <note>literally: "how woe it is to me after You"</note>, when You wish to spare me <note>God "sparing" the soul here refers to Him withholding His overwhelming presence so the soul is not consumed</note>,

->All creatures could not fully tell You,<-
->Even if they had to lament on my behalf,<-
->For I suffer inhuman distress;<-
->A human death would be much gentler to me.<-
->I seek You with my thoughts,<-
->As a maiden seeks her beloved in secret;<-
->Because of this I must fall very ill,<-
->For I am bound with You.<-
->The bond is stronger than I am,<-
->Therefore I cannot become free from love.<-

->In a voice of exile <note>original: "ellendiger stiñe" — 'ellende' refers to being in a foreign land; the soul feels like a stranger on earth while longing for God</note>.<-

->I cannot rest; I burn<-
->Unquenched in Your hot love.<-
->I hunt You with all my might.<-

->You would soon be overtaken by me,<-
->If I could truly find Your trail.<-
->O Beloved, now do not run ahead of her for too long,<-
->And rest a little, lovingly,<-
->So that I might catch You.<-

## Second Part.

O Lord, since You have withdrawn from me everything that I have from You, yet grant me by grace those same gifts that You have given by nature to a dog; that is, that I may be faithful to You in my distress without any kind of defiance <note>original: "widertrutz" — resistance or rebellion against God's will</note>: I certainly desire this more than Your kingdom of heaven.

> Dear dove <note>a traditional symbol for the soul in medieval mysticism, derived from the Song of Solomon</note>, now hear Me:
> My divine wisdom is so far above you,
> That I order all My gifts within you in such a way,
> As you are able to bear them in your poor body.
> Your secret seeking must find Me,
> Your heart's sorrow can compel Me,
> Your sweet hunting makes Me so weary,
> That I desire to cool Myself
> Within your pure soul,
> Wherein I am bound.
> Your sore heart’s sighing trembling
> Has driven My justice away from you,
> This is very right for both you and Me.
> I cannot be alone without you.
> However far we may be parted,
> We yet cannot be separated.
> I cannot touch you ever so slightly,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But that I do you immeasurable pain in your body.
> Should I give Myself to you at all times according to your desire,
> Then I would have to forgo My sweet lodging
> Upon the earth within you;
> For a thousand bodies could not
> Fully satisfy the desire of one loving soul <term>miñenden sele: a soul practicing "minne," or ecstatic, courtly-style divine love</term>.
> Therefore: the higher the love, the holier the martyr.
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;O Lord, You spare my foul prison <note>the physical body</note> far too much,
> Wherein I drink the water of the world
> And eat with great misery
> The ash-cake of my frailty <term>brôdekeit: the inherent weakness, fragility, or mortality of human nature</term>,
> And I am wounded unto death
> With the arrow of Your fiery love.
> Now, Lord, You let me lie
> Unanointed in great torment.
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Dear heart, my queen,
> How long will you be so impatient?
> Whenever I wound you most sorely,
> Then I anoint you most lovingly in that same hour.
> The greatness of My riches is yours alone,
> And over Myself you shall be powerful.
> I am lovingly well-disposed toward you;
> If you have the alloy <note>original: "gelôte" — refers to the base metal or solder used to join things, representing the soul's earthly imperfection</note>, I have the gold.

### Chapter XXV.

All that You have done, let go, and suffered for me,
I will repay it all to You
And will give myself to You eternally,
According to all Your will.
Lord, I want to ask You two things;
Instruct me according to Your grace:
When my eyes mourn in wretchedness <note>original: "ellendekliche" — suggesting a sense of being in exile or far from one’s true home in God</note>
And my mouth is silent in simplicity
And my tongue is bound with grief
And my senses ask me from hour to hour
What is wrong with me—then, Lord, it is
All because of You,
And my flesh falls away from me, my blood dries up,
My bones grow cold, my veins cramp,
And my heart melts for Your love <term>miñe: a Middle High German term for ecstatic, often courtly-style love between the soul and God</term>,
And my soul burns with the voice of a hungry lion.
How it is for me then, where You are then,
Most beloved, tell me that.

> You are like a new bride
> Whose only beloved has slipped away while she slept,
> Toward whom she leaned with all her faithfulness,
> And she cannot bear that he departs from her for even an hour.
> When she then awakes, she can have no more of him
> Than she can carry in her mind;
> From this, all her lamenting begins.
> As long as the youth has not given himself home to his bride,
> She must often be alone without him.
> I come to you according to My desire, when I will;
> If you are modest and still
> And hide your distress as best you can,
> Then the power of love <note>original: "miñe"</note> increases in you.
> Now I tell you where I am then.
> I am in Myself in all places and in all things,
> As I ever was without beginning,
> And I wait for you in the orchard <note>original: "bömgarten"</note> of love
> And pick for you the flower of sweet union <note>original: "sůssen einunge" — the mystical merging of the soul with God</note>
> And make you a bed there
> From the delightful grass of holy knowledge,
> And the bright sun of My eternal Godhead <note>original: "gotheit"</note>
> Shines upon you with the hidden wonder of My delight,
> Which you have secretly shown a little.
> And there I bend down to you the highest branch of My holy Trinity <note>original: "drivalteikeit"</note>,
> Then you pluck the green, white, and red apples of My gentle humanity <note>original: "menscheit"</note>,
> And then the shadow of My Holy Spirit <note>original: "heligen geistes"</note> shields you
> From all earthly sadness,
> So you cannot think of your heart-sorrow <note>original: "herzeleit"</note>.

<header>Part Two.</header>

If you embrace this bond, then I shall teach you the virgins’ song:
The melody, the words, the sweet resonance,
Which those who are permeated by impurity
Cannot understand in themselves;
Yet they shall have a sweet transformation.
Beloved, now begin to sing and let us hear how you can do it.
"Alas, my most beloved, I am hoarse in the throat of my chastity;
Yet the sugar of Your sweet mercy
Has cleared my throat so that I can now sing,
Like this, Lord: Your blood and mine are one, unstained;
Your love <term>miñe: the Middle High German term for the ecstatic, spiritual love between the soul and God</term> and mine are one, undivided;
Your garment and mine are one, unspotted;
Your mouth and mine are one, a kiss <note>original: "vnkůst" — likely a variant of "kust" (kiss), signifying the absolute union of the soul’s voice with God’s</note>, etc."
These are the words of the song of Love's voice,
And the sweet resonance of the heart must remain unwritten,
For no earthly hand can record it.

## XXVI. *Concerning this book and the writer of this book.*

I was warned about this book,
And it was said by people:
If it were not guarded,
A fire might pass over it <note>Mechthild refers here to the threat of her work being condemned as heresy and burned by church authorities</note>.
Then I did as I have been accustomed to do since childhood;
Whenever I was troubled, I had to pray.
Then I bowed to my Beloved and said:
"Ah, Lord, now I am distressed;
For the sake of Your honor, should I now remain uncomforted by You?
Then You have led me astray,
For You Yourself commanded me to write it."
Then God revealed Himself at once
To my sorrowful soul; He held this book in His right hand,
And said: "My beloved, do not be too troubled;
No one can burn the truth.
Whoever would take it from My hand
Must be stronger than I.
This book is threefold <note>Referring to the Trinity (Father, Son, and Holy Spirit)</note>
And signifies Me alone.
This parchment <note>original: "bermint"</note> that surrounds it here
Signifies My pure, white, righteous humanity <term>menscheit</term>,
Which suffered death for your sake.
The words signify My wondrous divinity <term>gotheit</term>;
They flow from hour to hour
Into your soul from My divine mouth.
The voice of the words signifies My living Spirit
And fulfills the true truth within itself.
Now look into all these words,"

# Chapter XXVI.

## How praiseworthy it is to reveal my secrets, and do not doubt yourself.

> O Lord, if I were a learned, spiritual man <note>original: "geleret geistlich man" — referring to a university-educated cleric or monk</note>,
> And if You had performed this small, great wonder in him,
> Then You might receive eternal honor for it.
> How is one to believe You now,
> That You have built a golden house
> Within this filthy puddle <note>original: "vnfletigen pfůl" — Mechthild uses this self-deprecating language to contrast her perceived lowliness as a woman with the brilliance of God's grace</note>,
> And truly dwell therein with Your mother,
> And with every creature,
> And with all Your heavenly household?
> Lord, in this I cannot find earthly wisdom.

> Daughter, many a wise man loses his precious gold
> Through neglect on a great highway,
> With which he might have traveled to the university <note>original: "hoher schůle" — literally "high school," but referring to the medieval universities like Paris or Cologne</note>;
> Someone must find it.
> I have by nature done this for many a day.
> For whenever I gave special grace,
> I always sought out the lowest,
> Smallest, and most secret place.
> The highest mountains cannot receive
> The revelation of My grace,
> For the flood of My Holy Spirit
> Flows by nature into the valley.
> One finds many a wise master of the scripture
> Who is a fool before My eyes in his own self.
> And I tell you even more:
> It is a great honor for Me before them,
> And it strengthens holy Christendom in them greatly,
> That an unlearned mouth
> Teaches learned tongues through My Holy Spirit.

> O my Lord, I sigh and long
> And pray for Your scribe <note>Heinrich von Halle, a Dominican friar who compiled and edited Mechthild’s writings</note>,
> Who has written this book from my words,
> That You would also grant him grace as a reward,
> Such as was never lent to any man;
> For Lord, Your gift is a thousand times more
> Than Your creatures are able to receive.

Then our Lord spoke:

> Because they have written it with golden letters,
> So shall all these words of the book
> Stand upon their uppermost garment,
> Eternally manifest in My kingdom,
> Written with heavenly, shining gold
> Above all their other adornments.
> For free love <term>vrie miñe: the soul’s noble, unforced, and ecstatic love for God that exists outside of duty or fear</term> must always be the highest thing in a human being.

## This is the third book.

---

*I. Concerning the kingdom of heaven and the nine choirs <note>The nine choirs of angels in medieval theology</note> and who shall fill the void left by the fallen. Concerning the throne of the apostles and Saint Mary and where Christ dwells within. Concerning the reward of the preachers, martyrs, and virgins, and concerning the unbaptized children.*

> The soul spoke thus to her longing:
> "Ah, go forth and see where my beloved may be,
> Tell him, I desire to love."
> Then longing drove swiftly away,

for she is by nature fast, and she came to the heights and cried out: "Great Lord, open up and let me in." Then the host spoke: "What do you want, you who burn so fiercely?"

> "Lord, I make known to you,
> My Lady cannot live long like this;
> If you would flow <note>Referring to the flow of divine grace</note> then she might soar,
> For the fish cannot live long upon the sand
> And remain fresh."
> "Go back, I will not let you in now,
> Unless you bring me the hungry soul,
> Whom I desire above all things."
> When the messenger now returned
> And the soul perceived her Lord's will,
> Ah, how lovingly she received it!
> She raised herself up in a gentle pull
> And in a joyful flight.

### Part Three.

Then two angels, wearing caps, came to her very quickly; God sent them toward her out of heartfelt love and spoke to her: "Lady Soul, why do you wish to go so far? You are still clothed with the dark earth." <margin>Gregory explains <note>original: "Gregorius exponit"; a reference to Pope Gregory the Great, whose theological writings on angels and the soul were standard for medieval authors.</note></margin> Then she spoke: "Yes, Lords, be quiet about all that and greet me a little better; I want to travel into Love. The closer you sink toward the earth, the more you hide your sweet heavenly glance, and the higher I climb, the clearer I shine." Then they took the soul between them and led her joyfully away.

> When the soul saw the land of angels,
> Where she is known to be without peril,
> Then heaven was opened to her,
> There she stood and her heart melted and she saw
> Her Beloved and spoke:
> "O Lord, when I see you,
> I must praise you in wondrous wisdom.
> Where am I?
> I have now come to be lost in you.
> Indeed, I cannot think of the earth,
> Nor of any of my heartache.
> I had intended, when I saw you,
> That I would complain much to you about the earth:
> Now, Lord, the sight of you has overcome me,
> For you have raised me entirely
> Above my own nobility."

Then she knelt down and thanked him for his grace, and took her crown from her head and set it upon the rose-colored scars <note>The wounds of the crucifixion, often described as beautiful or floral in medieval mysticism.</note> of his feet and desired that she might come close to him. Then he took her under his divine arms and laid his fatherly hand upon her breast and looked at her face. Notice whether she was well-kissed there! In that kiss, she was then snatched up into the highest height above all the choirs of angels.

> The smallest truth,
> Which I have seen, heard, and known there,
> To it the highest wisdom is not equal,
> Which was ever named on this earth.

I have seen unheard-of things within there, as my <term>confessors</term> <note>German: "bihter"; priests who acted as spiritual advisors and checked her visions for theological accuracy.</note> say, for I am unlearned in the scriptures. Now I fear God if

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page continues the visionary account of the soul's ascent into the celestial realms. The previous page ended with the soul being "snatched up into the highest height" following an intimate encounter with the Divine. This section shifts from the soul’s personal journey to a structural description of the heavenly hierarchy and the "breach" left by the fall of the angels.</meta>

# Chapter 1.

I am silent, and yet I fear the judgment of unknown people if I remain silent. Dearly beloved people, how can I help it, that this happens to me and has happened so often? In humble simplicity, in wretched poverty, and in oppressed lowliness, God has shown me His wonders. There I saw the creation and the order of the House of God, which He Himself built with His own mouth. Within it, He placed those most beloved, whom He made with His own hands. This creation of the house is called "Heaven"; the <term>choirs</term> <note>These are the nine traditional levels of angels—such as Seraphim, Cherubim, and Thrones—organized by their proximity to God.</note> within are called the "Kingdoms"; therefore, one speaks of them together as the "Kingdom of Heaven."

The Kingdom of Heaven has an end to its ordinance <note>The physical or structural boundaries of the celestial realm.</note>, but in its being, no end shall ever be found. Heaven surrounds the choirs, and between heaven and the corporeal choirs are placed the worldly sinners, who remain at the same height as the choirs so that they may improve and convert themselves. The choirs are so delicate, holy, and necessary that no one enters there without purity and the renunciation of the love of all earthly things; for all those who fell out from there were holy, and thereafter, those who come back in must be holy. All <term>married people</term> <note>original: "westbaren"; those living in the state of legitimate wedlock.</note> and children of six years fill the gap <note>Medieval theologians believed that human souls were created to fill the "breach" or empty seats left in heaven by the fallen angels.</note> no higher than the sixth choir. Beyond that, up to the Seraphim, the virgins shall fill the gap—those who defiled themselves with childish will, even though the deed never happened, and who purified themselves afterward in confession. They cannot, however, fully recover their state; they have lost their original luster. Those who are pure spiritual virgins shall, after the Day of Judgment, fill the gap above the Seraphim, where Lucifer and his closest companions were cast out.

Lucifer committed three mortal sins at once: hate, pride, and greed. These struck his choir so quickly into the eternal abyss that it happened in the time one could say "Alleluia." Then all the kingdom was terrified, and all the pillars of the Kingdom of Heaven trembled. Then some of the others fell. The <term>Exile</term> <note>original: "ellende"; in Middle High German, this refers to a "foreign land" or a place of alienation. Here, Mechthild uses it to describe a specific, pure space within the celestial architecture that remains empty or set apart.</note> is still empty and free; no one is within it, and it is entirely pure within itself, playing with joy to the honor of God. Above the Exile, God’s throne is arched with divine power in blooming, shining, fiery clarity, and it reaches down toward heaven near the Cherubim, so that God’s throne and heaven are one glorious house; and there the Exile and the nine choirs are contained within.

<summary>Mechthild describes the structural hierarchy of heaven and explains how different classes of humans (married people, repentant virgins, and pure spirits) fill the gaps left by the fallen angels. She concludes with a vision of the celestial architecture, including God's throne and the mysterious space known as the "Exile."</summary>
<keywords>heavenly choirs, Lucifer, the Fall, spiritual virginity, celestial hierarchy, Mechthild von Magdeburg, the breach, divine throne</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the visionary’s detailed mapping of the celestial hierarchy. Having established the boundaries of the Kingdom of Heaven on the previous page, the author now ascends to the very Throne of God to describe the specific placement of saints, the Virgin Mary, and the "Order of Preachers" within the gaps left by the fallen angels.</meta>

### Part Three.

Above the throne of God there is nothing but God, God, God—an immeasurably great God. High within the throne, one sees the mirror of the Godhead, the image of humanity, and the light of the Holy Spirit; there, one recognizes how these three are one God and how they join together as one. I am unable to speak any more of this.

<term>Lucifer’s breach</term> <note>The "breach" or "gap" refers to the vacant places in the heavenly hierarchy left behind by the fallen angels. Medieval theology taught that humans were created to fill these empty seats.</note> must be filled by John the Baptist, who shall possess his honor in that sweet <term>exile</term> <note>original: "ellende"; in this context, it refers to a "dwelling in a far-off land," specifically the high celestial regions far from earthly life.</note> above the <term>seraphim</term>. All pure, spiritual virgins are with him, for they are still reserved for that dwelling. At the throne of our Lady Saint Mary, however, no breach is to be filled, for she and her child have healed the wounds of all people—at least those who grant themselves the grace to remain healed. Her son is God and she is a <term>goddess</term> <note>original: "göttinne"; a rare and bold term used here to emphasize Mary’s unique, exalted status above all other created beings, though not suggesting she is a fourth person of the Trinity.</note>; no one can ever be her equal. The apostles live closest to God within the throne and receive the dwelling of the seraphim as their reward, according to their purity. John the Baptist is also a prince within the throne. The angels themselves do not dwell any higher than the rank of seraphim; above that, all must be human. The holy martyrs, God’s preachers, and spiritual <term>lovers</term> <note>original: "miñere"; those who practice "Minne," a form of mystical, intense divine love.</note> enter the choirs even if they are not virgins. Indeed, they enter honorably into the rank of the <term>cherubim</term>.

->There, unasked, I saw the preachers' reward<-
->As it is yet to come to pass;<-
->Their thrones are wondrous,<-
->Their reward is exceptional.<-

The foremost chairs are like two burning lights; these signify love, the holy image, and the faithful intention within. The back of these chairs is so softly unburdened and so sweet in its blissful rest—more than words can say—to compensate for the heavy obedience to which the preachers are subject here on earth.

->Their feet are adorned with many kinds<-
->Of precious stones, so beautiful,<-
->That I would truly rejoice<-
->If such an honorable crown were given to me.<-
->This they have in return for the labor<-
->That was laid upon their feet here below.<-

O, you preachers! How reluctantly you move your tongues now, and how barely you incline your ears to the mouth of the sinner! <note>The author concludes with a sharp reminder to the clergy (likely the Dominicans, the "Order of Preachers") that their heavenly reward for hearing confessions and preaching depends on their diligence in these humble earthly tasks.</note>

# Chapter I.

I have seen before God that it shall happen in the Kingdom of Heaven that a breath shall shine forth from your mouth <note>The visionary is likely addressing her spiritual advisor or the leader of her religious community.</note>, which shall rise up from the choirs before the throne and shall praise the Heavenly Father for the wisdom He has placed upon your tongue; it shall greet the Son for His honorable company—for He Himself was a <term>Preacher</term> <note>original: "predier"; a reference to the Order of Preachers (Dominicans), whom the author holds in high esteem.</note>—and thank the Holy Spirit for His grace, for He is a master of all gifts. Then God’s Preachers, the holy Martyrs, and the loving Virgins shall rise up, for the greatest honor is given to them in special <term>garments</term> <note>original: "gewete"; refers here to specialized liturgical or celestial vestments.</note>, in physical song, and in wondrous <term>chaplets</term> <note>original: "schappeln"; floral wreaths or coronals worn as symbols of victory or purity.</note> which they wear to the honor of God.

> The Virgins’ garment is of white lily-color,
> The Preachers’ garment is fiery and sun-clear,
> The Martyrs’ garment is glowing rose-red,
> For with Jesus they suffered a bloody death.
> The Virgins’ chaplet is of many colors,
> The Martyrs’ crown is clearly great.

The Preachers’ chaplet is made entirely of flowers; these are the words of God, through which they have come here into great honor. Thus, these three blessed groups go forth before the Holy <term>Trinity</term> <note>original: "drivalteit"</note> in a sweet <term>round dance</term> <note>original: "reien"; a courtly dance often used in mystical literature to describe the synchronized movements of the blessed in heaven.</note>.

> Then there flows toward them from God
> A threefold, playful flood,
> Which fulfills their spirit,
> So that they sing the truth
> With joy and without labor,
> Just as God has placed it within them.

Thus sing the Preachers: "O chosen Lord, we have followed Your mild goodness in <term>voluntary poverty</term> <note>original: "willeklichem armůte"; a core vow of the mendicant orders like the Dominicans.</note> and have driven in Your witless sheep, whom Your hired shepherds allowed to wander from the right path."

Thus sing the Martyrs: "Lord, Your innocent blood has fulfilled our death, so that we are companions of Your passion."

The blessed ones who now soar in heaven and live there so blissfully are all embraced by a single light, flowed through by one <term>Love</term> <note>original: "miñe"; specifically *Minne*, the Middle High German term for mystical, divine love.</note>, and united with one will. However, they do not yet possess the full dignity that lies upon the honorable thrones. They rest in God’s power and flow into the...

### Third Part.

...bliss and maintain themselves in the <term>divine procession</term> <note>original: "gotzzuge"; this refers to the movement of souls toward God, described as a magnetic or spiritual pull.</note> like the air exists within the sun, even more so after the Last Judgment.

When God wishes to have His supper, then shall seats be placed for the <term>brides</term> <note>In the mystical tradition, the "brides" are the souls of the faithful who are spiritually wedded to Christ.</note> before their Bridegroom; and so love shall come to love, the body shall come to the soul, and they shall then possess full dominion in eternal glory.

O You delightful Lamb and blissful youth Jesus, child of the Heavenly Father, when You then rise up and pass through all the <term>choirs</term> <note>original: "kŏre"; refers to the nine traditional levels of the celestial hierarchy of angels and saints.</note> and beckon to the virgins blissfully, they follow You praise-worthily to the most intimate place, of which I can tell no one. How they then play with You and consume Your <term>love-delight</term> <note>original: "miñelust"; from "Minne," the Middle High German term for sacred, mystical love.</note> within themselves is such heavenly sweetness and such intimate union that I know of nothing else like it. — The widows shall also follow in lordly delight, and they let themselves be satisfied in sweet contemplation of the highest things, as they must behold how the Lamb joins Himself to the virgins. The <term>married</term> <note>original: "ehte"; those who lived in the state of matrimony on earth.</note> shall also look upon one another lovingly, in so far as it may happen for them according to their nobility. For the more one satiates oneself here with earthly things, the more of the heavenly bliss must be <term>left behind</term> <note>original: "überbliben"; the author suggests that earthly over-indulgence reduces one's capacity for, or access to, certain heights of celestial joy.</note> for us.

The choirs all have a special <term>radiance</term> <note>original: "lůhtenisse"; a spiritual light or aura unique to each rank of the blessed.</note> in their appearance, and heaven has its own. This radiance is so exceptionally glorious that I neither must nor can write of it. To the choirs and to heaven, manifold dignity is given by God,

> There I may say a little word about each.
> It is no more than as much
> As a bee may carry of honey
> From a full hive upon its foot.
> In the first choir, delightfulness
> Is the highest gift they possess in all their graces.
> In the second choir is gentleness,
> In the third choir is loveliness,
> In the fourth is sweetness, in the fifth is joyfulness,
> In the sixth is a noble fragrance,
> In the seventh is richness,
> In the eighth is worthiness,
> In the ninth is the <term>burning of love</term> <note>original: "miñebreñen"; the intense, fiery fervor traditionally associated with the Seraphim, the highest angelic order.</note>,
> In the sweet <term>exile</term> <note>original: "ellende"; in Middle High German, this refers to being in a foreign land or "away from home," often a metaphor for the soul's life on earth before returning to God.</note> is pure holiness.

The highest thing upon the throne is the powerful honor and the mighty dominion. The highest thing overall that ever was in heaven,

# Cap. I.

...is the wonder. The highest wonder that exists is that they may behold that which is at rest and that which shall happen forever.

O, that honorable majesty <note>original: "tůme"; likely referring to a state of dignity or a domain of honor</note> and You, sweet eternity, and the powerful insight <note>original: "durschauen"; the ability to see through or perceive the essence of all things</note> into all things, and the special intimacy that passes without ceasing between God and every soul! This intimacy lies in such necessary tenderness that even if I had the wisdom of all humans and the voice of all angels, I could not express it.

The unbaptized children under five years of age dwell in a special dignity that God has prepared for them out of His kingdom.

> They are not in their creation
> Grown to thirty years, <note>It was a common medieval belief that all the resurrected would appear to be approximately thirty years old, the age of Christ at the time of His Passion.</note>
> Because they were not Christians with Christ.
> They have no crowns,
> God can reward them for nothing;
> He has, however, given them His goodness,
> So that they live in great comfort.
> The highest joy they have,
> Is the fullness of grace. They sing thus:
> We praise Him who has created us,
> Even though we never saw Him;
> Could we suffer pain, we would forever lament it;
> Now we shall be well at ease.

Now some people may wonder how I, a sinful human, can endure to write such words. I tell you truly and for a fact: had God not seized my heart seven years ago with a special gift, I would still be silent and would never have done it. Now, through God's goodness, it has never brought me any harm; this comes from the mirror of my open wickedness <note>original: "bosheit"; in this mystical context, the author refers to her perceived state of sinfulness or "nothingness" before God</note>, which stands directly open before my soul, and from the nobility of grace, which lies in the true gift of God.

Yet the higher the soul has climbed, the less praise it should give to the body <note>original: "lichamen"</note> with words and with gestures; one should also not lament its distress before your eyes, for it is by nature a coward <note>original: "zage"</note>. One should treat it like an old pensioner <term>pfrůndener</term> <note>A "pfründner" was someone who received a "prebend" or a fixed allowance of food and lodging, often in a monastery or hospital, in exchange for past services or an initial gift.</note> who can no longer serve at court; so one gives him alms only for the love of God. This is truly useful, for: the nobler the dog, the tighter the collar <note>original: "halsbant"</note>.

Now, dear Lord, I wish to commend these words to Your mild goodness—

Third Part.

...and I pray, my much beloved, with a sighing heart and with weeping eyes and with a wretched soul, that no pharisee <note>original: "pharisei"; here meaning a legalistic or hypocritical person who lacks spiritual depth.</note> may read this, and I pray You, my much beloved Lord, that Your children may hear these words just as You, Lord, have given them forth in the true truth.

## II. How the soul praises God in seven things and God bids her wait for the ointment. <note>The "ointment" or salve refers to a spiritual healing or anointing of the soul by the Divine.</note>

O sweet Jesus, most beautiful form, unhidden in needs and in love to my wretched soul, I praise You with the same, in love, in needs, and in love with the fellowship of all creatures. This I desire then above all things. Lord, You are the sun of all eyes, You are the delight of all ears, You are the voice of all words, You are the power of all piety, You are the teaching of all wisdom, You are the beloved in all living things, You are the order of all being.

Then God praised the loving soul worthily, for He took delight in her sweetly thus: You are a light before My eyes, You are a lyre before My ears, You are a voice of My words, You are an intention of My piety, You are a glory of My wisdom, You are a beloved in My life, You are a praise in My being.

### III. A lament that the soul is a virgin, and concerning the love of God.

**The Soul:** “O Lord, how poor and wretched is the soul who is a virgin <note>In this context, "virgin" refers to a soul that is untouched by worldly desires and reserved solely for God.</note> for the sake of Your love here on earth! O, who will help me lament how painful it is for her, for she herself does not know what it is she lacks.”

**Love:** Lady Bride, you speak in the Book of Love to your Beloved, asking that He flee from you. Tell me urgently, Lady, what has happened to you? For I would rather die—if such a thing could happen to me—in pure love, than bid God depart from me in dark wisdom. When I must urgently play with my Beloved, wisdom need not teach me any distinction. But when I labor at other things with my five senses, I gladly accept that she <note>Wisdom</note> brings me the Holy Mass. Hear me, dear companion. <margin>Remarkably, a later hand has cited two verses from Ovid in the margin.</margin> I was joyful because I was drunk on love; therefore, I spoke tenderly from my senses. But when I become over-intoxicated, I cannot even think of my Beloved, for you, Love, command me; what Love wills must be. And in that which God consoles Himself, I take courage; for if He takes my body, the soul is His.

->If you wish to go into the wine cellar with me,<-
->Then you must incur great costs.<-
->If you have a thousand marks of value,<-
->You will have consumed them in a single hour.<-

If you wish to drink the wine unmixed, you will forever consume more than you possess, so that the Host <note>God, as the tavern-keeper of the soul</note> cannot pour enough for you. Then you become poor and naked and despised by all those who prefer...

<summary>The text explores the paradoxical suffering and joy of the soul's "virginity" for God, using the metaphor of a wine cellar to describe the overwhelming and costly nature of divine intoxication.</summary>

<header>Third Part.</header>

...rejoice in the puddle, rather than spending their treasure in the high wine cellar. <note>In the mystical tradition of Mechthild von Magdeburg, the "wine cellar" represents the most intimate place of spiritual union between the soul and God.</note>

> You must also suffer this:
> That those who go into the wine cellar with you
> Will envy you.
> O, how greatly they sometimes despise you,
> Because they do not dare to undertake such great costs.
> They want the water mixed with the wine. <note>original: "Si wellent das wasser ze dem wine gemenget han." This refers to "lukewarm" believers who wish to dilute the intensity of divine love with worldly moderation.</note>
> Dear Lady Bride, in the tavern I will gladly
> Spend all that I have
> And let myself be pulled through the coals of love <note>original: "miñe"</note>
> And be struck with the torches of shame,
> So that I may very often go into the blessed wine cellar.

Here I will gladly choose, for I can lose nothing through love. Therefore, he who torments and despises me pours me the host’s <note>the "Wirt" or innkeeper, referring to God</note> wine, which He Himself has drunk.

> From that wine I become so drunk
> That I truly become subject to all creatures,
> So that it seems to me, in my human lowliness
> And in my inherent wickedness,
> That no person has ever done such evil against me
> That he could commit any sin against me, wretched as I am.

Therefore I cannot take out my suffering on my enemy. Yet I know this well: they can also break God’s commandments through me.

Dear companion, when it happens that the wine cellar is opened up <note>Meaning the time of ecstatic union has ended and the soul is sent back out.</note>, then you must walk in the street hungry, poor, naked, and so despised that you have nothing more of the nourishment of Christian life left within you than faith <note>original: "gelǒben"</note>. If you can still love then, you will never perish.

> Lady Bride, I have a hunger for the heavenly Father;
> In it, I forget all my distress.
> And I have a thirst for His Son,
> Which takes away all my earthly desire;
> And I have from the Spirit of them both such a longing <note>original: "not" — implying a pressing spiritual need or labor</note>
> That goes beyond the Father’s wisdom
> Which I can grasp,
> And beyond the Son’s labor
> Which I can endure,
> And beyond the Holy Spirit’s comfort
> Which can come to me.

<summary>Mechthild describes the soul's willingness to endure earthly shame and spiritual "hunger" for the sake of the ecstatic "drunkenness" found in the divine wine cellar. She emphasizes that true love remains even when the ecstasy fades and only bare faith is left.</summary>

### IV. *How our Lady Saint Mary could sin and how not, as the Holy Spirit teaches.*

O Mary, honorable empress, Mother of God and my Lady, I was asked about you: whether you could have sinned just like other people while you were here on this sinful earth. Now the Holy Spirit—who, Lady, knows all your secrets fully—has informed me that you *could* have sinned, because you were a human being made perfect by God, in the full nature of a woman and in every virginal creation, and you were not lacking in your nature. This is what makes your long-lasting purity so noble and precious before God.

But Lady, noble goddess <note>original: "göttiñe." Mechthild uses this high-status, courtly title to exalt Mary’s unique position, though she remains a created being.</note> above all pure human beings, you also *could not* sin. You did not have this ability of yourself; rather, the Heavenly Father shielded your childhood with the forethought of His ancient election, and the Holy Spirit bound your youth with the fullness of His new love, and Jesus passed through your body as the dew passes through the flower <note>A popular medieval metaphor for the Virgin Birth, suggesting that Christ entered the world without breaking Mary's physical virginity.</note>, so that your purity was never touched. 

And the power of the Holy Trinity had so pressed upon your nature that it never dared, nor was it able, to stir in a human way before its Creator. And the eternal wisdom of the almighty Godhead had given you, Lady, a shadow in which you maintained your human life, so that you could suffer pain without sin, and so that your blossoming humanity would not vanish in the sun of the powerful Godhead. In that shadow you carried Jesus as a human and nursed Him as a mother. But Lady, in the Father’s message, and in the Holy Spirit’s conception, and in the Son’s word, the fire of the Godhead and the light of the Holy Spirit and the wisdom of the Son were so great within you, Lady, that you could then feel very little of the shadow. God knows, Lady, after that you had to cool yourself wretchedly <note>original: "ellendekliche," which implies being like an exile or a stranger in a foreign land.</note> with poverty, with misfortune, and with many heavy heart-sorrows. Yet you remained in your heart greatly on fire with good works from the...

<summary>This page concludes a poem on the "distress" of divine love and begins a theological reflection on the Virgin Mary, explaining how her human nature allowed for the possibility of sin while her divine protection made it impossible.</summary>

<header>Part Three.</header>

...fire that burns without beginning and without help within itself. This fire has, Lady, shone through your walls <note>Likely referring to Mary's body as the "house" or "temple" of God.</note> and has driven all darkness out of your house.

### V. *How the soul laments that she hears neither Mass nor the Hours, and how God praises her in ten things.*

Thus a wretched soul <note>original: "ellende sele." In Middle High German, "ellende" implies being in exile or far from home—a common metaphor for the soul's life on earth.</note> laments, when God had cast her down from His compelling love and loved her with great torment. "O woe," she said, "how poorly a rich man may suffer when, after honorable wealth, he is directed into great poverty. O Lord, now I am very poor in my sick body and very wretched in my poor soul; so too, Lord, in spiritual order, for no one reads Your Hours <note>The "Liturgy of the Hours" or "Divine Office," the daily cycle of prayers marked by bells in a convent.</note> before me, nor does anyone perform Your holy service of the Mass before me."

->Then spoke the loving mouth<-
->Which has pierced my soul,<-
->With His great words,<-
->The likes of which I never worthily heard:<-
->"You are my desire, a delight of love,<-
->You are a sweet cooling for my breast,<-
->You are a powerful kiss of my mouth,<-
->You are a joyful joy of my finding,<-
->I am in you and you are in me,<-
->We cannot be any closer,<-
->For we two have flowed into one<-
->And are cast into one mold,<-
->Thus shall we remain eternally unwearied."<-

"O Beloved, how intimately You speak to me; yet I never dare to think joyfully on these words, because the dead dog, my body <note>A stark medieval ascetic metaphor for the physical flesh, viewed as a decaying burden to the spirit.</note>, stinks to me with misery without ceasing, and my other enemies <note>Likely referring to demonic temptations or worldly distractions.</note> rage so constantly, and I, Lord, do not know in my senses how it shall go with me at my end. But in Your contemplation <note>original: "anschőwunge," meaning mystical vision or gazing upon God.</note> alone, I know nothing of sorrow; there You have, Lord, taken me from myself and stolen Yourself into me. What You have then promised me must happen and must yet come to Your praise." Thus our Lord answered:

->My deep reaching, my broad wandering,<-
->My high desiring, my long waiting.<-
->I must teach you further:<-

### Chapters V—VII.

The noble young maidens <note>original: "juncfröwen"</note> find that their discipline <note>term: "zuht" — refers to the rigorous moral and social training of the nobility</note> costs them dearly; they must restrain all their limbs and must often tremble before their mistress of discipline. In the same way, my brides on earth are given such discipline in their bodies. For your love, I was seized by distress on earth, and my enemies carried my death cruelly in their hands before my eyes; and I suffered much poverty in shame <note>The manuscript reads "schame" (shame).</note>. Despite this, I trusted in my Father’s overflowing goodness. Direct your mind according to this.

### VI. *If you wish to follow God rightly, you must have seven things.*

Whoever wishes to follow God in faithful labors must not stand still; he must travel often. He should reflect on what he was in sin and how he is now in virtue, and what he might yet become if he should fall. He should lament and praise and pray night and day. Whenever the faithful bride awakes, she thinks of her beloved; if she cannot have him then, she begins to weep. Oh, how often that happens spiritually to the brides of God!

### VII. *Concerning seven manifest enemies of our blessedness, which cause seven harms.*

Uselessness is a very harmful habit in us, and bad habits also harm us in all places; earthly greed destroys the holy word of God in us, and the evil war of self-will works many a harmful murder within us. Enmity of the heart drives the Holy Spirit away from us, and a wrathful mind deprives us of God’s intimacy <note>original: "heimlicheit" — refers to a deep, private mystical union</note>. False holiness can never endure, but pure love for God <note>original: "gotzmiñe"</note> can never pass away from anyone. If we do not retreat from these enemies, they take more from us than the kingdom of heaven; for the life we live here in secret is a "fore-heaven" <note>original: "vorhimelrich" — a state of grace on earth that anticipates paradise</note>. But if we allow these enemies their cunning and their power over us, they rob us of the seven gifts of the Holy Spirit and they extinguish the true light of true love for God. They also bind the eyes of holy knowledge and lead us, blinded, into the seven deadly sins. Where does that path lead then, but into the eternal abyss?

# Part Three

## VIII. *Concerning seven things that all priests ought to possess.*

The heavenly Father has told me seven things that every priest of God should have within himself, and He spoke: They should be innocent in themselves, and the testimony <note>original: "gezůge" - likely referring to the liturgical requirements or the spiritual witness of the priest</note> must be perfect. If there is any doubt in this, then one should leave it be and not perform the rite. They should cast all fear away from them and should forget the Jewish law <note>original: "judeschen e" - referring to the Old Covenant or legalistic religious practice</note> and should eat my Lamb living, and should drink His blood with sighing <note>expressing deep devotion or contrition</note>; in this way, they may rightly remember His great suffering. But if he is guilty within himself, then my children eat the bread of heaven and Judas goes to hell. And if the testimony that belongs to the Mass is not perfect, then God’s table stands empty <note>original: "ital"</note> and the children are deprived of their food. But if they come upon the altar in the peril of their lives, it is better that they shed their own blood than mine.

## IX. *Concerning the beginning of all things that God has created.*

O Father of all goodness, I, unworthy M. <note>likely "Mensch" (human/man) or a specific name like Mechthild</note>, thank You for all the faithfulness with which You have drawn me out of myself into Your wonder; so, Lord, that I have heard and seen in Your entire Trinity the high counsel that took place before our time, when You, Lord, were enclosed within Yourself alone, and Your countless joy was shared with no one.

->Then the three persons shone so beautifully as one,<-
->That each of them shone through the other, and yet were entirely one.<-

The Father was adorned in Himself with the masculine spirit of omnipotence, and the Son was equal to the Father in countless wisdom, and the Holy Spirit was equal to them both in full mercy <note>original: "miltekeit" - can mean generosity, kindness, or grace</note>. Then the Holy Spirit played a game <note>original: "spilte... ein spil" - a common mystical metaphor for the dynamic interaction within the Trinity</note> for the Father with great mercy and struck upon the Holy Trinity and spoke to Him: "Lord, dear Father, I will give You a merciful counsel from within Yourself, for we wish no longer to be thus without offspring <note>original: "vnberhaftig" - literally 'unfruitful' or 'unproductive'</note>. We wish to have a created kingdom, and You shall fashion the angels after me, so that they are one spirit with me, and the other shall be the Human <note>original: "M." / "Mensch"</note>."

# Chapter XI.

She <note>The soul, traditionally referred to as "she" in mystical literature</note> also hangs high in the **sweet air of the Holy Spirit**, facing the **eternal sun of the living Godhead** upon the **cross of high love**, which becomes completely dry of all earthly things. Then, in a holy end, she is taken down from her cross. Then she says: "Father, receive my spirit. Now all is fulfilled." She is laid in a **closed grave of deep humility**, as she constantly considers herself the unworthiest among all creatures. She also rises joyfully on an **Easter Day** when she has shared a sweet lament of love with her Beloved in the **intimate bridal bed**. Then her Noble Lord comforts her early in the morning with Mary, when she receives from God that true certainty: that God has blotted out all her sins through the **repentance of love**. She returns to her disciples through **closed doors**, when her **five senses**—those holy teachers of God—so often fail. Then she goes out from Jerusalem, the **holy Christendom**, with many a virtuous host. Then the body becomes troubled, which with all its being and its unworthiness would gladly have its own way in all things. Then she says: "I am your master; you shall follow me and obey in all things. If I did not go to my Father, you would remain as fools." And she also ascends into **heaven** when God, through holy transformation, takes away all earthly things from her. She is received in a **white cloud of holy protection**, as she journeys lovingly and returns joyfully without any kind of sorrow. Then the angels return and comfort the **men of Galilee** <note>A reference to the disciples at the Ascension; here representing the soul's faculties</note>, when we think of God’s chosen friends and their holy image. Every soul suffers this martyrdom if, in the **holy tempering** of all its deeds, it is truly permeated with true love for God.

### XI. Between God and the loving soul, all things are beautiful.

When the loving soul looks into the **eternal mirror**, she says: "Lord, between You and me all things are beautiful; but between the devil and his bride—the damned soul—all things are terrifying and so gruesome. For when she <note>the damned soul</note> thinks of the **lovely Jesus**, she trembles, and all her hellish pain is renewed."

## XII. *You shall praise, thank, desire, and pray. Of the lampstand and the light.*

O dear Lord, how poor I was when I could not think of all these words, nor pray, nor love <note>original: "minen" - a Middle High German term for mystical, courtly love for God</note>. Yet I struggled toward you with my wretched senses and spoke thus: "O dear Lord, with what shall I now honor you?" Then you spoke to the unworthiest creature you ever created, saying: "You shall praise me for my faithful protection. You shall thank me for my generous gifts. You shall desire my holy wonders. You shall pray for a good end."
Then the soul asked with noble words: "Dearest one, what wonders shall I desire?" This I must write hereafter while weeping constantly. May God help me, the most miserable of humans, that I may remain with Jesus. —
Then my Beloved spoke thus: "I will set the light upon the lampstand, and all the eyes that have looked upon the light shall have a special ray shine into the eye of their understanding from that light." Then the soul asked with great humility and without fear: "Dearest one, what shall the lampstand be?" Then our Lord spoke: "I am the light and your breast is the lampstand."

## XIII. Of sixteen kinds of love.

Generous love from holy mercy
Drives away empty honor and evil infirmity.
True love from divine wisdom
Brings satisfaction and drives away ignoble greed.
Humble love from holy simplicity
Triumphs alone over pride
And brings the soul with power
Into holy, true knowledge.
Steadfast <margin>Manuscript: steste</margin> love from good character
Can practice no falsehood.
Great love from bold deeds
Knows good counsel in all things.
Perceptive love from God's intimacy
Blinds this earthly realm without effort.
Bound love from holy habit
Never rests and yet lives in itself without labor.

# Chapters XII—XIV.

The flowing love <note>original: "miñe" — a Middle High German term for sacred, courtly, or mystical love</note> of great abundance:
There you lie all still, and all things are bitter to you except God alone.
The crying love of noble impatience:
She is never silent, and she has blessedly forgotten all guilt.
The German love of God’s teaching:
She still bows herself very gladly toward a child.
The beautiful love of high power:
There the soul grows young and the body grows old.
The lovely love of open giving:
It wipes away the sour heart’s lament.
The hidden love carries a precious treasure
From good will into holy deeds.
The clear love of the playing flood
Causes the soul sweet distress;
She also kills her without death.
The wind-like love of overwhelming power:
That is what no one can endure.

### XIV. Of two false virtues; whoever dwells therein, he lives
->in lies.<-

I have a master, who is the Holy Spirit; he teaches me
very gently what I desire, and the rest he keeps for me. Now
he speaks thus:

> Wisdom without the strengthening of the Holy Spirit
> Becomes, at last, a mountain of pride.
> Peace without the bond of the Holy Spirit
> Very quickly becomes a vain madness.
> Humility without the fire of love
> Becomes, at last, an obvious falsehood.
> Justice without the depth of God’s humility
> Becomes, on the spot, a gruesome hatred.
> Poverty with constant greed
> Is in itself a sinful excess.
> Gruesome fear with true guilt
> Brings a terrifying impatience.
> A beautiful appearance <note>original: "gelas" — outward behavior or bearing</note> with the mind of a wolf:
> The wise will soon become aware of this.
> Holy longing from total truth:
> This happens to no one without labor.
> A goodly life without struggle <note>original: "bagen" — quarreling or resistance</note>
> Will be drawn toward useless things. <insert>Manuscript: carry.</insert>

### Part Three

You, arrogant virtue without God’s gift,
You shall be struck down by your own high-mindedness <note>original: "hohen mûte" - refers to the sin of pride or over-confidence in one's own abilities.</note>.
Beautiful vows without faithful deeds,
That is falsehood and the devil’s counsel.
Good comfort without true certainty
Of the soul and the Holy Spirit’s fulfillment,
That will become a joyless death at the final end.
Great patience without the heart’s inclination toward God,
That is a secret guilt;
For all those who, in all things,
Do not cling to God’s truth,
They must fall away from the eternal God
With great shame.
Effort without the mother of humility
And without the father of holy fear,
Is orphaned of all virtues.

XV. With eight virtues you shall go to God’s table <note>The "table" refers to the Altar or the reception of Holy Communion.</note>. With these ransom-pounds <note>term: "löserpfunden" - a spiritual currency of merit used to 'pay' for the release of souls.</note> a person may release seventy thousand souls from the gruesome purgatory, which is manifold.

The very foolish Beguines <note>term: "beginen" - lay religious women who lived in community but did not take permanent vows. The author is often critical of those she perceives as superficial.</note>, how bold you are, that you do not tremble before our almighty Judge when you take God’s body so frequently out of blind habit! Now, I am the least among you; I must feel shame, heat, and trembling. During a high feast, I was so overcome with weakness that I did not dare to take it, for I felt shame for my best piety before His eyes. Then I prayed to my Beloved that He would show me His honor in this. Then He spoke: "Truly, if you approach Me with humble lamentation and with holy fear, then I must follow you as the high flood follows the deep mill-race. But if you approach Me with the flowering desire of flowing effort, then I must enjoy you and touch you with My divine nature as My only queen. I must reveal Myself if I am to truly bring forth God’s goodness." This hindered me truly no more than a hot oven is hindered when one shoves it full of white wheat-bread. Then I went to God’s table in a noble company. They guarded me most faithfully and yet held me in great awe. Truth pulled me, fear scolded me, shame scourged me, repentance numbed me, desire drew me, the...

# Chapter XV.

Love led me, the Christian faith shielded me, a faithful intention toward all good things prepared me, and all my good works cried out like weapons over me. The mighty God received me, his pure humanity united itself with me, and his Holy Spirit comforted me.

Then I spoke: "Lord, now you are mine, for you are given to me today, even in that place where it is said: *A child is born to us* <note>original Latin: "Puer natus est nobis." A reference to Isaiah 9:6, commonly sung at Christmas.</note>. Now I desire, Lord, your praise and not your profit <note>The author distinguishes between loving God for His own glory versus seeking personal gain or "spiritual rewards."</note>, so that today your noble body may come to the comfort of the poor souls. You are truly mine; now, Lord, you shall be a ransom for the captives today."

Then she <note>The soul</note> gained such great power that she led Him in with His own strength, and they came to a place as gruesome as my eye ever saw—a bath so terrifyingly made, mixed of fire and pitch, of filth, smoke, and stench. A thick, dark mist hung over it like a black skin drawn tight. There the souls lay within just like toads in the mud. Their shape was like that of humans, yet they were spirits and bore the likeness of the devil upon them. They seethed and roasted together. They cried out and had countless misery because of their flesh, which had felled them so deeply. The flesh had blinded their spirit; for that reason, they seethed most of all. Then the human spirit spoke: "O Lord, how many of these poor ones there are! You are my true ransom; you must now have mercy." Then our Lord spoke: "They are beyond human numbering, and you cannot grasp their count as long as your flesh must have an earthly part with you. They have all been broken vessels and forgot the spiritual life while on earth. They are from all walks of life and from all lands."

Then the human spirit asked: "Ah, dear Lord, where are the anchorites <term>hermits or recluses who lived in solitude for religious devotion</term>? I perceive none of them here." Our Lord answered: "Their sins were secret; now they are bound in this abyss alone with the devils." Then the human soul was greatly troubled and laid herself at the feet of our dearly beloved Lord and desired to labor lovingly with all her might, and said: "Dearest, you well know what I desire." Then our Lord spoke: "You have rightly brought me here; I shall not leave them unconsidered."

# Third Part

There stood around them a very great multitude of devils, who tended to them in that unblessed bath <note>original: "ungesegenten bade"; likely a reference to a pit of boiling liquid or fire in Purgatory</note>. They were also beyond my counting—those who rubbed and washed and devoured and gnawed at them, and struck them with fiery whips. Then the human spirit spoke to them thus: "Listen, you devourers of sin <note>original: "sûndenfresse"; a derogatory term for demons who feed on human iniquity</note>, look upon the ransom <note>original: "lôsepffant"; literally a 'redemption pledge,' referring to Christ's sacrifice</note>; is it not precious enough to satisfy you?" Then they shrank back, all trembling in horrific shame, and said: "Yes, now they are departing from here. How wretched we are! We must acknowledge the truth to you." Then our Lord gave a sweet blessing to the poor souls out of His divine heart. Then they lifted themselves up with great joy and love. Then the stranger soul <note>The visionary narrator</note> spoke: "Ah, most dear Lord, where shall they go now?" He spoke: "I will bring them to a mountain of flowers; there they shall find more bliss than I am able to speak." There our Lord served them and was their chamberlain <note>term: chamberlain (kamrer); a high-ranking official in a royal household, here showing Christ's humility in serving the redeemed</note> and their most beloved companion. Then our Lord told me that there were seventy thousand of them there. Then the soul asked again how long their torment had been. Our Lord spoke: "Within thirty years they never came to their bodies <note>A reference to the state of the soul between death and resurrection</note>, and they were meant to remain in torment for ten more years, had such a noble ransom not been given for them." The devils fled; they did not dare to take it. "Most beloved," the soul spoke again, "how long shall they remain here?" Our Lord answered and spoke: "As long as it seems good to us."

### XVI.
*After the gift follows the lash, and after dishonor comes honor.*

This soul reminded our Lord of His old words in this way: "Lord, You have said that there is no gift upon this earth that does not have a lash <note>original: "geisel"; a metaphor for the suffering or discipline that accompanies spiritual grace</note> lying upon it. You foretold this to me with Your own mouth and have fulfilled it for me at many an hour. You also told me more than six years ago that spiritual people <note>Clergy or members of religious orders</note> would yet despise me very greatly; they do this now diligently and have often done so maliciously. Is this, Lord, the wonder that I am to desire?" Then our Lord answered me and spoke: "My Father gave me the power of His truth and gave me the knowledge of His holiness, and after that He gave me many a dishonor. But after that He gave me great honor and countless dignity. In this same way, I will give you my Holy Trinity."

<summary>The visionary witnesses the release of seventy thousand souls from the torment of demons through Christ's "ransom," followed by a dialogue where Christ explains that spiritual suffering and social rejection are necessary precursors to divine honor.</summary>
<keywords>Purgatory, demons, ransom of Christ, mountain of flowers, spiritual suffering, persecution by clergy, Holy Trinity, redemption</keywords>

<header>Chapters XVI—XVII.</header>

## XVII. Concerning a spiritual man’s purgatory, concerning his fivefold help out of pain, and concerning the nobility of the Preaching Order.

I also saw a spiritual man <note>A member of a religious order, likely a priest or monk</note> in the pains of purgatory;
I had held him in high regard during his lifetime.
I prayed for his soul for three months
With heartfelt sorrow,
Yet it could not happen for him,
That I might perceive his distress,
Until the evening of the final day <note>Likely referring to the end of his initial period of purification or a specific feast day</note>.

As he gave up his spirit, he was very quickly shown to me
in my prayer, which I offered for the poor souls. I saw (him)
alone, and he could not reveal his pain to me. He was of a pale
color in a white mist. Then I asked: "Alas, why
are you not in heaven?" Then he answered all in hidden words in
rueful shame, and he read a book while weeping, and all
the words cried out, and that smoke rose over him, and also all the books
that he had ever read. Then he said: "The world was
all too dear to me in my thoughts, words, and conduct." Two dragons
lay at his feet; they sucked out of him all the comfort that he
should have received from Holy Christendom against his sickly <note original: "sincranken">The manuscript reads "sicranken," likely meaning weak or failing</note>
obedience, because he wanted to follow his own will without necessity and not
the teaching of his prelates <note term="prelates">High-ranking church officials or superiors in a religious order</note>. I asked him: "Where are your
enemies <note>Demons or tormentors</note> who should punish you?" Then he answered: "Because of the nobility
of my order <note>The Dominican Order, known as the Order of Preachers</note>, no devil was ever allowed to touch me. I had
great strife in my body, and I desired one thing;
had that been fulfilled, it would have been very useless;
therefore God did not let me live any longer. I burn within myself;
my own will must torment me." Then I asked: "Ah, tell
me, with what may one help you?" Then he said: "Whoever would give me
every day for a year a hundred prostrations <note term="venien">From the Latin 'venia,' a ritual prostration or kneeling to ask for forgiveness</note> and twelve disciplines <note term="disciplines">Acts of self-flagellation or formal penance</note> and many tears
with a rueful heart from pure eyes, that should and shall be my
penance. Masses should also be read. Ah, tell the virgins
and priests that they should pray for me. The end of my
pain I will not tell you now, for I do not wish to [distress] my brothers with it."

<summary>The author recounts a vision of a deceased Dominican friar in Purgatory who is tormented by his own self-will and past intellectual vanity rather than by demons. He requests specific penances, including prostrations and prayers, to hasten his purification.</summary>
<keywords>Purgatory, Dominican Order, obedience, penance, visions, self-will, prostrations, intercession</keywords>

distress. Now depart from me. Then he <note>The devil or a demonic temptation mentioned in the preceding context.</note> took on the likeness of the devil and burned and became mute toward me.

### XVIII. *Of the knight’s struggle with full weapons against*
*desire.*

I prayed for a person, as I had been asked, that God would take away the stirrings of the body <note>original: "lichamen berürunge"; likely referring to physical lust or sexual temptation</note>, which yet happens without sin if the evil will does not contribute to it. Then our Lord spoke: Be silent. Would it please you if you were a knight with full weapons and of noble skill and with true manly strength and with nimble hands, who remained idle and neglected his lord’s honor and lost the rich reward and the noble sound of praise, which both the lord and the knight should possess in the lands? Moreover, if there were an untrained man who, through lack of action, never came to battle, and he wished to enter a princely tournament, his life would soon be taken from him. Therefore, I must spare the people who so easily fall. I let them fight with children, so that they may be certain of a crown of flowers <note>original: "blůmenschappel"; a garland given as a prize in minor contests, contrasting with the "rich reward" of a true knight</note> as their reward.

### XIX. *Of two kinds of poor people, (who) are moderately and painfully*
*poor.*

I have seen two kinds of people who are poor; the first are moderately <note>original: "mißenklich"; meaning moderately or voluntarily poor, likely referring to those in religious orders</note> poor and always have anxiety that they might receive too much of this poor earth. The others are, against their will, very painfully poor and always run about and have great anxiety that they might not obtain enough of this poor earth. To this our Lord answers and says: The painfully poor stand in my justice, for if they had many earthly things, they would neither want to miss me moderately nor kiss me holily; therefore, I must win them with the hardest means. To the moderately poor I give more than they dare to desire, for I cannot endure the dust <note>original: "stöb"</note> upon them—that they should burden themselves too far with earthly things—and I desire that their heart always stand open toward me, and that I may, without hindrance and without ceasing, light and shine through what is mine.

<summary>Mechthild uses the metaphor of a knight's combat to explain why God allows temptation, and distinguishes between those who embrace voluntary poverty and those who suffer involuntary, worldly poverty.</summary>
<keywords>knighthood, spiritual warfare, temptation, voluntary poverty, divine justice, light of God</keywords>

->Chapters XVIII—XX.<-

### XX. Concerning five prophets who illuminate this book.

Our Lord has promised me that he will illuminate this book with five lights: Moses’s great intimacy <note>original: "heimlichkeit," referring to a private, mystical closeness with God</note> and his holy labor and special humiliation, which he bore without guilt, and his honorable signs and his sweet teaching, and the chosen love-talk <note>original: "miñereden," the ecstatic dialogue between the soul and God</note> that he often held with the eternal God upon the high mountain. All of that shall be one light; and God has given and will give to me that I, through the wicked cunning of all my enemies, shall walk without guilty shame and float lovingly under His protection. Just as Moses did with his friends through the Red Sea. And Pharaoh and his friends,

->They shall not follow us too far.<-
->O woe, how they have drowned in this sea!<-
->Ah, have mercy, dear Lord,<-
->That our enemies might be converted!<-

King David is the second light in this book with the Psalter, wherein he teaches us and laments, prays, admonishes, and praises God.

Solomon’s words shine, though his works do not—for he himself is darkened—in the book *Canticles* <note>The Song of Solomon</note>, where the Bride is found so drunkenly bold and the Bridegroom speaks to her so right urgently: "You are altogether beautiful, my love, and there is no flaw in you." <note>Latin: "tota pulchra es, amica mea, et macula non est in te" (Song of Songs 4:7)</note>

Jeremiah also shines his part, where he speaks of our Lady’s <note>The Virgin Mary</note> intimacy.

->What God had told me thus,<-
->That was possessed by pure chastity,<-
->The height of the Mass <note>original: "mîße"</note> and that He suffered the martyrdom<-
->In the Christian faith,<-
->Whom he never saw with his fleshly eyes.<-

Daniel also shines in wondrous wisdom, in that God by grace gave him nourishment for soul and body among all his enemies. In the very same way, it has happened to me, unworthy as I am, in my distresses:

->Of this my enemies have seen a little<-
->And they cannot endure it;<-
->Therefore they cause me many pains.<-

H. Mechthild.
6

<summary>Mechthild claims divine authority for her book by linking her mystical experiences to five Old Testament figures: Moses, David, Solomon, Jeremiah, and Daniel. She frames her own persecution by critics as a parallel to the trials faced by these biblical prophets.</summary>

<header>Third Part.</header>

## XXI. Concerning hell, and how it has three parts. How Lucifer and sixteen kinds of people are tormented. No help shall come to them. Concerning Lucifer’s clothing.

    I have seen a city,
    Its name is Eternal Hate.
    It is built in the lowest abyss
    From many kinds of stones of the capital sins <note>The "Seven Deadly Sins" or root vices.</note>.
    Pride was the first stone,
    As was clearly seen in Lucifer.

Disobedience, wicked greed, gluttony, and unchastity: these were four very heavy stones that our father Adam first sent there.

    Wrath, falsehood, and manslaughter,
    These three stones were brought by Cain.
    Lying, treachery, and despair,
    And those who take their own lives, <note>original: "liblos" (lifeless). The scribe likely meant "leiblos" in the sense of destroying one's own body.</note>

With these four stones poor Judas also murdered himself. The sin of Sodom and false holiness <note>Hypocrisy or feigned piety.</note>,

    These are the wretched cornerstones
    That are laid into the work.
    The city has been building for many a year;
    Woe to all those who send their help <note>Meaning their sinful deeds which act as "building materials."</note> there!
    The more they send ahead of them,
    The more they follow after; they shall be
    Received with all the greater harm.

The city is so inverted that the highest <note>Those of highest rank or greatest pride.</note> are placed in the lowest and most ignoble place. Lucifer sits bound in the lowest abyss by his own guilt, and into him flows all the sin, torment, sickness, and shame with which hell, purgatory <term>fegfúr: a place of cleansing suffering</term>, and this earth are so miserably beset.

In the lowest part of hell, the fire and the darkness and the stench and the icing <note>The freezing cold, often associated with the deepest circle of hell in medieval theology.</note> and every kind of torment are the greatest, and there Christian people are arranged according to their works. In the middle part, every kind of torment is more moderate. There the Jews are [placed] according to...

# Chapter XXI.

arranged according to their works. In the uppermost part of hell, every kind of pain is at its very least, and there the heathens <note>non-Christians, specifically those from the classical world</note> are arranged according to their works.

The heathens lament in this way:
"Alas, had we possessed a Law <note>referring to the divine law or scripture</note>
Then it would not be eternally so greatly painful for us!"
The Jews also lament in this way:
"Alas, had we followed God in Moses' teaching,
Then we would not be damned so severely!"
The Christians lament even more,
That they have lost the great honor
Through their own stubbornness,
Though Christ had chosen them with great love.
They look upon Lucifer without ceasing with great misery,
And they must go before him naked, with all their guilt revealed.
Alas, how shamefully are they received by him!
He greets them horribly and speaks bitterly:
> "You cursed ones with me,
> What joys did you seek here? <note>original: "hie"</note>
> Indeed, you never heard anything good said of me;
> How then could you be so well pleased?"

Then he seizes the proud man first of all and presses him under his tail <note>the tail is often depicted in medieval art as a site of filth or secondary faces</note> and speaks thus: "I am not so sunken that I will not still have power over you." All the sodomites <note>a term used historically for those committing perceived unnatural sexual acts</note> travel through his neck and dwell in his belly. When he draws his breath, they travel into his belly; but when he coughs, they travel out again. The false saints <note>hypocrites who appeared holy but were not</note> he sets in his lap and kisses them most horribly and says: "You are my companions. I too was covered with beautiful falsehood, therefore you are all deceived." He gnaws upon the usurer <note>one who lends money at interest</note> without ceasing, and reproaches him because he was never merciful. He robs the robber himself and then commends him to his companions, that they may hunt and strike him and have no mercy upon him. The thief hangs by his feet and is a lantern <term>lûhtevas: a vessel for light or a torch</term> in hell; yet the wretched ones see no better for it. Those who were unchaste together here must lie bound in such a manner before Lucifer; but if one comes there alone, then the devil is his companion.
