# Part Three

The unbelieving masters <note>scholars or teachers who lacked faith</note> sit at Lucifer's feet, so that they must gaze directly upon their impure god. He also disputes with them, so that they must be put to shame. He devours the greedy, for they always desired to have more. When he has swallowed them, he passes them out through his tail. The murderers must stand bloody before him and receive fiery sword-strokes from the devil. Those who here on earth nurtured grim hatred must there be his snot-vessel <note>original: "trisemvas"; a receptacle for mucus</note> and hang forever before his nose. Those who here so diligently practiced gluttony and excessive drinking must stand before Lucifer with eternal hunger and eat glowing stones. Their drink is sulfur and pitch. There, everything sour shall be given in exchange for the sweet; we shall see the result of what we practice here. The slothful is there burdened with every pain. The wrathful is struck there with fiery whips. The poor minstrel <note>original: "spilman"; a traveling performer or entertainer</note>, who with high spirits can create sinful vanities, weeps more tears in hell than there is water in the sea.

I saw beneath Lucifer the bottom of hell; it is a hard, black flint stone that shall bear the weight of that work forevermore. Although hell has neither bottom nor end, it nevertheless possesses both depth and limit within its own order.

How hell burns and frets within itself, and how the devils grapple with the souls, and how they boil and roast, and how they swim and wade in the stench and mire, and among the worms and in the pool, and how they bathe in sulfur and pitch—neither they themselves, nor any creature, could ever fully describe it. When I, by God's grace and without effort, had seen this misery, I, poor soul, was so pained by the stench and the unearthly heat that I could neither sit nor walk, and I was deprived of all my five senses for three days, like a person who has been struck by thunder. Yet my soul suffered no distress there, for she had not brought the sickness there which is called the eternal death. Nevertheless, if it were possible for a pure soul to be present therein, she would be an eternal light and a great comfort to it. For the innocent soul must by nature always glow and shine, for she is born out of the eternal light without pain. But if she takes upon herself the likeness of the devil, then she loses her beautiful light.

<summary>A vivid visionary description of the specific punishments in hell for various sins, concluding with the physical and spiritual toll the vision took on the author.</summary>
<keywords>Lucifer, Seven Deadly Sins, Hell, punishments, sensory deprivation, pure soul, eternal death, sulfur and pitch</keywords>

<header>Chapter XXI.</header>

## Chapter XXI.

Whether in the eternal hell any single comfort
May come to the damned from prayer
Or from alms-giving,
That I have not heard,
For they are constantly in such a fierce disposition
That they shudder at everything good.
After the Last Day, Lucifer shall
Put on a new garment,
Which has grown within itself
Out of the filth of all foul sins
That ever came to the knowledge of men or angels,
For he is the first vessel of all sin.
Then he shall be unbound,
And yet his fury and his terribleness
Are so mingled in all souls and in all devils
That his presence is never missed anywhere.

Then he shall immediately distend himself <note>original: "derinten", meaning to swell or stretch out</note> so greatly and his snout <note>original: "grans", often used for a bird's beak or a beast's maw</note> becomes very wide; there, with one draw of his breath, he swallows down the devils, Jews, and heathens. Yet they have their full reward in his belly and their particular "festival." Woe then to soul and body! That which a human mouth cannot speak of! That is all as nothing compared to the countless distress that happens to them there. For truly, I cannot endure to think upon it for as long as one might say a "Hail Mary" <note>original: "Ave Maria"</note>. Oh woe, so gruesome is it there!

Hell has a head at the top, which is so immense and has upon it many a gruesome eye, from which flames strike out and encompass all the poor souls who dwell there in the "fore-court" <note>The Limbo of the Patriarchs or *Limbus Patrum*</note>, from which God took Adam and others of our fathers. That is now the greatest purgatory <note>term: "vegfûr" (Purgatory) — a place of cleansing suffering for those not yet ready for heaven</note> into which a sinner may come. There I have seen bishops, governors <note>term: "vôgte" (advocates or bailiffs) — secular officials often accused of corruption</note>, and great lords in long distress with countless sorrow. All who come there have barely been spared eternal hell by God, for I have found no one there who, at their end, ever spoke a pure confession with their physical mouth. When the outer senses were taken from them by the nature of death, the body lay still, yet soul and body still had one will. When they had lost the earthly darkness, God gave them true knowledge in that moment of parting! Oh, how narrow is the way there to the kingdom of heaven! Then the community of body and soul, still inseparable, spoke thus: "True God, have mercy on me, my sin..."

<summary>The author describes Lucifer's monstrous transformation after the Last Judgment and identifies the "fore-court" of hell as the most intense level of Purgatory, populated by high-ranking officials who failed to make a full confession.</summary>
<keywords>Lucifer, Hell, Purgatory, Last Judgment, bishops, confession, Limbo, spiritual visions</keywords>

# Part Three

are truly a sorrow to me. This is a brief hour, in which God has secretly recovered many a soul that was openly lost. I have never found that this happened to any person unless they had done something good with a good will. The devils lead the stained souls from the body to purgatory <note>original: "vegefûr"</note>, for the pure angels may not touch them as long as they do not shine with a clarity equal to their own.

A soul on earth may, however, have help from friends,
So that the devils take good care,
That the devils never attack them.
If the soul is heavily burdened with guilt, it must still endure other pains,
But it can bear all that much better,
Than if the devils had to hold them captive
And mock them without ceasing.

When our holy fathers <note>A reference to the Old Testament patriarchs and prophets</note> went to hell <note>Refers to the "Harrowing of Hell" or the Limbo of the Fathers</note>, that which they brought with them was true hope in the Christian faith with holy love of God and many a humble virtue and faithful labor. They all went to hell, yet they were prepared for the kingdom of heaven; nothing they brought with them could harm them in hell, except that which had to burn within them there. That was Love <note>original: "miñe"</note>, which shall burn eternally in all of God’s children.

Should they never come to the kingdom of heaven,
God has measured it thus:
What we carry up there with us,
That we must drink and eat there.
But the negligent ones, who now depart from here
Unchanged by such great sins,
They can never be so evilly un-damned,
As before the mouth of hell,
Where at all hours
Lucifer’s breath releases every pain,
And pierces through them so miserably,
That the poor ones are as greatly united
In the flame and in the manifold fury,
As the most blessed ones were united
In the sweet, acknowledged love of God.
I saw no more of all the ladies there
Than the high princes, who here loved
All kinds of sin equally with the princes.
Hell also has a mouth at the top of its head.
It stands open at every hour.
All those who come into that mouth,
From them eternal death shall never more be taken away.

<header>Chapters XXI—XXII.</header>

## XXII. I have heard of God’s mercy, of His longing, and His justice.

I have heard and seen such immeasurable mercy from God that I spoke: Lord, how can this be? Since Your justice is the equal <note>original: "genos" — a peer or companion</note> of Your mercy, how is Your goodness so exceedingly great?
Then our Lord spoke a most faithful word thus:
I tell you by My divine faithfulness,
That there are more in holy Christendom
Who travel from the mouth <note>likely referring to the "mouth" of the grave or the last confession</note> to the kingdom of heaven,
Than there are those who travel to eternal hell.
Yet Justice constantly maintains her power.
Whatever falls to her through guilt,
Shall never be taken from her by Me.
But I will first come like a father to the burdened soul.
If I have perceived anything good and despair-free from her,
That comes from the great longing <note>original: "bekorunge" — can mean temptation, but here implies a divine "testing" or "yearning" for the soul</note>
Which I have for My children.
Then the soul spoke: Oh, most Beloved, would You tell me of Your longing, so that Your desire and my yearning might meet?
Then our Lord spoke:
Now hear how I am drawn.
My goodness and My generosity, My faithfulness and My mercy
Constrain Me so greatly that I let them flow
Over the mountains of pride and over the valleys of humility
And over the thickets of wickedness <note>original: "verrikeit" — malice or perversity</note>
And over the smooth (*straight*) paths of purity.
And My goodness constrains Me even more
Than a wicked man is driven by his ill-will.
And yet My justice is greater
Than all the malice of the devils.
Then the soul spoke:
Lord, Your justice
Suits You so very well in the living truth,
That it gives me countless joy without heartache;
Wherever it may strike,
The truth always rejoices.

<summary>The author explores the tension between God's infinite mercy and His absolute justice, concluding that God's fatherly longing for the soul leads more people to heaven than to hell.</summary>
<keywords>mercy, justice, divine longing, salvation, heaven and hell, humility, goodness</keywords>

--- Page 122 ---
### Part Three.

### XXIII. The power of longing takes away words. God cannot do without virgins. Embracing God’s countenance and His delight overcomes a thousand deaths.

Whoever burns there in the fire of powerful love <note>original: "creftigen miñe"; "Minne" is a specific Middle High German term for mystical, divine love</note> cannot endure to cool themselves anywhere through sin. Oh, most beloved, when shall you desire that which I desire? Thus spoke a wretched soul; then the Beloved answered her and spoke as if he did not know what she wanted. "What do you desire?" Then she spoke again: "Lord, the power of longing <note>original: "gerunge"</note> has taken from me the voice of words." Then he spoke: "Virgins cannot easily woo, for their modesty is noble by nature." Then she lamented: "O woe, Lord! Truly you have been a stranger to me for all too long. If only I could win you, Lord, with magic <note>original: "zôfere"</note>, so that you could find rest nowhere but in me. Oh, then it would come to a love-match, and you would then have to beg me to proceed with caution." Then he answered and spoke thus:

->O you unstained dove,<-
->Now grant me this, that I must spare you,<-
->This earth cannot yet do without you.<-
->Then she spoke: Oh Lord,<-
->If it could happen to me for one hour,<-
->That I might gaze upon you according to my heart’s wish<-
->And embrace you with my arms,<-
->Your divine delights of love<-
->Must pass through my soul,<-
->As much as can happen to a human on earth!<-
->Whatever I should suffer after that,<-
->The like was never seen by human eyes;<-
->Yes, a thousand deaths would be too light.<-
->Lord, I am in such pain for you;<-
->Now I will stand in faithfulness.<-
->If you can endure it, Lord,<-
->Then let me walk in misery for you for a long time.<-
->I know well, Lord, that the first desire<-
->For me must yet seize you.<-

### XXIV. *Two kinds of people are offered two kinds of spirit. From God and from the devil. Of sevenfold love.*

Now I will write to you of a true spiritual sister and of a worldly Beguine <note>A Beguine was a member of a lay religious community of women who took no formal vows</note>, who converse with one another thus.

<summary>This page depicts an intimate mystical dialogue between a "wretched soul" and God, exploring the overwhelming power of divine longing that transcends speech. It introduces a subsequent debate between a dedicated nun and a secular Beguine regarding spiritual authenticity.</summary>
<keywords>mystical love, Minne, longing, divine union, virgins, Beguines, spiritual dialogue, suffering for God</keywords>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the mystical teachings on the nature of divine love and the distinction between holy and worldly spirits, likely part of a larger treatise for a female religious community.</meta>

# Chapters XXIII—XXIV.

The spiritual sister speaks: Out of the true light of the Holy Spirit comes freedom from <term>heart-sorrow</term><note>original: "herzeleit"; refers to the deep spiritual or emotional suffering of the soul</note>; but the worldly <term>beguine</term><note>original: "begiñe"; a member of a lay religious community, here used perhaps critically to denote one lacking true inwardness</note> speaks from her flesh with the spirit of Lucifer, in gruesome labor.

There are two kinds of spiritual people on this earth; to them are born two kinds of spirit. God offers His Holy Spirit to the pure spirits who live here in faithful, holy intention in all their being. There, two pure natures come together: the hot fire of the Godhead and the flowing wax of the loving soul. If there is then a pure wick of constant humility, a beautiful light is formed there, which can be seen from afar. O loving soul, then you become so rich that no one can make you poor, even though you are the poorest of all. Through humility one becomes rich; through well-bred, good manners one becomes noble and well-born; through love one becomes beautiful and praiseworthy; through lowliness one is raised very high in God. Meditate on this, spiritual sister, and let no one drive you from your good manners, so that you may remain holy.

The devil also offers his spirit to those spirits,
Who with hatred and with haughty greed
Are ready for the worst.
They do not know how love carries all that is good;
They become so poor from evil hatred and from the devil’s fury,
That it would be impossible
For them ever to find or follow God’s love.

Faithful love has a constant praise toward God;
Yearning love brings many a sweet distress to pure hearts;
Seeking love is for itself alone;
Known love gives itself in common to all creatures;
Shining love is still mingled with sadness;
Silent love enjoys without labor.
Oh, how quietly it works, so that the body does not even know it!
Pure love is still in God alone,
Because they both have one will
And there is no creature so noble that it may hinder them.
Knowledge has written this out of the eternal book.
Gold is often greatly stained by copper;
So do falsehood and vain glory,
Which destroy all virtues from the human soul.
The ignoble soul, which is so fond of transitory things
That it never trembled with love,
And to whom God never spoke lovingly,
Oh woe, alas! for such a one, this life is entirely night.

<summary>The text contrasts the "pure nature" of a soul united with God through humility and love against the "ignoble soul" driven by hatred and worldly greed. It describes various stages or types of love—faithful, yearning, seeking, known, shining, and silent—as the path to spiritual nobility.</summary>

<keywords>mysticism, Holy Spirit, humility, divine love, spiritual sister, beguine, virtues, union with God</keywords>

## This is the fourth book.

### I. Five things pure virgins should possess.

If you wish to adorn the state of virginity, <margin>1</margin>
Which God has honored so greatly, <margin>2</margin>
That for your love He became the son of a virgin; <margin>3</margin>
(Ah, consider how He speaks of this!) <margin>4</margin>
Then you should keep silence in humility <margin>5</margin>
And suffer grief with a loving heart <margin>6</margin>
And in all places <margin>7</margin>
Maintain a maidenly modesty all your days; <margin>8</margin>
In this way you may survive in purity. <margin>9</margin>
O virgin, what God will give you then! <margin>10</margin>
He wishes to be a beautiful youth for you, <margin>11</margin>
And wishes to dance the heavenly dance <note>original: "himelreigen" — a circular dance often used in medieval literature to describe the joy of the blessed in heaven</note> with you. <margin>12</margin>
O, I wretched, lame dog! <margin>13</margin>
I would also howl with you. <margin>14</margin>
Examine what I mean by this:
The number of pure virgins is small.

### II. This book has come from God. The soul praises itself in many things. Two angels are given to her, and two evil devils, and twelve virtues fight against the flesh.

In all the days of my life, before I began this book, and even if a single word of it came from God into my soul, I was one of the simplest people who ever appeared in the spiritual life. I knew nothing of the devil's malice, I did not recognize <note>original: "kaûfte" — literally "bought," here meaning to experience or be acquainted with</note> the world's frailty, and the falseness of spiritual people was also unknown to me.

<summary>The author introduces the fourth book with a poetic exhortation on the virtues of virginity and a personal reflection on her own spiritual innocence before receiving divine revelations.</summary>

# Chapters I—II.

I must speak to the honor of God and also for the instruction of this book. I, an unworthy sinner <note>original: "sünderin"; the feminine suffix confirms the author is a woman</note>, was greeted by the Holy Spirit in my twelfth year in such a flowing abundance, while I was alone, that I could never again endure it, so that I could never offer myself to any great daily sin. That most beloved greeting occurred every day and caused me a loving sorrow. All the world's sweetness and honor still increases every day. This has happened for over thirty-one years.

I knew nothing more of God than the Christian faith alone, and I always strove with diligence that my heart might become pure. God is Himself my witness to this, that I never prayed to Him with will or with desire that He should give me these things which are written in this book. I also never thought that such things could happen to a human being. While I was in my youth and among my dear friends, to whom I was always the most beloved, I had no knowledge of these things. I had long before desired that I might be despised through no fault of my own. Then, for the love of God, I went to a city where no one was my friend except for one person alone. I was afraid of that same person, lest the holy shame and the pure love of God be taken away from me. Then God never left me alone, and brought me into such loving sweetness, into such holy knowledge, and into such incomprehensible wonders, that I could make little use of earthly things. Then, for the first time, my spirit was brought out of my prayer between the heaven and the air. There I saw with the eyes of my soul, in heavenly bliss, the beautiful humanity of our Lord Jesus Christ, and I recognized Him by His lordly countenance: the Holy Trinity, the Father’s eternity, the Son’s labor <note>original: "arbeit"; refers to Christ's suffering and redemptive work</note>, and the Holy Spirit’s sweetness. There I saw the angel to whom I was entrusted at my baptism, and my devil. Then our Lord spoke: "I will take this angel from you and will give you two in return; they shall tend to you in these wonders." When you, O soul, looked upon the two angels, oh, how greatly she was terrified in humble powerlessness, and she laid herself at the feet of our Lord, and thanked Him and lamented to Him very much that she was so unworthy that such princes should be her chamberlains. One angel was of the Seraphim <note>The highest order of angels, associated with burning love</note>, and he is a

<summary>The author recounts her first mystical visitation at age twelve and her later decision to live in poverty and isolation, leading to a vision of the Trinity and the assignment of two Seraphim as guardians.</summary>

my defects <note>original: "breſter"</note>, and a holy lantern to the most spoiled soul. The other angel was of the Cherubim; he is a keeper of gifts and ordains wisdom in the loving soul.

Then our Lord let two devils come forth; they were great masters and were taken from Lucifer’s school, and they had seldom come out. When the soul beheld the very gruesome devils, she trembled a little and rejoiced in our Lord, and yet she received them very willingly. One devil is a deceiver in beautiful angelic clothing. Oh, alas, how many false ruses he first laid out for me! He came at one time during the Mass, down from the heights, and spoke: "Now I am very beautiful; would you worship me?" Then the soul answered: "One shall worship God alone in all goodness and in all need." Then he spoke: "Would you then look up to see who I am?" Then he showed below in the air a beautiful, false radiance—which has led many a heretic <note>original: "ketzer"</note> astray—and spoke: "In the throne upon the seat, you alone shall be the highest virgin and I the most beautiful youth beside you." But she spoke: "He would not be wise who, having come to the best, would then take the worst." Then he spoke: "Since you will not give yourself to me—you are so holy and I so humble—I will nonetheless worship you." Then she spoke: "No grace shall be given to you for worshipping a puddle <note>original: "pfûl"; the soul humbles herself by calling herself a stagnant pool or mire</note>." Then he showed, as if painted, the five wounds on feet and hands and spoke: "Now you see well who I am; if you will live by my counsel, I will give you great honor. You should tell the people of this grace, for much good would come of it." Then she spoke—and she was very weary of his useless tales, yet she listened to them willingly so that she might be the wiser for it: "You say to me that you are God; now tell me, who is he who is now here, the Son of the living God in the true priest's hands?" Then he wanted to go away, and she spoke:

## Chapter II.

That the game must turn against you.
You wanted to do that
So that I would fall into doubt and into sadness
And into unbelief and into impurity,
And thereafter into eternal heartache.
And you also do it for this reason:
That I should imagine that I am holy,
So that you may thus come to me.
Yes, you very old deceiver,
As long as God stands by me
You lose all your labor.

Then he cried out: "Woe upon your magic <note>original: "zöuer" - likely referring to the power of prayer or spiritual resistance</note>, let it be, I will never trouble you again."

The second devil was given to me; he is a breaker of peace and a master of holy (*secret*) impurity. However, God has forbidden him such that he himself may never come to me. But he sends me perverted people as messengers, who pervert good things for me, and take from me what they can with words against my honor; he also aims for this: when good people are together and they speak anything useless in an impure manner, then I, poor soul, cannot remain untroubled there. That never happened to me before.

One night I was in my first sleep during my prayer, then this same devil came traveling through the air and took great notice of the sinful earth. He was as large as a giant, he had a short tail and a crooked nose, his head was as large as a tub, and there came traveling out of his mouth fiery sparks covered in black flames. Then he laughed with a false grimace in a very gruesome voice. Then the soul asked him why he laughed, what he sought, and what his business was. Then he answered and spoke: "I rejoice in this, since I cannot torment you myself, that I find so many who appear to be angels and gladly do it for me, that they torment you." Now he speaks again: "I am the chamberlain of spiritual people and I seek in them two kinds of sickness which most quickly separate them from God; that is holy or secret impurity. Whenever a person in a holy life seeks the comfort of their flesh without right necessity and in all their five senses, then they become impure, which is coarse and shameful, and they become cold toward true god- <note>The text breaks off here; the final word "gotz-" likely completes as "gotzforcht" (fear of God) or "gotzlust" (delight in God).</note>

I, wretched person! I had committed such great sins in my earliest childhood that, had I remained without repentance and without confession, I would have had to be in purgatory for ten years. Now, dear Lord, whenever I die, I will gladly suffer therein for the sake of Your love.

<header>Chapters II—III.</header>

## Chapters II—III.

Love <note>original: "miñe" - referring to Minne, the personified divine love</note> and these wonders occupied me so greatly that I did not dare to keep them silent. However, in my simplicity, I suffered much distress. Then I said: "O, merciful God, what have you seen in me? For you know well that I am a fool, a sinful and poor human being in body and soul. You should give these things to wise people, so that you might be praised." Then our Lord became very angry with me, poor as I am, and asked me for a judgment: "Now tell me, are you not mine?" — "Yes, Lord, that is my desire." — "Must I then not do with you what I will?" — "Yes, most beloved of my heart, gladly, even if I should become nothing." — Then our Lord spoke: "You shall follow and trust me in these things, and you shall also be sick for a long time, and I myself will care for you; and everything you need for body and soul, I will give to you."

Then I, poor and trembling, went in humble shame to my confessor <note>likely Heinrich von Halle, who encouraged her writing</note> and told him these words, and also desired his teaching. Then he said I should proceed joyfully; God, who had drawn me to Him, would surely protect me. Then he commanded me to do that which often makes me weep with shame—for my great unworthiness stands open before my eyes—which was that he commanded a lowly woman to write this book out of God's heart and mouth.

Thus has this book come lovingly from God and is not taken from human senses.

### *III. How sinners fall away from God despite the three gifts of wisdom.*
*Of the stone. Of the praise of the Virgin, that is, Christendom.*

Just as one quiets the dear child and strikes the troublesome one; so does our dear Lord do, speaking thus: "He who has no good in him shall never enter my kingdom; and he who cannot get enough of transitory things shall be filled with eternal hunger. And woe to him who has wealth that clings to his heart and who wishes to set himself above other people; he shall fall away from me into the bottomless valley." To this, the holy knowledge that God has given us answers with three kinds of gifts of true wisdom, with which we should satisfy ourselves and guard against all harm. The first is priestly wisdom and Christian teaching; as [it was revealed] to me...

# Part Four

God has drawn [her] in great honor. I saw with the true eyes of my eternity, in sweet bliss without labor, a stone that was like a shapely mountain and had grown of its own accord; it had formed within itself all kinds of colors and tasted very sweet from noble heavenly spices. Then I asked the very sweet stone who he was. He spoke thus: *I am Jesus* <note>original: "Ego sum Jesus"</note>. Then I went out of myself in love and leaned my head against him. Then I saw that outside of him all darkness was locked away, and inside he was filled with the eternal light. Upon the stone stood the most beautiful maiden that was ever seen, except for our dear Lady Saint Mary; yet she is her companion. Her feet are adorned with a stone called jasper <term>jaspis: a green gemstone associated in medieval lore with healing and spiritual protection</term>. This stone has such great power that it drives away evil desires from the feet of her longing. It also gives a pure taste and stirs a holy hunger. It banishes all darkness from the eyes. This noble stone is the Christian faith. The maiden stood on two feet: one is the "binding" and the other the "loosing" in holy power, which all Christian, faithful priests possess <note>A reference to the "Power of the Keys" granted to the Church to forgive or retain sins</note>. She carries in her right hand a chalice with red wine, which she alone drinks in unceasing bliss; the angels never taste of it. This is the blood of the eternal Son, which fills her spirit so greatly that she gives us many sweet teachings. In her left hand she has a fiery sword, which is hung all over with golden bells; these ring so sweetly that all those who seek the Holy Trinity must come to her.

Then I asked the maiden how it was that she carried her sword in the left hand and the chalice in the right hand? She spoke: "I must be trusted, for on each person's last day, God strikes His blow. I shall also pour out His blood with my right hand, as Christ is named for the honor of His Father." She also has a great power in her hands, with which she draws to herself all that God has chosen, and also casts away from her all that has given itself to the devil. Ah! She has such a beautiful face, I could gaze upon her forever. Oil flows out of her throat; that is mercy, the ointment for sin. She also has golden teeth in her mouth, with which she chews the heavenly

# Chapter III.

...crane-roots <note>original: "kranwurtzen"; likely referring to a specific medicinal or symbolic root</note>, these are the sayings of the prophets. Honey drips from her tongue, which the swift bees—the holy apostles—have sucked from the sweetest wildflowers. She carries blooming roses before her mouth, and her nostrils are filled with sweet violets. She wears green and white lilies upon her forehead, which signify: She is a mother to widows, a friend to the married, and the honor of all virgins. Her eyes sparkle entirely with joy like the white-green dawn, wherein the playful sun moves. And just as her eyes are threefold by nature and yet whole, so it is with the form of the Holy Trinity. The white signifies the Father, the green the Son, and the delicate sun the Holy Spirit.

# Part Four.

To that place they carried the children of the heathens <note>non-Christians or those outside the faith</note>, who were all leprous and blind. Above this spring stood a most spiritual man; no one else was permitted to touch them there; he was John the Baptist. He washed the children in the spring so that they became seeing and beautifully healthy. Then I asked the maiden who she was. She said, "I am she whom you love so dearly, and I am your companion. I am Holy Christendom, and we both have one bridegroom." This is the maiden of the blessed priests, whom they so often gaze upon with love.

The second wisdom comes from natural senses: through which one can both act, lose, and gain. In this wisdom dwell many perverse laypeople and false priests and deceitful spiritual folk. No person ever becomes so holy that they can fully guard themselves against these three. So wicked is their disposition that they pervert all goodness. No one becomes spiritual through this gift unless they are a fool for the sake of God's love, for pure, holy simplicity is a mother of true divine wisdom. What does it help that a prominent man has many pennies, yet buys nothing but hunger and thirst, and long-lasting disgrace, and moreover eternal heartache!

The third wisdom is of grace, and it directs itself toward all of God's gifts. It never becomes so rich that it dares to compare itself to even the least of creatures. It never grieves over its own hardship; it rejoices alone in God's will. It also cannot endure that a single virtue should remain locked outside its door.

### IV. *Of two unequal paths: one goes down to hell, the other climbs up into heaven.*

The wealth of transitory things is an unfaithful guest; holy poverty brings a precious burden before God.

Vanity does not think of its own harm; constancy is fully laden with all virtues.

# Chapter IV.

Foolishness <note>original: "tumpheit," referring to spiritual ignorance or lack of discernment</note> pleases only itself,
but wisdom can never finish its learning.

Anger brings great darkness into the soul,
while holy gentleness possesses all grace for certain.

Pride always desires to be the best,
but humility cannot rest
until she has given herself to the service of all creatures.

Vain honor is deaf and blind before God,
but innocent lowliness <note>original: "smacheit," meaning being disregarded or held in low esteem by the world</note> sanctifies all God's children.

Falsehood has the most beautiful shimmer,
but perfection is despised by the highest-ranking people.

Greed always has a snapping mouth,
but blessed moderation always has a sweet foundation.

Sloth squanders rich treasures,
but holy diligence does not seek its own comfort too much.

Faithlessness always gives false counsel,
but complete faithfulness never neglects a good deed.

True spirituality cannot take revenge on anyone;
the uncultivated heart <note>original: "vngebuwete herze," a heart not yet disciplined or "tilled" by spiritual practice</note> always wants to break the peace.

Good devotion cannot commit evil;
the evil will is subject to no one.

Malice has by nature an evil foundation;
divine grace has a lovely countenance and a sweet mouth.

Worldly lords like to be held in high esteem,
but the spiritual soul always desires to be elsewhere.

Hidden bitterness has a smooth mouth,
but manifest love <note>original: "minësamkeit"</note> possesses the discovery of God.

Unfaithful deceit dwells very close to hatred;
holy mercy shall stand with God alone.

The lie is beautiful on the outside and hideous within;
for this reason, she is received very lovingly by her own kind.

Truth is cast out because of her lack of status;
all those who love her must suffer many insults with Jesus.

Hatred rages forever without ceasing;
love burns without pain, she feels better than all misery.

Evil envy hates God’s generosity;
the pure heart full of love rejoices in all blessedness.

Slander is not ashamed before people nor before God,
who nevertheless hears and sees all things.

->7*<-

### Part Four.

Doubt is a gruesome fall;
True hope preserves it all.

False comfort is never joyful;
True guilt troubles it so.

After this, our dear Lord spoke, when he had shown this to me, very quickly thus: He who considers how good I am, he holds himself fast ever to me. To that end help us, Lord, for your own honor!

V. *Our sin, future fall, earthly being, the kingdom of heaven, and God's gift, shall stand open before our eyes.*

> Lord, my guilt, with which I have lost you,
> Stands before my eyes like the greatest mountain
> And has made a long darkness between me and you
> And eternal distance from you and from <note>original: "mir" (me). The manuscript reads "owe" (alas/woe).</note> me.
> Ah, Love above all loves,
> Draw me back into yourself.
> But Lord, the future fall <note>The potential for future sin or spiritual failure.</note>
> Also stands before my eyes, like
> A fiery dragon's mouth,
> Which would gladly swallow me at all times.
> Ah, my only Good, now help me, so that I
> May flow unstained into you.
> Lord, my earthly being stands before my eyes,
> Like a parched field,
> Where little good has grown.
> Ah, sweet Jesus Christ,
> Now send me the sweet rain of your humanity,
> And the hot sun of your living divinity,

> That I may lament my heart's sorrow.
> Lord, your eternal kingdom
> Stands open before my eyes like
> The gemstone bridal-wedding and the greatest feast

> Ah, my Beloved,
> There you shall without ceasing
> Join to yourself your love-desiring bride.
> Lord, all your gifts,
> Which I have received
> From you, they are before my eyes

# Chapters V—VI.

### V.
It seemed like a wretched <term>reproach</term><note>original: "orschlage"; likely a stinging blow or an accusation of unworthiness</note> against me,
When your highest gift here humbles me.
    Thus God, who gives all things, answered:
Your mountain shall melt away in love.
Your enemies shall gain no part of you.
The hot sun has shone through your field,
And yet your fruit has remained unspoiled,
And in my kingdom you shall be a new bride,
And there I will give you a sweet kiss from my mouth,
So that all my divinity
Shall soar through your soul,
And my threefold eyes
Shall forever without ceasing
Play within your twofold heart.
Where has your sorrow gone then?
Even if you prayed for a thousand years,
I would not give you a single sigh in return <note>Meaning that in the joy of union, sorrow and the need for petitionary sighs vanish entirely.</note>.

### VI. God’s election no one can disturb. True repentance has forgiveness through God’s grace and is without purgatory.

A troubled person asked me to pray for them; I did so with fear and apart from myself. Then God heard me with his countenance, with his words, and with his true voice, and spoke thus: There is no lamb so white nor so pure that it is not constrained by its wool <note>A metaphor suggesting that even the purest soul is bound by its earthly nature or "clothing."</note>, and my <term>election</term><note>original: "vserwelunge"; the theological concept of being chosen by God for salvation</note> no one can destroy. This I have shown him with three things: the first, that I was merciful toward his guilt; the second, that I have given him my grace; the third, that I never wished to allow unfaithful people to ever exercise any power over him. Then I lamented for him in this way: "Lord, he still fears greatly that you have not completely forgiven his guilt." Thus God answered: That would be impossible. To him who is sorry for his sins, I forgive them; but to him who repents them with misery, I give my grace; and to him who repents so much that he would give his life before he would do it again, and remains steadfast in this, he shall be drawn to no more pain for that guilt after this life—unless he commits great daily sins and is caught in those that are unatoned.

<summary>God reassures the soul of its status as a "new bride" and promises a mystical union that erases all sorrow. The text also clarifies that profound, life-altering repentance exempts a soul from the pains of purgatory.</summary>
<keywords>bridal mysticism, divine kiss, election, repentance, purgatory, forgiveness, spiritual field, Trinity</keywords>

<page-num>102</page-num>
<header>Fourth Part.</header>

### *VII. How a joyful soul speaks to God in perfect love.*

Lord, because I have been subject to all creatures <note>The author refers to the spiritual practice of humility, placing oneself below all created things</note>, you have drawn me above all things to yourself; and because, Lord, I possess no earthly treasure, I have no earthly heart. Since you, Lord, are my treasure, you are also my heart; you alone are my good, and I am changeable in all things.

### *VIII. Concerning the Body of God, the sick, the forsaken, and strength.*

Regarding the fact that a sick person who is forsaken cannot receive the Body of God <note>The Eucharist or Holy Communion</note>: I was so simple-minded in this matter that I could not fully reconcile it with my senses and my faith, for one cannot lose God except through sin alone. Then my soul, in united love, asked our Lord how this could be. Our Lord answered me thus: "You are right, he cannot lose me except through sin; but his body, through illness, may lose my Body." In these words, I saw this interpretation <gloss>original: "glosen" - a scholarly or spiritual commentary</gloss> within the Holy Trinity: Whenever we receive the Body of God, the Divinity unites itself with our innocent soul, and God’s humanity mixes with our gruesome <note>The author uses "grůwelichen" to contrast the frailty and decay of the mortal human body with the perfection of Christ</note> body, and thus the Holy Spirit makes His dwelling in our faith. We should guard this blessed union with great care.

### *IX. Concerning the fourfold offerings of the priests.*

After this, our Lord told me that priests should receive their offerings from four sources and nowhere else: from the altar, from penance, with the Body of God <note>Mass stipends</note>, and for <insert>for?</insert> <unclear>for?</unclear> the sick. However, the sick person should give an offering for the anointing <note>Extreme Unction or Last Rites</note> according to his means and according to his own will. And in the field <note>Likely referring to ministry outside the parish church</note> he shall take what people wish to give him there. The priest shall not choose and shall not demand; rather, whatever the sick person has offered, he shall receive it all as a matter of grace and not as a right.

### *X. Concerning the offerings of the laity according to their means.*

The laity who give offerings should guard themselves against evil stinginess in their giving, just as the priest [must guard] himself...

<page-num>103</page-num>
->Chapters VII—XII.<-

One must beware of swift greed; that is very necessary for us both, for the layman should offer with great love and with a laughing soul into God’s merciful hand. The priest should receive it from God’s hands with humble fear and a trembling heart, and should praisefully give it back to God in all his doing; for this earthly wealth is deceitful <note>original: "schalkhaft," meaning treacherous or like a rogue</note> when one takes it, but it is very free when one gives it.

## XI. How *Christians* *should* conduct themselves toward the *Jews* in four things.

After this, God taught me how Christians should conduct themselves toward the Jews. One shall not keep their law <note>original: "ê," referring to religious law or covenant</note>. One shall not dwell with them. One shall also not be with them overnight. One shall buy and sell with them without friendly fellowship and without false greed.

## XII. How the *bride*, who is *united* with *God*, rejects the *comfort* of all *creatures*, except *God* alone, and how she *sinks* from the *pain*.

Thus speaks God’s bride, who has dwelt in the locked treasury <note>original: "triskameren," a chamber for valuables</note> of the holy, entire Trinity. "Ah, stand back and go from me, all creatures; you do me woe and you cannot comfort me." The creatures say: "Why?" The bride speaks: "My Beloved has escaped me in my sleep, while I rested in His union." "Can this beautiful world and all the good it has not comfort you?" "No, I see the serpent of falsehood, whose false cunning swallows up all the delights of this world. I also see the hook of greed in the bait <margin>Manuscript: bait</margin> of ignoble sweetness, with which it catches many." "Can the kingdom of heaven not comfort you?" "No, it would be dead in itself if the living God were not there." "Now then, happy bride, can the saints not comfort you?" "No, should they be separated from the flowing-through of the living Godhead, they would weep more bitterly than I, for they have risen above me and dwell deeper in God." "Can God’s Son ever comfort you?"

<summary>This page outlines the ethical exchange of offerings between laity and clergy, mandates strict social separation between Christians and Jews, and describes a mystical dialogue where the soul rejects all worldly and heavenly comforts in favor of direct union with God.</summary>

# Part Four

> Yes, I ask Him when we shall go
> Into the flowers of holy recognition;
> And I pray to Him most gladly
> That He might unlock for me
> The playing flood
> Which soars within the Holy Trinity,
> Wherein the soul alone finds life.

> For the playing sun of the living Godhead <term>Godhead: the essential nature of God, often distinguished from the personhood of the Trinity</term>

> This has taken away from me everything
> That dwells outside of the Godhead.
> I have no taste for anything but God alone;
> I am wondrously dead <note>A reference to "mystical death," where the ego dies so the soul can live in God.</note>.

> Then I send all creatures to the Court <note>The Heavenly Court.</note>
> And command them to praise God for me
> With all their wisdom, with all their love,
> With all their beauty and with all their longing,
> Just as they were created by God in their uncorrupted state,
> And also with all their voices as they sing now.
> When I look upon this great praise,
> I feel no pain anywhere.

I also cannot endure that a single comfort should touch me, except for my Beloved alone. I love my earthly friends within a heavenly fellowship, and I love my enemies in a holy sorrow for the sake of their salvation. God has enough of all things, but He can never have enough of the touch of the soul.

When this wonder and this comfort had lasted eight years, God wished to comfort me all too greatly regarding the nobility of my soul. "Ah no, dear Lord, do not exalt me so much!" Thus spoke the unworthy soul: "It is just as well for me in the lowest part; there I will ever gladly remain for Your honor." Then the poor soul fell down among the neglected and the tormented souls, and it seemed to her all too good. Our Lord followed her there in such a form as those in the lowest joy could endure.

Saint John says: we shall see God as he is. That is true; but the sun shines according to the weather. Many kinds of weather exist under the sun on the earth, and likewise there are many kinds of dwellings in the kingdom of heaven. However I am able to endure and see him, so he is to me.

Then our Lord spoke: how long do you wish to be here? The bride: Oh, withdraw from me, dear Lord, and let me sink further for your honor. After this, both soul and body entered into such great darkness that I lost my understanding and the light, and I knew nothing of God’s secrets, and the most blessed love also went her way. ### XIII. *The writing of this book is seen, heard, and experienced*
->*in every limb.*<-

I cannot nor may I write, unless I see it with the eyes of my soul and hear it with the ears of my eternal spirit and feel the power of the Holy Spirit in all the limbs of my body.

### XIV. *Of the Holy Trinity, of the birth and of the*
->*name of Jesus Christ and of the nobility of mankind.*<-

I saw and see three persons in the eternal heights, before God's Son was conceived in Saint Mary’s body. Then they were known and perceived with distinction by all the holy angels in their wholeness and in their names, and how the three were one God. However clear their eyes were, they yet saw neither bone nor flesh nor color, nor the Lord’s name, Jesus. This was wondrously hidden in the eternal Father’s breast. They named the Father the uncreated eternal God, the Son the unbegun wisdom. The Spirit of them both they named the true art <note>original: "kunst" - here meaning knowledge or skill</note> of truth. The burning angels of the highest counsel, who hang toward the love <note>original: "minne" - the Middle High German term for mystical, divine love</note> of the Godhead in a single breath-pull of the entire Trinity, they served and gazed upon the wondrous—

# Part Four

The divine counsel was held when God became the God-man <note>original: "gotmensche"; refers to the union of divine and human natures in Christ</note>. Gabriel brought down the name of Jesus with the greeting alone. Neither bone nor blood was given with him then. The second person was indeed the eternal Son, yet he had not yet put on humanity; he was ours, yet was never given to us until Gabriel delivered the message. Had that same person existed for our sake to redeem us before the message, he would have had to have a beginning, which never happened. That same second person had become one nature with Adam’s humanity before Adam corrupted himself with sins. Although Adam’s nature was broken and transformed, and his part forever lost—to which God never consented—for that reason we were able, and are still able, to return. God has kept his noble, loving nature whole; therefore, he could not restrain himself. God cast Lucifer away from him immediately into the eternal prison; but he went after Adam and asked him where he was, and brought him back to the right path. Lucifer had only a single nature in God; when he broke that, he could never return.

Humanity has its full nature within the Holy Trinity, and God saw fit to make us with his own divine hands. When he lost that most holy labor in us, he was compelled within himself by a threefold desire. Therefore, he wished to bring us back with his own feet and with his own hands, so that we might have such great union with him. Had man remained in Paradise, God would have been in harmony with him at all times, and would have greeted his soul and gladdened his body. Thus I saw God coming from heaven into Paradise, like a great angel. That same nature still compels God to greet us here with recognition and with holy inwardness <note>original: "iñekeit"; a central mystical term for deep, internal devotion</note>, as far as we are prepared with holy virtues and true innocence. Whenever I think on this—that the divine nature now has bone and flesh, body and soul—I raise myself up with great joy far above my own worthiness. But the angel is formed in some measure after the Holy Trinity; yet he is a pure spirit. The soul, with its flesh, is the only house before the kingdom of heaven, and sits by the eternal host, most like unto him. There plays...

<summary>The author reflects on the Incarnation and the Fall, contrasting the irredeemable nature of Lucifer with the redeemable nature of humanity, who shares a physical and spiritual likeness with God.</summary>

### Chapters XV—XVI.

Eye to eye, and there spirit flows into spirit, and there hand touches hand, and mouth speaks to mouth, and there heart greets heart. In this way, the Host <note>The "Host" (wirt) refers to God as the master of the house.</note> honors the Lady of the House <note>The "Lady of the House" (husfröwen) represents the human soul.</note> at His side. Moreover, the princes and the lords of service, who are the holy angels, are kept by the Host before His eyes. All the service and all the praise that the angels perform is given entirely to the Lady of the House along with the Host. Forever after, according to how rich we are here in holy virtues, so noble shall our servants <note>original: "dienstmañ"</note> be.

XV. True, pure *love* <note>original: "mîne" - a Middle High German term for mystical, courtly love for God.</note> has four things. *If you give yourself to God,*
->so God also gives Himself to you.<-

True, pure love of God <note>original: "gotzmîñe"</note> has four things within it that never rest. The first is the growing longing <note>original: "gerunge"</note>; the second is the flowing torment <note>original: "qwelunge"</note>; the third is the burning experience <note>original: "bevindunge"</note> of soul and body; the fourth is a steady drawing-together <note>original: "cinunge"</note> bound with great care. Furthermore, no one can reach this unless they make a complete exchange with God; so that if you give God all that is yours, both inward and outward, He truly gives you all that is His, both inward and outward.

->When the blessed hour has passed,<-
->In which God has given the loving soul<-
->His supreme consolation.<-
->Ah, then the beloved is so well-disposed,<-
->That everything seems good to her<-
->Which causes pain to strange souls.<-
->If you are then irritable, there is great distress,<-
->Unless the devil has anointed you.<-

XVI. Great *love* has more than ten parts and two kinds of
->complaint.<-

Hereafter, great love has its nature: it does not flow with tears, but rather it burns in the great heavenly fire <note>original: "himelfûre"</note>. Therein it flows the furthest, and yet stands within itself the stillest. It climbs closest to God and remains within itself the smallest. It grasps the most and retains the least. O most blessed love, where are those who know you? They are entirely consumed in the Holy Trinity <note>original: "drivaltekeit"</note>; they do not dwell within themselves. These blessed ones can never fall into mortal sin <note>original: "hôptsûnde"</note>. Why? They are so thoroughly flowed-through and embraced by God; the more they are tested,

<summary>This page describes the mystical union between the soul and God through the metaphor of a household and the four characteristics of divine love. It emphasizes a total exchange of self for God and the soul's transformation within the Holy Trinity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the spiritual reflections on the nature of divine love and transitions into a specific visionary narrative regarding a noblewoman at court.</meta>

<page-num>110</page-num>
<header>Part Four.</header>

the stronger they become. Why? The longer they remain here in the struggle and in love <note>original: "miñe" – refers to Minne, the Middle High German concept of mystical, courtly love for God</note>, the more noble God considers them and the more lowly and wretched they consider themselves. Why? The holier the love, the greater the anguish; and the more frequent the consolation, the more constant the fear. But the loving soul cannot feel a horrible fear; rather, she fears nobly. Two things I can never cease to lament: one, that God is so greatly forgotten in the world; the other, that spiritual people are so imperfect. For this reason, many a fall must occur, for perfect people never fell.

## XVII. Of a lady who loved to be at court, and of her devil
->who advised her toward seven wickednesses.<-

->A lady had committed herself to a religious life,<-
->And yet she still wished to serve at court;<-
->Then I prayed for her with all my might,<-
->Both day and night,<-
->For I saw her harm was so great,<-
->That after this life, if she remained there,<-
->She would forever have to be the devil's companion.<-
->She loved her lords and ladies all too much,<-
->And did not hold herself to God’s honor;<-
->Rather, she arranged the useless courtly manners<-
->And had forever before her eyes<-
->The nobility of her lord and her lady.<-

After this there came a great devil—fiery, bloody, black with claws and with horns and glassy eyes—and he went and stood before me. I did not fear him, yet I blessed myself and fell asleep. Then he rolled over me like a skin full of water and tormented me so severely that I sought grace from our Lord. Then a wise angel came to my aid, who was from the fourth choir of angels <note>The Powers, traditionally believed to stop the efforts of demons</note>, and was that same lady’s guardian. I asked him who this enemy was and what he wanted with me. "Ah," spoke the lovely angel with a heavenly voice, "it is one of the most evil devils that hell can afford, and he has the office <note>original: "ambaht" – a formal duty or position</note> of knitting together the hearts of people—who nevertheless wish to be good—with harmful love; and he torments you because you wish to cast him out from this lady." "Ah, shall he torment me for long?" — "No, God wishes to show His goodness in this way." After this the devil came again and shot

<summary>The author reflects on the paradox of mystical love increasing one's humility and fear of God, followed by a vision of a demon tormenting the author for intervening in the soul of a courtly lady.</summary>
<keywords>Minne, humility, courtly life, demonic vision, guardian angel, spiritual struggle, spiritual perfection</keywords>

# Chapters XVII—XVIII.

[The demon] shot at me with fiery arrows, which cast hellish pain behind me into my body and soul. Then I said: "Do to me everything that God permits you." At this, the devil grew weak <note>original: "erwachete," likely meaning he faltered or was startled here</note> and said: "Now that you give yourself so humbly to the pain, I have lost all my power." Then the soul said: "By the living God, I adjure you to tell me your name and what your office is regarding this lady." — "My name? Indeed, I will not tell you that, for it might harm me too greatly. You must wait until the Last Judgment <note>the final day of divine reckoning</note> to know it. I foster in her fierce pride, cunning wisdom, and powerful greed; and I am called <margin>-</margin> Wrathful Bitterness, which disturbs spiritual hearts."

## XVIII. The spiritual person is like an animal in thirty things of its nature.

Thus a troubled soul lamented and spoke miserably to her Beloved: "Alas, Lord, I have long desired two things of which I am not yet granted. The first is a faithful spiritual life. Woe is me, Heart’s-Love, that has all been left undone. The second is a holy end; I look forward to this so greatly that I lose my mournful earnestness." To this our Lord answered and showed me a lowly, insignificant little animal and said: "Look, you are like this tiny creature." Then I saw how the animal was generated on an island in the sea from the slime that rises out of the sea, between the hot sun and the water. Thus the sun was like the animal's father, the sea its mother, and the slime its matter.

In this way, Adam was made by God's power upon the earth from weak matter. This animal signifies true spiritual people. When a person receives a spiritual spirit, he is generated by the hot Godhead and is conceived in his mother, the humanity of God; thus his matter is the Holy Spirit, who destroys his sinful nature in all things. This animal grows toward the true sun. So does the spiritual person who has received God's spirit. That is such a noble seed; it comes and grows until the blessed person's end.

<summary>The text concludes a spiritual battle where humility defeats a demon named Wrathful Bitterness, then introduces an allegory comparing the spiritual soul to a small creature born of sun and sea-slime.</summary>
<keywords>humility, spiritual warfare, Wrathful Bitterness, allegory of the animal, creation of Adam, Holy Spirit, spiritual growth</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical bestiary, providing a spiritual interpretation of a mythical creature's physical traits and behaviors as a model for the soul's journey toward God.</meta>

# Part Four.

This beast eats nothing more; it has a large tail <note>original: "zagel"</note> that is full of honey, which it sucks every day. It also has golden whiskers <note>original: "grañe"</note> that ring out so beautifully as it sucks, so that the sweet voice and the joyful sound nourish its heart, and the body is nourished by the drink of sweet honey. This tail represents the end <note>the final goal or death</note> of holy people, which they keep joyfully and wisely before their eyes through good works and constant virtues, and yet they gladly bear great loyalty through long waiting. The golden whiskers, that is the noble love of God <note>original: "gotzmiñe"</note>, which rings through the loving heart into the noble soul. Blessed is he that was ever born human, who truly experiences this even once.

The beast sometimes has a natural desire to drink from the sea out of a useless thirst; if it does so, it can never recover unless it releases and gives back the bitter seawater. It is the same for us sinners. Whenever we drink the pool of the world and enjoy the ignobility of our flesh according to the counsel of the evil spirit; alas! then we have poisoned ourselves by our own hand. If we then wish to ever recover, we must leave our [sinful] selves behind and give back the debts of the world.

This beast has large ears that stand open toward heaven, and it listens for the song of the birds; it flees the terrifying beasts and fears the earthly snakes. Truly, the loving soul does the same: she constantly flees evil company and she hates false wisdom, and her ears are ready to hear the wisdom of God.

This beast has a noble spirit <note>original: "gemûte"</note>. It cannot remain in the sea when the beasts roar and the water rages. It also loves chastity and runs to the highest mountain it knows, and there chooses the most beautiful tree and climbs upon it with joyful labor, and then embraces the high trunk <note>original: "staîne"; likely referring to the stem or pillar of the tree</note>; and so it rests with great love in high freedom. The loving soul does likewise: vanity is bitter to her and she flees fleetingness <note>original: "zergenglicheit"; the transience of worldly life</note>, which passes away like water. She also knows well how she should run with great virtues and holy labors up to the highest mountain of the beautiful kingdom of heaven. Then she climbs further into grace without labor, onto the most beautiful tree of the holy Godhead; there she embraces the highest pillar and is herself embraced by the Holy Trinity.

<summary>This passage describes a mythical beast whose honey-filled tail and musical whiskers symbolize the spiritual nourishment and divine love found in a virtuous life. The beast's flight from the raging sea to a high mountain tree serves as an allegory for the soul's rejection of worldly vanity in favor of union with the Holy Trinity.</summary>

<keywords>bestiary, allegory, divine love, soul's journey, repentance, asceticism, Holy Trinity, Middle High German mysticism</keywords>

<header>Chapter XVIII.</header>

## Chapter XVIII.

This beast also has two sharp horns, with which it defends its body with such great wisdom that it goes free from all other beasts. O loving soul, do you understand this well? You drive the devil from you with God’s wisdom, and live in holy purity free from all sins.

This beast has two beautiful human eyes, which flow for him full of tears toward the beautiful mountain: for it would gladly be there. O loving soul, how beautiful are the eyes of your understanding <note>original: "bekantnisse" - refers to spiritual cognition or recognition of God</note>, for you have looked into the eternal mirror, and the sweet tears are most lovely and ready for you; yet you gladly suffer the bitterness of the sinful sea.

This beast has a pure mouth and a pure tongue. It also has no teeth; it can neither snarl nor bite. The loving person also has a useful mouth; he teaches and advises <note>original: "beribtet" - to counsel or set right</note> gladly at all times, and his tongue is withdrawn and bound from all harmful words. He also has no biting teeth; he gladly comforts the sorrowful always. He also has no fury, except only against sin and against the insults offered to God; indeed, no pain is so grievous to him. The beast’s mouth is open at the top and small at the bottom. The greatness of our mouth is the incomprehensible praise that we should offer to God in the fellowship of all creatures, in all our doing and in all things, at all hours. The lower part of our mouth speaks all too gladly of the sinful earth. Oh, woe above all speaking! Woe shall betide the false saints who falsely nourish their sinful bodies with the gifts of holy people, and present themselves just as if they had experienced it all in the true truth of God. May the faithful God, who alone has loved the truth, protect His pure friends from them.

The beast has fast feet and has no voice; it is still within itself. The disciplined soul <note>original: "gezogen sele" - a soul characterized by 'Gelassenheit' or courtly restraint</note> has this same nature; in the highest love she is both fast and still.

This beast’s skin and hair are of an ignoble color, for it is pale and wretched to look upon. Also, no one hunts it for its present beauty, but rather after its death; when other beasts rot, then its skin becomes so noble and its hair of such manifold beauty, that all the highest ones who may have it [consider] its skin the noblest.
H. Mechthild. 8

<summary>Chapter XVIII uses the allegory of a mysterious beast to describe the virtues of a "loving soul," emphasizing spiritual wisdom, tearful longing for God, and the transformation of earthly humility into post-mortem glory.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical discussion of a creature (likely the sable or a similar fur-bearing animal) used as a metaphor for the lives of holy people, following the previous section's focus on spiritual virtues.</meta>

<page-num>114</page-num>
<header>Part Four.</header>

...wear sables <note>original: "zobele"; the author uses the sable's precious fur as a metaphor for the enduring legacy of a saint's life</note>. The peace of perfect people, their useful customs, and their holy teachings are, alas, esteemed all too little during their lifetime. But after their death, whenever we sinners fall into distress and then remember how holily they lived and how faithfully they warned us, we are struck by a sinful shame that we were so estranged from them. Then their life becomes a beautiful sable fur, which we sinners carry beautifully in our hearts before our eyes. But during their blessed life, we always feared the "un-given" copper <note>likely a metaphor for base, worldly concerns or the "harshness" of discipline</note>, because we could not touch this noble gold.

The flesh of this animal is eaten on Friday. It also does not die unless it is struck dead by the enemies of the sea. The lives of holy people are entirely a "Friday," for they fast entirely from sins; they do not eat forbidden food, but rather live according to a divine manner. The great billows of the storming Love <note>original: "mîne"; Middle High German *minne*, referring to mystical or divine love</note> cause them to die to all things and live for God alone; indeed, then for the first time all things belong to them alone, held in common with God in Love. Thus, their Love has useful power in the praise of God for all things.

The bone of this animal is the spine of a noble fish, from which beautiful jewels are made, which noble people wear for their honor. How noble a jewel it is when a holy body is full of Love and free from sin! God shows this to us in His dearest friends, as we find through true signs <note>referring to miracles or the "signs" of sanctity</note>. God has given us many useful jewels in His holy friends; if we do not praise Him for this, we cannot become one with the saints who are raised up here from the earth. The name of this animal, translated into German, means "entirely useful." Blessed is he who was ever born a human being who holds this name before God!

### XIX. *The office of blessed Love is manifold.*

O blessed Love, this was without beginning
Your office, and it is so still:
That you bind God and the soul of man together;
This shall be your office without end.
Greetings to you, my Lady,
And see to it that I do not complain
To my beautiful Lord about you.
If He wishes to be away from me too long,
I shall freeze too severely;
Guard against that, Lady of my heart, O Queen!

## Chapters XIX—XX.

You have led me into God,
Where I am soulfully bound.
O my Lady <note>The author addresses "Lady Love" (Minne) in the tradition of courtly love adapted for mysticism.</note>, help me now,
That I may pass away in His arms,
Where I am embraced by Him;
Yet I will gladly suffer the pain of death
In this sinful body of mine.
Love, you have the greatest power
Above all virtues forevermore,
For this I will thank God,
You take away many of my heart-sores.
I have no virtue anymore,
He serves with His own virtues.
It would be heavier to me than death,
If I could do any good without my Lord.
All that I speak of Love,
I dare not, alas, claim for myself,
But God means all those by it,
Who are chosen in His heart. <margin>Manuscript: are.</margin>
Those whom this concerns will find it true;
Love makes empty hearts full.
But when we become full of resentment and bitterness,
Then the play of Love is quite unready for us.
Good night, Love, as I wish to sleep. Hallelujah <note>original: "Alleluia"</note>.

### XX. Of the six virtues of Saint Dominic.

On Saint Dominic’s day, I prayed to our Lord for the community of the Order of Preachers <note>The Dominicans</note>. Then our dear Lord deigned to come to me Himself and brought Saint Dominic, whom I love above all saints. I asked if I dared to speak, and our Lord said: "Dominic, my son, had four things within him while on earth, which all Priors <note>The heads of a religious house</note> should have in them. First, he held his brothers in such loving affection that he could never endure troubling them with matters that arose from his own self-will. The second: that he often made his own food poorer <note>original: "bösorte" - to lessen the quality of food as an act of asceticism or solidarity</note> to help and show love to his brothers, so that the young brothers would not think of returning to the secular world, and that the old would not succumb on the way. The third: that with holy wisdom he gave them the example that they should, for God’s sake, be moderate in all their being, in all their customs, and in all their necessities. The fourth: that he was so merciful that he..."

### XXI. *For sixteen things God loves the Order of Preachers.*

After this, our dear Lord spoke thus: Two things I love so greatly in the Order of Preachers <term>Bredier orden: The Dominican Order, known for their mission of preaching</term> that my divine heart smiles upon them without ceasing. The first is the holiness of their lives. The second is the great benefit they bring to Holy Christendom. Furthermore, they greet <margin><unclear>the seven</unclear></margin> my Holy Trinity with seven things, which they speak thus: Christ-like sighing, heartfelt weeping, living longing, hard discipline <note>original: "twang" - refers to ascetic restraint or physical penance</note>, burdensome exile <note>original: "ellende" - often used to describe the soul's earthly journey away from its true home in heaven</note>, faithful humility, and joyful love. Moreover, our Lord said: They also honor my <margin><unclear>the seven things</unclear></margin> three names with seven outward things: in praiseworthy song, with true preaching, with righteous absolution <note>original: "losunge" - the loosening of sins through confession</note>, with lovely consolation, with friendly help, with a holy example <note>original: "bilde" - literally 'image' or 'form', referring to their conduct as a model for others</note>, and they are also a healing <margin><unclear>the seven things</unclear></margin> bond of the Holy Christian faith. Furthermore, our dearly beloved Lord spoke thus: The alms <term>almûsen: charitable gifts given to the poor</term> which they give to the poor for the sake of my love are so holy that the sins of the poor people who receive them are diminished, and also that the devil can remain nowhere near where their alms are eaten. This comes from the holiness of their pleasing poverty. O, eternal fountain of the Godhead, from which I and all things have flowed forth, I, an unworthy creature, praise you with everything that is beneath you, that I, Lord, have been thus comforted by <margin><unclear>the seven things of God</unclear></margin> you. Amen. <margin><unclear>the seven things of God</unclear></margin>

## XXIII. Concerning the Burial of Saint John the Evangelist.

I have seen the body of Saint John the Evangelist
truly with the eyes of my unworthy soul. He lies in great
whiteness <note>original: "wûße"</note> and buried above all perishable things and the
creations of the eternal kingdom. His body has now received
so much of divine eternity that it shines like a fiery crystal.
He lies there created in such lovely human form, as if he had
spiritually fallen asleep in a heavenly jubilee. His eyebrows
alone are brown, and he has his eyes closed and lies upon his back.
Under him, out of him, and all around him, everything is clear, and every
seven hours the holy angels come to the body with
praiseworthy song, which sounds thus: holy, pure, simple, wise, to God

Between his body and the creation of the kingdom of heaven there is nothing more than a thin wall, like the membrane of an egg <note>original: "eies hût"; a common medieval metaphor for the fragility and thinness of the veil between worlds</note>, and yet it is as eternally firm as that, so that no body may pass through it until the Final Judgment Day.

### XXIV. How God receives the souls in the kingdom of heaven and how He crowns three kinds of people, and how He greets, adorns, praises, and
->thanks them.<-

The kingdom of heaven has many beautiful gates and yet has none. The "manifold gates" are the magnificent, distinct rewards with which God receives each soul, and the whole of heaven opens itself toward the blissful bride of God <note>The "bride of God" refers to the individual soul in the tradition of bridal mysticism</note>. God comes down through all the choirs <note>The nine choirs of angels</note> to meet the soul, and all the heavenly host follows Him, all in such beauty as she is able to receive as her reward. When the soul departs joyfully <note>The manuscript reads "vart" (departs/travels), though the scribe originally wrote "wart"</note> from purgatory or from this earthly misery <note>original: "elende"; in Middle High German, this specifically refers to being "out of one's land" or in exile, meaning earthly life</note>, many beautiful angels also follow her. In the gate of heaven, the two lovers come together: God and the soul. His noble countenance, with which He receives her, has such great power over her that she can never again think of her injuries nor of any of her heart’s sorrow.

A common crown of the kingdom is placed upon her head at the gate, which is God’s will: with this, He leads her in honorably. Therefore, it is called the crown of the kingdom. To the ruined sinner who remains so until his end, but to whom God sends repentance, no other dignity is given as a reward. God crowns three kinds of people with His fatherly hands: virgins, widows, and married people <term>Elûte: married couples or those living in the state of matrimony</term>. When He has received them with all praise, He then crowns them. Our Lord crowns the widows and those in marriage while sitting in His almighty glory; but for the virgins, He stands up and crowns them standing, like an imperial young lord. He greets them inwardly with His living divinity, He honors them outwardly with His almighty humanity, and He adorns them with the generosity of His Holy Spirit. He also rewards them without end with His entire Trinity, according to their order in His kingdom, for everything that they bring in with them. He thanks them all individually—

<summary>This chapter describes the soul's entry into heaven, where God meets the soul as a lover and crowns different classes of the faithful—virgins, widows, and the married—with varying degrees of celestial ceremony.</summary>

# Part Four

their own dwellings. They are guests in every city and suffer many hardships. They have neither house nor estate, nor silver nor gold kept anywhere. Each of them walks with a staff that is colored white and red. The staff has a cross-piece <note>original: "kruken" - a crutch-shaped handle</note> that is a span long and made of ivory. By the ivory, they are meant to be chaste and pure in all things. The staff is white and red; by this, they remember Christ’s death. On one side of the staff, the martyrdom of our Lord is carved; on the other, His ascension. They must have this staff with them in all places, whether they eat or sleep, pray or preach, sing Mass or hear confession; and wherever they let the staff out of their hands, they must thrust it into the earth before their eyes, so that they may constantly behold Christ’s martyrdom.

Whenever their journey is thirty miles long, where they must go out of necessity or need, two of them must lead a donkey with them. When they occasionally ride, they cannot carry their staff at their side, but rather they must carry it upright in their hand before them like a crucifix <note>original: "gottescrüze" - God's cross</note>. For this reason, they must ride the lowly beast, so that they liken themselves to God in humility, and also because their feet become so sore that they cannot complete the distance. However, those who wear shoes may not wear them longer than from All Saints' Day until the feast of St. Peter when he became Pope <note>February 22nd, the Feast of the Chair of St. Peter</note>.

They shall ask no one for books or for clothing; however, if bread is not offered to them, they shall ask for it humbly. They shall eat and drink with the common people whatever food is given to them, except for meat alone. They shall also not fast more than the Christian law commands, and they shall take lodging in such a way that they may pray and sleep apart from the people under a separate roof.

<margin>.</margin> When the people recognize and observe this holy life, they are so greatly improved by it that they gladly and with great love give them what is necessary for their needs. They shall also not lodge with any widow. The people shall wash their sore feet with great devotion and shall thank God fervently that they walk there and anoint the orphaned Christendom, just as Mary Magdalene did for our Lord. They also anoint them afterwards; these shall [be] men's names...
