# Chapter XXVII.

...to do <note>original: "tùn"</note>, since they are not gods. When people see that their clothes are too worn out <note>literally "sick" or "weak"</note>, they give them new ones. Many would gladly give them much; but they should not accept it; rather, they mercifully advise giving to all useful places.

Their great chapter <note>a formal assembly of the members of a religious order</note> is held twice a year for the benefit and necessity of Christendom: in summer in the forest, in winter in the city at the citizens' town hall. Whoever wishes to enter this order must himself have two kinds of books: From the largest book he shall preach. The first thing written in that book says: "I believe in God" <note>original Latin: "Credo in deum"</note>, and after that it is all masterly sermons, all arranged according to the Christian faith. From the smallest book he shall perform his hours <note>the Liturgy of the Hours or daily prayers</note> of the year for our Lord. 

The first master who shall establish this life <note>this way of living</note> shall be the son of the King of Rome. His name before God means "Alleluia" in German. To him the Pope shall give his closest authority, and thereafter he himself chooses and receives this life from the Pope. Then all high masters join with him; they shall not be younger than twenty-four years of age. They also receive no one unless he is healthy and has studied at a high school <note><term>high school: a university or stadium generale</term></note>, and they must all be priests, confessors, and highly chosen teachers. 

The first master they shall call their Prince, and he shall travel as one of four brothers <note>accompanied by three others</note>, for the Christian faith is most often tested in him; and every fourteen [brothers] shall have a master among them whom they shall call their Guardian, and he shall travel as one of three brothers. Their authority is very great, for no Bishop is their equal. Wherever they come, preaching, hearing confessions, and singing or reading Mass is permitted to them without prohibition. In every diocese there shall be seven of them, according to the seven gifts of the Holy Spirit; in an archdiocese thirteen, according to the holy assembly <note>the convent or gathering of the Apostles</note> of our Lord. In Rome there shall be thirty of them, according to the blessed price <note>the thirty pieces of silver</note> that was given for Christ. In Jerusalem there shall be the most of all, where Jesus suffered death for us.

Their lesser chapter they shall hold every three weeks, according to the complete union of the Holy Trinity, with five brothers according to the image of the holy five wounds [of Christ], or with seven, according to the seven gifts of the Holy Spirit, and thereafter more as they [come] together...

Wherever they eat or drink, the eldest in the order shall meanwhile speak something concerning **Christ’s life and conduct** <note>original: "cristi wandelunge"; refers to the earthly ministry and behavior of Jesus as a model for the friars</note> and concerning His holy life, and the others shall remain silent.

I also saw their beds, how they are to lie upon straw between two white **woolen cloths** <note>original: "wollentüchern"</note>, and a pillow is given to them under the head, which shall lie upon the lower cloth on the straw. Their loins shall never sit nor lie softly, for they are to remain healthy all their days until the holy martyrdom, just as Christ did. However, any old master who has been very useful and, due to old age, cannot fully endure the hardships until the end of the order—whether he becomes weak or sick—he shall be laid softly and held dear, for such men can still give very holy counsel; and they shall then live upon the best food.

This holy life shall stand in good peace for thirty years; during that time, they shall so greatly enlighten and teach Christendom that no one, out of unlearned simplicity, need turn away from the Christian faith. Alas, then the time of distress shall begin. Then the **Antichrist** <note>original: "endecrist"</note> comes and wins over the worldly princes with gold and with the noblest precious stones and with bottomless, false cunning, which they now love so much. Therefore, they follow him very gladly and say he is their God and their Lord, and they give him great escort, their seals, and their letters. Alas, then he comes to spiritual power; there he also finds greed and brings such great false wisdom that the **Bishops** and **Provosts** <note>original: "Brôpsten"; high-ranking administrative clergy</note> and the priests stand as far too small. Because these holy brothers have dealt so secretly with the people, many a holy martyr shall be made along with them. Many a Jew and such wise heathens shall receive the holy **baptism** <note>original: "tôf"</note> and the **Christian faith** from these brothers. This shall so greatly insult the Antichrist that he shall lay his great command and heavy compulsion upon all those who go to their preaching. He who then goes there and stands with them is a blessed man.

They take the blessed preacher, thus dead, and set him up very high in that same place where he spoke the word of God <note>original: "gotzwort"</note> and was martyred.

Those who wish to preach the Christian faith <note>original: "Cristangelöben"</note> after this must be living martyrs and high saints. The power of the Antichrist is so great that no one is his equal. Then Enoch and Elijah <note>original: "Enoch und Helyas"</note> come to their aid, who are now in the sweet Paradise, and live there in soul and body in that same joy, and eat the same food that was given to Adam, had he remained therein. They must also, in obedience to God, avoid that same tree from which Eve and Adam ate the apple when they broke God’s commandment. I have seen this tree; it is not large and its fruit is outwardly very beautiful and lovely as a rose, but inwardly it is by nature very sour. This signifies the bitter harm of sin, which God never wished for mankind. Because this fruit is so unsuited to noble humanity—for it is still our poison—God placed His commandment upon it, for He never created any hardship for mankind.

# Part Four

In the final time of distress, when these blessed brothers <note>likely referring to the mendicant orders or specific holy figures mentioned previously</note> have comforted the common people for so long that no good person remains who has not suffered martyrdom for God's sake, these brothers shall still be living for the most part. Their innocent suffering will be so great and their prayer so holy that God will then first send them Enoch and Elijah <note>original: "Enoch und Helyam"; two biblical figures traditionally expected to return to earth to combat the Antichrist</note>. These two shall comfort them and lead them out of the forest, and they shall go forth again to preach and prepare themselves for death. These two lords, who come out of Paradise, are so wise in divine truth that they shall overwhelm the Antichrist <note>original: "Endecrist"</note> with power. They shall tell him exactly who he is, by what power his signs are performed, where he came from, and what kind of end he shall meet. When the wayward people hear this—how wretched a god has been given to them because of their great greed and their lust for many evils which God knows are in their hearts—then many a noble man and many a beautiful lady, who had followed the Antichrist away from the Christians, shall be converted.

Then the blessed must be martyred, for the greatest power on earth <note>original: "ertriche"</note> is then given to the Antichrist. He commands that all men be gathered whom he can find believing in Christ. Then, boiling cauldrons <note>original: "pfauen"; likely a dialect variant of "Pfannen" or "Pfauen" used here to mean large pans or vats</note> are prepared upon the streets, and the men are driven toward them all at once; then he sends for their wives and their beautiful children. Then the men are commanded to choose: whether they would rather remain in unbelief and keep their beautiful wives, their dear children, wealth, and honor, or whether they will believe in Christ, be boiled in the cauldrons, and lose their loved ones. Then the men say: "O, dear wives and children, do not think of me, but rather remember that you are Christians and offer up one body to God, so that we shall not be parted." Then the men's hands and feet are bound and they are thrown into the cauldrons. Then the women and children also say: "Lord Jesus, O Son of Mary, for your love we will gladly suffer this same distress." Then a pit full of fire is made, into which the children and those who are mothers are thrown; and fire, wood, and straw are thrown upon them, and they are burned in this way.

The angel leads Enoch and Elijah out of Paradise. The radiance and the joy that they now have in their bodies must all remain behind there. When they look upon the earth, they are terrified—

### Chapter XXVIII.

## XXVIII. Concerning the fivefold power of Love. Because of the weakness and the falsehood of people, one must remain silent about the truth.

This book was begun in Love <note>original: "miñe" - a Middle High German term for divine and courtly love</note>, and it shall also end in Love; for there is nothing so wise, nor so holy, nor so beautiful, nor so strong, nor so perfect as Love. Then our Lord Jesus Christ spoke: "Speak, Father! I will now be silent as You are silent in the mouth of Your Son, burning with fervor because of the weakness of the people; and my humanity spoke all-trembling because of the falsehood of the world, for it rewarded me with a bitter death."

## This is the fifth part of the book.
---
*I. Concerning three kinds of repentance and ten types of benefit, and concerning the path of the angels and the devils.*

There are three kinds of repentance <note>original: "rûwe," meaning contrition or remorse</note> with which the sinner moves back toward the seal that was carved upon the cross, since our sins have broken us. The first is the repentance of guilt, which has three qualities within it: bitterness in the heart from which the sin flowed, shame in the senses that partook in the sin, and a good example of living where the person had previously become wicked. This repentance reconciles the Heavenly Father and the sinful soul and releases it from the eternal pain of hell. The second is the repentance of penance <note>original: "bûsse," referring to the outward acts of atonement</note>; this also has three qualities within it: diligent labor, constant security, and pure victory over all temptation. This repentance releases the sinner from all purgatory <note>original: "vegfûr"</note>. The third is the repentance of love <note>original: "mine," specifically mystical or divine love</note>, for it is faithful to God alone. To this soul, God’s dishonor is far more grievous than its own harm or heartache. It would even rather go with body and soul to eternal hell than to grieve its Beloved with a single mortal sin <note>original: "hôptsûnde"</note>. This love-repentance sanctifies and makes people perfect here on earth and exalts them in the kingdom of heaven before God. Whenever the blessed soul stands in this state, God is dearer to it than itself, and sin is its highest sorrow. The blessed person who possesses these three repentances receives such honor here on earth, that

# Chapter I.

The reflected gaze of God and the loving soul, which shines so blissfully from them both, has such great power and such manifest radiance,

->Before all those who are in the kingdom of heaven,<-
->In purgatory <note>original: "vegefür"</note>, and in hell.<-
->So that the highest angels, Cherubim and Seraphim,<-
->Must be intimate with the loving soul<-
->And wander down here, burning in countless love,<-
->To the loving, fiery soul in that same radiance.<-

This is the path of the noble princes <note>referring to the high-ranking angels</note> to the pampered soul in this poor body; for the angel and the love of the soul are of one complete nature from God, by inborn purity and by the fire of love in the Seraphim. But the adopted purity, adorned and illuminated with the flowing fire of divine love, remains in the Cherubim. In *them*, however, a fiery, clear delight of love <note>original: "miñelust"</note> descends from the Seraphim, for they are fiery in love.

->Therefore the noble radiance draws downward,<-
->So that they gaze back out of love.<-
->The angels who are given to us at baptism<-
->Cannot practice this burning love,<-
->For God has not given them that heat;<-
->Rather, they are given to us for this purpose:<-
->That they may tend to our virtues. —<-

Their noble cooperation and our best intentions <note>original: "mûtwillen"</note> sanctify all our works and drive away the devil's cunning and his power from our five senses. But the great fiery radiance, which descends all-illuminating from the Holy Trinity into the loving soul—the devils fear this so greatly that they never dare to travel through those holy rays. Because of this, they suffer many insults on the paths that God has given them in the air, because an earthly human being can take those paths away from them through union with God <note>original: "gotzeinunge"</note>. They may complete all their journeys which they desire out of malice, but wherever they become aware of a loving soul in a body, there they must go beneath the earth. Also, they cannot defile the air where they find the blessed who live truly without mortal sin <note>original: "höptsünde"</note>. All the sins that they bring upon us,

### *II. Concerning two kinds of pain and four kinds of benefit, and concerning the manifold host of sins.*

I thank God for all goodness, and I lament over myself all the days that I live, for God does not inflict pain <term>pain: "pine" in the original, referring to both physical suffering and spiritual penance</term> in vain. As long as a person is capable of sinning, they have as much need of pain as they do of virtues. The pain that a person takes upon themselves for God’s sake through wise counsel is very useful. However, the pain that God lays upon us through His enemies or through His friends is as much more useful and noble as God is nobler than all pains. Christ did not redeem us with the pain that He took upon Himself; rather, He taught us how we should serve Him with labor and with pain. But He redeemed us with the pain that His enemies laid upon Him without guilt, and with that miserable, shameful end, where no one was His faithful friend except for one maiden alone. Mary, His mother, who was truly united with Him inwardly, stood alone outwardly with Him.

When I, an unfaithful person, grew weary of my pain, God gave me this comfort and said: "Now see, no one can do without pain, for it purifies a person from hour to hour from their manifold sins." Oh alas, then I saw following us such a great, gruesome host of manifold sins, as if all mountains, all stones, all raindrops, all grass, trees, leaves, and sand were all living persons and wished to crush us so that we might never come up to God. Oh alas for the sorrowful "dust-sins" <note>original: "stůbsůnden"; likely referring to venial or "small" sins that accumulate like dust</note> that we cannot even put into words! Against these, pain is given to us here, which we carry secretly in our poor bodies. Secondly, the bitterness of pain shields us from a future fall, at which a pure heart—which carries God's spirit locked within it—often trembles. Thirdly, the nobility of pain makes us worthy of receiving the grace of God; for when I receive all my comfort, my necessities, and all my earthly consolation with anxiety and with fear and with a wretched heart, then God is there with His comfort.

<summary>The author reflects on the spiritual necessity of suffering, distinguishing between self-imposed penance and the more "noble" suffering sent by God, while illustrating the overwhelming nature of small sins.</summary>
<keywords>suffering, penance, Christ's Passion, Virgin Mary, purification, venial sins, divine grace, spiritual comfort</keywords>

--- Page 163 ---
### III. God will weigh all innocent pain and also the blood of three kinds of people.

On the Last Judgment day, Christ Jesus will hold up a magnificent scale before his Father. Upon it shall lie his holy labor and his innocent pain, and therein and alongside it all the innocent pain, mockery, and heartache that was ever suffered by humans for the love of Christ. Indeed, when it comes to the true scale, those who have much therein will rejoice the most. The blood of virgins by nature, the blood of martyrs for the Christian faith, and the blood of other human slaughter <note>original: "manschlahtige blût," referring to those killed unjustly</note> that happens without guilt in true distress—the holy Son of God will weigh these with his own blood, for it was given out in true innocence. Does the blood of the guilty <note>original: "rehte blût," here implying blood shed for one's own crimes</note> come into the scale? Why not? It is already defiled, yet it may still extinguish those same sins that arise from the knowledge of the flesh.

### IV. The wondrous love has manifold power. How it tastes. Of fourfold humility. Of the sevenfold beauty of the loving soul.

> O wondrous love of God,
> You have holy, great power,
> You enlighten the soul and teach the senses
> And give full might to all virtues.
> Woe is me, a poor peasant woman, <note>original: "dörperiñe," a term of self-deprecation meaning a simple or unrefined person</note>
> That I ever saw you, my joy.
> Ah, Love, you are blissful
> And praiseworthy in all works,
> This I experience in my soul;
> All virtues are subject to you.
> But sinking humility,
> Which is not undercut by pride in spirituality,
> And innate chastity
> Or that which is adopted, which stands equally pure—
> These two virtues must walk with Love,
> Yet they remain subject to her.

Love wanders through the senses and storms the soul with complete virtues. While love grows in the soul, she climbs with longing up to God and spreads herself, flowing out toward the wonder that is granted to her. She melts herself through the soul

# Part Five

into the senses; so must the body also have its share, so that it is drawn along in all things. <note>The author argues that spiritual ecstasy is not limited to the soul but physically affects the human body as well.</note>

Whether one can possess evil habits alongside the love of God <term>minne; a Middle High German term for a deep, spiritual, and often ecstatic love</term>, that I can find nowhere, so great is the power of unadulterated divine love. Moreover, the soul is never so thoroughly permeated with divine love that it is not frequently tempted by earthly things. But the soul that is permeated with false love cannot receive this <insert>divine love</insert>. When love has grown to its fullness in the soul, it has also climbed to the highest point, as far as is possible for a human being, for love has a measure in its order. If it had no measure—O sweet God—how many pure hearts would break in sweet joy <term>wunne; intense, blissful delight</term>!

When the soul, drawn by love and with the many longings of its hunting heart, has come after God onto the high mountain of powerful love and beautiful knowledge, it acts like the pilgrim who has climbed mountains with great desire: he then descends on the other side with great fear, lest he stumble and fall. This means that when the soul is so thoroughly illuminated in the heat of long-lasting love, and has become so powerless in the embrace of the Holy Trinity, it begins to sink and to cool.

->As the sweetness goes down from the highest place<-
->And sinks until it reaches the night.<-
->God knows, so it happens to the soul<-
->And is also fulfilled in the body.<-

The love-filled soul sinks down in the pull of bottomless humility and ever gives way. Whatever God does for her out of love is very pleasing to her because of the noble nature that God and she fulfill in one single intention. But she turns the eye of delight away from all things, so that she may win much praise for God.

The body also sinks very low when it serves its enemy <note>The "enemy" here refers to the physical appetites or the fallen nature of the flesh</note> and remains silent, and avoids its friends to honor God. The soul sinks even further because it has more power than the body; it sinks with great diligence into the lowest place that God has in His power. Oh, how do I dare name this place to those <margin>The manuscript reads: "to the" (dem) instead of "those" (den).</margin> who do not recognize sinking humility?

<meta>This page continues the discussion of spiritual growth, specifically focusing on the stages of humility as a path to divine love. It follows the previous descriptions of the soul's internal journey.</meta>

<page-num>133</page-num>
<header>Chapter IV.</header>

### Chapter IV.

The first humility lies outwardly in clothing and in one's dwelling, that they be modest and spiritually cut and sewn, and yet clean. The second lies in the manners within company, that they be loving in all needs and in all things. From this grows holy love-of-God. The third humility lies in the senses, so that one loves all things according to their right use and order. The fourth humility dwells in the soul; this is the "sinking humility" <note>German: *sinkende diemūtikēit*. A central concept in Rhineland mysticism where the soul finds God by sinking into its own nothingness.</note>, which performs so many sweet wonders in the love-filled soul. It hunts the soul up into heaven and draws it down into this abyss. It leads the soul to all creatures individually and says: "Now look, all this is better than you are!" and then brings the soul to its place, beyond which it cannot go, which is beneath Lucifer's tail <note>A metaphorical expression for the lowest possible state of self-abasement, placing oneself even below the fallen angels.</note>. If the soul could then remain there in longing according to its will for the honor of God, it would desire nothing further.
    Thus greatly is the poor, love-filled soul bound with humble love, so that it neither fears nor feels shame except in a restrained manner, as one is accustomed to fear in the kingdom of heaven. But the poor body must fear and feel shame both before the darkness of its heart and the weakness of its outward senses, for it is not yet transformed by death. But the soul is as beautiful in its body as in the kingdom of heaven, though it is not as certain. It is just as bold, but not as strong. It is just as powerful, but not as steady. It is just as loving, but not as joyful. It is just as generous, but not as rich. It is just as holy, but not as innocent. It is just as sufficient, but not full. This is only the soul that is here flowed-through with humility for the love of God.
    When it has thus ascended into the highest that may happen to it while it is harnessed to its body, and has sunk down into the deepest it can find, then it is fully grown in virtue and in holiness. Then it must be adorned with pain in the long waiting.

->Then she goes to stand upon faithfulness<-
->And looks upon all things with great wisdom,<-
->So that nothing may escape her,<-
->Lest she win for God His praise therein.<-

<summary>This chapter outlines four levels of humility, culminating in the "sinking humility" that allows the soul to experience both heavenly heights and the lowest depths of self-abasement. It describes the tension between the soul's spiritual perfection and the body's earthly limitations during the wait for the afterlife.</summary>
<keywords>humility, sinking humility, divine love, soul and body, spiritual growth, self-abasement, virtue, faithfulness</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the spiritual reflections and visions of the author, focusing on the dangers of spiritual pride and the necessity of obedience to religious counsel.</meta>

<page-num>134</page-num>
# Part Five

V. *Of a Beguine <note>A member of a lay religious community of women who lived lives of poverty and devotion without taking formal vows.</note> in purgatory, whom no prayer helped because of her own self-will.*

O woe to sin, you who are so harmful, since even holy works are so damaging when they are done without counsel <note>original: "ane rât"; referring to the guidance of a spiritual director or confessor.</note>; as when one says: "No, I am above the advice of men. I will live according to God's counsel alone." I shudder at these words whenever I hear them. For a person can nowhere humble themselves so truly and usefully as when they follow Christian counsel with a submissive heart.

I found this to be true in the case of a certain Lady: she loved our Lord from her heart, and she practiced that love with such superhuman labor that her nature withered so severely that she had to die. Then I prayed for her according to Christian custom. In the drawing up of my spirit, I saw her spirit; it was clear in itself as the sun. This she possessed from her pure heart and faithful intention. Yet she was surrounded by a great darkness and longed intensely for the eternal light. She was in a state of ascent, yet the dark night was always before her. This was the "self-will without counsel" which had so greatly hindered this perfect person.

I asked her: "How can one help you?" She answered thus: "On earth, I would follow no person's counsel according to the Christian order; therefore, no person's prayer or longing can help me now." Then I turned to our dear Lord and asked Him how it could be such a misery that a person should come to such pain, who here on earth had taken on such holy suffering for the sake of His love. Then our Lord spoke: "All virtues are unpleasing to me which are performed without counsel; for I came to earth with counsel, and I served on earth with great submission to my Father and to all people, and then I ascended to heaven in total freedom; but because I never acted alone <note>original: "das ich nie getet"; implying He never acted out of mere self-will.</note>, no one followed me in that [self-will]. The longings, prayers, and all the labors that are performed here for her will serve to adorn her when she departs for heaven." The soul: "For everything that is given to us on the way to the kingdom of heaven is rightfully ours. But when we arrive there, it becomes the common property..."

<summary>The author recounts a vision of a devout Beguine trapped in the darkness of purgatory because she relied on her own "self-will" rather than seeking spiritual guidance. It emphasizes that even holy acts are displeasing to God if performed with spiritual pride and a refusal to submit to human counsel.</summary>

<keywords>Beguine, Purgatory, Self-will, Spiritual Counsel, Obedience, Humility, Vision, Divine Love</keywords>

souls. May God do this for us out of love, so that they may come to us all the sooner and help us praise God in eternal glory. The justice of her suffering <note>The author is likely referring to a specific holy woman or the personified Soul</note> should have lasted seventeen years; but the mercy of God reduced it to seventeen months, because she performed it out of such heartfelt love. May God help us to find the right measure. Amen.

### *VI. How the soul praises the Holy Trinity.*

Lord Jesus Christ, you who have flowed without beginning from the heart of your eternal Father spiritually, and were born of a pure, intact virgin, Saint Mary, physically, and you who are one spirit, one will, one wisdom, one power, and one supreme strength with your Father over everything that ever was, without end! Lord, eternal Father, since I, the most unworthy of all people, have also flowed spiritually from your heart, and I, Lord Jesus Christ, was born physically from your side <note>A reference to the medieval devotion where the Church or the soul is born from the wound in Christ's side</note>, and I, Lord, God and Man, have been purified by the Spirit of you both—so I, a poor, troubled human, speak thus: Lord heavenly Father, you are my heart! Lord Jesus Christ, you are my body! Lord Holy Spirit, you are my breath! Lord, Holy Trinity <term>drivaltekeit: the Three-in-One</term>, you are my only refuge and my eternal rest.

### *VII. How God praises the soul in return.*

You are a foundation <term>gruntvestunge: a firm base or bedrock</term> of my divine flesh, you are an honor to virginal constancy <term>bestandunge: steadfastness or perseverance</term>, you are a flower of high joy <term>wûne: bliss or delight</term>, you are a conqueror <term>vögtin: literally a female advocate or overseer; here, one who has power over</term> of devils, you are a mirror of eternal contemplation <term>anschowunge: the act of looking upon God</term>.

### *VIII. A person should have three children for whom they should pray.*

No one knows what comfort or pain <term>pine: torment</term> or longing <term>gerunge: intense desire</term> is, unless they are first touched by these three themselves. I seek help, for I am, alas, in too much pain <term>alzewe: a state of great woe</term>. I have three children in whom I see great sorrow <term>jamer: misery or lamentation</term>. The first are the poor sinners who lie in eternal death; there is no more comfort for them than that they still possess a human body. Oh woe, I look upon this child with a bleeding <term>blûtigem</term> heart and I embrace <term>miñe: to love or hold dear</term> it with weeping eyes <term>ôgen</term> in the arms of my soul, and carry it before...

When I look upon all my children, I am in no such pain as I am for this one alone; for it has, alas, with outward senses in transitory things, separated itself so far and so greatly from heavenly things that it has lost all the noble habit and the sweet divine intimacy into which God had drawn it with special election. Hereafter they become so greatly perverted that no one can change them with words; so...

<page-num>137</page-num>
<header>Chapters VIII—IX.</header>

They scold true inwardness <note>original: "iñekeit"; refers to deep, heartfelt devotion</note> and pervert God’s sweetness, and they also consider everything they see and hear to be a danger. Thus they appear wise on the outside, and yet, alas, they are all fools on the inside. This child <note>The "child" here represents the soul or the novice in faith</note> can only recover with the greatest difficulty, for it falls first into willful conflict, then into sloth, then into false comfort, then into despair, and thereafter it becomes, alas, devoid of all grace. Then this poor child wanders wildly in a sinful life until its end; thus it is a very great risk where the neglected soul may go.

## *IX. Concerning the honor of the seventy men who stood with Christ as witnesses.*

On the exalted Easter day, when our redemption was so greatly revealed, when Jesus Christ arose so powerfully and so honorably vacated his grave, so that the Jews and the heathens lost their manly strength and all their honor, and the true Christian people were blessed by the Father’s will and were blessed by the Son’s power and were hallowed by the Holy Spirit’s teaching forevermore—

Then there arose with our Lord seventy men,
Who had been so subject to God’s commands,
When they had fought the battle of God—
Then they were found to be righteous people.
When they were tested,
When they, in their great thirst,
Threw the water to their mouths with the heathens. <note>This likely refers to the biblical test of Gideon's men (Judges 7), here typologically linked to the witnesses of the Resurrection.</note>

Their souls were given back into their bodies by God, so that one could well perceive that they had been dead people. But the sinful human sap <note>original: "ſaf"; refers to the corruptible essence or "humors" of mortality</note> that Adam bit from the apple, which still naturally permeates all our limbs, and additionally the cursed blood that arose for Eve and all women from the apple—that was not given back to them, because their transformation was to be divine, a witness with God that eternal death was dead. Therefore they died no more, since they did not have these two things within them. Then their souls separated from their bodies without pain and without woe. Their bodies lie beautifully above the air and above the stars. Because they did not die a second time in a human way, their bodies could no longer be committed to the earth. Adam retained that sap within him, and all men after him.

<summary>This page warns against the spiritual decline into sloth and despair before recounting the legend of seventy righteous men who rose with Christ, possessing bodies purified of Adam's original sin.</summary>
<keywords>inwardness, spiritual sloth, Resurrection, seventy witnesses, original sin, Adam and Eve, immortality, redemption</keywords>

# Part Five

Eve and all women retained this most shameful blood <note>original: "blût"; likely referring to the physical corruption or mortality inherited from the Fall</note>. This is the only thing that naturally compels <note>original: "pinget"</note> our flesh and our senses, and at last must die bitterly within us; for Jesus Christ, after Adam's fall, did not take away all our pain, except for eternal death and the fact that we may return through repentance. However, He has given us many comforts, counsels, and teachings, through which we may well look upon all our infirmities.

## X. *How sin is equal to God's greatness.*

To the greatness of Almighty God, no greatness is so equal as the sinful greatness of my wickedness.

## XI. *The spiritual name shall be exalted. Of the sisters of Zeal.*
*How they should pray and petition with God.*

O spiritual name, how noble you are above all earthly names! Therefore Jesus Christ himself wished to bear you so faithfully throughout his life, so that all high names—kings, emperors, counts, and all names that are called noble after them—must all wither away. Only the spiritual name alone shall be exalted, according to how nobly it was borne here. Yes, it shall be wonderfully, specially, and secretly exalted, beside the brotherhood of Jesus and the sisterhood of Mary, who were the very first who ever bore a spiritual name amidst such great outward damnation.

This is very much against those people who adorn themselves so greatly here outwardly in a spiritual manner, with such holy bearing <note>original: "gelasse"</note> and with such deep bowing, and hang themselves before the public with beautiful words, so that one might rightly think they have the flood of the Holy Spirit within them, driving it all forth. No, it is sometimes, alas, a very great and cunning temptation that a person willfully takes upon themselves, so that they might have a good reputation <note>literally "a good word"</note> without any labor, and yet they do not feel the full birth of the Holy Spirit in their heart. This will be made plain in the place where such a person becomes a grim bear and a roaring lion among their most intimate companions, where they ought to have been a lamb in gentleness and a dove in virtue.

<summary>The author reflects on the persistence of mortality after the Fall and warns against "spiritual" hypocrisy, where outward displays of piety mask a lack of true inner transformation.</summary>
<keywords>Eve, Adam's fall, repentance, spiritual name, hypocrisy, Holy Spirit, humility, virtue</keywords>

# Chapters IX—XI

Thus, their life is a deception before the world and a very harmful lie before God and their companions. O woe to you, most wretched greed <note>original: "girekeit"; refers here to a grasping spirit or worldly attachment</note>, how bitter my heart is toward you! For you rob my dear sisters of the inward sweetness of God and the outward loveliness which they should prepare and lead into the holy bridal bed of the Holy Trinity. You make them so hardened within and so unwilling without, that one dares not speak a spiritual word before them, lest it be immediately twisted by them.

No, dear sister, you must first have a broad mind; then you will receive a willing heart and an open soul into which grace may flow. If you make your physical needs too broad <note>i.e., excessive or self-indulgent</note> without counsel and without necessity, then truly the height of holy longing, the breadth of divine feeling, and the depth of the flowing sweetness of God will forever be unprepared for you. For it is an eternal shame and a great indecency that a King’s bride should so willingly wade in the puddle. <margin>how they should work.</margin>

Ah, sister, when you wish to pray rightly, give yourself entirely to God and say: "Most beloved, dear Jesus Christ, this hour is yours alone, and for the poor sinners, and for holy Christendom, and for the troubled souls—and not mine. All my heart’s might and power I give to you today, Lord, so that you, most Beloved, may come to help them for your own praise according to my longing; and grant me, Lord, thereafter that I may truly recognize who I myself am, for only then do I truly humble myself."

But dear sister, when you go to your work, bless yourself and say:

->Help me, Jesus, my heart’s love,<-
->That I may wind my soul and my senses so deeply into you,<-
->That I do not wither away through earthly greed.<-

Yes, sister, if you are wise by your own senses, then greed attacks you with fury; but if you are wise by grace, then no malice can mislead or betray you. For in the grace that flows down from the Holy Trinity into a heart that stands forever open toward heaven...

<summary>The author warns against the spiritual "greed" that hardens the heart and provides specific prayers to dedicate one's time and labor to Christ and the Trinity.</summary>

### XII. *How God answered a brother concerning the writing of this book.*

Master Heinrich, you wonder at the manly words that are written in this book. I wonder how you can wonder at that. Rather, I lament it very sorely from my heart, since I, a sinful woman, must write—that I can write the true knowledge and the holy, glorious contemplation to no one; instead, these words alone seem to me all too small compared to the eternal truth. I asked the Eternal Master what He would say to this? Then He answered thus: Ask him how it happened that the apostles came into such great boldness after such great timidity when they received the Holy Spirit. Ask further, where Moses was when he saw nothing but God. Ask even more, from whence it came that Daniel spoke in his childhood.

### XIII. *Of the tenfold benefits of a good person's prayer.*

This prayer has great power, which a person performs with all their might. It makes a sour heart sweet, a sad heart glad, a poor heart rich, a foolish heart wise, a timid heart bold, a sick heart strong, a blind heart seeing, and a cold soul burning. It draws down the great God into a small heart; it drives the hungry soul up to the full God; it brings together the two lovers, God and the soul, into a blissful place where they speak much of love. Oh woe, I, unhappy one, receive the sacrament <note>original: "sak(rament)"</note> there, so that I may not die. <margin>The text is obscure here.</margin>

<summary>The author defends her mystical writings to Master Heinrich by citing biblical precedents of divine inspiration and describes the transformative power of prayer as a union between the soul and God.</summary>

## **XIV.** *Concerning the purgatory of wicked priests.*
It has been a long time since I saw a purgatory that was like a fiery water, and it seethed like molten bell-metal <note>original: "gloggenspise" — a bronze alloy used for casting bells, known for its intense heat when liquid</note>, and it was covered above with a dark mist. In this water floated spiritual fish, which were shaped like human figures. These were the souls of poor priests who, in this world, had floated in the greed of all worldly pleasures and had burned here in cursed unchastity <note>original: "vnküschikeit"</note>, which so greatly blinded them that they could gain nothing good. Upon the water traveled fishermen who had neither boat nor net; rather, they fished with their fiery claws, for they were spirits and devils. When they brought them to the land, they spiritually and bitterly stripped off their skin and threw them immediately into a boiling cauldron; therein they poked them with fiery forks. When they had tormented them according to their will, they devoured them in their beaks. Then the devils rose up over the water again and passed them through their tails <note>original: "zagel" — implying a grotesque cycle of digestion and excretion</note>, and fished them out, and ate them, and digested them again.

## **XV.** *Concerning the purgatory of a good priest.*
A pure priest died in his own rightful parish. Then I prayed for him as for any other person in the Christian custom. Then my soul saw his in praiseworthy dignity, such that he was still in the waiting-place <note>original: "beitungewe"</note> of heavenly honor. Four angels led him above all storms into the first heaven, and they played for him with heavenly strings. That was his purgatory, wherewith they prepared him for the heavenly dwelling <note>original: "wuß"</note>. I asked him how he had received such special dignity. Then he spoke: "On earth I loved solitude, and I was afraid <note>meaning: "reverent" or "humbled"</note> alone in my prayer." Then I spoke: "O you most blessed one, why do you not travel at once with these wondrous angels to heaven?" Then he spoke thus: "The honor is so great which I am to receive for my pure priesthood <note>original: "pfafheit"</note> that I may not yet enter into it."

## **XVI.** *It is devilish that one sins.*
Certain people who are learned say that it is human to sin. In all the temptations of my sinful body—

<summary>The author describes two contrasting visions of purgatory: a grotesque, cyclical torture for lustful priests and a musical, angelic preparation for a holy priest who valued solitude. It concludes by challenging the scholarly notion that sinning is merely a human trait.</summary>

# Part Five

In all the perceptions <note>original: "gevâlunge" - the way the heart feels or experiences things</note> of my heart, and in all the knowledge of my senses, and in all the nobility of my soul, I could never find it to be otherwise: it is devilish when one commits sin.

->Whether the sin be small or large,<-
->The devil is always its companion.<-

However, our adopted devilishness, taken on by our own free will, is more harmful to us than all our human nature. These things are human: hunger, thirst, heat, frost, pain, misery, temptation, sleep, and weariness. These are things that Christ took upon Himself—He who was a true human—for us and with us. But if sin alone were human, then He must also have sinned, for He was a true human in the flesh, a righteous human in wisdom, a steadfast human in virtue, and a perfect human in the Holy Spirit; and above all that, He was an eternal God in eternal truth, and not a sinner. But if we are to become like Him, we must also live like Him or be preserved through repentance <note>original: "rûwe" - contrition or remorse</note>.

## XVII. This is a greeting, a praise, and a prayer of the sinful woman.

> Greetings to You, living God!
> You are before all things mine.
> It is an endless joy to me
> That I may speak with You without fear.
> When my enemies hunt me,
> I flee into Your arms;
> There I may lament my suffering,
> As You incline Yourself toward me.
> You know well how You can touch
> The strings <note>original: "seiten" - refers to the strings of a musical instrument, symbolizing the soul's responsiveness</note> within my soul.
> Ah, begin this at once,
> So that You may be forever blessed.
> I am an ignoble bride,
> Yet You are my noble beloved <note>original: "trût" - a term of endearment, like 'dear' or 'darling'</note>;
> In this I will forever rejoice.
> Remember how You can cherish
> The pure soul in Your lap,
> And fulfill this, Lord, in me right now,
> Even though I am but Your least companion.
> Ah, draw me, Lord, up to Yourself,
> Then I shall become pure and clear;
> If You leave me to myself,
> I remain in darkness and in heaviness.

### XVIII. How God answers this.

Thus God answered:

> My counter-greeting is such a great heavenly flight,
> That if I were to give myself to you according to my power
> You could not retain your human life.
> You see well, I must restrain my power
> And pass over my clarity,
<margin>-</margin>So that I may keep you all the longer
> In this earthly misery <note>original: "jamerkeit"; refers to the sorrowful state of mortal life compared to heaven</note>.
> For there all your sweetness rises up
> Into the heights of eternal dignity,
> And my strings shall sound sweetly for you
> According to the faithful price of your long labor.
> Yet I will begin beforehand
> And tune my heavenly strings within your soul
> So that you may be able to wait all the longer;
> For high brides and noble knights
> Must be prepared for a long time and with great effort
> Through costly means.

### XIX. How seventeen kinds of sin hunt mankind.

These things hunt a person so far from God that he may never come back to God unless great violence <note>In this context, "violence" refers to a powerful, transformative act of divine intervention or "holy force"</note> is done to him by the Holy Trinity. Vanity <note>original: "italkeit"</note> is the first sin that begins to hunt a person away from God; and if we do not leave it, then unchastity rises up. Moreover, if we do not leave unchastity, then greed rises up. If we do not leave that, then sloth rises up; if we do not leave that, then lying rises up; if we do not leave that, then perjury rises up; if we do not leave that, then anger rises up; if we do not leave that, then backbiting rises up; if we do not leave that, then pride rises up; if we do not leave that, then hatred rises up; if we do not leave that, then revenge rises up; if we do not leave that, then despair <note>original: "missetrost"; literally "mistrust" or loss of hope in God's mercy</note> rises up; if we do not leave that, then evil audacity rises up; if we do not leave that, then shamelessness rises up; if we do not leave that, then perverted wisdom rises up; if we do not leave that, then unbelief rises up and says: "It is not as they say."

<vocab>heavenly flight, misery/sorrow, dignity, faithful price, seventeen kinds of sin, vanity, unchastity, greed, sloth, lying, perjury, anger, backbiting, pride, hatred, revenge, despair, audacity, shamelessness, perverted wisdom, unbelief</vocab>

<summary>God explains why He must temper His divine light to preserve the soul's mortal life, while the subsequent chapter warns of a chain reaction of seventeen sins that hunt the soul away from the Creator.</summary>

<header>Fifth Part.</header>

Oh woe, they receive all those things that come from God so maliciously that one can hardly speak of it; and how they present themselves is so perverted and so heavily mingled with lies that, unfortunately, no one can find the Holy Spirit in their words. Although they sometimes show themselves to be praiseworthy, it is, alas, deceitful. —

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Perfected soul, rejoice,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You alone are like unto God.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Indeed, it is quite right,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;For you drink with divine patience,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Without guilt,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Many a bitterness into yourself.

You are often troubled by your enemies. Thus hell cried out against the heavenly flower; yet it still blossoms far on high in its noble beauty, for the root of its constancy is made green at all times by the Holy Spirit.

### XX. A praise of God from eight things. Of the offering for sin.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;O great dew of the noble Godhead!
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;O little flower of the sweet Virgin!
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;O useful fruit of the beautiful flower!
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;O holy offering of the Heavenly Father!
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;O <term>pledge</term><note>original: "pfant" - a legal or spiritual guarantee/security</note> for all the world, given without faithlessness!
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You are, Lord, my refreshment
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And I am your blossoming.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You are small to me, Lord, in your humility,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And I am great to you in the misery of my wickedness.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I offer to you, Lord, every day
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Everything that I have within me,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Which is entirely wickedness.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;There you shall, Lord, pour in your grace,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;So that I may flow from your love.

## Chapters XXI—XXII.

The soul that is alive in you, for that reason I am loved by your friends. Oh Lord, dear bridegroom, my sweet Jesus Christ, I bless you <note>The original manuscript reads "me," but "you" is the corrected sense for the context of prayer.</note> without ceasing in my heart above all earthly things, and I pray that you keep me unmixed <note>original: "vngemenget"; meaning pure or unadulterated by worldly concerns</note> before them; for however holy they may be, they still weigh me down away from you in the highest point of spirit. That I cannot endure, and therefore I must struggle against them.

### XXII. *Of seven things regarding the judgment. Of shame and good will.*

The most noble joy of the senses, and the holiest peace of the heart, and the most lovely radiance <note>original: "glas"; likely meaning luster or spiritual brilliance</note> of works—these come from a person being truthful in all their doing. <margin>Three things come from truthfulness.</margin> Here our dear Lord speaks and teaches me himself seven things, which all the blessed shall have within them, who shall occupy the Final Judgment with Jesus Christ over all human kin. Whoever does not have these things must stand before the judgment like a sold servant <note>term: "verkôfet kneht" — a bondman or slave who has no legal standing or autonomy</note> before his master; for all those who here exert themselves against God's truth with swift lies, they sell these virtues. The first is: just in opposition. This is the interpretation <note>original: "glose"; a marginal or interlinear explanation of a text</note>: If I see that my friend does wrong to my enemies and to God, then I should faithfully blame my friend and lovingly help my enemies. The second is: merciful in need. *Interpretation*: If I see my friend and my enemy together in equal need, I shall help them equally. The third is: faithful in companionship. *Interpretation*: I shall never scold my companions, except for the sake of their unfaithful souls alone. The fourth is: helpful in secret. *Interpretation*: That one should seek and ask where the miserable sick and the prisoners are, and comfort them with words, and ask them to tell you their secret needs, so that you may come to their aid. Oh alas, that one passes by the miserable sick without sighs and without tears and without all kinds of compassion!

<summary>Mechthild describes the soul's desire for unmediated union with Christ and outlines the first four of seven virtues—centered on radical impartiality and mercy—required for those who will judge alongside Christ.</summary>
<keywords>Bridegroom of the Soul, Final Judgment, Truthfulness, Mercy, Impartiality, Seven Virtues, Care for the Sick</keywords>

--- Page 178 ---
<meta>This page continues the moral and mystical instructions for the spiritual life, likely following a list of virtues or requirements for those in religious orders.</meta>

# Part Five

What poorly suits spiritual people <note>original: "geistlichen lúten"; referring to clergy or members of religious orders</note>,
And sadly separates them so far from God,
That they immediately lose
That sweet divine intimacy <note>original: "gotzheimlicheit"; a mystical term for the soul's private, "at-home" closeness with God</note>,
And yet they do not wish to know
That God’s judgment strikes in this way.

The fifth is that one should be speechless in times of distress. <gloss>Gloss: that is, that one should not speak the greedy words that rise up from a proud, angry heart; through this, one finds bottomless grace in God.</gloss> The sixth is that one should be full of truth. <gloss>Gloss: A person is truly full of truth when their heart, in its best conscience, purely finds no guilt and rejoices that God’s eye looks into their heart, and would not be ashamed if all people could see into their heart.</gloss> The seventh is that one should be an enemy of lies. <gloss>Gloss: That we rebuke lies in all people and that we do not hide them within ourselves.</gloss>

These seven things we ought to practice and fulfill
Against the taste of our poor flesh
And against the lust and weakness of human vision <note>original: "súe"; likely referring to the "sight" or "senses" of man</note>,
For we cannot fulfill them otherwise.
But the nobility of our soul toward all good things
Gives us, with true divine sweetness,
The first counsel;
Moreover, our corrupted flesh, with its ignobility,
Neglects many a divine day.
Whenever they think of the blessed hour,
When God, from His bottomless heart
And from His wise senses, and from His joyful mind—
Which flows without ceasing with all goodness—
And from His sweet mouth
Has so greatly tempered <note>original: "getempert"; meaning to blend, moderate, or infuse in proper proportion</note> spiritually into our soul,
Wisely into our senses, and of necessity into our body,
Then we must be ashamed outwardly
Of our evil habits, and inwardly
Of our unfaithful hearts.
We may also, alas, be ashamed of our senses,
That we carry the noble, manifold gifts of God
So uselessly,
That they bring so little fruit
Back to that same place
From which they flowed out: that is, God’s heart.

<summary>This page outlines the final three of seven spiritual requirements—silence in distress, truthfulness, and hatred of lies—while lamenting how human fleshly weakness fails to return God's abundant spiritual gifts to His heart.</summary>

# Chapters XXII—XXIII.

O woe is my guilty pain!
Good will brings all virtues into their right state,
Even if the body is not capable of the works.

### XXIII. Concerning Saint Mary’s prayer. Concerning Gabriel’s light. Concerning the child’s cloth. From whence the milk came and the child’s offering.
Concerning the devils and the Lenten veil <note>original: "hungertůch" — a cloth used to veil the altar during Lent, here used metaphorically or in a visionary context</note>.

I saw a maiden at her prayer; her body was bowed toward the earth and her spirit had raised itself up toward the eternal Godhead. For before the time that Jesus Christ <note>original: "J. cristus"</note> opened heaven with the key of the holy cross, no human was ever so holy that their spirit could or must ascend with labor and soar, with longing and embracing, with the love of the Holy Trinity in the eternal heights. Therefore, the pure virgin’s spirit could not enter into heaven, because Adam had pushed the bolt <note>original: "grendel"</note> much too far across. <margin>Sin is the bolt.</margin>

But God inclined Himself, and He stood so near to the earth that He comforted His friends, and they perceived His will. However, the prophets cried out loudly and invited our Lord earnestly to come down. But this virgin drew our Lord down with a sweet voice of her soul; and she spoke in her prayer, when she was alone, thus: "Lord God, I rejoice that You wish to come in such a noble manner, that a virgin shall be Your mother. Lord, I wish to serve that cause with my chastity <term>kūscheit: purity of body and soul</term> and with all that I have from You." Then the angel Gabriel stepped down in a heavenly light. The light encompassed the virgin all around, and the angel had such bright garments that I can find nothing like them anywhere on earth. When she saw the light with fleshly eyes, she stood up and was terrified. When she looked upon the angel, she found the likeness of her own chastity in his countenance. Then she stood with great modesty <note>original: "zuht" — refers to disciplined, courtly, or virtuous behavior</note> and inclined her ears and raised up her senses. Then the angel greeted her and announced God’s will to her. His words were pleasing to her heart, and her senses became full, and her soul became fiery. Nevertheless, she asked after—

# Part Five

<note>The text describes the Annunciation</note> At that distinction <note>original: "vnderscheide"; likely referring to the moment of choice or the angelic greeting</note>, she was moved by maidenly shame and divine love. When she was informed, she opened her heart with good will and all her might. Then she knelt down and said: "I give myself to the service of God according to your words."

Then the entire Holy Trinity stepped—with the power of the Godhead, and with the good will of humanity, and with the noble fittingness <note>original: "gevûgheit"</note> of the Holy Spirit—through the whole body of her virginity into the fiery soul of her good will, and settled into the open heart of her most pure flesh. He united Himself with all that He found in her, so that her flesh became His flesh, so that He grew as a perfect child in her body, and so that she became the true mother of His flesh and remained an inviolate virgin. And as she carried Him longer, the lighter, more beautiful, and wiser she became. Then she stood up and said: "Lord, Father, I praise You, for You have made me great, and my lineage shall become great in heaven and on earth."

When the time came, when other women are sorrowful and walk with heavy burdens, Mary was light-footed and joyful. Her body was nonetheless completely full from it, for she had embraced within her the well-formed Son of God. Mary did not know the time beforehand, when God wished to be born of her, until she saw Him in her lap in the street and in the night at Bethlehem, in the foreign city where she herself was a poor, unhoused guest.

Almighty God with His wisdom, the Eternal Son with His human truth, the Holy Spirit with His delicate sweetness, went through the entire wall of Mary’s body with soaring joy without any labor. This happened as quickly as the sun gives its shine after the sweet dew in loving stillness. When Mary beheld her beautiful child, she bowed her head to His face and said: "Be welcome to me, my innocent child and my powerful Lord, to whom all things belong."

And with the gestation <note>original: "drahte"</note> of His mother,
And in the birth, and in the lap of His mother,
Before He came into the manger,
The power of the Holy Trinity
And the blissful heavenly fire upon Mary was so hot,

### Chapter XXIII.

How the spirit of hell, who travels through all the world
And knows all the events of things,
Could never come so near
To the land and the city where Mary was,
That he might have perceived the miracle
Of how the child had come here.

Mary took from Joseph’s saddle a coarse cloth
Which the donkey carried on its back under the saddle,
And also the upper part of her smock,
In which she had carried our Lord;
The other part she bound
Back together around her body.
In these cloths
The delicate maiden wrapped the great Savior
And laid him in the manger. Then he wept at once,
Like a newborn child.
<note>The author reflects a medieval belief that infants only cry when in genuine distress or need.</note>
For as long as children are speechless,
They never weep without true need.
So did our Lord,
Because, contrary to his noble nature,
He was given such a hard bed
In a cattle stall,
On account of wicked sin.
Then he turned away from all human kin;
Then he hid all his joy and all his power.
Then the maiden became sorrowful
And the child became hungry and cold.
Then the mother had to quiet her son;
That was his Father’s will
And the delight of the Holy Spirit.
Then the maiden inclined herself with motherly love
In virginal modesty
Toward her <term>suffering child</term> <note>original: "gepingeten" – referring to the physical distress of the infant Christ</note>
And offered him her childlike breast.
Hear now a wonder:
The shining bloom of her beautiful eyes,
And the spiritual beauty of her virginal countenance,
And the flowing sweetness of her pure heart,
And the blissful play of her noble soul—
These four things drew themselves together
According to the Father’s will,
And according to the Son’s necessity,
And according to the Holy Spirit’s delight,
Into her virginal breast.
Then the sweet milk flowed out from her pure heart
Without any pain.

# Part Five

Then the child sucked in human fashion,
And his mother rejoiced in secret.
The angels sang a song of praise to God.
The shepherds came; they sought and found <note>The manuscript reads "wûden," likely a scribal error for "funden" (found).</note>
Our true ransom <term>ransom: "lösepant," referring to Christ as the payment to redeem humanity from sin</term>.

"No," she said, "that is the food of rich people;
We do not have that in this poor life."

Satan came to Bethlehem and followed the three

Upon the child most wickedly.
When such great honor was offered to the child with the high sacrifice <note>Referring to the gifts of the Magi: gold, frankincense, and myrrh.</note>,

This may well be the same child
That the prophets have written about,
Whom your master Lucifer

That you should bring to his account

Then the whole world would remain ours in hell.
This child was conceived and born without sin;
Otherwise, it would never have been hidden from me.
Now I have lost all my cunning;
Now I must return to my master
And lament this distress to him,
For this child will yet become far too great for us.
If he is to rise above us,
How shall we endure it?
There was never any child born before
To whom this honor was offered."

When Lucifer heard these tidings,
The foundational enemy sat in grief
Gnashing with his teeth and snarling
So that the fire of his wrath shone throughout all hell.

# Fifth Part

...they never became dry from their sins, but rather they were dark with guilt. The creation of the images <note>original: "scheffenisse der bilden"</note> was entirely due to the guilt and the distress that so greatly moved the Great God, and how He preserved Noah, the righteous man, along with his household, while letting all the rest of the world perish.

So then, in the house of prayer <note>likely referring to the Tabernacle or a specific chapel</note>, the cloth was of noble white. This was a prefiguration of the pure, clear chastity of Saint Mary, through which we all should still overcome all our heartfelt sorrow. Upon it, images were sewn with gold that resembled the birds Noah had sent out from the ark; by these were known the unfaithful, greedy ones <note>original: "gircere"</note> who seek all their comfort here on earth. But there was also sewn upon it the pure dove with a green branch,

    Which returned so innocently,
    That she did not take the carrion into her mouth.
    By this, those were known,
    Who come to God every day with new virtues,
    And keep themselves in the faithful flight toward heaven
    Under the guidance of the Holy Spirit.

Farther down, where the pieces joined together, there was a golden border. Right across the middle went a green band, which was set with precious stones. This signified that most noble wood <note>the wood of the True Cross</note>,

    Which bore our Lord’s body upon itself,
    When the gates of heaven were pierced through
    And struck open with hammers,
    So that Adam’s bolt <note>the barrier of original sin</note> fled from there.

Although few people knew the meaning, these two ornaments nevertheless formed an honorable cross. Upon the cross was sewn a white sacrificial lamb, and it was adorned with precious stones and with bright gold, as if it were truly meant to be burned <note>referring to a burnt offering</note>. This was prefigured and then fulfilled when the innocent Lamb of God took a great death of love <note>original: "miñetot," a mystical term for a death motivated by divine love</note> upon the high cross. Therefore, the dead Lenten veil <note>original: "hungertuch," a cloth used to cover the altar during Lent</note> fell down with the dead lamb during the passion of our Lord, so that the living Lamb of God should be worshipped in that same place forevermore.

## XXIV. Concerning the sixfold garments of our Lord God and the virtues of Saint Dominic, and how God honored his Order in four things.

A high prince, who has a useful son for himself and a consoling son for his people—that son is such a praiseworthy son to his father and such an honorable, beloved son, that the memory of the son and all his works awaken the father’s honor wherever the son turns. This high prince is our dear Lord, the Heavenly Father; He has gained seven useful sons and a very beautiful daughter by our mother, Holy Christendom.

--- Page 187 ---
<page-num>155</page-num>
<header>Chapters XXIII—XXIV.</header>

His first son is our dearest brother, our Lord Jesus Christ <note>original: "J. Cristus"</note>. What honor the Heavenly Father has for the Son, and what comfort His people receive from Him, is well evident. And how the Heavenly Father has united Himself with this Son, and how He has set Him at His right hand, and how much power and honor He has given Him, is beyond measure and yet perfectly measured. The second son of the Heavenly Father was the holy Apostles, who preserved for us the precious treasure that was dug out of the high mountain, which a tree <note>A reference to the Cross</note> bore, and which our enemies pierced in five places <note>The five wounds of Christ</note> and hunted out and struck down all our heavenly treasure there. The third son was the brave Martyrs, who watered the road to heaven with their blood. The fourth son was the steadfast Confessors <note>term: Confessors; those who lived holy lives and "confessed" the faith through their deeds and teaching, though not martyred</note>, who give and teach us pure truth <insert>truth</insert>. The fifth son was the pure Virgins, who preserved their chastity for the love of God. These may move the Heavenly Father, for they bear His likeness entirely within them. He desires to have them for Himself alone, and they shall wear their bridal wreaths <note>original: "schappel"; a floral crown or chaplet</note> in His betrothal eternally. Indeed, they shall not cover their heads with shame, as earthly brides are accustomed to do, since our Lord has seated these useful children in the high festival so honorably beside Him, that all their suffering and their piety on earth was forgotten.

Then His common people went so far astray from the right faith and from pure confession, that the Heavenly Father took pity and then conceived two sons in one womb <note>original: "trahte"; meaning a single pregnancy or "litter"</note> by our dear mother, Holy Christendom; and she herself suckled these two sons with her breasts, which are so full of sweet milk that they never could, nor ever again will be able to, suck them dry. These breasts were and are the Old Law <note>The Old Testament</note> and the New Law <note>The New Testament</note>, with which our mother Holy Christendom suckles all of God's children.

Our Lord also spoke this:
> No one should be ordained
> as a priest unless he knows both the Old Law and the New Law.
> For on one foot no one can go to court,
> Nor stand for long in service.

These two sons are the Preachers <note>The Dominicans</note> and the Lesser Brothers <note>The Franciscans</note>, of whom Saint Dominic and Saint Francis [were] the first roots...

<summary>This page describes a spiritual hierarchy of "sons" of God—Christ, Apostles, Martyrs, Confessors, and Virgins—before introducing the Dominican and Franciscan orders as twin sons born to revitalize the Church through the Old and New Testaments.</summary>

# Fifth Part

they were. Oh woe, how much has passed away of that which they faithfully maintained! The more it passes away, the weaker the Order becomes; the longer it stands, the sooner another son will be born from the faithful heart of the eternal Father, who will not leave his children altogether forsaken. Saint Dominic <note>Founder of the Dominican Order (Order of Preachers), d. 1221.</note> watched over his brothers with faithful devotion, with a lovely countenance, and with holy wisdom, and not with deceit, nor with perverted senses, nor with unfaithful presence. He taught the wise man further that he should temper all his wisdom with divine simplicity; the simple man he taught true wisdom; the tempted <note>original: "bekorten" - those undergoing spiritual trials or temptations.</note> he helped to secretly bear all his heartache. He taught the young to keep much silence, whereby they are drawn away from outward things and become wise within. The sick and the infirm he comforted most lovingly, and he also considered all their needs with faithful diligence. They all rejoiced together in his long presence, and his sweet company made all their cumbersome labor easy for them. This Order was in its first times pure, simple, and moreover full of burning love for God. That pure simplicity, which God gives to some people, is sometimes so mocked by certain people that one loses the gift in which God's wisdom is found and kissed. Then God's burning love also goes out.

He who in the Order is grieved at heart because he becomes esteemed, and who receives all earthly honor as a great temptation, cannot abandon this because of the true nobility of his spiritual spirit, which he has received from God with a holy sinking of his heart beneath all creatures. Either he must keep the honor with fear of shame and with faithful diligence and with merciful help and with gentle joy, or he must strive with all wisdom to leave the burden with honor; for a spiritual heart must have still peace and should bloom lovingly toward the Holy Trinity.

God has especially honored these two sons <note>Likely referring to St. Dominic and St. Francis as the "two sons" or founders of the mendicant movements.</note> with four things. This He has done so that they should no longer worry about themselves, except only that they leave sin; rather, all their care and labor, said our Lord, should be for this: that my people may become blessed and holy. The first is a beautiful reception...

# Chapters XXIV—XXV.

...from the people, the second is faithful help in times of necessity <note>original: "notdurft"</note> from nothingness, the third is the holiest wisdom from divine truth, the fourth is the most useful power of holy Christendom. When the brothers are driven too hard without mercy and without sweet teaching, harmful things happen because of it, about which I must now remain silent.

### XXV. *One thing is enjoyed in the kingdom of heaven, which exists in seven ways, followed by seven things. The praise of the afflicted person is useful in seven ways.*

One thing I enjoy in heaven most of all; it is also the most noble and shines most beautifully before the Holy Trinity and also costs the most in this life: that is, that one—in poverty, in shame, in exile <note>original: "ellende," referring to being far from one's true spiritual home</note>, in pains, in spiritual poverty (which is the heaviest of all), in the constraint of obedience, in all kinds of bitterness inward and outward—yes, that one here within desires and is able and knows how to praise God from the heart, to thank Him with joy, to reach up with longing, and to fulfill it with works. Because of this, soul and body become so esteemed and praiseworthy in the kingdom of heaven that they sing and love more beautifully than the others, and shine more clearly in joy than the others, and they soar higher than the others, and live more blissfully than the others, and they are more exquisitely adorned than the others, and possess a greater dignity of riches than the others, and they enjoy more blissfully and suckle more deeply <note>A common medieval mystical metaphor for intimacy with the Divine</note> into the Holy Trinity than the others.

Lord God, I ask you: How does this praise and this acceptance taste to you, which an afflicted person offers you without any sweetness? Hear now what He says:

> It rises up with power,
> And its honor is and becomes manifold,
> For everything that ever was must make way for it,
> Until it comes into the divine place
> Of my Holy Trinity.
> And there it performs such wonders,
> That it permeates all my three Persons.
> And it touches and stirs and makes joyful
> My entire Trinity.
> The taste that I have,

It concludes with a poetic description of how the soul's praise permeates the Holy Trinity.</summary>

The soul herself experiences this well;
I cannot be fully intimate with her,
Unless she wishes to be truly still and stripped of all else
And lay herself upon my divine arm,
And that I must play with her,
For I have given myself into her power for this very reason:
Childlike, poor, naked, bare, despised,
And finally unto death,
So that she alone shall be (Ah, if she only wills it!)
My closest, my dearest companion,
And she shall forevermore in my Holy Trinity
Soar and play to her heart's content with soul and body,
And drown like the fish in the sea.
Where then has all her suffering gone,
Which she endured for my sake and after me?
In this way, I will give her a sweet exchange.

## XXVI. How God praises Himself and sings.

Ah, now hear how the Holy Trinity <term>Drivaltekeit: The Three-in-One</term> praises itself with its beginningless wisdom, its endless goodness, its eternal truth, and its entire eternity. Now hear the sweetest, the highest, the most blissful voice—how the Holy Trinity sings within itself with a single voice, from which all the sweet voices of the saints have flowed, which were ever sung in the kingdom of heaven and on the earthly realm, and shall yet be sung eternally:

The Father’s voice speaks in a song of praise: I am an overflowing fountain <note>original: "vsvliessende bruñe"</note> that no one can exhaust. But a person might perhaps block his own heart with a useless thought, so that the restless Godhead—which forever works without labor—cannot flow into his soul.

The Son sings thus: I am a returning wealth that no one can possess, except through that generosity which has ever flowed and shall ever flow from God; it all returns again with His Son.

The Holy Spirit sings this praise: I am an unconquerable power of truth; this is found in the person who stands laudably with God, no matter what trials afflict them.

<summary>This passage describes the mystical union of the soul with God as a "drowning" in the Trinity and presents a poetic song where the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit define their divine natures as overflowing grace and truth.</summary>
<keywords>Holy Trinity, mystical union, divine song, overflowing fountain, spiritual intimacy, suffering and reward, Middle High German mysticism</keywords>

<page-num>159</page-num>
->Chapters XXVI—XXVIII.<-

Thus the entire Trinity sings: I am so strong in my indivisibility <note>original: "vngescheidenheit"; referring to the theological concept that the three persons of the Trinity cannot be separated</note> that no one can divide me, nor break me in my complete eternity.

## XXVII. *With twelve words the Heavenly Father received His Son, Jesus.* <note>Reference to Greith, page 258.</note>

With these words the Heavenly Father received His Son when He had come from this earthly strife into the heavenly peace: You are welcome, my honorable Son, for I myself am: my hand in your work, my honor in your power, my strength in your struggle, my praise in your victory, my will in your return, my wonder in your ascension, my wrath in your judgment. The immaculate bride <note>The Church or the collective soul of humanity</note> whom you bring to me shall be yours and mine, never to be parted evermore. My divinity is your crown, your humanity is my atonement <note>original: "sûne"; the reconciliation between God and man</note>, the Spirit of us both is one will, one counsel, one power in all things, without beginning and without end. Your soul is the closest bride to us three persons.
Oh, how blissfully Christ’s soul plays within the entire Holy Trinity! Just like the wondrous shimmering that floats in the beautiful sun, which no one can see unless they have very beautiful eyes.

## XXVIII. *Concerning the seven crowns of Brother Albert. God’s ordinance is one thing, His election is another.*

Wherever skill possesses wisdom and love, there the fruit of election <note>Divine choice or predestination</note> is brought forth; and no one knows what good he has within himself unless he is first tempted by evil. I prayed out of love for the soul of Brother Albert; then God showed me his worthiness. There I saw seven maidenly crowns hovering above his head. I wondered greatly how this could be, since he had been a penitent <note>original: "rûwere"; one who lived a life of public or severe penance</note>. Then our Lord spoke: He won these crowns because he preserved seven virgins in their chastity through many labors, solely for the sake of my love; and eternally they shall adorn all his dignity, yet they shall never touch his body nor his soul. I have seen this in the kingdom of heaven: reward, dignity, and crown.

<summary>This page describes the mystical reception of Christ into Heaven by the Father and relates a vision of the celestial rewards given to a penitent named Brother Albert for protecting the chastity of others.</summary>
<keywords>Holy Trinity, Ascension of Christ, Brother Albert, Seven Crowns, Chastity, Divine Election, Mystical Vision</keywords>
