# Part Six.

I touch without ceasing this bottomless <term>Godhead</term> <note>original: "gotheit"; the divine essence beyond the personhood of the Trinity</note>,
Thereby I remind the heavenly Father
Of His endless love, which He bears toward the human soul.
I also greet my divine <term>humanity</term> <note>original: "menscheit"; referring specifically to Christ’s human nature</note>
And thank Him for my salvation
And remind Him of His companionship,
Since He Himself was an earthly man,
That He remember from whence He has come,
How great and how noble the human kinship is in Him,
And let the human being not be lost,
For no one has destined or born themselves.
Therefore You have overcome all Your distress without sin;
Thus I remind God’s humanity
To show special mercy,
And that He remember how weak the human being is,
And that he is not created free from his enemies,
And that the human must forevermore fight
As a well-armed man,
To whom, however, his eyes are bound—
That is their dark humanity <note>A metaphor for the limitations and spiritual blindness of the mortal condition.</note>,
With which they are bound.
Remember, noble Son of God,
How wretchedly I was in the earthly realm
In this sorrow,
And still stand by all people like a father,
Who bear my likeness within them,
For I am Your soul.
I must also compel the Holy Spirit to His gift,
For He must bring all salvation
To the human being from the kingdom of heaven into the earthly realm.
If You, eternal Father, should slide the bolt of Your justice
So firmly before the door of the kingdom of heaven,
That the poor sinners may not enter in here,
I lament it to Jesus, Your dear Son,
Who has the key to Your kingdom
In His human hand
With Your almighty power.
This same key was forged in that same land
By the hand of the Jews <note>A reference to the nails of the Cross or the instruments of the Passion, which medieval theology viewed as the "keys" that unlocked heaven.</note>.
Whenever Jesus turns the key,
Then the rejected sinner may come to Your favor.

### This is the word of the heavenly Father:

> My soul cannot endure it,
> That I turn the sinner away from me;
> Therefore I follow many a one for so long...

### *XVII. God looks upon the sinner as good. What a good, right will is. Of the good burdens.*

This is bottomless <note>In this context, meaning "unfathomable" or "infinite."</note>, that God looks upon the sinner as a converted person; and it is a right will, in serving God, that one hurries toward me and does not look back, and I carry all burdens that are borne for the sake of my love.

### *XVIII. You should look upon your heart at all times.*

> Look into your heart at all times
> With the truth of the Holy Spirit,
> Then all lies will be purely loathsome to you;
> For lies drive away divine <term>love</term> <note>original: "miñe"; the high, spiritual love between the soul and God</note>
> And they establish in the mind hidden, false thoughts,
> Hatred, and malice.

<header>Sixth Part.</header>

### **XIX.** *Of the good will, which one cannot bring to action. ¹)* <note>original: "Greith 261." This refers to the 19th-century edition of this text by Carl Greith.</note>

I have carried much sorrow because of this:
That I could no longer bring my good will to good works.
This is taken from me by instability and weakness,
And because no one dares to advise me on it,
And I, alas, do not dare to venture it against my nature.
This comes from the fact that God let me fall
From the bliss of the heights according to my own choice,
When I was so greatly filled with wonder
That I could find no end to those things,
When powerful <term>Love</term> <note>original: "miñe"; a Middle High German term for mystical or courtly love, representing the soul’s intense, reciprocal relationship with the Divine.</note>
Had drawn me away with the flames of her fire.
Now she has pressed me into a bottomless swamp,
Where I find no ground;
That is all that I suffer.
Yet I do not call this pain,
For I would gladly be further
In the truly lowest place,
Which is cast off like a rabid dog
And no man’s friend,
In misery, unknown,
With poor people in a foreign land.
Now I do not wish to be without <term>obedience</term> <note>original: "gehorsami"; a core monastic virtue involving the submission of one's will to a spiritual superior or God.</note>,
For holy, humble obedience
Is the seal of all virtues.
The good will that a good person has,
And yet cannot bring to a good deed,
Is like noble, beautiful flowers
With a sweet scent but without fruit.
Thus God has comforted me:
That all the good will of the multitude of a good life
Shall become the flowers of eternal bliss,
From which God, at His endless <term>feast</term> <note>original: "hohgezit"; literally "high time," referring to a festival or the heavenly banquet.</note>, will make wreaths
That His chosen ones shall wear there,
Who here followed Him so faithfully across the land
With such manifold good will,
Which they were unable to bring to good works.
O, merciful God, now reach out to me still
Your fatherly hand
And lead me into the land of Love,
For I have, alas, lost much beautiful time,

---
¹) Greith 261.

<summary>The author laments their physical or spiritual inability to perform outward good deeds, but receives a divine revelation that a sincere "good will" is as precious to God as the deeds themselves, eventually becoming wreaths of glory in heaven.</summary>

<header>Chapters XIX — XX.</header>

With this, Lord, I would still like to recover myself with you,
For bodily ease and the comfort of the senses
Must be received with humble fear,
If one is to stand in the full truth.

**XX.** *This book has come from a threefold gift. Love flows. She is rich and generous. She becomes sick. Who possesses the kingdom of heaven. God gives pain and also comfort.*

->This gift, which stands written in this book,<-
->He has given to me in a threefold way:<-
->First with great tenderness,<-
->Then with great intimacy,<-
->Now with heavy pains.<-
->In these I would rather remain<-
->Than in the other two, because: although<-
->God’s tenderness and intimacy are eternal and noble in themselves,<-
->They are, alas, so foreign in this world.<-
->All those who truly know them<-
->Cannot name them,<-
->And I also fear myself most in such delight,<-
->Because Christ suffered so many sharp needs in this world.<-

But the nature of <term>Love</term> <note>original: "miñe"; the Middle High German term for the intense, reciprocal love between the soul and God.</note> is that she first overflows with sweetness; afterward, she becomes rich in knowledge; and thirdly, she becomes <term>greedy</term> <note>original: "gitig"; in this mystical context, it refers to an insatiable, spiritual hunger or longing for God.</note> in her state of <term>abandonment</term> <note>original: "verworfenheit"; the experience of feeling cast off or rejected by God, a common stage in the "dark night of the soul."</note>. Indeed, you are quite unstable; but alas, true love for God sometimes becomes so sick from the evil sweetness of empty honors, and from the caresses of pride, and from the loathsome madness of anger, and from the broad longing for earthly things, that she is lamed in all her limbs—this is the very practice she naturally possesses from the beginning. 

But no one has a full <term>kingdom of heaven</term> <note>original: "himelrich"</note> in their heart except for the one alone who has surrendered all comfort and all grace in this world. For <term>delight</term> <note>original: "wollust"; refers here to worldly, sensory pleasure or gratification which distracts from the divine.</note> has separated us from God; therefore, we must return through pain. Yet God cannot refrain from this, and we cannot do without it. May He give us His delights in all that we do, omit, and suffer.

<note>Vocabulary from the source: Love, kingdom of heaven, delight, threefold gift, tenderness, intimacy, pain, sweetness, knowledge, abandonment, love of God, pride, caressing, madness.</note>

<summary>This page describes the author's spiritual journey through three stages: tenderness, intimacy, and suffering, arguing that true union with God is found in enduring pain and world-abandonment rather than sensory delight. It portrays "Love" (Minne) as a force that can become "sickened" by worldly pride and distractions.</summary>
<keywords>threefold gift, Minne, spiritual suffering, abandonment, pride, kingdom of heaven, humility</keywords>

<header>Sixth Part.</header>

### XXI. *How the wicked clergy shall be brought low. How preachers alone should preach and be bishops, and regarding the last preachers.* ¹)

Oh woe, crown of holy Christendom, how greatly you are soiled! Your precious stones have fallen from you, for you weaken and shame the holy Christian faith. Your gold is rotted in the pool of unchastity, for you have become poor and do not possess true <term>love</term> <note>original: "miñe"; the intense, reciprocal love between God and the soul.</note>. Your chastity is burnt in the greedy fire of gluttony, your humility is sunk in the swamp of your flesh, your truth has become nothing in the lies of this world, and the flowers of all your virtues have fallen away. Oh woe, crown of the holy <term>clergy</term> <note>original: "pfafheit"; referring to the priestly class or the ecclesiastical estate.</note>, how you have withered! Indeed, you have nothing left but your own self-deception, and that is clerical power; with it, you fight against God and His chosen friends. Therefore God will humble you before you <note>original: "ê du icht wisest"; literally "before you know anything of it."</note> realize it,²) for our Lord speaks thus:

> I will touch the heart of the Pope of Rome with great sorrow, and in that sorrow I will speak to him and complain that my shepherds of Jerusalem <note>In this context, Jerusalem represents the holy Church or the spiritual leadership.</note> have become murderers and wolves; for before my eyes they murder the white lambs, and the old sheep there are all sick in the head, for they cannot eat the healthy pasture that grows on the high mountains—which is divine love and holy teaching. Whoever does not know the way to hell, let them look upon the corrupted clergy, and how directly their path leads to hell, with wives and with children and with other open sins.
> 
> Thus it is necessary that the <term>youngest brothers</term> <note>original: "jungesten brůder"; likely a reference to the Mendicant orders like the Franciscans and Dominicans, who were seen as a new, purifying force in the 13th-century Church.</note> come; for when the cloak is old, it is also cold. So I must give my bride, holy Christendom, a new cloak; these shall be the youngest brothers, as was written before. Son Pope, you shall fulfill this, and so you may lengthen your life. That your predecessors now live such short lives comes from this: they do not fulfill my secret will.

### XXII. *Of seven things, five of which are found in the kingdom of heaven and two in the earthly realm.*

Seven things I must speak to the honor of God. Lord God, if it be possible, grant them to me, so that I may never forget them while in the <term>earthly realm</term> <note>original: "ertrich"; the physical world of mortal existence.</note>. Five are found in the kingdom of heaven; two must remain here. The first is the damage of my guilt, because I have sinned, and the neglect of good works which I might well have done. The second is that I, Lord, wait for You without ceasing, [to see] when You wish to come, and in what manner You offer a holy end to me. The third is the fiery <term>longing</term> <note>original: "gerunge"; a central concept in German mysticism describing the soul's intense desire for God.</note> I have for You. The fourth is <term>love's</term> <note>original: "miñe"; specifically mystical, divine love.</note> burning and extinguishing within me for Your sake. The fifth is Your first reciprocal glance of Your lordly countenance toward me. This, alas, could never happen to me in the earthly realm according to my longing; because of this my soul often sings, "O woe!" The sixth I hardly dare to take up; I would become mute as I acknowledge it, for I never heard it named in the earthly realm: that is the playing flood of love which flows secretly from God into the soul, and flows back again with His power according to her capacity. What bliss they then share between them both, no one else knows how they work with one another, for each finds its part; what a person has laid out here, that shall all be given back to them there.

->This is the heavenly love of God,<-
->Which here so very small begins<-
->And there never an ending wins.<-

The seventh can hardly be touched with words; with Christian faith one can feel how great, how high, how wide, how blissful, how honorable, how joyful, how full of imperishable joy it is. Blessed is he who shall dwell there eternally! The joyful sight full of all delight and the holy enjoyment according to desire—that over-sweet longing, blissful, hungry, and full of love—flows evermore into the soul abundantly from God. Even then the soul retains her sweet hunger and yet lives without distress.

<header>Part Six.</header>
<page-num>200</page-num>

### *XXIII. How God speaks with the soul in three places.*

In the first place, the devil often speaks to the soul; in the other two places, he cannot do so. The first place is the human **senses**. This place is common to God, the devils, and all creatures, to enter and to speak according to their own will. The second place, where God speaks with the soul, is **in the soul** itself. Into this place no one can come except God alone. But when God speaks in the soul, this happens without any kind of knowledge of the senses, through a great, powerful, and swift union of God within the soul. Then the senses cannot perceive that blissful speech. They become so humble that they cannot endure any creature beneath them. Should a person humble themselves before the devils? Yes, with such devotion that it should seem to them that they have offered God such great dishonor with their life—as if they had often painted the devil's likeness on their soul with daily sins and sometimes struck great wounds upon their soul with <term>mortal sins</term> <note>original: "hůbetsünden"; literally "head-sins," referring to the cardinal or deadly sins.</note>.

The soul that is encompassed by the Holy Spirit cannot restrain itself; it must sink from all earthly comfort and pleasure into [divine] comfort; but the soul that is encompassed by its own self-will inclines with many a pleasure toward earthly things.

The third place where God speaks with the soul is the **kingdom of heaven**, when God raptures the soul with the pleasure of His will and draws it there, where it must delight in His wonders.

### *XXIV. How Christ reveals His wounds in suffering. Four things knock before the gates of heaven.*

In my great <term>suffering</term> <note>original: "wetagen"; literally "woe-days," referring to periods of physical illness or spiritual trial.</note>, God revealed Himself to my soul and showed me the wounds of His heart and said: "See how they have hurt me!" Then my soul said: "O Lord, why do you suffer such great distress? Since so much of your pure blood was shed in your pure prayer <note>A reference to Christ's agony in the Garden of Gethsemane.</note>, all the world should rightly have been redeemed by that." "No," He said, "that was not enough for my Father; for all that poverty and all the labor, and

> Those who love much are gladly silent,
> Those who do not love are ever further from love.

### XXVIII. When you are to die, then take leave of ten things. ¹) <margin>Greith 263.</margin>

When I am to die, I take leave in this way of all those from whom I must part: I take leave of Holy <term>Christendom</term><note>original: "cristanheit"; referring to the collective body of all Christian believers on earth.</note> and I thank God for it, that I was called a Christian person and have come to the true Christian faith; and if I were to remain here longer, I would wish to be a help through my labor to Holy Christendom, which stands in many sins.

I take leave of all the poor souls who are now in <term>purgatory</term><note>original: "vegefůr"; the place of cleansing for souls after death before they can enter heaven.</note>. Were I to remain here longer, I would gladly help to pay your debts, and I thank God that you shall have grace.

I take leave of all those who are in hell, and I thank God that he exercises his justice upon them. Were I to remain here, I would never wish them any good. <note>This reflects a common medieval theological view that the blessed in heaven agree with God's perfect justice, even regarding the damned.</note>

I take leave of all the sinners who lie in <term>mortal sins</term><note>original: "hőptsůnden"; literally "head-sins" or capital/deadly sins that sever the soul's relationship with God.</note>. I thank God for it, that I am not their companion, and were I to remain here, I would gladly bear their burden before God.

I take leave of all the penitents who stand in their <term>penance</term><note>original: "bůsse"; the act of making amends or atonement for sins.</note>.

<header>Chapters XXVII—XXIX.</header>

I thank God for it, that I am her companion. Should I remain here longer, I would have to love her <note>In the context of the previous page, "her" refers to Holy Christendom.</note> even more.

I take leave of all my enemies. I thank God for it, that I remain unconquered by them. Should I remain here longer, I would want to lay myself beneath their feet.

I take leave of all earthly things. I lament to God that I never used them according to his holy order.

I take leave of all my dear friends. I thank God and them that they have been my help in times of need. Should I remain here longer, I would have to be forever ashamed of my faults <note>original: "vntugenden"; refers to moral failings or lack of virtue.</note>, which they recognize in me.

I take leave of all my wickedness. I lament this to God, that I have so corrupted his holy gift in my soul, that no flaw was ever so small that it would not be known in the kingdom of heaven upon my soul. However it may have been changed, the damage still remains. Lord Jesus, I lament it to you, yet the shame is all yours <note>This likely expresses the mystical idea that the soul's shame is shared by Christ through His intimacy with the believer.</note>.

I take leave of my suffering body. I thank God for it, that he has preserved me in many places from many sins. Should I remain here longer, its wickedness is so manifold that I would never be truly well-disposed toward it.

<margin>XXIX.</margin>

### *Of ten pieces of divine fire from the nobility of God.*

An unworthy person thought simply about the nobility of God. Then God gave him to recognize in his senses and to behold with the eyes of the soul a fire that burned without ceasing in the heights above all things. This fire had burned without beginning and shall yet burn forever without end. This fire is the eternal God, who has kept eternal life within himself and has given all things out from himself. The sparks of this fire that have flown out are the holy angels. The flashes of this fire that have come are all of God’s saints, for their lives have given many beautiful flashes of light to Christendom. The coals of this fire are still glowing; these are all the blessed ones who burn here in <term>divine love</term><note>original: "miñe" (Minne); the Middle High German term for the intense, reciprocal love between the soul and God.</note> and shine with a good example; those who have grown cold in sin may warm themselves by these coals. The embers <note>original: "geneiste"; tiny flying sparks or glowing particles that drift away from a fire.</note> of this fire have scattered and become as nothing; these are all the blessed...

...bodies that still wait in the earth for the heavenly reward. The mastery of this fire is yet to come; it is Jesus Christ, to whom his heavenly Father has entrusted the first release and the final judgment. On the last day, he shall make the most beautiful vessels <note>original: "köpfe," literally "cups" or "bowls"; used here as a metaphor for the perfected souls of the saints from which God "drinks" their holiness</note> from the refined ones for the heavenly Father, from which he himself will drink, at his eternal wedding feast, all the holiness that he, with his dear Son, has poured into our soul and into our human senses.

> Yes, I shall drink from you,
> And you shall drink from me,
> All the goodness God has kept in us.
> Well for the one who now stands firm <note>The manuscript uses "vaste," meaning firmly or steadfastly.</note>,
> And does not spill here what God has poured into them.

The smoke of this fire is all earthly things, which people often use with wrongful pleasure <term>wollust: in medieval German, this refers to intense sensory delight or desire, which can be either holy or sinful depending on its object</term>. However beautifully they shine in our eyes, however delightfully they play in our hearts, they still carry much bitterness hidden within them; for they vanish like smoke and blind the highest, and indeed, they even make the eyes of the holiest smart <note>original: "surögende," suggesting eyes becoming red or tearful from smoke</note>.

The comfort <note>original: "gemach," meaning a room, a place of rest, or solace</note> of this fire is the blissful delight that our soul receives inwardly from God with such holy warmth of the divine fire, so that we burn here in return in the divine fire and stand firm in virtues, so that we are not extinguished. The bitterness of the fire is the word that God shall speak on the last day: "Go from me, you cursed ones, into the eternal fire" <note>This is a translation of the Latin Vulgate, Matthew 25:41: "Ite maledicti in ignem aeternum."</note>. The radiance of this fire is the shining contemplation of the divine countenance of the Holy Trinity, which shall illuminate our body and our soul, so that we may see and know the wondrous bliss there, which we cannot now name here.

These things have come forth from this fire and also flow back into it, each according to God’s order with eternal praise.

> Whoever wishes to speak more of this, let him lay himself in the fire,
> And see and taste how the Godhead flows,
> How the humanity pours,
> How the Holy Spirit wrestles <note>original: "ringet"; implies the dynamic, active movement of the Spirit within the soul</note>
> And compels many a heart,
> So that it loves God manifoldly.

<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of divine love, moving from the metaphor of fire to the practical application of virtue and a deep theological reflection on the soul's mystical union with God. It addresses a potential theological conflict regarding whether our relationship with God is based on "nature" or "grace."</meta>

<page-num>205</page-num>
<header>Chapters XXX—XXXI.</header>

### XXX. *Pure love has four aspects.*

Pure divine love <note>original: "gotzmiñe," the medieval term for the mystical, ecstatic love between the soul and God</note> has these qualities within it: first, that one should be in harmony with God in whatever happens to us (provided it is without sin), so that we thank God with inwardness <term>iñikeit: a state of deep, heartfelt devotion and focused inner life</term>. The second: that we make orderly use of the gifts we have received from God in both body and soul. The third: that we live purely in good character, without any sin. The fourth: that we possess all virtues within us. Oh, if only I had these and truly fulfilled them in all things! I would prize that above all the contemplation <note>original: "contemplacie," referring here to mystical discourse or intellectual meditation</note> I have ever heard tell of. What use are lofty words without works of mercy? What use is love toward God if one is full of fury toward good people? But you might say: "If God gave it to me, I would gladly do it." Hear now: Virtues are half a gift from God, and half they are virtues within ourselves. When God gives us the knowledge, we must then put those virtues into practice.

### XXXI. *How God made the soul from delight and pain.*
### *How God is like a sphere.*

I stated in one place in this book¹) that the Godhead is my father by nature; do not misunderstand this and say: "Everything that God has done with us is entirely by grace and not by nature." You are right, and I am also right. Now hear a parable: However beautiful a person's eyes may be, they can see across a mile's distance; yet however sharp a person's senses are, they cannot grasp spiritual <note>original: "vnsiñelicher," meaning things beyond the reach of the physical senses</note> things except through faith, and they reach out like a blind person in the darkness. The loving soul, which loves all that God loves and hates all that God hates, has an eye that God has illuminated. With it, the soul sees into the eternal Godhead, observing how the Godhead has worked with its own nature within the soul. He has formed the soul after himself, he has planted it within himself, and he has united himself with it more than with any other creature. He has enclosed the soul within himself and has poured so much of his divine nature into it that the soul can say nothing else except that he, through this total union, is even more to it than a father.

---
¹) Part I, Chapter 44.

<summary>This page defines the four practical requirements of "pure love" and defends the mystical idea that the soul shares in God's nature through a profound union that transcends mere grace.</summary>
<keywords>pure love, Minne, inwardness, virtue vs grace, mystical union, nature of the soul, divine fatherhood</keywords>

--- Page 238 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 205, the text transitions from the debate over nature versus grace to the specific roles of the Trinity in human labor and the physical body, leading into a profound cosmological metaphor of God as an infinite sphere.</meta>

<page-num>206</page-num>
<header>Part Six.</header>

The body <note>original: "licham," the physical form</note> receives its dignity from the Son of the heavenly Father through brotherly fellowship and through the reward of labor. The Son of God, Jesus Christ, also performed his work in heartfelt love out of necessity—in poverty, in pain, in labor, and in disgrace, right until his holy death. The Holy Spirit has also performed his works, as you say, with his grace in every gift we have ever received.

These works are of three kinds, yet a single, undivided God has performed them within us. Two things work upon the earth and in purgatory <note>original: "vegefůr"</note> through divine power without ceasing; one works alone in hell—and that is delight in the kingdom of heaven without pain, and pain in hell without delight.

Where was God before he created anything at all? He was within himself, and all things were present and manifest to him, just as they are today. In what form did our Lord God exist then? In exactly the same way as if he were a **sphere** <note>original: "clote," meaning a ball, globe, or sphere</note> ¹) and all things were enclosed within God without lock and without door. The lower part of the sphere is a bottomless foundation beneath all abysses. The uppermost part of the sphere is a height above which there is nothing. The circumference of the sphere is an incomprehensible circle. Even then, God had not yet become the Creator; but when he created all things, the sphere was "opened up." No—he is still whole, and he shall remain whole forever. When God became the Creator, all creatures became manifest in him. Humanity was made to love <term>miñende: the ecstatic, mystical love for God</term>, to enjoy, and to know God, and to remain obedient; birds and beasts were made to know their nature; and the inanimate creatures were made to stand within their own being. Now hear: what we know is all as nothing unless we love God in an orderly way in all things, just as he himself created all things in orderly love, and has commanded and taught us to do.

---

<margin>¹) The "clote" was the "falling of the door." *Clote* = close, equivalent to a latch, lock, or a monk’s cell.</margin> <note>The scribe's marginal note attempts to explain the word "clote" as a closure or lock, though the main text uses it to describe the "divine sphere"—a famous mystical image of God's infinity.</note>

<summary>This page describes the dignity of the physical body through Christ's suffering and presents a mystical vision of God as an infinite, eternal sphere that contains all of creation even before it was manifested.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the nature of God and the soul. <page-num>207</page-num>
<header>Chapter XXXII.</header>

## XXXII. *How we become like God, St. Mary, and the angels.*

As much as we love mercy and practice steadfastness <note>original: "stetekeit," meaning constancy or spiritual stability</note>, so much do we resemble the heavenly Father, who practices these things within us without ceasing.

As much as we suffer here in poverty, contempt, abandonment, and pain, so much do we resemble the true Son of God.

As much as we overflow here with all the generosity of our hearts—giving our goods to the poor and our bodies to the service of the sick—so much do we resemble the Holy Spirit, who is a generous outpouring <note>original: "vsv̊lv̊t," a flowing-forth</note> of the Father and the Son.

As much as we are truthful, temperate, and discerning in holy simplicity <term>einvaltekeit: a state of being "one-fold" rather than "multi-fold," meaning a heart focused purely on God without ulterior motives</term>, so much do we resemble the Holy Trinity, who is one true God and has performed—and still performs—all His works in an orderly measure.

As much as we are chaste with all purity, humble with all submission, helpful with all holiness, and innocent of all malice, so much do we resemble our dear Lady Saint Mary, who is ennobled with these virtues, so that she became a virgin mother and remained a mother virgin, and is alone the Empress over all creatures.

As much as we are godly, loving, and peaceful, so much do we resemble the angels, who never again do anything evil.

As much as we live secretly in exile <note>original: "ellende," which in Medieval German implies both being in a foreign land and a state of wretchedness</note> and in hardship without comfort, so much do we resemble Saint John the Baptist, who is exalted above many saints.

As much as we have a longing for God’s praise, a recognition of His gifts, and an orderly use of God’s will, so much do we resemble the prophets and the holy fathers <note>The Patriarchs of the Old Testament</note>, who constrained themselves toward God with great virtues.

As much as we learn wisdom and convert other people with it, standing firm with God in all necessity, so much do we resemble the holy apostles, who abandoned themselves even unto death.

As much as we have patience in all necessity, and as great as our Christian faith <note>original: "cristangelůbe"</note> is even unto death, so much do we resemble the...

<header>Sixth Part.</header>

...holy martyrs, who with their blood have sprinkled for us the true way to heaven.

As much as we diligently bear the distress of holy Christendom, both of the living and the dead, so much do we resemble the holy confessors <note>Saints who suffered for their faith but were not killed; often associated with those who "confess" the faith through teaching or hearing confessions</note>, who watch with many labors and hear confessions with care.

As much as we have struggle and overcome it, and maintain maidenly <note>original: "mågetlich," meaning modest or virginal</note> honor, so much do we resemble the holy virgins, who have not lost the true victory.

As much great repentance as we have, and as many kinds of holy penance as we perform, so much do we resemble the holy widows, who after their sins earned such great honor.

As much as we have all virtues within us, so much do we resemble God and all His saints, who have followed God with all piety <note>original: "vrůmekeit," here meaning spiritual excellence or righteousness</note>.

### XXXIII. *Of the sharp chapter which a pilgrim entered, who appeared to be a great lord.*

For a long time, a person was accustomed to entering into his heart with a "sharp chapter" <term>sharp chapter: an internal, rigorous examination of conscience, modeled after the monastic "Chapter of Faults" where monks confessed their sins to the community</term> and observing his own failings and the dishonor he did to God. Then he cast out from his heart all the sinful tastes of his flesh and replaced them with a willingness to suffer all pain for God's sake. He also cast out all delight in his kinsmen <note>original: "magen," meaning relatives or family</note> and friends and replaced it with the contempt that his attackers wished to inflict upon him. He also cast out all love for riches and honors, in which the sinful world rejoices, and replaced it with all the poverty that is possible to endure according to counsel <note>original: "nach rate," referring to the "evangelical counsels" of voluntary poverty, chastity, and obedience</note>.

Into this "chapter" came our Lord Jesus Christ in the likeness of a poor pilgrim. Then the person's spirit was so enlightened that he recognized it was our Lord and said: "O dear pilgrim, where do you come from?" He answered: "I come from Jerusalem, where I was severely wounded; there I suffered great dishonor, poverty, and pain, and I have brought these to you." — "For this I thank you, most dear Lord, and I have truly felt this for many a day." Then our Lord took a simple crown and placed it upon the person's head and said: "This is the crown of poverty, which..."

### XXXIV. *The person who scorns the world shall be honored with eight things.*

A voice was heard and these words were spoken thus:
> Yes, behold, she is known, she who has scorned the world
> And has fled from lies
> And has loved the truth
> And has been blessed.
> She shall be received with all honor,
> She shall be confirmed in the truth,
> She shall be blessed without end,
> She shall be clothed with all beauty,
> She shall be crowned with all dignity,
> She shall be seated upon the throne of eternal rest,
> She shall be greeted with all tongues,
> She shall be served with every gift,
> She shall be gladdened with every bounty.

### XXXV. *How the blessed soul speaks to its body on the Last Day.*

> Stand up, my beloved,
> And recover yourself from all your pain,
> From all your aches, all your dishonor,
> All your sorrow, all your misery,
> All your soreness, all your labor.
> The morning star has risen <note>original: "vfgegân"; the manuscript uses the form "vfgegangen"</note>
> This is the birth of Saint Mary and her life.
> The sun has shed its light,
> This is when God became man,
> His work and His ascension.

### XXXVI. *When John the Baptist sang mass for the poor maiden, that was spiritual knowledge in the soul.*¹)

One cannot grasp divine gifts with human senses; therefore, people who do not have the open spirit of invisible truth take offense. That which one can see with fleshly eyes, hear with fleshly ears, and speak with a fleshly mouth is as unlike the open truth of the loving soul as a wax candle is to the bright sun.

That John the Baptist <note>original: "Johañes baptista"</note> sang mass for the poor maiden <term>maiden: the original "dirne" refers to a young woman or servant, often used by the author to describe her own humble soul before God</term> was not physical; it was so spiritual that the soul alone beheld and enjoyed it. But the body had nothing from it, except what it could grasp in its human senses from the soul’s nobility; therefore, the words must sound human.

My Pharisee <note>a term the author uses for her critics or legalistic theologians who questioned her visions</note> said regarding this speech that John the Baptist was a layman <note>meaning he was not an ordained priest and therefore could not traditionally "sing mass"</note>: The holiest thing in the mass is God's body. John the Baptist touched that same Son of God with humble, trembling fear, in such great dignity of his holy life, that he heard the heavenly Father's voice and perceived His words and saw the Holy Spirit and recognized Him in them both. John the Baptist also preached the holy Christian faith openly to all people and pointed with his fingers to the true Son of God, who was present there. "Behold the Lamb of God" <note>original Latin: "Ecce agnus dei"</note>. That John the Baptist spoke God's word—no pope, bishop, or priest can ever accomplish it so fully, except through our invisible Christian faith. Was this a layman? Advise me, you blind ones; your lies and your hatred will never be forgiven you without pain.

### XXXVII. *You shall praise God, lament, and pray for twelve things.*

Blessed be you, dear Lord Jesus Christ, God of the living God, Son in the power <note>original: "acht," which can mean attention, power, or esteem</note> of my faith; thus I truly know that you are present here, true God and man. In that same name I pray to you, Lord, look upon me, as my God and my Lord, as my Creator and my Redeemer, as my most beloved of all men and most worthy of all lords; guard me now and forevermore.

Lord, heavenly Father, now I lament to your holy Trinity that I have sinned before your eyes without fear and without shame. Oh woe, help me today, merciful God, with your full grace, for my heart is dark from the habit of sin. Cleanse, Lord, my heart today from all earthly love and pour down, Lord, your heavenly flood into my parched soul, that I may weep for your great humiliation <note>original: "smacheit," likely referring to the insults and suffering of the Passion</note> and the misery of my sins.

Lord, I thank you for all the graces that you, dear Lord, have shown to us and now do for us, and will do for us eternally. I pray to you, Lord heavenly Father, in the name of Jesus Christ, that you purify me with your grace from all my sins and protect me from all sin and hallow me with all virtues into eternal life.

I pray to you, Lord Jesus Christ, through your holy death and through the grievous distress that your holy body suffered on the holy cross, that you, Lord, with the eyes of your divine mercy and your human faithfulness and the favor of your Holy Spirit, would deign to look upon all my distress and my final death; and give me then, Lord, your own body, so that I, Lord, may then receive you with true Christian faith, with heartfelt love, so that your holy body must be and remain the final food <term>final food: the "viaticum" or the last Communion given to a person near death to strengthen them for the journey to the afterlife</term> of my body and the eternal bread of my poor soul.

I pray to you further, most dear Lord, that you then would comfort my poor soul with yourself and release me then from all enemies. I pray to you, most dear Lord Jesus Christ, that you then would receive my poor soul into your fatherly hands, and bring me then with all joy out from this—

---

### XXXVIII. No one can disturb God’s heaven. Hell is banished by God.

Ah dear Lord, almighty God, how long shall I stand here in the earth of my flesh like a stake or a target <note>original: "male," a mark used for target practice</note>, toward which people run, throw, and shoot, having long aimed at my honor with swift malice? Hear now this answer: No one is so cunning in his protection, and no one is so wicked in his wrath, that he may destroy, break, or harmfully touch my heavens, wherein I dwell. But those who take me into their lodging today and cast me out tomorrow, they are like hell. Whose foundation I am, whose pinnacle <note>original: "tynaphel," likely a variation of "Zinnapfel," referring to a decorative peak or a pinnacle of a building</note> I will also remain. Ah Lord, who shall help me so that I walk all my ways in such a way—even if I slip—that I do not fall? Fear shall hold me up, and God’s will shall lead me.

---

### XXXIX. Of the reflection of God’s radiance upon our Lady and her power.

O o o, three persons have one name in one undivided God. They flow toward Mary’s face blissfully in an undivided stream with a full flood, in a generous gift with the clear radiance of heavenly honor. With an unspeakable greeting He touches her heart, so that she shines and glows in such a way that the high reflection <note>original: "gegenblik," a beautiful mystical term for a "counter-glance" or a mirrored radiance</note> of the holy Trinity arises before our Lady’s face.

In this presence, our Lady <note>a traditional title for the Virgin Mary</note> may well wait; she desires to pray more, which she can do in humility. Since God became human through His divine love <note>original: "miñe," referring to the intense, mystical love between God and humanity</note> in her humility, our Lady no longer needs humility in the kingdom of heaven, except that she honors almighty God above herself in submission, along with all the blessed who follow her example.

Our Lady’s countenance <note>original: "gegenblik," a technical term in mysticism for the direct, face-to-face vision or presence of God</note> is clarified with all the incorruptible gifts she received from God. She is also adorned with all perfect virtues; she is crowned with all dignity. Through this, she flows back into God, full of all acceptability.

How our Lady enjoys the Holy Trinity and how God unites Himself with her above all pure humans is unspeakable. However, just as they were united here [on earth], so much does our Lady enjoy [Him in heaven], and so much does our Lord pour Himself into her above all other saints. Our Lady has the power to hinder all devils from harming people. Therefore, we gladly recite our Hail Mary <note>original: "Ave Maria"</note> in her presence, so that she may remember us here.

### XL. *Temptation, the world, and a good end prove us.*

No one knows how firmly he stands until he is first pushed by the temptation of the body.

No one knows how strong he is until he is first sought out by the world's malice.

No one knows how good he himself is until he reaches a good end <note>meaning a faithful and holy death</note>.

### XLI. *Concerning the presence of God in humans and in angels. Five things hinder the writing.*

You wish for me to write further, but I cannot. The joy, the honor, the clarity, the endearment, and the truth are so great above me that I would become mute¹) in speaking further of what I recognize. However, a mirror was seen in the kingdom of heaven before the breast of every soul and body, in which shines the...

<header>Chapters XL — XLIII.</header>

...mirror of the Holy Trinity <note>original: "drivaltekeit"</note> and gives truth and knowledge to all the virtues that the body ever performed, and to all the gifts that the soul ever received on earth. From this, the noble countenance <note>original: "gegenblik," a term used by Mechthild to describe a reciprocal gaze or a face-to-face reflection between the soul and God</note> of each person shines back into the high Majesty from which it flowed forth.

The angels' countenance is fiery and clear with love <note>original: "miñenclar," combining the concepts of intense mystical love and spiritual clarity</note>, for they have great love for our salvation. They serve us without effort, and their reward increases as long as this world exists. True divine love <note>original: "gottesmiñe"</note> has the same power over the angels as it has over humans. The fact that we serve with labor is because we are sinful.

### *XLII. Sister Mechthild wrote this on a slip of paper to her brother B., of the Order of Preachers, and said:*
<note>The "Order of Preachers" refers to the Dominicans. "Brother B." is often identified as Baldwin, a Dominican who may have been her biological brother or a spiritual confidant.</note>

The greatest joy that exists in the kingdom of heaven is the will of God. That reluctance should become will—from this comes divine joy into the heart of the troubled person. It is a spiritual person's confession when one scorns the gifts that come from God. We should receive painful gifts with joy. We should receive comforting gifts with fear <note>meaning a holy awe or caution against becoming complacent</note>; in this way, we can make all things useful to us that pass over us. Dear beloved, be in harmony with God and rejoice in His will.

### *XLIII. This writing flowed forth from God.*

This writing which stands in this book flowed forth from the living Godhead into Sister Mechthild’s heart, and is set down here as faithfully as it was given from her heart by God and written by her hands. Thanks be to God <note>original: "Deo gratias"</note>.

## This is the seventh part.

---

I. *Of the crown and of the dignity <note>original: "wirdekeit," meaning glory, honor, or worthiness</note> of our Lord Jesus Christ, which he shall receive after the Last Day.*

Our Lord, the heavenly Father, has still reserved in his divine wisdom many unspeakable gifts with which he will adorn his chosen children after the Last Day, specifically his only-begotten son Jesus our Redeemer. For him, the heavenly Father has prepared a crown, made and adorned with such great, honorable, and manifold works that all the masters who ever were, and now are, and ever shall be, could not fully describe the clarity <note>original: "clarheit," referring to a spiritual radiance or brilliance</note> and the manifold joy <note>original: "wuñe," meaning bliss or intense spiritual delight</note> of the crown. This crown was seen with spiritual eyes by the loving soul in eternal eternity, and its creation was made known to her. What is that, eternity? That is the uncreated wisdom of the endless Godhead, which has neither beginning nor end. The crown has three arches: the first arch of the crown was the patriarchs, the second the prophets, and the third holy Christendom. The crown is formed and blossomed <note>original: "gebluͤmet," meaning decorated as if with flowers</note> with the presence of all the blessed who shall possess God’s kingdom on the Last Day. Yet they shall possess their dignity in an orderly fashion according to their works. The first arch of the crown is set <note>original: "gewiret," meaning mounted or secured with metalwork/wire like a piece of jewelry</note> and illuminated with the precious stones of all the holy devotion <note>original: "iñikeite," meaning deep inwardness or spiritual intensity</note> and good works which the

<header>Cap. I.</header>

...the patriarchs have fulfilled. The arch is also formed with the human image of soul and body. The first image on the arch of the crown is Saint Stephen and all the martyrs depicted with him, who have ever shed their blood for the Christian faith; alongside him, Saint Peter and all God’s apostles are also depicted. Nearby are all the blessed who followed the apostles’ teachings. Married people shall also be depicted on the arch with their children, those who followed God with good works.

The second arch of the crown is formed with all the Popes <note>original: "Bebisten"</note> and all the spiritual fathers with them, to whom God entrusted his sheep. The arch is set <note>original: "gewiret," referring to the metalwork or wire used to secure jewels</note> with all spiritual power and blossomed <note>original: "geblůmet," meaning decorated with flower-like patterns or filigree</note> with Christian teaching.

The third arch of the crown is formed most beautifully of all with the noble humanity of our Lord Jesus Christ, and beside him his honorable mother Mary with all her virgins who shall follow the Lamb. Saint John the Baptist is depicted there very close to the Lamb, and all those blossomed near him who became Christians under his hands <note>referring to those he baptized</note>. The arch of the crown is set with the creation of all creatures according to the love and intention of the Creator which he had for them when he created all things according to his will. The crown is blossomed all over with many knightly shields of the holy, strong Christian faith. The Imperial Realm <note>original: "keyserrich"</note> shall also stand depicted on the crown, set and blossomed, down to the lowliest peasants <note>original: "jungesten geburen," referring to the common people or farmers</note> who thereafter shall be worthy for having served God. The crown shall also be battlemented <note>original: "geziñet," decorated with tooth-like projections like the ramparts of a castle</note> during the time of the Antichrist with many honorable images, such as Elijah and Enoch and many holy martyrs before them, blossomed with the holiness of their lives and consecrated with their faithful blood.

The crown shall also be colored with the blood of the Lamb and illuminated and gilded with the powerful Love <note>original: "miñe," the specialized Middle High German term for mystical or divine love</note> which broke Jesus’ sweet heart within. This crown was created by our heavenly Father, Jesus Christ earned it, the Holy Spirit worked and forged it in the fiery Love and made it so fittingly <note>original: "vůge," meaning with skill or proper proportion; the manuscript reads "wı̊ge"</note> with the noble art of the holy Trinity, that it...

<page-num>218</page-num>

<header>Seventh Part.</header>

...befits our Savior <note>original: "lůser," literally "looser" or "redeemer"</note> Jesus Christ so well and stands so honorably, that the Heavenly Father receives more joy from His only-begotten Son. This must be so. Even though the eternal Godhead, without beginning, possesses all bliss and joy within Himself—and now has and shall ever have—it nevertheless pleases Him especially and eternally that He shall gaze so joyfully upon the eternal Son with all His followers. When Jesus Christ has performed His Last Judgment and has served and celebrated His Supper <note>the wedding feast of the Lamb</note>, then He shall receive this crown from His Heavenly Father in great honor. With Him shall be those who have come through great labor <note>spiritual toil and struggle</note> in body and soul to the eternal high feast <note>original: "hohgezit," meaning a wedding or high festival</note>. Then every soul and body shall see its own dignity reflected in the crown.

The crown is fashioned <note>original: "gezůget"</note> on earth with precious materials—not with silver nor with gold, nor with precious stones, but rather with human labor, with human tears, sweat, and blood, with all virtues, and finally, with a painful death. The angels are not seen upon the crown because they are not human; but they must praise God at the crown with blissful song.

<margin>•</margin>The first choir sings thus: We praise you, Lord, for your lawful marriage <note>original: "elichen ê," referring to the union of Christ and His Church</note>, from which all these have come who are depicted on your crown. The second choir: We praise you, Lord, with the faith of Abraham and with the burning desire and prophecies of all the prophets. The third choir: We praise you, Lord, with the wisdom and piety of all your apostles. The fourth choir: We praise you, Lord, with the blood and with the patience of all your martyrs. The fifth choir: We praise you, Lord, for the holy prayers and Christian teaching of all those who baptize <note>original: "baptisten"</note> and all confessors <note>original: "bihtern," saints who suffered for the faith but were not martyred</note>. The sixth choir: We praise you, Lord, with the repentance and constancy of your widows. The seventh choir: We praise you, Lord, with the chastity of all virgins. The eighth choir: We praise you, Lord, with the fruit of your mother and maiden <note>referring to Mary</note>. The ninth choir: We praise you, Lord, for your holy death and for your honorable life after your death, and for your great outpouring <note>original: "vsvlus," from the Latin *effluxus*</note> of all gifts and all goodness, by which you, Lord, have exalted us and honorably ordained us. We praise you, Lord, with your fiery love <note>original: "vůrinen miñe"</note> in which you have united us.

### Chapter I.

At the very top of the crown floats the most beautiful banner that was ever seen in this imperial realm <note>original: "keyserriche," referring here to the Kingdom of Heaven</note>. This is meant to be the Holy Cross, whereon Christ suffered His death. The cross has four ends; the lowest end is adorned with delight, clearer than the sun. At the front end beneath the cross, the columns float upright, colored with the blood of the Lamb, flowered and adorned with the nails wherewith our Lord was wounded. At the top of the tree of the cross floats the most beautiful imperial crown of thorns of the realm.

->The thorns are flowered<-
->White as lilies, rose-colored,<-
->Delightful, heaven-clear.<-

This is the banner of the crown, with which Jesus Christ won the victory and returned living to His Father. Immediately after the Last Day in the eternal wedding feast <note>original: "hochgezit," a high festival or wedding celebration</note>, when God has made all things new, then this crown shall be revealed and float above the head of the humanity <term>humanity: the human nature of Jesus, as opposed to His divine nature</term> of our Lord, to the honor and praise of the Holy Trinity and to the joy of all the blessed forevermore.

The humanity of our Lord is a perceptible image of my eternal Divinity. Thus, so that we may perceive the Divinity through the humanity, we enjoy the Holy Trinity equally, embrace and kiss and encompass the incomprehensible Divinity, which neither the kingdom of heaven nor the earth, nor hell nor purgatory <note>original: "vegefür"</note> can ever comprehend or resist.

->The eternal Divinity shines<-
->And glows, and makes filled with the desire of love<-
->All the blessed who are present before Him,<-
->So that they rejoice without labor<-
->And praise forever without heartache.<-
->The humanity of our Lord greets,<-
->Delights and loves without ceasing<-
->His flesh and His blood.<-
->Even though there is now neither flesh nor blood <note>referring to the glorified, spiritualized body after the resurrection</note>,<-
->Yet the brotherly kinship is so great,<-
->That He must especially love<-
->His human nature.<-
->The Holy Spirit also pours out<-
->His loving heavenly flow,<-
->Wherewith He gives drink to the blessed<-
->And so fully saturates them,<-

<header>Part Seven.</header>

There they sing with joy,
Tenderly laughing and leaping
In a graceful manner; they flow and swim,
They fly and climb
From choir to choir and before the heights of the realm.
There they gaze into the mirror of eternity
And recognize the will and the works of the Holy Trinity;
They see how they themselves are formed in body
And in soul, just as they shall remain forevermore.
The soul within the body is formed in the likeness of a human,
And possesses the divine radiance within it,
Shining through the body
As glowing gold shines through clear crystal.
Then they become so joyful and so free,
Swift, powerful, and full of love,
Radiant and God-like
As much as that may be possible.
Thus they travel wherever they wish, covering over a thousand miles
As quickly as one can think a single thought.
Consider what a journey that is!
Yet they can never grasp the end of the kingdom,
Nor touch the vast space and the golden streets;
They are exceedingly large, yet perfectly proportioned;
And yet they are not truly made of gold, for they are eternally better
Than gold and precious stones;
All that is of the earth
Must come to nothing.

### Here follows the conclusion of the crown:

The Holy Spirit is still forging the end of this crown
Until the Last Day;
Then the Father and the Son shall reward Him for His labor.
He shall give Him as a reward all the souls and bodies
That are gathered in God’s kingdom.
There the Holy Spirit shall rest eternally,
And He shall without ceasing greet and gladden them
With all the good that was ever done,
Or ever shall be done, through the love of God;
Everything that is forsaken or suffered for God’s sake,
All of that must stand as a blossom upon the crown.
Oh, what a crown!
Oh, who will help me so that I might yet be
A tiny blossom upon that crown,
Like the wild berries <note>original: "westbaren," likely referring to humble, small forest berries or blossoms</note>, which are the smallest flowers upon the crown!

If this account is at all too long, it is because I found so many kinds of joy in the crown; yet I have shortened many long accounts with...

<header>Chapters I—II.</header>

...set down in few words. This I say of myself: How long will you, wretched world <note>original: *snôde welt*</note>, bark? You must be silent, for I must keep silent about my most beloved.

## *II. How on All Souls' Day a person prayed for the souls in general.*

On All Souls' Day <note>November 2nd, the day the Western Church commemorates the faithful departed</note>, I prayed with all of holy Christendom for the common souls who are performing their penance in purgatory <term>vegevůr: purgatory, a place of cleansing suffering after death</term>. There I became aware of a purgatory that was like an oven; it was black on the outside, and inside it was full of fiery flames. There I looked inside and saw how they stood in the flames and burned like a bundle of straw. There stood one by me who was like a great angel; I asked him why it was that the souls struggled so much to break out whenever prayers came to them from good people. Some broke out, and some could not get out. Then he whom I questioned answered me: "When they were on the earth, they would not help those who begged them in their need." Then my soul took pity beyond its power and its worth, and I cried out to heaven: "Lord God, if only I could go in there to them and suffer with them, so that they might come to You all the sooner!" Then our Lord revealed that He was the angel who stood by me, and He said: "If you wish to go in, then I will go in with you." Then our Lord embraced the person’s spirit and led her inside. When the soul came in with our Lord, she felt no pain. Then she asked how many of them there were? Our Lord said: "You cannot count them; and these are the ones for whom you have prayed while they were on earth."

There I found the one for whom I had been accustomed to pray for thirty years; I was troubled, for I had desired to give myself [as a ransom] for him, and yet because of my wretchedness I did not dare to ask such a great Lord for such great things. Then I spoke a word thus: "Ah, dear Lord, would You release them?" Then they all rose up together in a great multitude, wonderfully, whiter than snow, and they floated toward paradise in a sweet, clear joy <note>original: *wůñe*</note>; there they rested with gladness. As they rose out of the fire, they sang the entire psalm: *Praise the Lord, ye servants* <note>original Latin: *Laudate pueri dominum*, the opening of Psalm 113</note>. After that they sang: "We praise You, Lord, for the greatness of Your goodness <note>original: *gůti*</note>, for the generosity of Your gift, and the faithfulness of Your help."

<summary>The narrator experiences a vision of purgatory as a fiery oven and, accompanied by Christ in the guise of an angel, enters the flames to intercede for suffering souls. Through this divine mercy, a vast multitude of souls is purified and ascends to paradise while singing psalms.</summary>

<header>Part Seven.</header>

Our Lord still stood by the place of fire and had embraced the person’s spirit; then the person’s soul spoke:

> O Lord, You know well what I desire.
> She would have liked to be upon our Lord’s feet,
> So that she might thank Him.
> Then our Lord let her down,
> And she thanked Him in return,
> That she might behold the great honor
> Which had been granted by God to the poor souls.
> There she found upon His feet
> The rose-colored wounds <note>original: *rosevarwen wunden*; a reference to the stigmata, the wounds Christ received during the Crucifixion, often described in mystical literature as blooming like roses</note>
> Of our true redemption <term>lỏsunge: the act of freeing or delivering mankind from sin</term>.
> Then she prayed: Lord, give me Your blessing.
> Then our Lord spoke:
> I bless you with My wounds. —
> May this happen to me
> And to all of God’s friends and mine.
> This has unfortunately not happened through my own labors,
> For I have much of holy Christendom <term>cristanheite: the collective body of the Christian world</term>
> Which is much worthier than my own.

### *III. How useful it is that a person examines their heart without ceasing with humble words.*

I know of no one so good that it is not necessary for them to examine their heart without ceasing and recognize who dwells therein, and also to very frequently scold all of their own works. This should be done with humble words. This the voice of God taught me, for I never performed any work so well that I could not have done it better. This is my scolding; now let us scold our weakness <note>original: *blỏdekeit*; refers here to spiritual frailty or lack of resolve</note> thus: "O you most wretched creature, how long will you harbor your useless habits in your five senses?" Our childhood was foolish; our youth is tempted—how we have triumphed therein is manifest to God. Alas, unfortunately my old age now stands in great need of scolding, for it is useless in visible works and is, alas, cold and lacking in grace. It is also powerless, so that it does not have what youth has, wherewith it may carry the fiery love of God <term>gotzmiñe: the intense, mystical love between the soul and God</term>. It is also impatient, so that small pains hurt it greatly, which youth would not heed. Yet, a good old age is gladly long-suffering and it trusts in God alone.

Seven years ago, a troubled old person lamented this harm to our Lord. Then God answered them thus: "Your childhood...

<page-num>223</page-num>
<header>Cap. II—III.</header>

...as a companion of My holy spirit; your youth was a bride of My humanity, your old age is now a mistress of the house <note>original: *husvrǒwe*; in a medieval context, this refers to the lady or manager of a household, suggesting a mature, stable relationship with God compared to the "bride" of youth</note> of My divinity. —
Alas, dear Lord, how does it help that the dog barks while the host sleeps, so the thief breaks into his house? The prayer of the pure heart still awakens that same dead sinner at times. Alas sinner, how greatly one may weep for you, for you are a murderer of yourself, and you are a harm to all good things and also to their profit! The good person receives great benefit; whenever he sees that another behaves wickedly <note>original: *snŏdet*; to act basely or contemptibly</note> or falls into sin, he looks far around himself so that he might not fall into that same distress; thus the good person improves through bad things, and good works gladly follow after; but the wicked one becomes worse. Whenever he sees bad examples, he becomes so wicked that he scorns good works and good people; then his own perverted wisdom pleases him best.

My dear schoolmaster <note>likely referring to the Holy Spirit or Christ as the inner teacher</note>, who taught me—simple and foolish—this book, he also taught me this speech thus: Whatever a person does, if he is not truthful, you should not be intimate with him. I recognize an enemy who is an extinguisher <term>dilker: one who puts out a light or erases something</term> of divine truth in the human heart. Before one gives him a place, he writes—with the person’s own will—false wisdom into the person's heart and says: "I am by nature angry and weak." —
With that, you cannot excuse yourself before God nor with honor. You shall become gentle and strong through grace. "I have no grace." — Then you shall call upon the merciful God in your lack of grace with humble tears and with constant prayer in holy longing <note>original: *gerunge*; a deep, spiritual yearning or desire</note>, so the worm of anger must die. You should do violence to yourself <note>referring to spiritual self-discipline or asceticism</note>, so no painful violence needs to come over you from God or anyone; then the worm of the seed <note>original: *wurm des kornes*; likely a metaphor for a pest that destroys the "seed" of virtue, mirroring the "worm of anger" mentioned earlier</note> becomes nothing. If we wish to overcome and drive away our anger and all our imperfection with God, then we must truly keep our sinful temptations secretly hidden and show an outwardly holy and joyful demeanor <term>gelesse: outward appearance, bearing, or conduct</term>.

Alas, poor ones! As long as we storm in anger, we have nothing good in us. We must indeed always come back to our heart, then we must be ashamed of our guilt, for anger has consumed our power and has withered our flesh, and

<meta>This page continues the spiritual reflections and dialogues typical of Mechthild’s work. # Part Seven.

...then we have lost our useful time, in which we should have served God. Oh woe, that is an eternal loss! But alas! I regret the sinful tears that one weeps in proud anger <note>original: *homůtigem zorne*; this refers to tears shed out of wounded pride or resentment rather than true contrition</note>. Because of this, the soul becomes so dark that the person cannot rightly enjoy any good things during that time.

Repentant tears are so holy; if a great sinner could weep a single repentant tear for all his sins, he would never go to eternal hell, provided he remained in that state. No matter how small the daily sins a good person has within them, which they will not give up while they live; if they die thus without confession and without penance, no matter how holy they are, they must <note>the manuscript reads: *v́bein*</note> enter into bitter purgatory. For, as merciful as God is, He is also just toward all sins and grief.

This I advise myself: love must dwell there; we should never be near dark things. There, humility gladly makes its home.

### IV. Of Our Lord’s Broom.

When I came to the convent, not long afterward, I was so sorely tormented by sickness that my ladies <note>original: *vrỏwen*; referring to the fellow sisters or perhaps the superiors in the community</note> took pity on me. Then I said to our Lord: "Dear Lord, what do you intend with this pain?" Then our dear Lord spoke thus: "All your ways are measured, all your footsteps are numbered, your life is hallowed, your end shall be joyful, and my kingdom is very near to you." — "Lord, why is my life hallowed when I can do so little good?" Then our Lord spoke: "By this is your life hallowed: that my broom <term>beseme: a broom or rod, used here as a metaphor for the "scouring" or cleansing effect of physical suffering</term> never leaves your back." — *We praise You, O God* <note>original Latin: *Te deum laudamus*</note>, that God is so good.

### V. Why the Convent was at one time Assailed.

They should secretly do good to those who show that they are in need, for the goods that one withholds from them, I will not have in the convent. — This is the gloss <note>original: *glosa*; an explanatory commentary or interpretation of a text</note>: That each person, according to their office <term>ambahte: a specific duty or administrative role within the monastery</term>, should mercifully do good to those whom they know to be in need.

<header>Chapters VI — VII.</header>

## **VI. Of the chapter and how a person should examine their failings and weep for them. Of two golden pennies and of good will and longing.**

Whoever has this knowledge, let them lament and weep with me. For when the chosen children of God often take and sacredly receive the Body of God <note>This refers to the reception of the Eucharist, the central act of Catholic worship</note>, then I must go with a burning conscience into my chapter house <note>original: *capitelhus*; the room in a monastery where the community meets to discuss business and where sisters publicly confess their infractions against the Rule</note>. Then my unworthiness comes and touches me, then my negligence comes and accuses me, then the lightness of my mind comes and reproaches me for my inconstancy, then the wretchedness of my useless life comes and saddens me, then the divine fear comes and scourges me; then I crawl away like a little worm in the earth and hide myself under the grass of my manifold negligence all my days; then I sit and cry out to heaven: "Oh, merciful God! Grant me that today I may share in the grace that your chosen ones have now received."

Here our Lord answers thus: "Take two golden pennies <note>original: *guldine pfenĩng*; here used as a spiritual metaphor for the value of the soul's intent</note> that are both equal in weight and buy with them; if they are worth the same amount, then they are equally good."

"Oh woe, dear Lord, how can my wretchedness equal your goodness? For I am not such that I could grant you the honor you deserve! I have nothing that is fitting for you, and I cling to nothing for my soul's comfort in the world. Thus I am cast out and have become more sorrowful. I am not what I have long desired to be."

Our Lord speaks thus: "With good will and with holy longing <note>original: *gerunge*; a key term in Mechthild's mysticism, describing the soul's active, burning desire for union with the Divine</note>, you can repay as you wish."

## **VII. How a person may be united with God at all times.**

That a person be united with God without ceasing—that is heavenly joy above all earthly pleasure. How shall this happen to us? Our longing should unceasingly move through all our work, and with Christian faith and with divine knowledge we should unceasingly look upon all our works and never be useless; so we live for our Lord God with all our works, because of all His works which He ever performed on earth for the sake of our love. Thus we are united with Him in His earthly works with heavenly love. After this [we] shall become...

->H. Mechthild. 15<-

She concludes by explaining that constant union with God is achieved by dedicating all one's daily work to Him.</summary>

<page-num>226</page-num>
<header>Part Seven.</header>

...spiritually enlightened, then we praise our Lord God with all the gifts that were ever given: our body and goods, friends and kin, and all earthly delights that we might desire. Herewith we thank God for all His generous gifts that He ever gave us on earth, in body or in soul. Thus we are united with God in a particular love and humble gratitude. With this, we should press all of God's gifts into our heart; then our Lord becomes full of love <note>original: *miñenvol*; filled with "Minne," the Middle High German term for sacred, courtly, or divine love</note>, then our senses are opened, and then our soul becomes so clear that we see into divine knowledge, just as a person sees their own face in a clear mirror. Then we may recognize God’s will in all our works—that we honor and love the will of God in painful gifts just as in comforting gifts—and rejoice in what happens to us without sin. We should mourn and hate sin, for it is wasted time. In this way, we are united on earth with the saints in the kingdom of heaven, for they rejoice most of all in the will of God in the heavenly realm.

I do not know how the Enemy <note>original: *vient*; the Devil</note> became aware of this when God gave me this knowledge in the night and I was united therein with great joy, when he came to me and spoke "faithfully," for he wished to deceive me. I heard his voice with my physical ears, and I saw a creature with spiritual eyes: black, filthy, and like a gruesome man. Yet I was not afraid of him. This is why: when God’s gift hovers in the soul and struggles in the senses, the body cannot be afraid in His presence. But when the body is instead struggling in useful works and he comes then, it becomes so painful for the body in his presence that I have never encountered such great pain on earth.

Then he said to me: "I dreamed tonight how I was rich and had much." By this, he wanted me to believe that this holy union of God with the soul was all a dream. Then the mistress of the house <note>original: *husvrowe*; a common mystical metaphor for the soul's role in governing the "house" of the body</note>—the soul of the body—spoke within: "You are not truthful." Then he said: "Indeed, I shall endure as long as God lives." Then the soul said: "Now, you are indeed learned; tell me..."

<page-num>227</page-num>
<header>Chapter VIII.</header>

What should I do? The Devil made it all too much: "You should rejoice and should carry this great thing in a great spirit." *The Soul:* "I am, alas, still so small that I cannot pass through the eye of the needle <note>A reference to the biblical metaphor (Matthew 19:24), here suggesting the narrow and difficult path to salvation amidst spiritual enemies.</note> of all my enemies into the gates of heaven of my eternal land." *The Devil:* "You are far too fenced in." *The Soul:* "In your words I recognize your falsehood, doubt, idle honor, and pride. Even if a wall of steel reached into the clouds all around me, even then my heart would never be safe and free from my enemies." Then he stood there and trembled before me. Oh, how full of falsehood he seemed to me! Then he shook his head and sprang away in a rage.

### VIII. How a person seeks God.

When God wishes to seem a stranger to a person, that person seeks our Lord God and says: "Lord, my pain is deeper than the abyss, my heartache is more bitter than the world, my fear is greater than the mountains, my longing is higher than the stars. In these things, I can find You nowhere." — In this misery, the soul became aware of her Beloved near her, like a beautiful youth, so beautiful that it is beyond words. And yet she would have hidden herself; then she falls at His feet and greets His wounds, which are so sweet that she can feel none of her pain and none of her old age. Then she thought: "Alas, how gladly you would see His face, yet then you would have to leave the wounds; and how gladly you would hear His words and His desire!" Then she stands up, dressed and adorned in unwavering virtue. Then He says: "Welcome, my most beloved!" In the sound of that word, she recognized that every soul who serves God in His grace is the most beloved to Him. Then He said: "I must spare you in the enjoyment <term>*Bruchunge*: a technical term in medieval mysticism referring to 'fruition'—the direct, blissful experience of God’s presence.</term> of both yourself and Me." "The enjoyment is beyond words." — Then He said: "Take this crown of virgins." Then the crown came from Him and went onto her head, shining as if it were made of pure gold. The crown was twofold and was also the crown of love <note>original: *miñen crone*; referring to "Minne," the sacred, courtly love between the soul and God.</note>. Then our Lord said: "This crown shall be visible before all the heavenly host." Then she prayed: "Lord, if You will receive my soul tomorrow, just as I have received Your holy body..."

Then He spoke: "You shall become even richer through suffering." — "Lord, what should I do here in this convent?" <note>Mechthild spent her later years at the Cistercian convent of Helfta; this question reflects her search for purpose in her communal life.</note> — "You shall enlighten and teach them, and you shall remain with them in great honor." — Then she thought: "Oh, now you are here alone with our Lord." In that thought she saw two angels standing by her, who were as distinguished as earthly princes are before other poor people. Then she spoke: "How shall I hide myself now?" Then they spoke: "We want to bring you from pain to pain, from virtue to virtue, from knowledge to knowledge, from love <term>love: original 'miñe', referring to the specific medieval concept of mystical, courtly, and divine love</term> to love." — That a sinful mouth should and must speak this is heavy for me, and yet I dare not leave it because of God; and before obedience, human shame, and divine fear, I must keep this all my days.

### IX. *How the loving soul praises our Lord with all creatures.*

The loving soul never tires of praise; therefore, she gathers into herself all that God ever created in her longing and then calls into heaven: "Lord, if all these persons were as perfect and as holy—if it were possible—as your blessed mother Mary, yet it would not suffice for me, poor as I am, that I could not fully praise you with your only-begotten Son." "Lord, can one fully praise You?" "No, and of this I rejoice." Then our Lord answered thus: "The virgins who have served me long, they shall praise me."

### X. *This happened at a time when there was great unrest.*

I prayed to our Lord God for the distress of war <note>original: 'vrlůges not', likely referring to local conflicts or the broader political instability in the 13th-century Holy Roman Empire</note> and for the many sins of the world; then our Lord answered thus and spoke: "The sins stink to me, from the abyss of the earth into heaven. If it were possible, they would drive me out. Sins once drove me out; then I came humbly and served the world until my death; now that can no longer happen. Now I must at times establish my justice <gloss>sic: the original uses 'buwen', meaning to build or cultivate, which is an unusual phrasing for 'exercising' justice</gloss> because of the sins." — "Dear Lord, what shall we poor ones do now?" Then our Lord spoke: "You shall humble yourselves under the trembling...
