Lord, heavenly Father, receive your service and your praise from your troubled children and release your people from this present distress, and release us from all our bonds, except alone the bonds of love <note>original: 'miñe', referring to the mystical bond between the soul and God</note>, which must never be taken from us.

### XI. How our Lord was seen in the likeness of a working man.

Our Lord showed me a parable <term>parable: original 'glichnisse', a likeness or metaphorical vision used to explain spiritual truths</term> which he has fulfilled in me and still does. I saw a poor man standing on the earth, who was dressed in poor linen cloths like a working man. He had a carrying-board in his hands, upon which lay a burden like the earth. Then I spoke: "Good man, what are you carrying?" "I carry," he said, "your pain. Turn your will toward the pain and lift and carry." Then the person <note>the 'mensche' here refers to the visionary or the soul in its human aspect</note> spoke: "Lord, indeed I am so poor that I have nothing." Then our Lord spoke: "Thus I taught my disciples when I said: *Blessed are the poor in spirit.* <note>original: 'Beati pauperes spiritu' (Matthew 5:3)</note> That is, when a person is unable to act but would gladly do so, that is spiritual poverty." *The person:* "Lord, is it you? Turn your face toward me, that I may recognize you." Then our Lord spoke: "Recognize me within." *The soul:* "Lord, if I saw you among thousands, I would recognize you well. My heart has built me inside into a vessel <note>or 'a safe journey/passage' (vare)</note>, and I dared not acknowledge to him who he was." Then I spoke: "Dear Lord, this burden is too heavy for me." Then our Lord spoke: "I will lay it so near to me that you may easily carry it. Follow me, and see how I stood before my heavenly Father on the cross and remained so." Then she spoke: "Lord, for this give me your blessing." — "I bless you without un-

### XII. *How a person should resist vain glory and temptation.*

Whenever a person thinks something good of themselves, vain glory <term>vain glory: original 'ital êre', referring to pride or seeking empty praise for one's spiritual achievements</term> immediately comes springing from the corner of the secret <note>original: 'himelschen', likely a scribal error or variant for 'heimlichen' (secret/inner), as vanity typically arises from the hidden parts of the heart</note> heart with a sinful pleasure and seeks to establish itself within the five senses. Then the person should restrain their mind and immediately strike before their heart with humble fear and bless themselves with the sign of the holy cross; then that glory will immediately come to nothing, as if it had never existed. I, poor woman, have often experienced this. One should do the same thing immediately whenever evil, fleeting thoughts come. They also vanish through the power of the holy cross, provided the person feels regret for them.

### XIII. *How our Lord was seen in the likeness of a pilgrim.*

I, a poor and unworthy woman, renounce myself and speak of what I have seen and heard in God. One night, I saw our Lord standing in the likeness of a pilgrim, and he acted as if he had traveled throughout all of Christendom. Then I fell at his feet and said: "My dear pilgrim, where do you come from?" Then he said: "I come from Jerusalem (by which he meant Christendom), and I have been driven out of my lodging. The heathens did not know me; the Jews do not want me; the Christians attack me."

Then I prayed for Christendom. Our Lord then excused himself most beautifully regarding the great contempt he suffers from Christendom, and he explained how much good he has done for Christendom from the beginning, and how much he has labored for Christendom, and how he still seeks a place within them every day where he might pour his grace into them. Then our Lord lamented again and said: "By their own free will <term>free will: original 'willekůre', emphasizing the human capacity to choose to accept or reject God</term>, people drive me from the lodging of their hearts; and when I find no place in them, I let them remain in their own self-will, and when they die, as I find them then, so I judge them."

Then I prayed for the community: "Dear Lord, do not let them perish; I will set a light in their churchyard <note>original: 'frithof'. While usually meaning a cemetery, here it likely refers to the spiritual grounds or the communal living space of the convent</note> so they may recognize themselves by it."

<summary>This page contains two mystical visions: a teaching on using the sign of the cross to dispel vanity, and a poignant encounter with Christ as a rejected pilgrim seeking a home in the hearts of believers.</summary>

<header>Chapters XII—XV.</header>

### *XIV. Of God’s choosing and blessing.*

On another night, while I was at my prayers and in a state of longing, expecting nothing, I became aware of our Lord. He stood in the churchyard <term>churchyard: original 'frithove', literally a 'yard of peace,' referring to the consecrated ground surrounding the church</term> and had before him the entire community, arranged exactly as they had first entered the convent. Then our Lord spoke to them: "I have chosen you; if you choose me, then I will give to you." Then I said: "Lord, what will you give them?" He said: "I will make them into shining mirrors upon the earth, so that all who desire them shall recognize their own lives through them. And in the kingdom of heaven, I will make them into glowing mirrors, so that all who see them shall recognize how I have chosen them."

Then our Lord reached out his hand and gave them his blessing, saying: "I bless you with myself; you shall keep me in all your thoughts." — Those who wish to have our Lord in all their thoughts are the blessed ones who praise our Lord rightly. Then I said: "They will ask me in what form I saw you." He replied: "There are some among them who already recognize me."

### *XV. How the person who loves the truth should pray.*

The person who loves the truth gladly prays like this: "O dear Lord, grant me and help me to seek you without ceasing with all my five senses, secretly in all things, for I have chosen you above all lords, and I have chosen you above all princes as the bridegroom of my soul. Grant me also, Lord, that I may find you with all my longing, whether it be burning or extinguished. I also desire that I may enjoy you through the flowing love of all your gifts. Give me, Lord, the full return-flow <term>return-flow: original 'widervlus', a mystical term for the cyclical movement of grace where God's love flows into the soul and the soul's love flows back to God</term> of yourself; may it fill <note>The original manuscript reads 'er vollen', which the editor has corrected to 'ervolle' (to fill/fulfill).</note> my mouth, so that pain, scorn, bitterness, and misery always seem sweet to me. May this always happen to me through your grace; merciful God, grant it to me now. Help me also, Lord, to keep you by renouncing my entire will for the sake of your honor, so that I may never again lose unquenchable love." Amen.

<header>Seventh Part.</header>

### XVI. *How a person speaks and prays.*

A person longed, above all gifts and above all pain, for God to release their soul with a holy end <note>holy end: a peaceful death in a state of grace, allowing the soul to finally join God</note>. Then our Lord spoke: "Wait for me." Then the person said: "Dear Lord, I cannot control my longing; I would so gladly be with you." Then our Lord spoke: "I have longed for you before the beginning of the world; I long for you and you long for me. Where two burning longings come together, there love <term>love: original 'miñe', referring to the specialized Middle High German concept of 'Minne'—a reciprocal, often mystical love between the soul and God</term> is perfected."

### XVII. *How Knowledge speaks to Conscience.*

Knowledge <term>Knowledge: original 'bekantnisse', meaning spiritual recognition or self-awareness</term> spoke to Conscience: "However much people mock you and cause you pain, you still stand pure in God."
*Conscience.* Lady Knowledge, you have spoken a good little word. Whenever all one's troubles <note>The original manuscript likely intended 'wirrenisse' (troubles/confusions) despite the scribal error 'wenisse'</note> weigh upon them, they must have a humble heart.
*Knowledge.* Lady Conscience, you have such a noble mirror in which you look at yourself so often. That may well be the living Son of God with all His works. It could not be otherwise, for you are so wise.
*Conscience.* Lady Knowledge, whenever I <unclear>...</unclear> <note>Lücke in der Handschrift: a gap exists in the original manuscript here</note> I feel both well and woe; well, because God is the flowing goodness toward me; woe, because I am so small in good works.
*Knowledge:* Lady Conscience, in all things you love God's will and God's honor more than your own benefit in body and soul; you are the devil's hell and God's kingdom; what then can be compared to you? <note>the devil's hell: a striking mystical image suggesting that a holy soul is a place of torment for the devil, just as it is a heaven for God</note>
*Conscience.* Lady Knowledge, everything that I have from God, He has given to me as a loan, so that I may use it to earn His praise and His honor and also my own benefit; for when I must give it back to Him, I shall certainly need His grace.
*Knowledge:* Lady Conscience, you are bound tightly by the sins of the world, and the imperfection of spiritual people causes you—

<header>Chapters XVI—XVIII.</header>

...to you many heart-aches. They have free will, so that they may travel to the kingdom of heaven or to hell, or into the long purgatory <term>purgatory: original 'vegefür', a place of purification after death</term>; that is also a heavy burden to you.

*Conscience:* Lady Knowledge, I do not complain that I have ill-will or that I suffer pain. I grieve for the world's sins in the same way that the pain of love <term>love: original 'miñe', referring to a deep, mystical love for God</term> purifies the body from sins and sanctifies the soul in God; thus we wish to stand with joy at His command.

*Knowledge:* Lady Conscience, the well-meaning wealthy of the world offer God their goods and their alms; the clergy offer God their flesh and blood in His service; above all things, they offer God their own will in obedience. The more something weighs, the more it must be worth <note>Knowledge argues that the sacrifice of one's own will is the "heaviest" and therefore most valuable offering to God</note>.

*Conscience:* Lady Knowledge, this alone is not enough; if we wish to enjoy God in the heights, then we must have the crown of humility <term>humility: 'diemůtekeit'</term> and purity <term>purity: 'luterkeit'</term>, of chastity <term>chastity: 'kůscheit'</term>—whether innate or adopted—and the height of love above all things. The Holy Trinity itself wears this same wondrous garment: the Father, the height of love; the Son, the humble and pure chastity, which He has shared with all His chosen ones; the Holy Spirit, the burning of love toward us in all our good works.

*Knowledge:* Lady Conscience, constancy in good things is a laboring love that one cannot do without, if one wishes to possess the highest honors with God in both places: here and in His eternal kingdom. Blessed is he who applies himself to this.

### XX. How one should entrust the Hail Mary to our Lady.

## XXI. *How a person should examine their heart before they go to God’s table.* <term>God’s table: 'gotz tische', a common medieval metaphor for the Altar or the celebration of Holy Communion.</term>

You wish to have teaching from me, and I myself am unlearned. Whatever you might desire, you will find a thousandfold in your books.

Whenever I, a poor woman, go there and must receive the body of our Lord, I gaze upon the face of my soul in the mirror of my sins. <note>original: 'spiegel miner súnden'; mirrors were a frequent motif in medieval literature used for moral self-examination and seeing the true state of the soul.</note> There I see within myself how I have lived, how I live now, and how I still wish to live. In this mirror of my sins, I see nothing inside but "Oh woe!" and "Oh woe!" Then I cast my face to the earth and lament and weep if I can, that the eternal, incomprehensible God is so good that He wishes to bow down into the filthy pool of my heart. <term>filthy pool: 'vnvletigen pfůl', a strong expression of the mystic's sense of spiritual impurity compared to the divine.</term> Then I think thus: that it would be more just by right that my body be dragged to the gallows like a thief who has squandered <note>original: 'verstossen'; literally pushed away or rejected.</note> from his rightful Lord the precious treasure of purity, which God gave to me in holy baptism.

> For this we wish to lament piteously
> All the while we live,
> That we have often darkened our souls;
> This You must, Lord, forgive us fatherly.
> Whatever sin a person has not confessed, nor even wishes to confess,
> With that sin they should not receive God’s body.
> Now I wish to step into true hope
> And thank God for this, that I was ever seen, <note>original: 'gesehen'; likely implying being acknowledged or brought into existence by God’s gaze.</note>
> That this may happen to me, a poor soul,
> That I must receive God’s body.
> Now I wish to go with joy to God’s table,
> And I wish to receive the same bloody lamb,
> That willed to stand upon the holy cross,
> Bloody and unbound, <note>original: 'vnvérbunden'; meaning his wounds were open and not bandaged or dressed.</note>
> With His holy five wounds.
> Blessed are we that this ever happened!
> In His holy suffering
> I will lament all my hardship.
> So let us go then with joy and with heartfelt love,
> And with an open soul, and receive our Beloved,

<vocab>God's table, body (Eucharist), mirror of my sins, filthy pool, purity, baptism, joys, bloody lamb, five wounds</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the spiritual preparation for Holy Communion, where the author uses the "mirror of sins" to reflect on her unworthiness before approaching "God's table." It concludes with a poetic passage celebrating the sacrifice of Christ as the "bloody lamb" whose wounds offer comfort for human hardship.</summary>

<header>Part Seven.</header>

Our most heart-dearest beloved,
And lay him in our soul
As if in a sweet, soothing cradle, <note>original: 'sůsse sůssende wagen'; literally a sweet, lulling wagon or carriage, here used as a metaphor for a cradle.</note>
And then sing him praise and honor
For the first hardship he chose to suffer,
When he lay in the manger.
Then we bow before him with our soul
And with our five senses
And thank our beloved and speak thus:
Lord, I thank you for yourself.
Now I pray you, dear beloved,
That you might give me your treasures, <term>treasures: 'cleinôter', often referring to spiritual jewels or the merits of Christ’s life.</term>
That I may live purely,
Apart from all sins.
Lord, where shall I then lay you?
Whatever I have, I will give to you.
I will lay you upon my bed.
This little bed is entirely pain; <note>original: 'pin'; the mystic views their own suffering as the place where they can truly host Christ.</note>
Whenever I think of your passion,
Then I forget my own.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You shall, my Lord, be the bolster for my support. <note>original: 'hůffe'; literally "hips," but in the context of allegorical bedding, it suggests a supportive cushion or bolster.</note>
The pillow is my heart-sorrow,
That I am not ready at all times
To receive your painful gifts; <note>original: 'pinlichen gabe'; referring to the trials and sufferings sent by God as spiritual refining fires.</note>
For this, Lord, is my entire lament.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;This bed’s cover is my longing,
With which I am bound.
If you wish now, Lord, to quiet me,
Then do my will,
And give me the sinners who are in mortal sins, <term>mortal sins: 'hỏbetsůnden', the 'head' or cardinal sins that are considered most spiritually dangerous.</term>
Then you will gladden my soul.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Lord, how shall we now speak of love,
Since we lie so close together
In the bed of my pain.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I have received you, Lord,
Just as you rose from death upon the earth.
Dear heart-beloved, now comfort my spirit,
That I may stand purely by you without ceasing;
Great blessedness follows after that.
Give me, Lord, the guilty souls from purgatory <note>original: 'vegefůr'; the place of cleansing before entering heaven.</note>
To me alone; the ransom for them is so very dear. <note>original: 'alzetůr'; literally "all too dear" or "too costly."</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Now I have received you, Lord,
As you have ascended into heaven.
Now you must not, dear beloved, spare me too much.
I must surely die of love; <term>die of love: 'sterben von miñe', the mystical ideal of the soul's self-extinction through total union with God.</term>
You can never, Lord, quiet me in any other way.

<summary>This page uses the intimate metaphor of a bed to describe the soul's union with Christ during or after Communion, where the soul's suffering and longing become the bedding for the Savior. The author intercedes for the souls in Purgatory and expresses a desire to "die of love" through mystical union.</summary>

->It is my belief that you are<-

->I lament to you all my heart’s sorrow. — *Hail Queen.*<- <note>original: "Salve regina." This is a famous Marian hymn (antiphon) traditionally sung at the end of the day, asking for Mary's intercession in this "valley of tears."</note>

## XXVII. How the spiritual person should turn their heart away from the world.

When the spiritual person sees their kin <term>kin: 'mage', referring to family and social relations who might still live a secular life.</term> and their dearest friend before them, beautifully adorned and dressed according to the fashions of the world, they have great need to be armed with the Holy Spirit, so that they do not think: "You could have done just as they did!" From such a thought, their heart becomes as dark and their senses as unprepared for God, and their spirit so sluggish toward holy prayer, and their soul so truly exiled <note>original: 'ellende'. While it means 'miserable' today, in this context it means 'in a foreign land' or 'exiled' from its true home with God.</note> from God, that they then become inwardly more like their worldly relatives than a spiritual person.

->If they wish to stand pure with God,<-
->Then it must come to a struggle,<-
->For then their conscience is troubled,<- <term>conscience: 'gewissede'. In medieval mysticism, the conscience is the mirror or vessel that reflects God's light.</term>
->Which is a lantern of the Holy Spirit,<- <note>original: 'lůhtevas', literally a 'light-vessel' or lantern. Mechthild uses this image to show how the soul's interior must be clear to hold the divine flame.</note>
->For the conscience does not shine<-
->Without the Holy Spirit’s light.<-
->When the light is beautifully kindled in the lantern,<-
->Then the beauty of the lantern is clearly known.<-
->So it is with the spiritual person,<-
->To whom all the world’s finery<-
->Is an abomination in their heart;<- <note>original: 'eisunge', a strong word for loathing or a shuddering horror.</note>
->They keep their lantern beautiful and unextinguished;<-
->But if their heart is open toward the world,<-
->Then their lantern is broken,<-
->Then comes the bitter north wind of greed<-
->Of the world from our relatives,<-
->So that they complain much to us,<-
->That they have too little of the mire,<- <note>original: 'pfůles', meaning a slough, puddle, or mud. A common derogatory term for earthly wealth and property.</note>
->In which they, alas, sink,<-
->And drown in their sins.<-
->This extinguishes our light<-
->And yet we still have nothing of the world.<-
->Afterward comes the wind of sin,<-
->The false pleasure of the world, though it shines brightly,<-
->And yet contains many bitter pains;<-
->If this should please us,<-
->Then we suffer eternal harm.<-
->We should gladly guard against this,<-
->For no sin is so small...<-

<sig>H. Mechthild. 16</sig>

<summary>This page discusses the spiritual danger of envying the worldly wealth and status of one's relatives, using the metaphor of a lantern to describe how the Holy Spirit illuminates a pure conscience. It warns that longing for secular finery "breaks" the soul's vessel, leaving the person with neither spiritual light nor actual worldly gain.</summary>

<page-num>242</page-num>
<header>Part Seven.</header>

Let it <note>The "it" refers to the worldly desire or social envy discussed on the previous page.</note> be an eternal harm to our soul. Why? No sin was ever so completely atoned for <note>original: "gewandelt". In this context, it suggests the transformation of a sin through penance.</note> that it would not have been better to leave it undone. Therefore, we must live in constant fear ¹) whether we can stand sternly before God. What we have given to God <note>This refers to the vows of religious life or the gift of one's will.</note>, we can never take back from Him without harm to ourselves, for we have given it to Him honorably.

The fish in the water looks with great desire at the red bait with which one wants to catch him; but he does not see the hook. So it is with the world's poison; it does not recognize its own harm. If you now wish to truly return, then look upon your Bridegroom, the Lord of all the world—see how beautifully he stood clothed in silken garments <note>original: "pfellorinen cleidern". 'Pfeller' was a luxury silk fabric. Here, Mechthild uses it as a daring metaphor for Christ's flayed skin and blood.</note>: red blood and black bruising, beaten with whips, bound to the pillar. There, for the sake of your love, he received many sharp wounds.

->Let this sink into your heart,<-
->So you may escape the world’s deceptions.<-

If you wish to follow further with your holy thoughts, then look up at how he stood on the cross, raised up high before the eyes of all the world, streaming with blood. Those "garments" <note>Referring back to the blood and wounds.</note> shall be your heart’s delight <term>delight: 'wuñe'. A central term in German mysticism meaning supreme joy or bliss.</term>. His imperial eyes were overflowing with tears; his sweet heart was pierced through with love. ²) Now hear also the voice; the love of God <term>love of God: 'gotzmiñe'. 'Minne' specifically refers to the reciprocal, often ecstatic love between the soul and God.</term> teaches you how the smiths’ hammers knocked and struck through his hands and feet on the cross.

->Think also of the spear’s wound,<-
->Which went through the side to the depths of his heart,<-

And lament to him all your sins; thus you will gain knowledge of God. Look at the sharp crown that he wore upon his head; kiss him above all things, and he will give you plenty of all delights.

<header>Chapter XXVIII.</header>

Thank Him for how He chose to die.
Through your great love
And let no one deceive you,
So you may be a queen of His kingdom forever.
If you wish to choose <note>original: "kriesen" (to choose/strive for)</note> this, you will overcome
With joy all the world’s heartache.

### XXVIII. Concerning the distress of a war.

I was commanded with a holy earnestness that I should pray for the distress that is now in Saxony and Thuringia <note>original: "Sahsenlanden und in Dúringenlanden". Mechthild lived during the Great Interregnum, a period of extreme lawlessness and internal conflict in the Holy Roman Empire.</note>. When I offered myself with praise and longing, our dear Lord did not wish to receive me, and He remained silent with a grave stillness. I had to endure this for seventeen days with loving patience. Then I said to our dear Lord: "Ah, dear Lord, when shall the favorable hour <note>original: "behegeliche stunde"</note> come when You will—and I must—pray for this distress?" Then our Lord revealed Himself to me and said:

> The delightful morning-red
> With many colors—these are the poor
> Who now suffer many kinds of distress.
> After this, the eternal sun of the eternal light shall rise,
> Which shall shine upon them with eternal joy after this distress.
> Thereby they shall be sanctified
> And purified like the dancing sun,
> As it presses upward toward mid-morning
> And treads upon the heights.
> Such are those among the lords <note>original: "hêr". Refers to the nobility or ruling class involved in the fighting.</note>
> Who are in distress and in fear;
> I allow them to be captured and shorn of life's comforts <note>original: "libelof". Suggests being deprived of property, status, or physical health.</note>,
> So that they might come to Me.
> Those who are the cause of the war <term>vrlûg: war or armed conflict</term>,
> They are gruesome in themselves
> And fierce in their deeds,
> For they dared to attack the images of my house of God <note>original: "bilde mines gotzhuses". This likely refers to the desecration of churches or the killing of the faithful, who are the "living images" of God.</note>.
> Then I recognized that eternal death follows them.
> Those who rob on the roads on foot—
> If there were no war, they would still be thieves and false people.
> Thus the wicked always make the blessed good <note>A common medieval theme: the suffering caused by the wicked serves to purify and "make" the saints.</note>.
> Thus must God love His own with pain;
> He cannot win them in any other way.
> Thus has God spoken to me concerning the upright,
> And I do not know how or where it shall end.
> I know this well for a truth:
> That I shall yet heartily please God’s friends.

<sig>16*</sig>

<summary>Mechthild receives a divine revelation regarding the civil wars in Saxony and Thuringia, explaining that God uses the suffering of the poor and the capture of the nobility to draw them toward Him, while the instigators of war face eternal death.</summary>
<keywords>Saxony, Thuringia, war, prophecy, suffering, Interregnum, the poor, divine justice, prayer</keywords>

<header>Seventh Part.</header>

I truly know how God's friends <note>original: "gotz vrůnde". The "Friends of God" was a 14th-century lay and clerical mystical movement in the Rhineland and Low Countries.</note> suffer,
That He never forgets them,
For He is their help and their comfort in all their distress.
Therefore we should strive <note>original: "kriegen", meaning to struggle or strive for a goal</note> and gladly suffer with joy,
So we may gleam and shine before God.

### XXIX. *Concerning a teaching.*

If you wish to turn your heart entirely to God,
Then you should hold three things as a teaching:
Fear all sins,
Be well-disposed toward all virtues,
Be steadfast in all good things,
Then you may bring your life to a good end.
If you wish to compel yourself toward this,
Then you may well accomplish it with God’s help.
Pray to God constantly for this,
Then you will carry all your sorrow easily.
Pray purely and serve God with diligence,
Then you will be rich in joy.

### XXX. *A prayer for when the virgins are crowned.* <note>The "crowning" refers to the ritual of a nun taking her final vows, symbolizing her marriage to Christ as a "bride of God."</note>

Receive, Lord, Your brides ¹) and meet them with the lilies of pure chastity all their days.
Receive, Lord, Your brides and meet them with the roses of diligent labor toward a good end.
Receive, Lord, Your brides and meet them with the violet of unfathomable <note>original: "grundelosen" (literally bottomless)</note> humility and lead them into Your bridal bed and embrace them with all love, never to be separated.

### XXXI. *Concerning a lament.* ²)

This is the lament of the loving soul,
Which she cannot bear alone;
She must tell it to God’s friends,
So that the service of Minne <term>Minne: A Middle High German term for mystical or courtly love, often used to describe the soul's intense longing for God.</term> may please them.
Sick with love and weak in body,
Pain, distress, and hard compulsion,
These make the way too long for me
To my dear Lord.
How shall I, beloved, do without You for so long?
Yes, I am, alas, all too far from You.

> If You, Lord, will not receive my lament <note>original: "clage"; a formal expression of grief or a legal-spiritual appeal</note>,
> Then I must return into my mourning <note>original: "truren"</note>,

> **Our Lord.** When I come, I come in greatness.

> That I could not heal it.
> You must wait yet longer,
> I will prepare you better,
> Before I bring you before my Father,

> Whenever our human senses become dark,
> We awaken divine love
> In our hearts with this lament.

## XXXII. How the works of the good person shine in relation to the works of our Lord.

How the works of the good person shall glow and shine in heavenly glory; note these words:

In proportion to how we have been innocent here, so shall God's innocence shine and glow in our holy innocence.

In proportion to how we labor here in good works, so shall God's holy labor <note>original: "arbeit"</note> shine and glow in our holy labor.

In proportion to how we have inwardness <note>original: "iñekeit"; a technical term in Rhineland mysticism for deep, focused spiritual devotion</note> here in God in secret, so shall God's holy inwardness shine and gleam in our holy inwardness in manifold ways.

In proportion to how we receive our pain <note>original: "pine"</note> here with gratitude and suffer it with patience, so shall God's holy pain shine and glow in our pain.

In proportion to how we have practiced all virtues <note>original: "tugende"</note> here with diligence, so shall God's holy virtues shine and glow in our virtues in manifold glory. This shall be forever and ever.

In proportion to how we burn and glow here in love <note>original: "miñe"</note> in a holy life, so shall God's love burn and glow in our souls and in our bodies without ceasing, never to be extinguished for all eternity.

> These reflections <note>original: "gegenblike"; literally "counter-glances," suggesting that human virtues act as mirrors for God's own attributes</note> shine and glow from the eternal Godhead.
> These good works we have received from God's holy humanity <note>Refers to Christ's earthly life as the model for human action</note>.
> And we have completed them with the assistance <note>original: "volleist"</note> of the Holy Spirit.

<summary>This page concludes a dialogue where Christ encourages the suffering soul to wait for divine union. It then outlines a theology of "mirroring," where human innocence, labor, and love are reflected and perfected by God’s own attributes in eternity.</summary>
<keywords>Minne, inwardness, divine mirroring, Godhead, humanity of Christ, suffering, virtues, Friends of God</keywords>

--- Page 278 ---
<meta>This page concludes Chapter XXXII regarding how earthly virtues are transformed in heaven and begins a series of reflections on "spiritual medicine"—specifically a drink made of bitter suffering and roots of virtue, likely authored by the mystic Mechthild von Magdeburg.</meta>

<page-num>246</page-num>
<header>Part Seven.</header>

Thus our works and our life return again into the Holy Trinity. 
There it will be made manifest how it stands with us now here on earth. 
According to how we live here secretly in divine love <note>original: "miñe"; the specific medieval tradition of mystical, courtly love between the soul and God</note>, 
accordingly shall we soar there in high bliss, 
and according to that, the power of love shall be given to us there as a reward, 
so that we become powerful in doing all our will, 
that we may be known by the saints as we have been; 
herewith we must be their companions. Amen.

### XXXIII. Of the spiritual drink. <note>original footnote 1: Greith p. 265. Carl Greith was a 19th-century scholar who published early editions of these mystical texts.</note>

I am sick; I long greatly for a healthy drink, 
which Jesus Christ himself drank. 
When he, God and man, came into the manger, 
there this drink was immediately prepared for him. 
He drank so much of it 
that he was so fire-drunken with love <note>original: "miñenvůrig trunken"; a classic mystical image of being intoxicated with divine passion</note>, 
that he went in all virtues through <note>original footnote 2: The manuscript reads "vir," likely a scribal error for "vůr" (through)</note> all his heartache. 
He always gave virtue; his goodness never became sick.
> I long for this healthy drink.
This drink is pain for the sake of God's love: 
The pain is bitter, 
so we grind into it a root called: *willing suffering*. 
The other root is called *patience in pain*, 
which is also bitter; 
so we grind into it a root called *holy inwardness* <note>original: "iñekeit"; a technical term for deep, focused spiritual devotion</note>, 
which makes patience sweet, and all our labor. 
The third root is to wait long in pain 
for our eternal life and our salvation; 
that is also very bitter. 
So we grind into it a root called: *unwearied with joy*. 
Ah, dear Lord, if You would give me this drink, 
then I could live unwearied with joy in pain. 
Then I would for a time do without the kingdom of heaven, 
so sweet is my desire for it. 
Now You must give this to me, Lord, 
according to Your most beloved will, 
and to all those who desire it through Your love.

### XXXIV. Of the spiritual food.

After a bitter drink, one has need of gentle food. Rising longing and sinking humility <note>original: "diemůtekeit"</note> and the flow—

<page-num>247</page-num>
<header>Chapters XXXIII — XXXV.</header>

...send love <note>original: "miñe"; the specific medieval German term for mystical, divine love</note>; these three virgins bring the soul up to heaven before God, and then she becomes aware of her Beloved. Then she says: "Lord, I lament that you are so severely attacked by the dearest thing you have on earth, which is the Christian person. Lord, I complain to you that your friends are so greatly hindered by your enemies."

*Our Lord:* "If they have true goodness within them, everything that happens to them without sin, they consume blissfully into the true knowledge of God. Therefore, pain cries out loudest: 'Beyond all divine service, yield to me!' For it is better that the person remains uncomforted according to the will of God than that the person were comforted according to their own will. God’s will is pure; our will is heavily mixed with the flesh. All who love deeply within are stilled on the outside, for all outward labor hinders the inward spirit. That which the spirit then sings within goes above all earthly voices."

Patience <note>original: "gedult"</note> sings most beautifully above all the choirs of angels, for the angels have no patience, as they feel no pain. We have this from the humanity of our Lord, as well as all the honor with which we are honored by God on earth and with which we shall be exalted in the kingdom of heaven. By the noble labor of our Lord and by His holy suffering, our Christian labor and our willing suffering are ennobled and sanctified, in the same way as all waters are sanctified by the Jordan, where our dear Lord was baptized.

Ah, dear Lord, help us, so that our holy longing may never rest <note>original: "gerůwen"</note> <margin>Manuscript: rest.</margin> and our sinking humility may never raise itself up with pride <term>homůte|Arrogance or high-mindedness; the spiritual opposite of the "sinking" humility Mechthild praises</term>, and let the flowing burning of holy divine love <note>original: "gotzmiñe"</note> be our purgatory <term>vegfůr|Purgatory: Here used metaphorically; the fire of love cleanses the soul on earth so it might bypass the afterlife's cleansing fire</term> here, in which all our sins may be blotted out.

### XXXV. Of the seven psalms. <margin>The Penitential Psalms.</margin>

Dear Lord Jesus Christ, I speak these holy seven psalms to the praise and honor of all your holy suffering, in which you were willing to die for me on the holy cross.

<summary>The text describes the soul’s ascent to God through personified virtues and explains how earthly suffering, when united with Christ's passion, becomes a sanctifying "purgatory" of love. It emphasizes that patience is a uniquely human virtue that even angels cannot possess because they do not suffer.</summary>

<page-num>248</page-num>
<header>Seventh Part.</header>

> Most beloved, I pray to you, when the time comes that you wish to fulfill your command upon me through my death, that you will then come to me as a faithful physician <note>original: "arzat"</note> to his child. And give me then, Lord, a holy illness, in which I may prepare myself with right senses and with true Christian faith. — O Lord, rebuke me not in your anger. <note>original: "Domine ne in furore," referring to Psalm 6</note>
> 
> I pray you, most beloved Lord, that you then will come as my most beloved friend in my time of need; and bring me then, Lord, such true repentance that all my sins are blotted out therein, so that after this life I may remain untroubled. — Blessed are they whose transgressions are forgiven. <note>original: "Beati quorum remis," referring to Psalm 32</note>
> 
> I pray you, most beloved Lord, that you then will come as a faithful confessor <note>original: "bihter"</note> to his dear friend, and bring me then the true light, the gift of your Holy Spirit, so that I may see and recognize myself therein, and lament all my sins from my heart with such holy hope that my spirit may be bound [free] from all my sins and that I be found pure. And give me then, Lord, your own body, so that I may then receive you, most beloved, with as great a love as a human heart can ever hold; so that you must then remain the <term>provision for the journey <note>original: "wegespise"; the "viaticum" or final communion given to the dying to sustain the soul on its way to the afterlife</note></term> of my wretched soul, so that I, most beloved, remain your dear companion with you unto eternal love. Amen. — O Lord, rebuke me not in your anger. <note>original: "Domine ne in furore," referring here to Psalm 38</note>
> 
> I pray you, dear Lord, that you then will come as a faithful brother to his dear sister—and bring me the holy armor <note>original: "waffenkleit"; literally a 'weapon-garment' or surcoat worn over armor, here symbolizing spiritual protection</note> with which my soul may be prepared, so that my enemies may not harm me when they wish to accuse me, and so that they must then be ashamed of all the labor they have spent against me. — Have mercy on me, O God. <note>original: "Miserere mei deus," referring to Psalm 51</note>
> 
> I pray you Lord, that you will come to me as a faithful father to his dear child, and then guard my end.

### XXXVI. Of a spiritual cloister.

I asked this of God, if it were his will, that he let me understand it, so that I should write no more. Why? Because I know myself now to be just as wretched and unworthy as I was thirty years ago and more, when I first had to begin. Then our Lord showed me a little pouch <note>original: "sekelin"</note> in his hand and said: "I have more herbs <note>original: "wurzen," literally roots or spices used for healing and seasoning</note>." Then I said: "Lord, I do not recognize the herbs." Then he said: "You shall recognize them well when you see them. One shall refresh the sick with them, strengthen the healthy, awaken the dead, and sanctify the good with them." After this I saw a spiritual cloister, which was built with virtues. ¹)

<header>Part Seven.</header>

The **Abbess** is true **Love** <note>original: "miñe," the Middle High German term for divine love</note>,
Who possesses many holy senses,
Wherewith she diligently guards the community
In body and in soul, all to God’s honor;
She gives them many holy teachings;
Whatever may be God’s will,
Through it her own soul becomes free.
     The **Chaplain** of Love is divine **humility**;
She is always subject to Love,
So that pride must stand aside.
     The **Prioress** is holy **peace of God**.
To her good will, patience is given,
So that she teaches the community with divine wisdom;
To whatever things she turns,
It is always to God’s honor.
     The **Subprioress** is **loving-kindness**.
She shall gather the small crumbs <note>likely a metaphor for minor faults or small spiritual duties</note> together
And erase them with divinity.
Whatever wrong is done, one should not bear it long in the mind;
Thereby God increases a person’s goodness.
     The **Chapter** <note>the formal assembly of the religious community</note> shall have four things within it:
Namely, the manifestation of holiness,
Which lies in God’s service. Her gentle labor
Causes the enemies <note>referring to demons or spiritual temptations</note> much sorrow
And God much honor,
In which she may greatly rejoice.
Let her guard against empty honor,
And be a help to the honor of others.
If they serve with diligence, then God rewards them equally.
     The **Chantress** <note>or Song-mistress, the leader of the choir</note> is **Hope**,
Filled with holy, humble devotion,
So that the heart’s frailty
Sounds so beautifully in song before God,
That God loves the notes that sing in the heart.
He who sings thus with her shall succeed with her
In heavenly love.
     The **Schoolmistress** is **Wisdom**,
Who with good will diligently teaches the simple,
Thereby the cloister is hallowed and honored.
     The **Cellarer** is an **overflow** <note>original: "vsvlus" (Ausfluss), meaning an outpouring or abundance</note> of helpful gifts.
Because she does this in divine joy,
She gains a holy mind through divine grace;
All those who desire anything from her,
Should be well-mannered and content
Always without complaint.
Then the sweet gift of God flows into her heart.

<summary>This page presents a spiritual allegory where the various offices of a convent—such as the Abbess, Prioress, and Chantress—are personified by virtues like Love, Peace, and Hope. It illustrates how a well-ordered religious life reflects an internal spiritual harmony.</summary>

<keywords>monastic life, allegory, Love (Minne), Humility, Prioress, Cellarer, Chantress, spiritual virtues, convent hierarchy</keywords>

<header>Chapter XXXVII.</header>

Those who are her helpers there,
They shall forever win
Even as they receive the sweet gift of God.

->The **Chamberess** <note>original: "kamererin," the official in charge of the convent's treasury and supplies</note> is **Generosity**,<-
Who forever wishes to do good in orderly measure.
She gives what she does not possess through her divine will <note>implying that even if she lacks material goods, her charitable intent is credited to her by God</note>,
For this, she must win a special gift from God.
Those to whom she gives anything thank God for it
With holy devotion; she finds the heart’s state
Like a noble drink in a pure vessel.

->The **Mistress of the Sick**, that is diligent **Mercy**,<-
Who forever hungers after this:
That she may be ready for the sick without weariness,
With help and with purity,
With refreshment and with joy,
With comfort and with loveliness.
So God gives her His reward,
That she may always do it gladly;
He who shall send her help for this,
The same shall happen from God.

->The **Portress** <note>the doorkeeper of the convent</note> is **Watchfulness**,<-
Who always follows her duty with a holy spirit
To carry out what has been entrusted to her;
Thus her labor remains not lost,
So she may come prepared to God
Whenever she wishes to pray.
Then God is with her in a holy silence,
To hear the lament of her heart’s sorrow.
For though she sometimes performs her task with difficulty,
Holy **Obedience** reconciles it all,
To which she is then subject with joy.

->The **Mistress of Discipline** <note>original: "zuhtmeisterin," the person responsible for the conduct and training of the novices</note> is **Holy Habit**,<-
She shall forever burn like a candle,
Unextinguished in heavenly freedom;
Thus we carry all our heart’s sorrow gently
Until a holy end.

->The **Provost** <note>an administrative overseer</note> is **Divine Obedience**,<-
To whom all virtues are subject;
Thus may the cloister stand in God.
Whoever wishes to give themselves into this cloister,
They shall forever live with divine joy,
Here and in the eternal life.
Well is it for those who remain therein!

# Seventh Part

Whoever must indeed begin it:
He shall follow and serve the Heavenly Father
Without ceasing with holy fear
And with humble humility in all things.
He shall follow and serve His Son
With pain and with patience,
With willing poverty in holy labors.
He shall follow and serve the Holy Spirit
In holy hope above all words
With a sweet heart in a gentle mood;
Thus one tastes His goodness.

The pure, loving maidens <note>original: "juncfrŏwen"; refers to the consecrated virgins or pure souls within the spiritual community</note>,
They shall henceforth follow the noble youth
Jesus Christ, the child of the pure virgin,
Who is full of love.
As if He were eighteen years old <note>medieval mystics often envisioned the resurrected Christ and the souls in heaven in the "prime of youth," often cited as age 30 or, as here, a youthful 18</note>, His person is
To the maidens most lovable and He the most beautiful;
So they follow Him with blissful tenderness
Into the flowering meadow of their pure conscience.
There the youth plucks for them
The flowers of all virtues;
From these they make the noble crowns
Which one shall wear at the eternal high-wedding <note>original: "hohgezit"; a term for a great festival or a wedding feast</note>.

When the noble courses <note>original: "gerihte"; referring to the various dishes served at a banquet</note> have been served,
Where Jesus Christ Himself wishes to serve,
Then one sees there the highest praise-dance <note>original: "lobetanz"; a celebratory circle dance common in medieval courtly and religious imagery</note>,
There then every soul and body shall
Wear their crown of virtues,
Which they have completed here on earth
With many holy devotions.
So they follow the Lamb in countless joy,
From joy to love, from love to delight,
From delight to clarity <note>original: "clarheit"; meaning divine radiance or spiritual transparency</note>, from clarity to power,
From power into the highest heights,
Before the eyes of the Heavenly Father.
Then He greets His only-begotten Son
And also many a pure bride,
Who have come there with Him.

"Ah, dear Son, what You are, I am,
And what they are, of that I rejoice.
My dear brides, rejoice evermore,
Rejoice in my eternal purity,
Now gently let go of all woe and all sorrow <note>original: "verklagent"; to move past the state of lamentation</note>.
My holy angels shall serve you,
My holy ones shall honor you,
The mother of my Son's humanity..."

### *XXXVIII. How a spiritual person should lament and confess their sins to God every day.*

->I, a sinful human,<-
->I lament and confess all my sins to God,<-
->Of which I am guilty before God's eyes.<-
->I confess and lament all my good works,<-
->Which I have neglected.<-
->I confess and lament the sins I committed,<-
->When I did not know what sin was.<-
->I lament the sins that are worse,<-
->Which I have done knowingly<-
->And with malice and with restlessness <term>vnmůsekeit; a state of spiritual distraction or being too "busy" for God</term> and with vanity.<-
->Have mercy on me, Lord,<-
->For they are truly sorrowful to me,<-
->And give me, Lord, Your complete assurance,<-
->That You have forgiven them all for me,<-
->Otherwise I cannot live with joy.<-

->Leave me in true repentance<-
->And in heartfelt love for You,<-
->And let me never grow cold,<-
->So that I may feel Your heartfelt love <term>miñe; the medieval term for the ecstatic, reciprocal love between the soul and God</term><-
->In my heart and my soul<-
->And in my five senses,<-
->And in all my limbs<-
->Without ceasing;<-
->Thus I may never grow cold.<-

<header>Part Seven.</header>

### XXXIX. *How the devils beat and chase themselves, bite and gnaw themselves, when a loving soul, who burns with divine love <note>original: "gótlicher miñe"; "minne" is the Middle High German word for a refined, spiritual, or courtly love</note>, departs from this world.*

Blessed is the good person who was ever born,
Who follows God with all virtues,
Which are possible for him to fulfill!
His soul goes free in love;
At his final end, the holy angels come
And receive the pure souls
With untold love in heavenly joy <term>wuñe; a word for intense, ecstatic bliss or delight</term>
And lead them away from here with joy,
And with great praise they bring them to God.
The enemies from hell, who had come there,
All their labor was taken from them.
They had come there with hatred and with wrath;
When they then see
That their will has not been done,
How they then beat and chase themselves,
How they then bite and gnaw themselves,
How they then howl <term>húwelent; an onomatopoeic word for the screeching of owls or howling</term> and groan,
For they fear the ghastly pains
Which they must receive from their masters <note>The "masters" are the higher-ranking demons or Lucifer, who punish the lesser devils for failing to capture a soul</note>,
Because they have lost the soul. —
Then they scold one another:
"Unfortunate one, it was your fault!" —
"Be silent, comrade! I never found her in great impatience.
Whenever I shot evil thoughts at her,
Then repentance was always her companion.
That whispering with the confessors <term>bihtern; priests who hear confession</term> robs us of all our honor;
Our companions were much more numerous,
To whom ¹) she was dearly entrusted.
How shall we now come to court?
O woe, Master, how have you directed us,
That you entrusted this person to us!
We could recognize no great sin in her.
I tempted her very hard many times,
Then it turned into weeping;
(I) and my other companions,
We could never fell her.
With weeping she drove me away,
With sighing she burned me,
My hair and my claws,"

<header>Chapter XXXIX.</header>

I could nowhere approach her;
Her obedience was so great,
She never had an equal.
Because of her, it has come to pass
That she is rightfully taken from us,
Which is our greatest loss.
All her good works burned within
From divine love <note>original: "gåtlicher miñe"; spiritual or mystical love</note>,
For she did all her good works with good will."
->Thus speaks their master:<-
"You have come to my court in disgrace.
I had entrusted her to you;
The pain will never be taken from you,
Which I intend to give you for this failure.
You do not wish to be among the people as I would gladly be,
If that honor were given to me;
Now you must live here with me in hell, ¹) <note>The manuscript reads "ligen" (to lie), but the context suggests "leben" (to live).</note>
That shall be your penance.
I will send out higher masters,
So that they may blind the understanding of good people.
If we could destroy their great diligence
Which they have toward God,
Then we would achieve all our honor;
Then the young would follow them,
And thus our whole race <note>or lineage/kindred; referring to the population of hell</note> would be increased.
If only one of those souls could be mine,
Who burn so fiercely with divine love!
With that, I would crown myself,
And reward myself for my long labor;
Then I would easily find comfort for all my heart's sorrow."
->Abandon your lowly desire,<-
You long for that which never happened to you
And never shall happen to you.
Whether it brings you evil or woe,
All the blessed ones who love God in their hearts within Christendom,
They are so thoroughly saturated
And flooded with love <note>original: "miñe"</note>,
That they shine with holy virtues
And burn lovingly in all their works.
You know well, it helps you nothing
That you so greatly try to pervert them.
They can hardly wait until the time comes
That they may praise God therein.

### *XLI. How a preaching brother was seen.*

<note>A "preaching brother" refers to a member of the Dominican Order, to which Mechthild's confessors belonged.</note>

Forty years ago I knew a spiritual man; in those days spiritual people were simple and full of love <note>original: "miñenvůrig"; literally "fire-loving" or "burning with love".</note>. He grew in spiritual life and in piety and performed many holy works for our Lord openly. He has now passed away; then I prayed to our Lord for his soul in a Christian manner, asking that if there were any guilt in him, God would forgive it. Then I first saw a radiance that was prepared for him by God; yet I did not find him within it, and so my soul was troubled. Afterward, at another time, when I prayed for him again, I found him in a fiery cloud; there he begged that one (might) give him relief. Then I spoke with

with all my power to our dear Lord: "O dear Lord, grant me this, that I may repay evil with good." You have followed me inwardly <note>original: "heleklich"; can mean secretly or in the heart, reflecting an internal devotion.</note> and served me faithfully; you shall wear the virgin's crown <note>A special reward in medieval theology for those who remained celibate.</note>, the crown of righteousness, and the crown of truth." — Then he journeyed, shining, past eight choirs <note>The nine choirs of angels.</note> and touched the ninth; then I saw him no more. Had the false liars not brought reports to him, he would have traveled to eternal joy without pain. That he chose to trust them was his harm.

<header>Part Seven.</header>

Holy simplicity
Is a medicine for all wisdom.
It makes the wise man consider himself a fool.
Since the simplicity of the heart
Dwells within the wisdom of the senses,
Much holiness comes from this to the human soul.

### XLIV. On five sins and five virtues.

Greed in poverty
And deceitfulness in truth,
Sluggishness toward mercy,
Mocking scorn in the presence of others,
<unclear>....</unclear> in the rule:
These five imperfect things
Make the spiritual life deathly ill. <note>original: "hỏptsiech"; literally "head-sick," meaning a critical or mortal illness that strikes at the foundation of life.</note>
Truth without falsehood,
Manifest <term>love</term> <note>original: "miñe"; this refers to *Minne*, the medieval concept of a deep, reciprocal, and often mystical love between the soul and God.</note> toward one another,
Fear in three fears, <note>In medieval theology, these are often servile fear (fear of punishment), initial fear (fear of offending God), and filial fear (pure reverence for God as a Father).</note>
Hidden love for God made manifest in my heart,
Diligence in all good things—
These five things keep spiritual love healthy.

### XLV. On seven things in the longing of love.

Seven things I must speak to God’s honor:
Lord God, if it is possible, grant them to me,
That I may never forget them while in the <term>earthly realm</term>. <note>original: "ertriche"; the world of mortals as opposed to the kingdom of heaven.</note>
Five are found in the kingdom of heaven,
But two must remain here.
The first is the harm of my guilt,
For I have sinned and neglected good works
Which I could well have done.
The second is, Lord, that I wait for you without ceasing, to know when you
Wish to come to me, and in what manner
You command a holy end for me.
The third is your restless <term>longing</term> <note>original: "gerunge"; an intense, driving spiritual yearning.</note>
Which I have for you.
The fourth is an unquenchable <term>burning of love</term> <note>original: "miñenbůrnen"; a mystical state where the soul is consumed by divine fire.</note> forever for your sake.
The fifth is the first reciprocal glance
Of your noble face toward me.
Alas, in this earthly realm,
This could never happen according to my longing;
For this, my soul often sings: "O woe!"
The sixth I hardly dare to name;
I become mute as I acknowledge it.
I never heard it named in the earthly realm.

This is the playing flood of <term>love</term> <note>original: "miñe"; here describing the dynamic, joyful, and reciprocal movement of love between God and the soul.</note>,
Which flows secretly from God into the soul
And carries her back with its power according to her strength.
What bliss exists between the two of them then,
No one else can know,
Nor how they act with one another,
For each finds their own portion.
Whatever one has lent out here on earth,
Shall all be given back to him there in heaven.
This is the heavenly love of God,
Which begins here very humbly
And finds no end there.
The seventh thing can hardly be touched with words;
With <term>Christian faith</term> <note>original: "cristangelöben"; the lived, experiential belief in Christ.</note> one may perceive it:
How great, how high, how wide, how blissful,
How honorable, how full of joy, how rich it is.
Blessed is he who shall dwell with Him eternally!
The joyful sight, full of all delight,
And the holy enjoyment according to one's heart's desire;
These are manifold beyond counting
And are ever more honorably drawn forth without ceasing,
For they soar out from the living God.
The over-sweet longing, blissfully hungry and full of love,
Flows forevermore into the souls,
Abundantly from God;
Yet even then the soul retains its sweet hunger
And lives without distress.

### XLVI. How the soul announces itself in spiritual poverty.

Here the soul announces itself in spiritual poverty and in eternal love for God, and in a restless longing to travel toward God. She speaks thus: The long waiting is passing away; the future union brings it about that God and the soul shall be united and inseparable forevermore. Whenever I think of this, my heart rejoices greatly.

### *XLVII. Of a sin that is evil above all sins.*

I have heard a certain <term>sin</term> <note>original: "sůnde"</note> named. I thank God that I do not know it personally; it seems to me and indeed is more evil than all other sins, for it is the highest <term>unbelief</term> <note>original: "vngelůbe"; likely referring to a contemporary heresy where individuals claimed a state of perfection that bypassed the need for Christ’s humanity or the Church’s sacraments.</note>. I am hostile toward it with all my soul and with all my body, and with all my five senses, and with all my heart. I thank Jesus Christ, the living Son of God, that it never entered my heart. This sin did not <term>arise</term> <note>original: "vfkomen"</note> from Christian people; the "humble" <note>The original "diemůtige" means humble, but the author uses "sic" or a note of irony here, as the "enemy" (the devil) uses a facade of humility to deceive the simple-hearted.</note> enemy has deceived simple people with it. They want to be so holy that they try to pull themselves into the eternal <term>Godhead</term> <note>original: "gotheit"; the pure essence of God.</note> and place themselves alongside the eternal holy <term>humanity</term> <note>original: "menscheit"; the human nature of Christ.</note> of our Lord Jesus Christ. Whenever they find themselves in such <term>arrogance</term> <note>original: "bobenheit"; a sense of spiritual pride or "uppityness."</note>, they give themselves over to the eternal curse. They still wish to be considered the holiest of all. They mock God’s words which are written concerning the humanity of our Lord.

You most wretched person, if you truly knew the eternal Godhead, then that [arrogance] would be impossible; you would also know the eternal humanity which floats there in the eternal Godhead; you would also have to acknowledge the Holy Spirit, who enlightens the heart of the Christian and tastes in their soul above all sweetness and teaches the human senses beyond all mastery, so that they might speak <term>humbly</term> <note>original: "diemůtekliche"</note> there, through which one may become perfect before God. ¹) <note>1) The original German editor notes: "The text appears to be corrupted." The sentence structure in the manuscript becomes difficult to parse here.</note>

<summary>This page contains a poetic dialogue about the indwelling of the Trinity and a stern warning against a heresy of spiritual pride where people claim equality with God while mocking Christ's humanity.</summary>

<header>Chapter XLVII — XLVIII.</header>

### *XLVIII. How Love was seen with her maidens.*

In the night I spoke thus to our Lord: Lord, I dwell in a land called <term>Exile</term> <note>original: "ellende"; in Middle High German, this term refers to being in a foreign land or banished, though it carries the connotation of the modern word for "misery."</note>, which is this world, for everything that is within it can neither comfort nor gladden me without pain. Furthermore, I have a house called Full-of-Pain; that is the house in which my soul lies imprisoned, my body. This house is old, small, and dark. This should be understood spiritually. In this house I have a bed called Unrest, for I am pained by all things that do not belong to God. Before it I have a chair called Discomfort. This discomfort gives me the <term>sins of others</term> <note>original: "vrômde sůnde"; literally "stranger-sins," referring to the spiritual burden of atonement for the sins of the community.</note> to confess, of which I was never guilty. Before the chair I have a table called Reluctance, because I find so little of the spiritual life among spiritual people. Upon the table lies a tablecloth; it is pure and is called <term>Poverty</term> <note>original: "armůte"</note>, which contains within it many holy virtues. If one were to use it rightly, one would love it from the heart. The love of riches is a thief of poverty. Upon the table comes a food to me called Bitterness of Sins, and with it what is called Willing Labor. The drink is called Scant Praise, for I have, alas, all too few good works in me.

I saw this darkness within me; then the true <term>Love of God</term> <note>original: "gotzmiñe"; personified here as Lady Love or "Frau Minne."</note> revealed herself to me. She was like a noble empress of maidens. She was of noble form in her body, white and red in blooming youth. She had with her many a virtue, who were all like maidens; with these she served me if I myself wished it. Even so, they all gladly wished to give themselves to my service. She was crowned with more than shining gold. Her garment was like green <term>cendal</term> <note>original: "zendale"; a thin, costly silk fabric highly prized in the Middle Ages.</note>.

When I looked at her properly, my dark house was illuminated, so that I recognized all that was within it and all that ever happened there. When I saw her, I recognized her well, for I had seen her before when she was my dear <term>companion</term> <note>original: "conpanine"</note>. Of this I will now be silent, for those things are also written in the book. — Then I spoke: Oh, most beloved maiden, now you are more than a thousandfold above me; and yet you serve me with such great...

(This means: However many earthly things one possesses, they should not stick to the human heart.)

Ah, dear Lady <term>Chastity</term> <note>original: "kûschekeit"</note>,
I entrust to you my maidenly garment,
That it may ever be pure and clean,
For my dear Bridegroom Jesus Christ
Is with me at all times.
Lady <term>Patience</term> <note>original: "gedult"</note>, I have great strength
In silence and in suffering;
You take away the power of all my <term>assaults</term> <note>original: "anevehtunge"; refers to spiritual temptations or demonic attacks.</note>,
So that they may not harm me.
I will keep you with me through my labors.
Lady <term>Holiness</term> <note>original: "helikeit"</note>, come here to me,
And kiss my soul’s mouth,
And dwell in the depths of my heart,
So I shall remain forever healthy.
Lady <term>Hope</term>, I beseech you,
That you bind together all my heart’s wounds,
Which <term>Love</term> <note>original: "miñe"; the divine, personified love of God.</note> has dealt me,
So that I may ever keep God’s blessing,
Whatever hardship may be given to me.
Ah, honorable, holy Christian <term>Faith</term> <note>original: "gelŏbe"</note>,
You have ever enlightened my soul’s eye,
So that I well know where I have turned
In Christian matters;
I entrust my works and my senses to you.
Ah, dear Lady <term>Prudence</term> <note>original: "verhûte"; literally "protection" or "guarding," here personified as the virtue of watchfulness.</note>, do not sit,
Stand by me at all times, <margin>The manuscript reads: "by me."</margin>
So I remain free from evil.
Lady <term>Moderation</term> <note>original: "messekeit"; the virtue of temperance and balance.</note>, be with me at all times,
So that I may at all times
Be ready for God’s service.
Lady <term>Contentment</term> <note>original: "genûglicheit"; the state of being satisfied with what God provides.</note>, you are my dear <term>chamberlain</term> <note>original: "kamereriñe"; a female official in charge of a noble household's private chambers.</note>,
I must love you greatly;
You make my hard bed soft,
My coarse food savory;
You give me power in poverty;
This comes from God’s goodness.
Peace and stillness I cannot do without;
You must walk with me in all my ways.
Those who speak much and whisper much
Hardly preserve their honor;
Those who speak many idle words,
Nothing of it can ever be of use to them.
<term>Wisdom</term> is at all times with Love
And is the mistress of all maidens.

### *XLIX. Of a lay brother.*

In the <term>Dominican Order</term> <note>original: "predierorden"; literally the "Order of Preachers."</note>, a brother was struck dead by lightning; then prayer was offered for his soul with faithful longing, asking whether anything in him remained <term>unatoned</term> <note>original: "vngewandelt"; referring to sins or character flaws not yet transformed through penance or confession.</note> that might be forgiven him. Then his soul was revealed to the same person who prayed for him; he was beautiful, in heavenly joy, and suffered no pain. This was because, as his soul spoke: "I was humble in my works, I was fearful [of God] in my senses, I was well-intentioned in all my deeds; therefore I have no pain."

*The Soul:* "Why did you not go immediately to the kingdom of heaven?"

Then he spoke: "I must first receive divine knowledge and heavenly love here, which I did not have on earth."

— "Why is it that you have that small blemish on your face?" —

Then he spoke: "I showed a stern face to those who did not do my will; that remained unatoned in me."

— "With what can this blemish be taken from you?" —

Then he spoke: "If only I had a <term>sigh</term>!" <note>In this context, a "sigh" refers to a living person's prayer or act of compassion offered on behalf of the deceased.</note> — Then this could not be done for him by the person, for in that moment it was given to him [by God]. Then he rejoiced and said: "Now it is gone."

— "Why do you wear this crown? For you have not yet entered the kingdom of heaven." —

Then he spoke: "I had a singular death <note>original: "sunderlichen tot"; referring to the sudden and dramatic nature of being struck by lightning.</note>; for that reason God has given it to me."

Oh, dear Lord Jesus Christ, who is one eternal God with the eternal Father, remember me. I thank you, Lord, for your

<term>filial gifts</term> <note>original: "sůnlichen gaben"; refers to the gifts given by God to his children, or specifically the grace flowing from the Son.</note>, with which you touch me without ceasing, which pierce through all my bones and all my veins and all my flesh. When I can thank you, Lord, with holy gratitude, then I am safe and not otherwise. You can well treat your <term>wretched</term> <note>original: "snůden"; meaning lowly, base, or of little worth.</note> servant wretchedly, for, Lord, your intention is good and better than good; for many a thing is called good that is not as good as yours, which you do for me. But when you touch me with your supreme <term>sweetness</term> <note>original: "sůssekeit"; a key term in medieval mysticism describing the sensory-like experience of God’s presence.</note>, which goes through my soul and my body, then I fear that I may draw too much of your divine delight into myself, for I am unworthy of it on <term>earth</term> <note>original: "ertriche"; literally "the realm of the earth."</note>. Therefore I sometimes pray for other people more than for myself, so that I may renounce my delight for the sake of God's love and Christian loyalty.

Next I feared the rising of <term>pride</term> <note>original: "homůtes"; literally "high-mindedness" or arrogance.</note>, which threw the worthiest angel <note>Refers to Lucifer.</note> out of the kingdom of heaven. I also fear the serpent of vain glory which deceived Eve. I fear the unfaithfulness that separated Judas from God. If I am faithful to God, then I stand with all virtues, with all goodness in all protection, with God and with our dear Lady, his <term>virginal</term> <note>original: "megtlichen"</note> mother.

->*LI. A prayer against neglect.*<-

I, the least, I, the most wretched, I, the most unworthy among all the human race, I desire, I pray to you, heavenly Father, Lord Jesus Christ, Lord Holy Spirit, Lord Holy <term>Trinity</term> <note>original: "drivaltekeit"</note>, that you would today forgive me all the <term>neglect</term> <note>original: "versumekeit"; often referring to "sloth" or the failure to perform divine offices with proper focus.</note> with which I have neglected myself in your holy service—not only out of utility or necessity, but rather out of my sinful wickedness, which I could well have left alone if I had wanted to. Now receive, Lord, this small penance, which I now perform for you with my will and to the honor of your dear mother and all the saints who are celebrated today in holy Christendom, and to the praise and honor of all God's saints in the blessedness through which they, dear Lord, have come to you.

Now help me, dear Lord, toward such a transformation in my life, so that I may become your holy companion on earth as in a holy life, so that [I may enjoy] their company in your kingdom...

### LII. How the loving soul bows beneath the hand of God.

I say to my five senses: Bow yourselves beneath the almighty hand of God, for the enemies from hell must bow and bend, however <term>proud</term> <note>original: "homůtig"; in this context, it refers to the arrogance or haughtiness of fallen spirits.</note> they are, in their fiery bonds under the hard compulsion of almighty God.

> Those who are in <term>purgatory</term> <note>original: "vegfůr"; literally "cleansing fire."</note>,
> They must bow in their guilt beneath penance,
> Until the final hours,
> That they may be found pure.

The sinners on the <term>earthly realm</term> <note>original: "ertrich"; a common medieval term for the world of the living.</note> must bow beneath the burden of their guilt in judgment, with repentance into penance or into eternal hell.

The good people on the earth must bow with [unnamed virtue] <note>The word is missing in the manuscript.</note> into penance all their days.

The chosen pure ones, who seek our Lord God with all faithfulness, are greatly constrained, and they suffer many a holy sorrow. They bow and bend themselves beneath all pain and beneath all creatures with soaring <term>love</term> <note>original: "miñe"; a central term in German mysticism referring to the ecstatic, reciprocal love between the soul and God.</note>. To them, pride is very <term>costly</term> <note>original: "tůre"; suggesting that pride is something they cannot afford or which is foreign to their nature.</note>. I shall think on this, and I will and must drink from the same cup that my Father has drunk from, if I am to possess His kingdom.

The kingdom of heaven bows with all holy angels with blissful holiness, for what they are and [the fact] that they live, God has given to them freely.

> The saints bow and bend before God
> In flowing love and blissful longing
> With diligent acceptance.
> So they thank God,
> That He so lovingly prepared His gifts for them
> In their times of need upon the earth;
> Thereby they endured all their <term>heart-sorrow</term> <note>original: "herzeleit"; deep emotional or spiritual suffering.</note>.
> So must it happen to me,
> For I also, through His love,
> Am in many a pain.

<summary>This page explores the necessity of humility, describing how all beings—from the damned in hell and souls in purgatory to the saints and angels in heaven—must bow before God's authority. It emphasizes that the path to the kingdom of heaven requires sharing in the "cup" of suffering and enduring earthly sorrow through divine love.</summary>

### *LIII. Concerning the imprisonment of spiritual people.*

My heart is moved with compassion for the distress of this <term>community</term> <note>original: "samenunge"; referring to the monastic or religious group the author lived with.</note> where I am. In the night, in the solitude of my heart, I spoke to our Lord in this way: "Lord, how does this <term>imprisonment</term> <note>original: "gevengnisse"; a metaphorical and literal reference to the strict enclosure of monastic life.</note> please you?" Then our Lord spoke: >"I am imprisoned within it."< — Through these words, I was given to understand the meaning of all these sayings as follows:

I fasted with them in the <term>wilderness</term> <note>original: "wóstunge"; suggesting both the biblical desert and the spiritual isolation of the cell.</note>.
I was tempted by the enemy with them.
I work all my days modestly in useful fruit with them.
I was betrayed with hatred with them.
I was sold in <term>faith</term> <note>original: "glóben"; here implying the faithfulness or the "pledge" given by the religious to God.</note> with them, as they offered themselves to me in divine service.
I was sought out in danger with them.
I was attacked with them in complete fury.
I was captured with greedy envy with them.
I was bound in obedience with them.
I was mocked in great disfavor with them.
I was struck with great innocence with them.
Whatever distress they hear of, it shall not trouble them.
I was dragged before the court with them like a guilty thief.
They should think of this in the <term>chapter</term> <note>original: "capittele"; the daily meeting of the monastic community for business and discipline.</note> and in <term>confession</term> <note>original: "biht".</note>.
I was scourged with them; when they scourge themselves, they should think of me.
I carried my cross with them.
Whenever they are burdened, they should think of me in that moment.
I was nailed to the cross with them,
For which they gladly suffer and lament their painful distress.
I commended my spirit in my death to my Father with them;
Thus should they commend themselves to me in all their needs.
I died with them in a holy end,
Thus shall all their bonds be loosened.
I was buried with them in an earthly stone;
Thus should they be and remain pure from all earthly things.
I rose up from death, so should they ever rise up from their <term>failings</term> <note>original: "brůchen"; can mean sins, shortcomings, or the "breaks" in their spiritual discipline.</note>,
That they may receive the heavenly radiance into their souls.
I ascended to heaven with my divine power,
There they shall follow me in the power of all this holy fear.

I truly hope that you perform and acknowledge this without ceasing. In whomever this is not yet present, may the true God bring it to fulfillment in them!

### *LIV. Concerning four things of faith.*

That one believes in God in a Christian way, and that one loves God holily, and that one truly acknowledges Jesus Christ, that...

<meta>Digitized by Google watermark in bottom right corner. Catchword "de" (that) at the end of the last paragraph.</meta>

<summary>This page contains a mystical dialogue where Christ identifies his own suffering and life with the daily hardships and spiritual disciplines of a monastic community. It frames the "imprisonment" of religious life as a shared experience with the Savior.</summary>
<keywords>monastic life, imitatio Christi, community, suffering, passion of Christ, obedience, confession, spiritual imprisonment</keywords>

--- Page 300 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous page's reflection on following Christ's suffering, moving into a formal confession of faith in the Trinity and a mystical letter regarding the soul as a vessel for divine love.</meta>

<page-num>268</page-num>
<header>Part Seven.</header>

If one follows his teaching faithfully until the human end, I believe that in these four things one finds eternal life. We believe in a Christian manner, not like the Jews, nor like the unfaithful Christians. They want to believe in God but not in His most holy works which He performed; that is, that He gave us His only-begotten Son; they despise Him. Lord God, we lament this to You. We believe in Him according to the will of God, who sent His only-begotten Son to us in this world. We believe in the works and the death of our Lord Jesus Christ, through which He has redeemed us. We believe in the Holy Spirit, who has perfected all our <term>blessedness</term> <note>original: "selekeit"; can also mean salvation or the soul's happiness.</note> in the Father and in the Son, and still perfects it in all our good works.

How should we love God holily? We should love everything that the Holy Trinity commands. God did not create sin, therefore He hates it in us. God loves the goodness in us, which He Himself is.

How should we acknowledge Jesus Christ? We should acknowledge Him by His works and should love Him above ourselves. How should we follow His teaching? As He has taught us and as His followers still teach us. As long as we are here, our blessedness shall be increased.

### *LV. Thus a friend writes to his friend.*

Because you love God beyond your human power, because you love God with all the strength of your soul, because you acknowledge God with all the wisdom of your soul, because you have received <term>God’s gifts</term> <note>original: "gotzgabe"</note> with many holy thanksgivings—therefore I send you this letter.

The great abundance of divine <term>love</term> <note>original: "miñe"; specifically referring to *Minne*, the high, often mystical love of the medieval period.</note>, which never stands still and flows forevermore without ceasing, without any kind of labor, with such a sweet flow forever untiringly, so that our <term>little vessel</term> <note>original: "clein vesselin"; a common mystical metaphor for the soul's capacity to receive God's grace.</note> becomes full and overflowing—if we do not block it with our own will, then our vessel flows over forever from God’s gift.

Lord, You are full and You also make us full with Your gift. You are great and we are small, how shall we become like You?

<summary>This page contains a profession of faith in the Trinity and the redemptive work of Christ, followed by a mystical letter describing the soul as a vessel that overflows with the untiring abundance of divine love.</summary>
