--- Page 301 ---
<meta>This page continues the "letter from a friend to a friend" begun on page 268, expanding on the metaphor of the soul as a vessel and the reciprocal relationship of love and suffering between the soul and God.</meta>

<page-num>269</page-num>
<header>Chapter 55.</header>

Lord, you have given to us and we should give even more in return. Even though we are but a small vessel, you have nonetheless filled it. One may pour a small, full vessel so often into a large vessel that the large vessel becomes full from the small one. The large vessel is the <term>satisfaction</term> <note>original: "gnůgunge"; in a theological context, this refers to the 'sufficiency' or 'atonement' God receives through human deeds.</note> of God, which he receives from our works; we are, alas, so small that a little fear of God or of the Holy Scripture makes us so full that we can take no more at that hour. So we pour the gift out again into the large vessel that is God. How shall we do that? We should pour it with holy longing upon sinners, so that they may be purified; then the vessel becomes full again. Then we pour it again upon the imperfection of spiritual people, so that they may strive further and become and remain perfect. Then it becomes full again; then we pour it out upon the distress of the poor souls who suffer in <term>purgatory</term> <note>original: "vegefuͤr"</note>, so that God, through his goodness, may take away their manifold distress. Then we pour it with holy mercy upon the distress of holy Christendom, which stands in many sins. Our Lord God loved us first of all; he also worked for us first of all; he also suffered most of all for our sake. This same we should give back to him, if we wish to be like him.

Thus spoke our Lord to a person: Give me all that is yours, and I will give you all that is mine. The repayment of <term>love</term> <note>original: "miñe"; refers to the medieval concept of "Minne," a mystical, intense, and often reciprocal love between the soul and God.</note> that we perform for God is very sweet. The repayment of work is, alas, very often difficult for us, because when love has consumed the interior, the human being must sometimes do without the exterior. How difficult it may be, you ask me? I could never express that with human senses. Our Lord suffered much for us, even unto death. Now, alas, a small suffering seems so great to us; for this I must despise myself and complain to God that I have such small virtue. Love makes suffering sweet, more than one can speak of; and if we wish to become like God, we must be victorious over many a battle. The <term>remembrance</term> <note>original: "gehuͤgenisse"; suggests a mindful union or a shared memory.</note> of God and the loving soul come together just as the sun and the air, with the noble power of God, mingle together in a sweet pressing-together, so that the sun overcomes the coldness and <term>darkness</term> <note>original: "vinsternisse"</note> of the air.

<summary>The author uses the metaphor of a small vessel to describe how the soul receives God's grace and "pours" it out onto others—sinners, the clergy, and souls in purgatory—to achieve a reciprocal union with the Divine. It concludes by comparing the union of God and the soul to the sun warming and illuminating the air.</summary>

That one cannot perceive it as anything but all one sun;
This comes from the divine <term>bliss</term> <note>original: "wuñe"; refers to an ecstatic, heavenly joy or delight.</note>.
May God give us and keep for us all this <term>love</term> <note>original: "miñe"; the Middle High German term for the reciprocal, mystical love between the soul and God.</note>! Amen.

### *56. How God touches his friends with pain.*

When a person suffers a <term>sorrow</term> <note>original: "trůbekeit"; can refer to sadness, tribulation, or spiritual affliction.</note>,
For which he is not responsible
And has little guilt within him;
Our Lord speaks thus to this:
*I have touched them.* <gloss>original: "Glosa"; a commentary or explanation of the preceding scriptural-style statement.</gloss>
Just as my Father allowed me to be touched while on earth,
So too [with] those whom I draw to myself while on earth,
For the drawing causes much pain.
They should truly know this:
The more heavily I draw them to myself,
The closer they come to me.
When a person triumphs over himself,
So that he weighs pain and comfort equally,
Then I will lift him into the sweetness,
So that eternal life shall taste sweet to him.

### *57. A little about paradise.*

This was shown to me, and I saw how paradise was created. Of its breadth and its length, I found no end. When I first arrived at the path between this world and the beginning of paradise, I saw trees, foliage, and lovely grass, and nothing that was not cleared <note>original: "nit vnerutes"; implying a perfectly tended garden where no weeds or wild brush grew.</note>. Some trees bore apples, and the greatest multitude were nothing but foliage with a noble fragrance. Swift waters flow through it, and south winds blow toward the north. In those waters, earthly sweetness met with heavenly bliss. The air there was sweeter than I can say. There were neither beasts nor birds within, for God had entrusted it to man alone, that he should dwell there in comfort.

There I saw two men within: <term>Enoch and Elijah</term> <note>In medieval theology, these two biblical figures were believed to have been taken up alive to a terrestrial paradise to wait until the end of time.</note>. Enoch sat and Elijah lay upon the earth in great devotion. Then I spoke to Enoch. I asked him how they lived according to human nature? Then he spoke: "We eat a little of the apples and drink a little of the water, so that the body may maintain its vitality, but the greatest thing is the <term>power of God</term> <note>original: "gotzkraft"; the sustaining divine energy that nourishes the soul and body.</note>." I asked him: "How did you come here?" — "I came here, so that I might not

<header>Seventh Part.</header>

### LVIII. Of Saint Gabriel.

Holy angel Gabriel, remember me!
I entrust the message of my longing to you.
Tell my dear Lord Jesus Christ
How <term>love-sick</term> <note>original: "miñesiech"; a common theme in medieval mysticism where the soul's intense desire for God is described as a physical illness that only He can cure.</note> I am for Him.
If I am ever to recover,
Then He Himself must be my physician.
You may tell Him in confidence:
The wounds that He Himself has dealt me,
I can no longer bear them un-anointed
And unbound.
He has wounded me
Unto death;
If He lets me lie now un-anointed,
Then I can never recover.
Were all mountains a wound-salve
And all waters a medicinal drink
And all trees with flowers a healing bandage,
Even with these I could never recover.
He must lay Himself into my soul’s wounds.
Holy angel Gabriel, remember me!
I entrust this <term>love-message</term> <note>original: "miñe-botschaft"</note> to you.
Whoever wishes to love God,
This <term>love-letter</term> <note>original: "miñebrief"</note> awakens their senses,
If they desire to follow God.

### LIX. How the message came before God.

I have clearly perceived the truth in my spirit:
My message has come to God.
The answer that shall come back to me
Is so great,
So powerful, so bottomless,
So manifold, so <term>wondrous</term> <note>original: "wuñerich"</note> and so super-clear,
That I cannot <term>receive</term> <note>original: "enpfân"</note> it
While I remain an earthly being.
I separate for a brief moment
From this poor life,
So that I might never remain there.
Now I must quickly fall silent in speech;
I could receive no more of it
That one might speak of openly.
But I saw Saint Gabriel in <term>wondrous</term> <note>original: "wuñenklicher"</note> honor
Standing in the heavenly heights before God,
Insofar as I, a poor woman, could perceive it.

She then describes a momentary ecstatic state where her message reaches God, though the divine response is too vast for her earthly senses to fully articulate.</summary>

<header>Chapters LVIII—LXI.</header>

He was clothed in 
New, <term>love-fiery</term> <note>original: "miñevůrige"; a word combining "love" and "fire," suggesting a passion that is both spiritual and transformative.</note> garments; these were his reward,
Because he can carry out a true message so honorably.

I could neither understand nor hear his words,
For I am still like an <term>earthly fool</term> <note>original: "irdenschen toren"; likely referring to the limitations of human intellect when compared to divine wisdom.</note>.

### LX. How the child was seen.

In the night when God’s Son was born, the child was seen wrapped in poor cloths and bound with cords. The child lay alone on the hard straw before two animals. Then I spoke to the mother: "O, dear Lady, how long shall your dear child lie alone like this? When will you take him onto your lap?" Then our Lady spoke—though she never let the child out of her sight; she reached out her hands to him <margin>Handwritten: in her hands</margin> and said: "He shall lie on this straw for these seven hours of the night and day. His heavenly Father wills it so." That the heavenly Father was well pleased with this, I perceived then. I prayed to the child for those who had commended themselves to me. Then a voice spoke from the child, though he did not move his mouth at all: "If they will keep me in their <term>memory</term> <note>original: "gehůgenisse"; a deep, meditative remembrance.</note>, then I will keep them in my grace. I have nothing to give them but my body and eternal life." The child lay on the hard straw <note>original: "In presepio"; in the manger</note>, for his heavenly Father willed it so.

### LXI. How one should prepare oneself for God.

When the bird stays long upon the earth, its wings become spoiled and its feathers become heavy. So it lifts itself up into a height and stirs its feathers and draws itself up into a height for so long until it catches the air; then it begins to fly. The longer it flies, the more blissfully it soars, hardly touching the earth at all to refresh itself. In this way, the wings of <term>love</term> <note>original: "miñe"</note> have taken away earthly pleasure. In the same way, we should prepare ourselves for how we are to arrive. We should always stir the feathers of our longing toward God. We should

### Chapter LXII.

...like pilgrims, who had left what they loved and wanted to seek the very best, which is God. Some of these turned back to the <term>worldly pleasure</term> <note>original: "wollust"; in this context, it refers to any sensory or earthly delight that distracts from spiritual goals.</note> they had left behind and did not finish the journey. Some rested in the grass of manifold pleasure and among the flowers of vanity; these remained a very long time on the path. To them shall later be given the very heavy broom of the bitter <term>purgatory</term> <note>original: "vegevůres"; the state of cleansing after death.</note>, even if they lived without <term>mortal sins</term> <note>original: "hǒbetsůnde"; serious sins that lead to spiritual death.</note>.

To this our Lord answered thus: Such people, who walk with good will in holy works and yet have within themselves such difficult habits, and make themselves so unappealing with their <term>irritability</term> <note>original: "swindekeit"; can imply sharpness of temper or a difficult, prickly nature.</note> that one can hardly endure them—in these people my judgment is reserved. They should greatly seek my mercy with humble words; in this way, they would keep their good works from being lost, and the bitterness of their hearts would come to nothing, so that they might come to themselves. He who seeks my mercy cannot endure darkness.

A man went alone on the path. He was in such woe because earthly pleasure could not give a single comfort to his soul. Then he saw two people walking before him. One went on the left hand and the other on the right hand of the road. Then the man asked who they were and what they practiced. Then the one on the left hand spoke:

> I am God’s justice,
> God’s judgment was given to me, it is mine,
> Ever since Adam committed sin in paradise.
> My judgment has been long and great;
> Now this young maiden has come, who walks beside me,
> She has become my companion,
> She is called Mercy.
> All those who seek her and constantly call upon her,
> They overcome all their heart’s sorrow.
> She is most perfect,
> She has taken my <term>authority</term> <note>original: "rehtekeit"; literally "rightness" or "legal standing." Justice is saying Mercy has overmatched his legal claims.</note> from me.
> Whatever grief happens to a person,
> And if they then flee to me with repentance,
> She lays her gentle hand upon the crookedness,
> Then I stand like one who is <term>mute</term> <note>original: "tumbe"; can mean simple, foolish, or speechless. Here, Justice is rendered powerless by Mercy’s intervention.</note>
> And can do nothing against it.
> The true Son of God brings all this about,
> Who has, with His mercy, to me...

We are virgins, noble and well-bred,
And serve God to His praise
In the service of His most beloved queen,
Whom God has chosen above all things—
That is the soul and body of man.
We serve our lady the queen,
So that she, with all diligence and all her senses,
May fulfill her Lord’s will in all things
In Christian order;
Thus she shall never be found guilty.

With your sister, Discretion?" —
We teach my lady the queen,
That she may always be able to distinguish the evil from the good
With divine wisdom

That she may consider how things are now—

Constant longing within the longing,

From the north had come evil spirits; they stayed nearby, for they were required to witness my <term>judgment</term> <note>original: "gerihte"</note>. They had huddled together and were cowed like whipped dogs. They strained their necks toward me as if gagging. I did not fear them; I rejoiced.

### *LXV. How God adorns the soul with suffering.*

When the <term>virgins</term> <note>refers to the "wise virgins" of the Gospel or more broadly to those dedicated to a life of purity and devotion</note> are at all times clothed according to the will of their bridegroom, they need nothing more than wedding garments; that is, that one should be full of <term>suffering</term> <note>original: "pine"; refers to both physical pain and spiritual trial</note> in sickness, in physical ache, in temptation, and in manifold heartfelt suffering, of which we find much in sinful Christendom.
These are the wedding garments of the loving soul; but the workday clothes are fasting, vigils, <term>discipline</term> <note>original: "discipline"; likely refers to the practice of self-flagellation or other ascetic penances</note>, confession, sighs, weeping, prayer, fearing <note>The manuscript reads "these" instead of "the"</note> sin, hard restraint of the senses and the body in God for God's sake, sweet hope and unceasing loving desire, and a prayerful heart without ceasing in all works. These are the workday clothes of the good person. When we are sick, we wear the wedding garments; but when we are healthy, we wear the workday clothes.
Thus speaks the tormented body to the wretched soul:

> When will you fly with the wings of your desire
> Into blissful heights, to Jesus, your eternal love?
> Thank him there, lady, for me,
> Although I may be base and unworthy,
> That he still wanted to be mine
> When he came into <term>exile</term> <note>original: "ellende"; literally "other-land," referring to Christ's incarnation and earthly life as a state of displacement from heaven</note>
> And took our humanity upon himself,
> And pray that he may keep me without guilt
> In his pure grace until a holy end,
> When you, dear soul, turn away from me.

> On the <term>Last Day</term> <note>original: "jungesten tage"; the Day of Judgment</note>.
> Then we shall complain no more,

<term>Obedience</term> <note>original: "gehorsami"</note> is a holy bond; it binds the <term>soul</term> <note>original: "sele"</note> to God, the <term>body</term> <note>original: "lichamen"</note> to Jesus, and the <term>five senses</term> <note>the physical faculties through which one experiences the world, here consecrated to the divine</note> to the <term>Holy Spirit</term>. The longer it binds, the more the soul <term>loves</term> <note>original: "miñet" (from minne); refers to mystical, divine love</note>. The more <term>humbly</term> <note>original: "snőder"; can mean base, lowly, or simple</note> the body carries itself, the more humble its works appear before God and before people with good will.

->*The book ends.*<- <note>original Latin: "Explicit liber"</note>

## Addition concerning the seven canonical hours.

->(By the same hand and from the same period.)<-

One should examine at the hour of **Matins** <note>original: "mettinzit"; the first prayer service of the liturgical day, traditionally held in the darkness of late night or early dawn</note> whether the power of the **Godhead** <note>original: "gotheit"</note> has come into the **soul** <note>original: "sele"</note>, and has drawn the person up from the stinginess of your body and the blindness of your heart. To this belong two witnesses: a binding of the body with searching diligence, and steadfastness of the spirit in God.

One should examine at the hour of **Prime** <note>original: "primezit"; the prayer service for the first hour of daylight, around 6:00 AM</note> whether the wisdom of the Godhead has come into the soul, so that one can recognize perfection and imperfection. To this belong two... <note>The witnesses are not named in the source text.</note>

One should examine at the **mid-morning hour** <note>original: "mittem morgenzeit," corresponding to Terce; the third hour, approximately 9:00 AM</note> whether the fire of the Godhead has come into the soul and has burned away all stains of sin. To this belong two witnesses: a heartfelt longing for our Lord and inward tears for divine love.

One should examine at the **midday hour** <note>original: "mittemtagezit," corresponding to Sext; the sixth hour, approximately 12:00 PM</note> whether the generosity of the Godhead has come into the soul, and has abandoned all worldly friends. To this belong two witnesses: estrangement from all created things, and steadfastness of the spirit in God.

One should examine at the hour of **None** <note>original: "nonezit"; the ninth hour, approximately 3:00 PM, the traditional hour of Christ's death</note> how mercy toward humanity and divine faithfulness brought God to the cross. To this belong two witnesses: that one knows our Lord and that one **loves** <note>original: "miñe"; specifically mystical or divine love</note> Him. However many virtues we might have, we should always have a hunger and a thirst for our Lord.

One should examine at the hour of **Vespers** <note>original: "vesperzit"; the sunset prayer service</note> whether the peace of God has come into the soul, whether the person has peace with God and with all people and with themselves and with all created things. To this belong two witnesses: silence and solitude.

---

<note>The witnesses are not named.</note>

<vocab>Canonical hours, Godhead, soul, Matins, Prime, mid-morning/Terce, midday/Sext, None, Vespers, witnesses, steadfastness</vocab>

<summary>This appendix provides a contemplative guide for the seven canonical hours, instructing the reader to examine their spiritual state at each prayer interval. It links specific divine attributes—such as power, wisdom, and peace—to the progression of the day.</summary>
<keywords>canonical hours, Matins, Prime, Terce, Sext, None, Vespers, spiritual examination, Godhead, soul, witnesses, contemplative life</keywords>

--- Page 315 ---
<meta>This page continues the sequence of the seven canonical hours, concluding with Compline, and transitions into a separate mystical fragment concerning the "abyss" of the soul—a core concept in 14th-century German mysticism.</meta>

One should examine at the hour of **Compline** <note>original: "completezit"; the final prayer service of the liturgical day, observed before sleep</note> whether the divine wonders have come into the soul, which God promised upon the cross. Four witnesses belong to this: that one seeks God diligently, that one keeps Him in heartfelt **love** <note>original: "miñe"; a Middle High German term for mystical or divine love</note>, and that one partakes of Him. The faithful servant of our Lord should never pass a single day without being either in the practice of good works or in the diligence of learning, so that he may teach his senses how they should love God from the heart, or in the experiencing of sweetness, or in the enjoyment of joys. A truly spiritual person is always more concerned about the good fortune of this world—lest it go too well for him—than he is concerned about his own needs. These are the ones who please God well, truly, whether things go well or poorly for them.

---

## Fragment on the mystical life by an unknown author.

The noblest and most useful things that all masters and all **friends of God** <note>original: "gotzvrůnde"; a term for a 14th-century informal movement of people seeking a deep, personal union with God</note> can say about God are the articles of the Christian faith. But now there is a hidden **abyss** <note>original: "abgrunt"; a central mystical concept referring to the bottomless, uncreated depth of the soul where it meets the essence of God</note> in the soul, which calls out without ceasing with a wild, abyssal, and incomprehensible voice <gloss>out of</gloss> the divine abyss, whenever it is revealed to the **reason** <note>original: "vernunft"; the intellectual faculty</note> as if in the blink of an eye. Then the soul is snatched into a wondrously great hunt after it, yet it cannot be attained in this time. But the highest, most useful, and noblest thing that can happen to the soul here is that it should draw all words, all thoughts, all desires, all love—the soul entirely—after itself, and sink and drown them in the divine abyss; and that the reason should bring forth however much it can. Yet that is still nothing but <gloss>only</gloss> the alms-bowl and the **crumbs** <note>original: "brosemen"</note> that fall from the Lord’s table. And however high and transcendent it may be and appear to the reason, as if it were given to her as her own through love, she can never keep it better or more securely than by losing it again through love in the divine, bottomless abyss, where alone all things are held eternally.

<summary>This page concludes the reflections on the canonical hours with Compline and introduces a mystical treatise on the "abyss of the soul," describing the soul's longing to sink into the incomprehensible depth of God.</summary>
<keywords>Compline, divine love, abyss of the soul, Friends of God, mystical union, reason vs. faith, spiritual discipline</keywords>

--- Page 316 ---
<meta>This page concludes the mystical fragment, focusing on the "daily food" of the soul—the balanced spiritual life that integrates inner contemplation with outer action and warns against the dangers of false spiritual freedom.</meta>

But the daily food, which must necessarily remain for the **inner and the outer person** <note>a common mystical distinction between the spiritual soul and the physical/sensory self</note> in this life, is a rational awareness of the **order of God** <note>original: "ordenunge gotz"; the divine arrangement or harmony of all things</note> toward God, toward oneself, and toward one's fellow man. This food is sufficient in whatever way it presents itself: whether it drives one toward the sacrament, or toward an inner, **detached rest** <note>original: "abgescheiden růwe"; a state of peaceful withdrawal from worldly attachments, central to the concept of "Gelassenheit"</note>, or toward an inner rational striving to recognize divine truth, or toward prayer, or toward revelations, or toward spiritual vision, or toward divine sweetness, or toward outward **works of love** <note>original: "miñewerken"; acts of charity or devotion motivated by *Minne*, or divine love</note>, or toward a rational, loving, striving, and intimate conversation among the **friends of God** <note>original: "gotzvrůnde"</note> about the noblest divine truth. 

And everything that is born and gained anew here should be shared such that the noblest part is "lost" and offered back into the aforementioned **abyss** <note>original: "abgrůnde"; referring to the mystical union where the individual self is surrendered into God's infinite, bottomless depth</note>, while the rest feeds the aforementioned order in a single, increasing growth of divine wisdom in Christ Jesus. This alone is true **voluntary poverty** <note>original: "willigů armůt"; a spiritual state of having no personal desires or "ownership" of one's own will or experiences</note> and the most perfect life, which all true friends of God pursue. Whoever is "born" in any other way remains and falls into various disordered and painful states that God never answers; or they fall into a disordered "freedom of the spirit" <note>a reference to the "Heresy of the Free Spirit," a medieval movement where individuals claimed to be so united with God that they were above moral laws and the Church</note>, which is the most harmful fall, or they turn back to the world. ¹)

---

¹) In the manuscript, three blank pages follow, with which the fourteenth **quire** <note>original: "Sextern"; a gathering of six folded sheets of parchment or paper used to construct the book</note> ends.

---

<summary>This page describes the "daily food" of the spiritual life as a balanced ordering of one's relationship with God and others, emphasizing that all spiritual gains must be returned to the "divine abyss." It concludes with a warning against the "freedom of the spirit," a common medieval concern regarding mystical overreach.</summary>
<keywords>daily food, inner and outer man, divine order, works of love, voluntary poverty, abyss, friends of God, freedom of the spirit, quire</keywords>

# Some Word Explanations.
---

Abe, from that / thereof.
abegunst, envy.
achter = after, toward (a goal).
adan, breath, also atten, aten.
agestein, amber, lodestone <note>a naturally magnetized mineral</note>.
ahte, aht, eight <note>can also refer to 'attention' depending on context</note>.
alleine, although.
ambaht, office, service.
amehtikeit, powerlessness / fainting.
anderhalp, on the other side.
anderwarbe, once again.
aneth, without.
ar, eagle.
arnen, to atone, to do penance.
artedine, female guardian of treasure.
arzat, physician; arzatine, female physician.
ass, rather than.
aten, atten, see adan.
aureole, nimbus, glory <note>the halo or radiance surrounding a sacred figure</note>.
Bagen, to quarrel, to scold.
bat, bath.
beiten, to wait, to go without.
bekeme, pleasant / agreeable.
bekeñen, to know / recognize.
bekoren, to tempt; bekorung, temptation.
bermint, parchment.
beſagen, to slander.
beſlagen, struck / hammered.
beſmen, broom, rod <note>often referring to a bundle of twigs used for discipline</note>.
bewellen, bewollen, to stain / defile.
bewaren, to perceive / notice.
bewiſen, to show, to point toward.
beworcht, wrought? <note>worked or created</note>.
bibenen, biben, to tremble / quake.
biñen, within; also: to consider oneself to be something.
bleken, to show, to be exposed.
bliken, to flash, to shine.
blûme (the), virginity <note>literally "the flower"</note>.
bobe, over, above.
bobenheit, highness / majesty.
borien, drill / auger.

<column-break/>

Bremen, zubremen, to grumble, to mutter at someone. <note>original Latin: "fremere", meaning to growl or roar</note>.
brôde, brodekeit, frail / fragile.
bruch, space? <note>room or area</note>.
bruchung, enjoyment / use.
bulge, wave, billow. Also a drinking vessel.
bûrnen, to burn.
bûten, to offer.
C see K.
Dahte, wick.
den, to stretch.
deñe, dañe, then.
der, whose / of which.
dilker, eradicator / extinguisher.
doln, to bear, to endure.
drahte, pregnancy, from "to carry" <note>referring to the gestation or 'carrying' of a child</note>.
druhten, to serve, to care for.
dürfen, to need / require.
durnehtig, perfect, whole.
E, before, previously.
ê, law <note>in Middle High German, "ê" often refers specifically to divine or natural law</note>.
eb; before.
egeslich, egeſtlich, hideous / dreadful.
ehte, married couple?
eigenſchlich, belonging to.
einvaltig, simple / single-minded.
eiſen, to shudder, to be terrified.
eiſunge, shuddering / horror.
enbeiſen, enbizen, to partake / to enjoy <note>often used in the context of eating or experiencing something spiritual</note>.
end, place / end.
engetar (I), I dare not / I am not permitted.
entgelten, to make amends / to let stand.
enthalten, to restrain / hold back.
entreinen, to defile / soil.
entrichten, to confuse / disarrange.
entriſen, reis, rirn, to fall away / to escape from.
entſchulden (oneself), to complain / to excuse oneself.
entwichen, to soften / yield.
erarnen, to atone for.
erdriezen, to be weary of, to be oversatiated.
erſchellen, to resound / ring out.
ervlougen, to put to flight. <note>original Latin: "fugere", meaning to flee</note>.
erwegen, to stir up / excite.

# Word Explanations.
---

Ettesweñe, etzweñe: Sometime, approximately.
**F**, see **V**.
Gaden: House (monastery).
gebende: Women's head-dress.
gebur: Peasant.
gebürlich: Decent, well-mannered.
gedenke: Thoughts.
gegen: Against, toward.
gegerwe: Holy adornment, vestment <note>specifically a garment used for the Mass</note>.
gehúgenisse, gehúgnisse: Memory / remembrance.
gelass: Garment?
gelôte: Weights for a scale.
gelten: To be valid (active), see "Debt."
gemeine: Commonly / in common.
geneiste: Sparks.
genemen: To name.
genenden: To venture / to dare.
gere: Desire / longing.
geringe: Light, nimble.
gerúchen: To deign, to please.
getempert: Properly tuned, ordered.
getrosten: To do without / to lack.
geweñen: To wean / to unaccustom.
gewete: Garment.
gift: Dowry, morning-gift <note>a traditional gift from a groom to his bride the morning after the wedding</note>.
grañe: Beard of an ear of grain, mustache.
grans: Beak, snout.
grein, greinen: To grumble, to mutter.
grel: Shrill / glaring.
grendel: Bolt / bar.
grinen: (Same as "greinen") to mutter.
groiren: To glory / to boast.
Harte: Very / strongly.
heimlich: Familiar / intimate.
herten: To persevere / to endure.
hinderrede: Back-biting (evil talk).
hitzen: To become hot.
hohen: To exalt / to raise.
hor: Filth / mud <note>Original text notes masculine gender and the genitive form "horwes"</note>.
horwetig: Filthy / muddy.
húffe: Cheek.
húfhaltz, húffehalz: Lame in the hip.
húgenisse, húge: Joy.
húlzin: Wooden.
hungerlachen, hungertúch: "Hunger cloth" <note>a long cloth used during Lent to veil the altars</note>.
Iergen: Anywhere / somehow.
joch: And yet.
jtal: Empty.
juncherre: Young lord / squire.
**K** and **C**.
careñe: Quadragesima <note>The forty days of Lent</note>.
klelich: Fertile <note>derived from "kle" meaning seed</note>.
clote: Cell / hermitage.
kopf, köpfe: Cup / beaker.
kosen: To choose, to elect.
kouwen, kiuwen: Palate / jaw.

Krank: Weak / sick.

kriegen: To cry out, to quarrel.

culter, culteren: Cover over the mattress.
kume: Hardly / scarcely.
kúnde: Knowledge.
kuñe: Lineage, kinship.
Langen: To reach; *aft langen*? <note>to reach after?</note>.

Magen: Strength, also relatives.

Olei: Oil; *oleien*: to give the last anointing.

Peize, beize: Lure / bait.

Qwelen: To suffer.
Ram: Throat, abyss.

rief: Hoarfrost <note>original Latin: "pruina"</note>.

schôpnisse: Creation.
schreigen: To shout at.
schriken: To jump / to spring.

# Word Explanations.

Sege, Net.
segen (one’s self), to bless.
seist, you say.
selwen, to discolor or fade.
semlich, collectively or all together.
sere, Wound.
sid dem male, since then or because.
siech, sick or ill.
simelen, Fine bread rolls <note>original: "Semmel"; typically refers to high-quality wheat bread</note>.
sinkrank, weak-minded or feebleminded.
sinwel, sine-welle, Curvature or vaulting.
sleht, simple or plain.
slinden, sclinden, to swallow or gulp down.
smaken, (neuter), to be or become shameful or insignificant.
snôd, poor or despised.
sogetan, sogtan, such or of such a kind.
sômer, Beast of burden or pack animal.
spengen, to stretch or span.
sprechen, to be called or named.
stein, Stone or rock.
stral, Arrow.
stůle, Throne; stůlen, to sit on a throne.
stuppe, Dust.
sůmlich, someone; sůmliche, some or several.
sůnlich, childlike or filial.
surôgge, bleary-eyed <note>likely related to "triefäugig," describing eyes that are red or running</note>.
sus, thus or so.
swarheit, heaviness or gravity.
swindekeit, a fierce, intense, or tough nature.
Tepet, Carpet, tapestry, or wall-hanging.
togen, dogen, to be useful or fit.
tôren, to act foolishly or be fooled.
torsten, to be allowed or to dare.
tôtlich, mortal.
tris, Treasure; triskamer, treasury or treasure chamber.
trisemvas, Treasure-vessel or treasure.
tumbe, Fool or simpleton (dumb).
twagen, to pinch or tweak.
twahen, to wash or to make colorful.
tynavel from *tiñe*, Battlement <note>original: "Zinne"</note>. Also a pediment or paneling <note>original: "frontispicium"</note>.
Ueberhere, exceedingly great or oversized.
ufwegen, to weigh upwards or counterbalance.
ulin, Cave or hollow.
umbetal, circumference or scope.
unberhaftig, barren or infertile.
undersniden, to cut between or contrast.
ungebe, worthless.
ungewandelt, uncompensated or unpracticed?
unschuldigen, to clear of guilt or to excuse.
unsehelich, invisible.
urlůg, War.
ûwele, Owl.

Var, Deception or trickery.
vare, Color.
vederschlagen, the flapping of wings or fluttering.
verdůmet, damned or condemned.
vergebens, in vain.
verkiesen, to forget or overlook.
verslinden, to devour or swallow up.
verwahsen, to become weak, to be cursed, or overgrown.
verworchten, to forfeit <note>for example, "to forfeit grace"</note>.
verzehren, to consume or make use of.
vielaten, Violets.
vôgen, to join or fit together.
volburt, Confirmation; *voll-borten* means to agree or confirm.
volger, Companion or follower.
volleist, full performance or effect.
voren, to lead or guide.
vôre, why.
vôrhtelin, a small fear or slight dread.
vosspor, Footprint.
vreislich, terrible or dreadful.
vriesen, to freeze.
vrom, foreign or strange.
vrome (the), Joy.
vrômelich, useful—from *frumen*, meaning benefit or use.
vrôwen, to gladden or rejoice.
vůlen, fûlen, to feel or perceive.
vuoge, skillfully joined or artistic.
Waffen! Woe! <note>An old exclamation of distress or a cry for help</note>.
wage (the), Cradle.
wan, because or for.
war, whence or whither.
wegen, to turn or move.
weinig, sorrowful or distressed.
werlich, constant or durable.
westbâre, <italic>Plural</italic>, children who died soon after baptism.
wil, meanwhile or while.
wirren, werren, to hinder, prevent, or defend.
wlu, as in "Eya wlú miñe," meaning full love <note>The term "Minne" refers to courtly or divine love</note>.
wor, in which or wherein.
wôstunge, Desert, wasteland, or devastation.
wrang, wrestling or to struggle <note>Latin: "luctari"</note>.
wunderlich, strange, moody, or wonderful.
Zage, fearful or cowardly.
zagel, Tail.
zelen, to aim, beget, or count.
zendal, half-silk fabric or buckram-taffeta.
zihen, to accuse or charge.
ziñen, to create or manage?
zôfer, Magic or sorcery.
